# Runtime architecture

This document owns the shape of the runtime: what the Rust core holds, what the shell may do, what Herdr keeps for itself, and the decisions that were made when those boundaries were crossed the wrong way.
"The shell" means the web shell (`web/`) drawn by hided's snapshot, whether that page is open in a browser or inside the Electron desktop host (`desktop/`).
Read it in full before changing anything under `herdr-core/`, `hided/`, `web/` or `desktop/`.
[AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md) keeps the short form of the ownership split; this is the long form with the reasons.
The code is the executable authority: `herdr-core/src/` for the core, `hided/src/` for the daemon that hosts it, `web/src/` for the shell, `desktop/src/main/` for the desktop host, and the tests beside each.

## State placement and publication

Choose the authority, publication need and durability of a value separately before adding a field, store or worker.
`Mutex<Runtime>` serializes Hide's accepted domain and UI transitions; it is not the place to perform the reads, writes or analyses that produce them.
An off-lock store remains core-owned and must name its scope, freshness rule, resource bound and shutdown owner; a second copy in the shell cannot become an authority.

| Decision | Placement and allowed work | Existing precedent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A value decides the accepted UI or domain transition | Keep the authoritative in-memory value in `Runtime`; admit one typed event and apply a completed worker result under a short lock. | [Stored primary checkout](#stored-primary-checkout), [Workspaces in the web shell](#workspaces-in-the-web-shell); `runtime/workspace_view.rs`. |
| Producing a value needs filesystem, SQLite, network, a subprocess or substantial analysis | Take owned request data under the lock, perform the work in the existing reader/worker boundary, and validate scope or generation when applying the result. | [Project sessions and Memory](#project-sessions-and-memory), [Agent labels in the core](#agent-labels-in-the-core); `runtime/memory.rs`, `labels/analyzer.rs`, `reader.rs`. |
| A transition changes what the operator can see | Publish the changed state through the existing coalescing notifier; a wake, unchanged read or repeated observation alone is not a reason to publish. | [The core, the shell and Herdr](#the-core-the-shell-and-herdr); `handle.rs::ChangeNotifier`, `session_sync/coordinator.rs::agent_tick_needs_publish`. |
| A value must survive restart but is not a screen transition | Save it without publishing a new frame merely to announce the save. | [Agent sleep](#agent-sleep): the last-look stamp is saved when visited and on its minute pass; `runtime/agent_sleep.rs::tick_agent_sleep`. |
| Time passing can be derived from an accepted value | Publish the reference time once and derive the display in memory. | [Agent labels in the core](#agent-labels-in-the-core): `changed_at_unix_ms` and the shared elapsed clock in `web/src/components/elapsed.tsx`. |
| Accepted UI state needs persistence | Reuse the coalesced save: one active worker and one pending flag per store, copying state under the lock and serializing, writing, syncing and renaming outside it. | [Projects and Overview cost contract](PERFORMANCE_TESTING.md#projects-and-overview-cost-contract); `runtime/agents.rs`, `runtime/workspace_view.rs`, `persistence.rs`, `workspace_views.rs`. |

On a high-frequency path such as keystroke ingress, added observational work is limited to bounded in-memory writes.
It must not introduce lock waiting, disk access, a subprocess or large serialization into that path.
The existing short Runtime admission is not permission to add another contended lock or blocking observer; pass owned data to an existing off-lock owner when work needs those resources.
Snapshot capture takes immutable typed sections under the lock and `handle.rs::snapshot_delta_payload` transfers them out of the core owner thread.
The WebSocket transport encodes the selected sections and its envelope once on a blocking transport worker; internal roots and phone followers use the retained typed state without decoding transport bytes.
Each rest field has its own revision and `Arc`, with navigator selection and lists, terminal metadata fields and status fields stamped independently; an unchanged value keeps its allocation and a changed value alone is copied.
Usage lives in its own top-level snapshot section, revisioned independently of navigation; later deltas carry it only when limits or ledger values change.
An omitted usage section preserves the previous value; an empty `nodes` array clears it, and a future revision cursor resets to a full snapshot.
The shell stores usage separately, so a usage-only delta preserves navigation identity and an unrelated menu or layout change neither serializes nor compares historical day arrays.
Pane input never enters the coalesced persistence queue: it must not be dropped or merged to reduce observer cost.
The save workers' pending flags bound the number of pending saves, not the byte size of the saved state; these stores have no general serialized-byte cap.
`Core::drop` joins the UI and Workspace-view save workers outside the mutex, but forced termination cannot promise a pending save reached disk (`handle.rs`).
Before adding resident work, use the [tick/timer/watcher ledger and cost review](PERFORMANCE_TESTING.md#resident-ticks-timers-and-watchers).

### Sidebar tab names

`TabSnapshot.sidebar` serializes the existing `TabNaming` as a manual name and stable tab number, using the same automatic-number convention as the tabbar.
The automatic task label remains the tabbar's own projection; the shell never guesses a sidebar name from that label.
The shell derives tab segments and physical agent counts from the snapshot and keeps a root's complete subtree under that root's tab, even for cross-tab or cross-device children.
A tab has no disclosure state: the former `sidebar_collapsed_tabs` field and its `sidebar_tab_expanded` event are gone, and a state file that still carries the field loads without error and loses it on the next save.
Nothing in this feature writes state; typing and terminal echo add no event, I/O, timer or subprocess for it.

### View openers and comments

Who opened a View and the operator's comments on Views are core state (PRD view-opener-feedback, `herdr-core/src/view_feedback.rs`, `herdr-core/src/runtime/view_feedback.rs`).
A View's opener lives on its display in `workspace-views.json`, kept by device, Herdr terminal id, native session and provider, never by pane id, which a restarted Herdr server gives another shell; the pane is found again from terminal and session each time a snapshot is made, and an opener found nowhere is shown closed.
It is written only by a workspace control open whose pane caller Hide proved and by a terminal-link open that carries the clicked pane, and a navigate the operator types clears it; a caller with no proven pane writes none, which `opener.none` in the diagnostic log records with its reason.
A checkout's comments live beside its Views in the same file under `feedback`, saved by the existing coalesced save: at most 50 per View and 500 per checkout, each note at most 4000 characters, each quoted range at most 20 lines, checked in the core; each add, edit, delete, undo, relocate and Send is one event.
The workspace-view snapshot carries `feedback` only while there are comments or a Send to say: the comments of the Views on screen, the recipient groups, the opened Views per pane, and the last Send's state; each display carries its unsent count.
Finding a comment's lines again after a save is the shell's work on the text it already holds, reported once per change as a `relocate` event, so nothing reads a file for it under the lock or on a keystroke.
A Send runs on one feedback worker off the lock with at most 64 jobs: it reads the screenshots from the node that staged them, places them on the recipient's node, then stores the letter as the operator ([delivery.md](delivery.md#the-operators-view-feedback)); no screenshot byte passes through an event, the lock or a snapshot.
Before the core, hided keeps each operator window's comments and Sends on one queue per window, at most 256 waiting and four of them with a screenshot: it stages a comment's screenshot off the socket loop and hands the events to the core in the order the window made them, so a Send never overtakes the comment before it, and a full queue is refused to that window as an error.
The worker waits on a moving core's effect hold, and the comments, openers and waiting Sends travel with the move like the rest of `workspace-views.json`.

## The core, the shell and Herdr

The core (`herdr-core`) owns all state behind one `Mutex<Runtime>`.
The shell dispatches typed JSON events in and receives snapshot and delta frames out over hided's WebSocket (see hided and the WebSocket boundary) when the change notifier announces.
The event sync coordinator (`session_sync/coordinator.rs`) delegates Herdr snapshot and subscription lifecycle to `session_sync/subscription.rs`, opens `events.subscribe` first and reads `session.snapshot` second, applies the topology events that follow, and refreshes agent telemetry with `agent.list` once per second.
The thread that applies Herdr's events never blocks on anything else (PRD instant-pane-topology D-13): `agent.list`, a new pane's `pane.get`, a workspace's `workspace.get` and the `agent.get` of a start Herdr holds open each open a connection, so they run on one sync-read worker (`session_sync/sync_reads.rs`) with one read of each kind in flight and the answer returned as a coordinator message fenced by subscription generation; `lsof` and the Codex usage request run on their readers' `BackgroundRead` threads, and the label store writes `labels.json` on its own thread.
Their output and intervals are what they were before the move.
Agent labels are made by the core itself and shown only for the session they were proven for; see Agent labels in the core under hided and the WebSocket boundary, and [status-model.md](status-model.md#task-identity) for how they reach a row.
Tab names use core focus and the same agent row as the sidebar; process names arrive through a coordinator-owned off-lock `pane.process_info` reader on each host's connector.
Its single worker reads at most the five attached tabs' focused panes, on focus or agent-state changes or a 30-second recheck, rejects obsolete generations, and publishes only changed names.
Stopping its coordinator cancels the rest of the batch and joins the active read off the runtime lock.
Small process responses have a one-second absolute read deadline and a 64 KiB cap; SSH session contention, connection setup, socket discovery, channel opening and disconnect each use the remote transport's 15-second bound, so shutdown can include those setup and cleanup stages.
`rename_tab` keeps retry intent separate from the committed label; only Herdr success commits it, and a refusal or timeout preserves the prior label and a request-correlated inline retry result.
Herdr's stream carries no sequence and cannot be resumed from a position, so subscribing before the snapshot is the only way not to lose an event between the two, and every reconnect is a fresh subscription followed by a fresh snapshot.
A subscriber that falls behind Herdr's retained event history gets an `events_lost` error and its connection closed (Herdr skipped those events silently before 0.9.2); the Socket API's remedy is that same fresh subscription and snapshot, so the coordinator logs `subscription.events_lost` and reconnects on the usual schedule without publishing a failure, because Herdr is still answering; the last projection stays on screen until the snapshot replaces it.
Only a reconnect that then fails reaches the screen, and the reconnect delay (100 ms after a successful connect) bounds a stream that keeps falling behind to one subscription and snapshot per interval; any other subscription error is still published as stale.
The price of that order is that an event emitted just before the snapshot was taken arrives as well; for one second after the snapshot the replica reconciles (`ApplyMode::Reconcile`), dropping with a diagnostic an event the snapshot already accounts for, and after that window an event the replica cannot apply is a real divergence that rebuilds it.
Herdr 0.9.1 also orders some events after their cause, and the replica models that order rather than resyncing on it, because a resync is a window in which the latest close is still unconfirmed and Reopen Closed Tab is refused.
When `workspace_created` names the first active tab before `tab_created` supplies its membership, that workspace waits for the tab's complete layout before publication, so the incomplete event cannot retire a pane already projected by the creation worker.
Closing a workspace's last tab or pane emits `workspace_closed` before the `tab_closed` or `pane_closed` that caused it, and a workspace's creation events arrive after the call that created it returns: a seed tab that `layout.apply` replaced is announced, closed, then focused, given a pane and laid out, and a tab created with focus right after its workspace is announced as focused before it is announced at all.
The replica remembers the latest ids an event removed (`retired_ids`), and an event about one of them that is absent from the replica is already applied and logged as `event.already_applied`; it holds a focus event whose tab or pane has not arrived (`early_focuses`) and applies it when that tab or pane does, unless a focus Herdr applied later superseded it; it holds a `pane_created` or `layout_updated` that reaches it before its tab's `tab_created` (`early_children`, seen on CI) and applies them in arrival order when the tab does, because a dropped layout left the tab waiting for one that had already come and froze its workspace on screen; a tab created in a workspace waiting for its close, by `tab_created` or `pane_moved`, cancels the wait and reads the workspace's active tab from Herdr.
Each is bounded (64 retired ids, 16 held focuses, 64 held panes and layouts), and anything else the replica cannot apply is still a divergence that rebuilds it.
A pane announced by `pane_created` carries a cwd Herdr 0.9.1 can get wrong (`/tmp` for a tab created in a project, seen on CI), and on a loaded machine a read right afterwards can still answer a folder above the project (seen on Linux CI under load in 3 of 300 new tabs), while Herdr sends no event when the cwd settles and `agent.list` lists agent terminals only.
So the replica holds such a pane's cwd as unconfirmed, with the last cwd it held and how many times it was read, and the coordinator asks the sync-read worker for the pane's `pane.get` right after the event; until that first answer lands no publish groups the pane under the event's cwd: a pane laid out beside a confirmed one publishes at once with that sibling's cwd standing in, as does a pane in a tab Hide created (PRD instant-pane-topology B20), and only a pane with neither holds the publish; an answer that repeats the cwd before it (the event's, for the first read) confirms the pane and publishes only when a publish was held for it or the stand-in it was shown at is not the cwd it confirms, since otherwise the stand-in already published what it confirms (PRD grid-ahead-of-herdr D-12), and a different one is published, logs `pane_cwd.corrected` and keeps the pane waiting.
A waiting pane is read again through the same worker on the 250 ms coordinator tick below, starting a whole tick after its first read so two answers never come from the same instant, and a tick the worker cannot take yet leaves it due for the next one; after 8 reads the last answer stands and `pane_cwd.unsettled` is logged.
The first snapshot can be read while Herdr creates a pane, and then holds the same early cwd while the creation event arrives inside the reconcile window and is skipped as already applied; that skipped event marks the pane unconfirmed too, so the read still happens and a corrected cwd publishes again; without it the pane's tab stayed under the parent folder's project row (seen on CI as a project that never appeared).
At most 64 panes wait at once; a pane past the cap keeps its event cwd and logs `pane_cwd.unconfirmed_over_cap`, and a failed read rebuilds the replica like a rejected event.
`wire.rs` names the day Herdr sequences its stream: a test fails when the event schema declares `sequence` or the subscribe params take `after_sequence`, because a resume cursor would then be worth building back.
A separate 250 ms coordinator tick advances every bounded asynchronous operation, so an acknowledgement or topology wait reaches its deadline even when Herdr emits no event.
A tick whose `agent.list` is unchanged publishes nothing, so an idle session recomputes no projection; the catalog's own refresh window still publishes, because the rebuild can only happen inside `publish_replica`.
The pinned Herdr settles an `agent.start` only when the agent's state moves again or it is read with `agent.get`, never on `agent.list` and never when the start's settle delay or deadline passes (herdrdev/herdr#3853), so an agent whose first turn ends within the 3-second settle delay, as Pi's and a resumed OpenCode's do, stays `launch_pending` and refuses letters and renames until something reads it.
Each `agent.list` answer is therefore scanned once for agents listed `idle` or `done` with `launch_pending` (`session_sync/launch_settle.rs`), and the starts among them that Hide made are read with `agent.get` through the sync-read worker, which settles them on Herdr's side; what they settle to arrives with the next `agent.list`, and the flag itself never reaches the shell.
Only Hide's own starts are read, because a read past a start's deadline is where Herdr applies the deadline and clears the agent's name, and Herdr's agent list carries neither a start's time nor its deadline.
Every start Hide makes asks for the same deadline (120 s, `AGENT_START_DEADLINE`), and `agent_start` records when Herdr took each one, by the start's terminal, which no other Herdr server names; a start is read from 3 s after that (Herdr's settle delay, before which a read settles nothing) until 90 s after it, well inside its deadline.
A pending start Hide has no record of is cold and never read: one an operator made with `herdr agent start`, whose `--timeout` may be anything above 3 s, or one Hide made before this daemon started, since the records live in the daemon's memory; it is logged once as `launch_settle.cold` with its pane id, and it stays pending, taking no letter or rename, until its agent's state moves.
A start past its 90 s is logged once as `launch_settle.window_passed` and left to Herdr in the same way.
A batch reads at most 4 panes, one batch is out at a time and no pane is in a second batch before its first answered, a start is read at most once a second and at most 8 times, after which `launch_settle.exhausted` is logged once with its pane id and it is left to Herdr, and at most 64 starts are tracked, one past that logging `launch_settle.over_limit` once; at most 256 start records are kept, each dropped once its 90 s have passed, and one past that drops the oldest and logs `launch_settle.start_limit`.
A start leaves the set when a list shows it otherwise or not at all: listed with its start closed it logs `launch_settle.settled` with its read count, closed or no longer at rest with its start still open it logs `launch_settle.left`, and a failed read logs `launch_settle.read_failed` without touching the subscription.
The set is keyed by terminal and kept across the coordinator's reconnects, so a reconnect neither refills a start's reads nor reports a cold one again, and a pane id a restarted server reuses is never taken for an earlier start.
When herdrdev/herdr#3853 is fixed in the pinned Herdr, the workaround is deleted whole: `session_sync/launch_settle.rs` with its start records and tests; `SyncRead::Launches`, `SyncReadAnswer::Launches` and the fourth sync-read worker in `session_sync/sync_reads.rs`, whose per-kind arrays go back to three; `fetch_agent_launch` in `session_sync/subscription.rs` and its re-export; the coordinator's `launch_settle` observe call and answer arm; `ProjectedAgent::launch_pending` and `ProjectedAgent::terminal_id` with their lines in `wire.rs`'s `From<res::AgentInfo>`, and `wire::started_agent_terminal`; the `record_start` call in `agent_start.rs`; and those two fields' lines in the test literals of `coordination/lineage.rs`, `coordination/hook_tokens.rs` and `coordination/identity_wait.rs`, with the end-to-end test in `session_sync/tests.rs`.
Before the coordinator's first path answer, a registered checkout's empty tab list cannot prune its saved Agent area layout, and a saved selection whose pane still exists keeps its restore hint until the path facts settle its checkout.
Only an unknown directory of the saved pane holds that restore choice; another pane's unanswered directory cannot delay a known restore or keep a removed checkout selected.
An answered missing path or a pane Herdr no longer holds is still a removal; the absence of an answer alone is not.
A publish whose catalog inputs did not move costs nothing per agent, tab or checkout beyond the projection itself (D-18): the catalog is rebuilt only when the registrations, the session's spaces or the worktrees change or its refresh window passes, the purpose mirror is synced only when that catalog or the unconfirmed created purposes change, and the core compares paths by their names while the node resolves them, repositories and branch notes included, only when the set of paths asked changes or the catalog's refresh passes (`workspace::PathIndex`), so an agent status change repeats none of them and the worktree projection under the lock walks no path.
The node's answer for the paths asked stands until that set changes or the refresh passes, so a path created or relinked inside that window is read as the node last answered for up to `CATALOG_REFRESH_INTERVAL`.
The Git context refreshes local worktree state when a watched Git HEAD, index or ref changes, or Herdr worktree topology changes; content-only working-tree edits wait for the next of those.
Worktree and pull request readers run outside the runtime mutex, and a changed repository reuses the other projects' cached answers.
While the Overview's Projects tab is visible it sends `disk_watch {workspace_ids}` with the projects in the order it shows them, and `[]` when it is hidden (`herdr-core/src/runtime/disk_sweep.rs`): the core measures them one at a time on the sweep reader beside the right panel's request, every worktree and the shared Git directory of a project in one measurement that counts each inode once, keeps each answer by path while the daemon runs and treats it as fresh for ten minutes, and never measures a device it dials; each workspace row carries the result as `disk`, absent from the wire until measured, with `measuring`, `queued` and the last size kept beside a failure's reason.
Selecting a checkout re-measures its project for the right panel.
The measurement runs on the checkouts' node and sorts each checkout's blocks into build cache, dependencies, worktree source and other (`hide-host/src/disk_layers.rs`), and the workspace row's `disk` adds the volume's free space and the project's layer totals; folder paths stay in the core and never reach the wire.
The disk cleanup sheet reuses the merged-worktree cleanup events: `cleanup_review {workspace_id}` reviews one Git project of C's or of a node that dials C (an M project's runs on M, see [A core on another machine](#a-core-on-another-machine)), `cleanup_confirm {id, paths, cells}` empties the chosen build cache and dependency cells and removes the chosen worktrees after closing their panes through the delete path's Herdr close, and the review, progress and result ride the reviewed project's `cleanup` snapshot, one active cleanup per daemon.
Pull requests are read for every registered local Git project from the moment a window attaches (at most 64), asked for again five minutes after the project's last answer (30 seconds, doubling to five minutes, after a read that failed), and refreshed from a project's menu or PR popover; all of these reuse the same background reader, cache and generation coalescing.
A repository whose remotes name nothing `gh` could read is answered "no GitHub remote" with no `gh` call, from the remotes its node reads with the catalog's path facts (`PathFacts::remotes`, from the same config read as the branch notes, the repository each remote names and never its URL), and is asked again only when those remotes change or on a refresh; see [status-model.md](status-model.md#github-status-in-the-workspace-row).
A pull request a local agent's session prints the address of, and that the last answer does not hold, has its project read without waiting for the five-minute re-read: the local label worker's read keeps each such sighting under fifteen minutes old (32 at most between hand-offs), and the coordinator passes them to `Runtime::read_sighted_pull_requests` under the brief lock that hands the worker the pull request times back; see [status-model.md](status-model.md#github-status-in-the-workspace-row) for which project is read and when.
The last successful answer per project is saved to `github-snapshot.json` in the state folder by a store thread of its own (`github_store.rs`: one thread, one pending slot, an atomic replacement through `hide_platform::fs`, joined when the runtime drops) and restored before the first frame as stale, so a restart shows the previous run's pull requests until the first read replaces them; see [status-model.md](status-model.md#github-status-in-the-workspace-row).
The Changes reader is also the only Git-status owner for Explorer decorations: it reads the checkout in front, on this machine or a device, while a renderer is attached and Explorer or Changes is visible or a diff tab or new-tab page needs it, and publishes one root-scoped changed-file set for every surface.
With no renderer attached it reads nothing, and the Explorer watch in hided stamps nothing (`WatchService::set_drawn`, set with the core's `ui_attached`); the selection the operator left is kept, so the first renderer back reads the same checkout again.
The read is `Call::Changes` to that checkout's host (`hide-host/src/git.rs`), which runs Git with its working directory at the opened root, normalizes rename and conflict state, and answers only the registered folder's paths; the reader (`changes.rs`) runs it on a `BackgroundRead` thread every two seconds or on a changed request, driven by its own pump (`ChangesPump`) that the core starts once rather than by a Herdr session's coordinator, so a device's History answers even when this machine runs no Herdr; a device helper that failed is not asked again by the refresh.
The Explorer tree (`web/src/ExplorerTree.tsx`) derives file and ancestor-folder decorations from that snapshot in memory; it never starts Git from a row, scroll, hover, or paint.
Pane output never enters the runtime; it goes from the pane's node to the screen (see The terminal path).
Everything the shell renders comes from that one snapshot pull.
The shell decodes it strictly: one string value it does not know fails the whole decode, the bridge keeps its last good frame, and every later notification repeats the failure until the two sides agree, so an unknown value is a stalled shell, not a blank field.
The string enums the core serializes into the snapshot are therefore pinned in `contracts/snapshot-wire-enums.json`; `model.rs` tests that each variant emits the listed value and `SnapshotWireEnumTests` that each listed value decodes, so a variant added on one side fails a suite before it can reach a running shell (a `PullRequestTitle` origin once shipped as `pull_request_title` against a shell that read `pr_title`).
The bridge publishes `bridgeError` only on change and names the coding path in it, because a repeated failure republished every notification rebuilt every view observing the bridge at the notification rate.

## Stored primary checkout

A local registered project stores `primary_checkout_id` on its `WorkspaceRegistration` in `core-state.json`.
An absent choice keeps the root default for existing registrations; a stored choice remains stored while its checkout is unavailable.
The single `set_primary_checkout` event carries `{workspace_id, checkout_id}`, sent by a checkout row's `Set as default checkout`, and accepts only an existing checkout of that registered local Git project.
Missing projects or checkouts, plain folders and remote registrations are refused with a diagnostic, without changing the selection or presenting an alert.
The catalog projects `CheckoutSnapshot.is_primary` after assigning registration identities and adding Git worktrees; sorting and inactive folding consume that flag, and the web reads it for the house glyph rather than comparing paths.
A change updates the accepted catalog immediately, so stale worker results cannot restore the old choice, then uses the existing coalesced off-lock UI save.
An unchanged choice publishes and saves nothing; there is no additional worker, subprocess, timer or queue.
Checkout fields remain in the hand-maintained `web/src/snapshot.ts` shape; the WebSocket frame generator does not generate this part of the core snapshot.
SSH catalogs currently read Herdr and the device helper, not another hided core's registrations, so they retain the remote root default and do not synchronize a primary choice stored by another daemon.

## Project sessions and Memory

`hide-project` is the single durable identity boundary for session discovery, Memory storage, core projection, and hook retrieval.
It folds linked worktrees into their canonical main worktree, identifies plain folders in device scope, and returns a typed failure instead of falling back to another Project or a display name.
`hide-session` owns native file discovery and parsing for Claude Code, Codex, Pi, omp and Grok, the read-only Cursor SQLite/protobuf graph and the read-only OpenCode database, while `herdr-core` receives only provider-neutral catalog rows and archived ledger events.
Raw transcript bodies remain in the providers' files.

`hide-memory` owns Hide's extraction prompt and output schema (`engine.rs`), one app SQLite store, its schema and migrations, Project hard filters, session cursors, card and revision lifecycle, provenance, receipts, usage counts, the matcher, and the FTS5 projection.
No third-party memory engine, service, prompt asset or vector database is part of it.
The extraction prompt is Hide's own, held under 8 KiB by a test, and asks for each card's text, a one-line title, three to eight aliases in the card's language with the English word for each one that is not English, its kind, the files it rests on (`cited_paths`) and its relation to a similar card (PRD project-memory-v2 D-09, D-26).
Every analysis result is a proposal only: the write service validates Project identity, provenance, redaction, lifecycle, capacity, relation authority and transaction boundaries before any durable change, and keeps a cited path only when it names a file inside the checkout (`Call::RealPaths`, a repository-relative path with no `..`).
Normalized session events and the comparison cards both pass through the current local redactor at the final provider egress boundary.
The disclosure acceptance is versioned; a material egress-copy change disables previously enabled Projects until the operator accepts the current disclosure, without deleting their local data.

A card has a number inside its Project (`m1`, `m2`), never reused, which agents name it by.
`hide_memory::matcher` is the one model-free matcher: a card is indexed by its words, the words with a Korean particle or ending taken off, its aliases and every two-syllable piece of its Hangul words, FTS5 ranks the candidates by BM25, and a card is kept only above a minimum score, so a prompt that shares no word, stem, alias or piece with a card never receives it (B11, B12).
The prompt hook, `hide memory search` and the comparison set a new card is checked against all use it, so a same-meaning card outside the most salient ones still reaches the provider (D-13, B17).
The comparison set is bounded by cards and by bytes so it and one event group fit the 64 KiB request.
A conflict is settled by rule inside the write, never by asking the operator (D-14, B18): the side resting on the operator's own words wins and, between equals, the newer text wins.
A winning new text becomes the card's current revision and the old one stays in its history, which the card's Undo restores (B19); a losing new text is kept in the history of the card it lost to and is never injected, and `add` answers that the card stays (B7).

The app owns the only writer connection.
The store is `project-memory.sqlite3` in the daemon's state folder, and `hide_memory::database_path` is the one function that names it; only the core opens it, so Memory has one store whichever machine its agents run on (PRD core-host-node-move D-12, B14).
A hook (Claude Code's and Codex's, OpenCode's, Pi's and omp's helper) asks the core for its context with the scoped `memory_hook` request (`hide workspace memory`, `herdr_core::memory_hook`), through the node of the machine it runs on when that is not the core's: the core takes the store path and the caller's node link under the lock (`Runtime::memory_scope`), and the node's Project read (`project`), the read-only open, the retrieval and the receipt tag run off it within 1 s (`hide_memory::hook`).
That second is one deadline, set as the daemon starts the read: the wait for the owner thread's scope, the node call and the store read all end by it, and the hook is answered without Memory when any of them reaches it (`core_deadline` in the `hook.unavailable` record for the owner wait).
The Project is the one holding the caller pane's attested checkout, keyed by that checkout's node, never one the hook names; the hook sends no working folder.
The core's own node and every registered linked device have Memory, regardless of connection direction; a node whose link is down is refused at once, and a helper without an advertised Memory reader is refused with `session_reader_memory_unsupported` (missing reader facts give `session_reader_support_unavailable`).
The device receives context through its existing pane-scoped return route, never a copy of the store.
A daemon started with a relocated state folder (`HIDE_STATE_DIR`, `XDG_STATE_HOME`) answers its panes' hooks from that folder's store when the pane reaches it, the same condition as a letter pull: a hook asks the daemon its pane credential names and takes no store path from anywhere, and a pane without a credential bootstraps on the state folder its own environment names (`workspace_cli::bootstrap_local`), so a pane whose environment lacks the relocated folder finds no daemon there (`hide_unavailable`).
The store `~/Library/Application Support/hide/project-memory.sqlite3` that the removed native shell wrote is never read, moved or deleted: its Project ids name this machine `local`, which the node-id conversion rewrites only inside the state folder, so a copy would key Projects wrongly, and Memory is opt-in per Project, so a fresh store starts with every Project off.
The hook answer and render-facing reads open read-only connections, fail closed on schema or projection drift, and never rebuild the index in the prompt path.
Schema 8 adds card numbers, titles, aliases, cited paths, usage counts, and the human flag and time on sources, and the migration copies the store aside first and settles each pair the old conflict path left waiting for review by the rule: no version-7 source marks the operator's words, so the newer card stays and the older is forgotten (D-23, B25).
A source's time is when its words were said, read from the conversation event; a version-7 source gets its batch's analysis time, the closest the old rows hold.

A session start receives an index of at most 20 rule, decision and lesson cards, one line each, and no body; a prompt receives the bodies of the cards the matcher finds that the session was not already given in full (D-03, D-04, B10-B12).
A body injection begins with one line of Hide's own, `<hide-memory-instruction>`, outside the untrusted block, that tells the agent how to report a card the operator's current words contradict ([A card an agent reports stale](#a-card-an-agent-reports-stale)); the index carries none, since it carries no receipt, and the conversation reader treats both tags as injected text, never the operator's words.
Each body injection carries a receipt authenticated over Project, runtime, session, event and the exact ordered card revisions, with one random key per Project kept in the store; an index carries none, because an index line is no delivery.
Transcript text prefixes remain display classification only: receipt authority additionally requires Claude Code's own `hook_additional_context` attachment record (the shape since 2.1.296, read through `hide-session/src/claude_attachment.rs`, which the wake reader shares; the `hook_success` record repeating the same text in its `stdout` is never read) or a user record its hook metadata marks, a Codex developer message, an OpenCode synthetic user part (1.x) or synthetic message (2.x) or a Pi or omp custom message of Hide's own extension (`customType` `hide`), so human or Project instruction text cannot assert a receipt.
A receipt is recorded by one function, `memory_analysis::record_receipt`, under the turn id `event:<offset>`, so whichever reader sees it first records it once (D-16, B13).
Two readers feed it.
The label worker's read of a live pane with a known session id, on this machine or on any linked device, hands its receipts up (`LabelTranscript.memory_receipts`, at most 64 a read) with the set of sessions that machine's live panes hold, and `Runtime::record_memory_receipts` records them on one owned thread off the lock, at most 256 waiting.
A receipt is checked against the Project of the machine whose sessions carried it, read through that machine's Memory-capable node (`Runtime::memory_node`); an unavailable link or reader drops the receipt with a `receipts.no_memory` diagnostic naming the reason.
The pass below walks every session of each Memory-enabled Project that no live pane holds, a session started outside Herdr or whose pane closed, and reads its receipts past a durable receipt cursor (`receipt_cursors`) through the same conversation read the label worker uses, so the event offset or message index, and the turn, is the same: a JSONL session (Claude Code, Codex, Pi, omp, Grok) one bounded read at a time, an OpenCode session message by message.
`hide memory show` records a receipt too, under the turn `show`.
A Project keeps at most 20,000 receipt rows; past it, receipts of sessions quiet for 30 days go first, and a receipt that still does not fit is logged and the session's prompts get no Memory until there is room (B26).

One pass at a time walks every Memory-enabled Project of this machine and of each linked device (`Runtime::memory_nodes`), whatever the screen shows, on a worker off the runtime lock, every 60 s or at once when a live session's pane closes (D-07, D-08); a node whose link or Memory reader is unavailable has its Projects walked once it is back, and its closed sessions stay queued meanwhile.
A core whose move has not committed starts no pass, since a pass asks the provider (PRD core-host-node-move amendment 3).
A session's new conversation is analyzed once it has been quiet for 30 minutes (its file's modification time, or an OpenCode session's newest write to its row or any of its messages) or within a minute of its pane closing; a session still being written waits.
A closed session stays queued (at most 256, past which the log names the one left to the quiet rule) until a pass finds nothing left to analyze in it, so a pass that was cancelled, held back by a deferral or a provider failure, or ended early does not drop it into the 30 minute wait; one no Memory-enabled Project lists is let go after a pass that walked them all.
A pass records a session's row only when it changed, so an idle pass writes nothing.
It reads only complete events after the durable analysis cursor and sends bounded groups through `hide-ai`; the cursor moves only when every group was written, and the same provider, session and content hash converge on one batch instead of repeating revisions.
The durable cursor stores file identity and the safe start offset of an incomplete final line, never that line's bytes; a later read resumes from the provider-owned session file.
An OpenCode session has no file: its cursor counts messages, the checkpoint the phone pages with (`ConversationCheckpoint::at_message`: the messages read, the session's creation time and a digest of the last message read, so a session an OpenCode revert rewound is read again from its start), and its version is the message count and newest write the database answers (`session_activity`, in place of `session_stat`).
A read stops before an assistant message OpenCode is still writing and counts it as read until it changes, as a torn final line is, so an unchanged session is not read again on every pass.
Each incremental read takes at most 1 MiB and retains no JSONL line larger than 256 KiB, while catalog and archive detail reads reject a complete session larger than 64 MiB.
A longer line, most often a tool result carrying a screenshot, is read past without being held: the conversation reader keeps its first 256 KiB only long enough to scan its structure and counts it by reason (`skipped_reasons`), the pass logs that count once as its cursor moves past it (`receipts.records_skipped`, `analysis.records_skipped`), and the turns before and after it are analyzed and their receipts recorded.
A node advertises the Memory reader for a runtime only beside its conversation reader, and the pass reads a session for Memory only when the node holding it advertises both: the JSONL agents and, since OpenCode's database read answers `session_conversation` too, OpenCode; Cursor's graph has none.
A protocol-24 node predates `session_conversation` (protocol 26) and advertises no Memory reader: Memory admission refuses it explicitly, the pass records `analysis.node_unavailable` only when the node's refusal reason changes, and its closed sessions stay queued for a capable connection.
Its other independently supported readers remain available; an unknown protocol is refused by the existing handshake.
A session that still cannot be read is logged once per version of its file (its size and modification time), not on every pass; the pass remembers at most 4,096 such failures, and one past that is logged on every pass.
A machine makes at most 1,000 analysis calls a UTC day, counted when a call is admitted rather than when it answers, so a provider that keeps failing still meets the bound; the cap's deferral is logged and never shown (D-10, B5), and the operator's usage limit (a share of each agent's week, 90% by default) is judged by the router per request, not by the pass ([AI_PROVIDERS.md: The usage limit](AI_PROVIDERS.md#the-usage-limit)).
With no window attached the usage reader runs for 30 minutes after a pass last had a session due, so the weekly figure is read on a headless daemon too; an agent with no figure is never held back by the usage limit, and a pass with every agent past the limit builds no router and waits for the next usage read or settings change.
A provider failure backs off from one minute, doubling to an hour, honors a `retry_after` the provider names, and reaches the screen only as an action the operator can take, a sign-in or an agent choice (B4, B23).
Changing the Hide AI agent clears what a provider failure decided, and turning Hide AI off stops analysis while receipts go on being recorded (B33).
All filesystem, SQLite, hook-config, provider, and serialization work occurs outside the runtime mutex; reporting a Project's state and finishing a pass are the only locked transitions.
The session files are read by the node that holds them and the Memory store is the core's (PRD core-host-node-move B14): the pass, the Memory tab, `hide memory` and the right panel's Sessions read a Project on this machine or on any linked device, saying the node is not connected while its link is down; the core asks that node for the Project's facts (`project`, its id named by the node's id), its session list (`project_sessions`), a file's size and time (`session_stat`; an OpenCode session's message count and newest write, `session_activity`), one bounded conversation read past a saved cursor (`session_conversation`, which answers the events, their offsets and the next checkpoint) and a whole session for the archive (`session_text`), and parses and stores what comes back.
The analysis provider is always asked on the core's own machine, where Hide AI's agents are signed in.
Search and archive detail dispatch only to an explicitly supported session provider; archive detail refuses an unsupported provider before asking the node to read its locator or opening the Memory store.
A node reads a session file the core names, for these calls, only when the path, with every link resolved, lies in the Claude Code or Codex CLI session folder of its home, and opens it without waiting, so a pipe in its place is refused at once; an OpenCode session it reads only by the id and checkout the read proves inside its database transaction, and the locator the core sends with it names nothing.
Disabling Memory stops new analysis and injection without deleting its data, while Forget, Undo, and confirmed Project deletion have their own explicit lifecycle operations.

### What a card rests on

Every card carries its evidence (PRD memory-card-quality D-05, D-06): the repository files it cites with a SHA-256 content fingerprint, and the repository paths and script names its text writes, each kept when it existed when the card was written (a name with a file extension that did not exist is kept as not found, which `hide memory show` says, and never breaks the card; prose such as `Pin/Unpin` is not kept) (`hide-memory/src/evidence.rs`, `herdr-core/src/runtime/memory_evidence.rs`), at most 16 rows per revision.
The facts come from the node that holds the checkout, on the analysis worker and never under the runtime lock, with no protocol change: `Call::RealPaths` for existence, `Call::Revision` for a file's fingerprint (at most 300 a Project's walk) and `Call::Index` for a script's file name, read once a walk, with a truncated index unable to say a name is gone.
They are read against the Project's own root, so a session in a worktree does not fingerprint a branch's files.
A node that cannot answer leaves a card as it was.

A card is unverified (`확인 대기` while queued, `확인 중` only while its check runs) when its current revision has no verification time (`memory_revisions.verified_at_ms IS NULL`), the one stored fact that the hooks, which have no node and no model, can exclude with a single predicate: an unverified card is left out of the session-start index and the prompt match, and still comes out of `hide memory show` and `search` (marked) and still serves as a comparison target, so analysis does not make a duplicate of it.
Schema 9 starts every migrated card unverified, after keeping the store as it was as a backup, and the re-verification bound below drains that backlog four cards a pass, within the maintenance share of the day's calls, so the backlog is bounded by passes and the day's share of calls, not by a day per card.
Each pass compares up to 200 verified cards' evidence with the node, oldest check first, and unverifies the ones that moved; that needs no model and runs with Hide AI off.

An unverified card is read again in the background, at most four a pass and one question per card per day (`reverify_at_ms`), under the shared daily call cap and the router's usage limit; a deferral leaves it unverified (D-07, D-08).
An already verified revision awaiting its first document comparison shares that queue and its caps, while remaining indexed, retrievable and eligible for suggestions until a comparison withholds it.
`memory_revisions.docs_compared` records the first comparison independently of `verified_at_ms`; a new creation check already supplies it.
The v11 upgrade preserves verified dates and queues current verified revisions once, clearing their old check cooldown rather than withholding them.
Its table transition and comparison queue commit together, so a failed upgrade retries from v11 without losing the first comparison.
Schema 13 repairs the destructive v12 upgrade atomically: current unverified revisions with no current comparison recover only the exact verified date stored under their revision id in the adjacent `.v11-backup`, then enter that same queue.
The backup is opened read-only and retained unchanged; if absent or unreadable, dates remain unchanged and the writer records one `migration.verification_restore_unavailable` diagnostic after committing the migration.
Schema 11 counts checks separately from total calls in `analysis_calls.checks`, with atomic admission in `MemoryStore::admit_memory_call` and policy in `hide-memory/src/budget.rs`.
Checks can use at most 500 of the total 1,000 calls per UTC day, including failed calls; analysis reserves the smaller of the currently due, enabled and consented checks and the unspent check share.
`MemoryStore::due_checks` is the pass and reservation's shared predicate, covering unverified cards, verified revisions awaiting their first document comparison and verified rule or lesson cards whose promotion check is due.
With no due cards, nothing is reserved; explicit `add` shares the same analysis admission.
Migration preserves the earlier total and starts the new check count at zero, since earlier calls had no category, so the migration day can never exceed its remaining total.
Each accepted card returns to injection as soon as its answer is stored; a later call's failure retains that progress.
The worker reports the admitted card number and check-budget deferral into the snapshot, which also carries re-consent and the usable-card count; the web shell draws these facts in existing slots.
The usable count is zero while the Project is disabled, and a budget hold carries its next UTC-day reset timestamp so the shell can format it in the viewer's timezone.
Only a check-budget hold confirmed by the current pass survives its end, so a usage pause after a UTC reset cannot retain yesterday's hold.
An earlier build rejects schema 12 with `WrongSchema`; rolling back the binary does not roll back this schema, and the database remains preserved.
The provider (`project_memory_verify`, `hide-memory/src/verify.rs`) gets the card, the facts and excerpts of at most five cited files (8 KiB each, chosen by the sections that share words with the card when a file is longer), all locally redacted, and answers `valid`, `outdated` or `gone`: valid takes the evidence again and returns the card to the index, outdated writes the corrected text as the next revision of the same card with a change record that the card's Undo takes back, and gone forgets the card, which Undo takes back too.
A rule or lesson about to be suggested gets one more question (`project_memory_promote_check`, at most three a pass, once per text): the repository's `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and the documents they point to (at most five, 8 KiB each, chosen the same way) and the operator's own global instruction files (at most three, read as the account this process runs as, logged by name only) are put beside the card, and the answer is `propose`, `outdated` (handled as above) or `in_docs`, which turns the card into a pointer at that repository file, the merged promotion state with no pull request, and forgets it when only a global file says it.
Schema 12 keeps one revision-bound repository comparison per card and one membership row per promotion batch, backing up schema 11 and queuing its non-pointer cards for the existing re-verification pass.
The same re-verification and promotion-check calls additionally answer `docs_disagree`, with a repository path and an exact conflicting excerpt; re-verification can also answer `in_docs`.
The worker compares the current revision's operator-source time with that file's last commit time through the typed node `GitCommand::DocumentTime`, off the lock.
A changed or untracked file, a missing time or equal times asks the operator; a later document makes the card Outdated, and a later operator decision proposes correcting the document.
Every unresolved comparison, including an accepted correction waiting for merge, is excluded from both injection paths and further automatic checks; changing the card revision invalidates that comparison.
Preferences stay injectable but never become repository pointers or promotion candidates.
An Undo of any of these verdicts restores the old text as verified and drops the evidence rows of that revision, so the evidence walk does not unverify it again at once.
An `in_docs` answer naming a file the request did not carry is refused.

Before writing new non-preference candidates, the worker searches the same bounded repository documents without a model and groups lexical matches into one comparison call (`project_memory_creation_docs`).
Each candidate carries one excerpt of at most 3 KiB; the complete request is trimmed or omitted to stay within 15% of that extraction request's input characters and bytes.
A candidate already said in a document is discarded and counted in the diagnostic log.
That same creation answer classifies each sent candidate as same, differs or unrelated; a differing claim is written atomically with its comparison, with dates read from the node and no initial re-verification call.
An unrelated claim remains ordinary; an existing claim whose revision changes uses the established re-verification queue.
Merged pointers apply only to the revision they describe, so a changed claim is checked instead of inheriting its older pointer.
A session makes at most two new cards in all, counted across the groups and passes that analyzed it (`new_card_limit`; `hide memory add` has none), and a session with fewer than three operator events makes none and asks no provider (D-02, D-03).
The operator's events are the session's `human` events; in a session some agent started, which the coordination ledger records with a `parent` (a handoff has an `origin` and is the operator's), they are sent as `parent_agent` instead (D-04); such a session counts no operator events, so it makes no cards.

`hide memory show|search|add|forget|undo|placed|building|unbuilt|outdated|skipped|stale|release` (`herdr-core/src/memory_command.rs`) goes through hided like `hide links`: the core checks the caller under its lock (`Core::prepare_memory`), answering a caller on this machine or any registered linked device with a Memory reader, and naming the Project by the caller's checkout as its node reads it, and the command runs on hided's blocking task.
`show` answers a card with its state, kind, source counts, cited paths and whether it is waiting to be checked (`verifying`) and counts the read for the caller's session, `search` answers at most ten matching cards, and `add` writes through the same `write_cards` path background analysis uses, as an `agent_note` the provider sees, never as the operator's words (D-11, B7, B8, B14-B16, B27).
An `add` is one batch per Project and text, so the same text added again answers the card it made and asks no provider; once every card of that batch is gone (forgotten, undone or never kept), the same text starts the next batch and is added again.
Its provider tries end within 50 s and the cards are not written past 55 s, before the caller's minute runs out, so a refused or timed-out `add` has written nothing.
`forget` tombstones a card the operator asked the agent to drop, and `undo` steps one card back one change: a forgotten card comes back, a replaced text gives way to the one before it, an added card goes.
Every write reports to the core, which reads the opened Project's Memory again; a forget names its card on the Memory tab with its Undo.
A card write checks the Project's switch inside its own transaction, so a card analyzed or added while the operator turned Memory off is never written after the switch.

The core owns the fourth right-panel section, the focused checkout's session list with its filters and editor preview identity; Memory is not shown there.
The shell renders those snapshot values and dispatches typed actions; it does not discover sessions, rank memories, infer counts, or classify failures.

There is no Project-wide Conversation history (PRD overview-device-tasks D-03, D-20): its screen, the copied-body search index and its worker are removed, `sessions_refresh` carries no payload and reads the focused checkout's list as above, `archive_open` names no Project, and neither a `project_sessions` nor a `session_search` section rides the snapshot.
A node answers no `SessionIndexRead` or `SessionStamps` call, which is part of what node protocol 35 changed (see [A core on another machine](#a-core-on-another-machine)).
The labels, the phone's conversation and Memory read sessions through the catalog and the readers above, which stay.
The old `session-search.sqlite3` is kept read-only for one fact: the per-project `Copied history` choice it holds is how long the link record keeps a session's record (`links/store.rs::policies`).
Hide never deletes or rewrites that file in place, has no screen to change the choice, and reads a missing file or table as the 90-day default without an error on screen; neither a state conversion nor a core move writes it: each records the project ids it replaced in the link record's `retention_aliases` table (`LinkStore::alias_retention`, a chain of moves pointing at the newest id), and `policies` reads a row under a replaced id as its project's under the new id, unless the new id has a row of its own.
The session list's read is `load_sessions` (`herdr-core/src/runtime/memory.rs`), which opens the Memory store read-only only for the sessions Memory recorded and no longer finds, lists no card, writes nothing, schedules no analysis, cancels none, and leaves the due-work poll alone.
A session's conversation names, on each event, the cards a prompt hook gave it there (`memory_received`, from the event's verified receipt) and the cards Memory learned from it and still keeps (`memory_learned`), by number.

Project Memory is its own snapshot section, `memory`, on its own delta revision (`herdr-core/src/runtime/project_memory.rs`), keyed by the Project a shell opens rather than by the Overview's Sessions or the focused checkout, and absent until a Project is opened.
It is drawn by the lane's Memory tool (`Tool::Memory`, D-17), the rail's second tool, after Work and before Files and Changes.
`memory_open` with a catalog `workspace_id` opens that Project's Memory for every window of the daemon (none closes it), and a read on a worker lays its switch, its cards with their usage counts and the analysis state on `memory.project`; it waits for no Sessions history read.
A Project on any registered linked device reads the core's Memory store through the device's node; an unavailable link or Memory reader answers `unreadable` and records its reason in the diagnostic log.
Project identity remains the node id plus that node's main worktree root, so equal paths on different devices never share cards.
`memory_expand` shows one card in full on the same read (body, cited files, sources with their runtime's name, time and whether they were the operator's words, and the text the current one replaced), and `memory_search` runs the matcher over the Project and answers the card numbers, best first.
An active promotion row also carries up to 512 Unicode characters of its body's preview, cut from the already-read card on that worker, and the promotion's durable start timestamp.
The shell groups confirmed PRs or ledger-bound running attempts and uses the existing agent request's live PR association and Hide AI origin for post-PR conversation links.
`memory_action` with a `workspace_id` and a card number turns Memory on or off, deletes its data, forgets a card or undoes one card's latest change on a worker off the lock, one action at a time.
Turning Memory on when this Mac's agent hooks do not carry it yet is the operator's word to update them: the kit's Reinstall runs first and Memory turns on once the hooks report the version that carries it, so the tab has no separate hook button (B4).
The analysis state is `idle`, `analyzing` or `ai_not_ready`; the last shows at once while Memory is on and Hide AI is off, or after a pass the provider refused for sign-in or for having no agent, and the tab draws Hide AI's own provider and name from the Hide AI settings section (D-27).
The analysis state, the actions and a promotion's state are enums pinned in `contracts/snapshot-wire-enums.json`.

A rule, decision or lesson card the operator's words or the agents' own pulls make a candidate for the repository's documents is asked about on the Memory tab (`memory.project.questions`, D-25, B28-B32); the store keeps one promotion row per card (`card_promotions`) and computes the suggestion on each read: only a rule or a lesson; the operator's own words in two or more different sessions, or pulled by `hide memory show` in three or more different sessions within 14 days, counted by session and never by how often it was said; its evidence not moved since it was last verified; and the check before a suggestion passed for its current text (PRD memory-card-quality D-09, D-10, D-11).
At most three new promotion suggestions are open; document corrections for rule, lesson and decision cards are also selectable, and a dismissal holds only for its revision.
`memory_action` `promote` takes one card or up to five selected card numbers through one path.
The store reserves every card atomically before starting one agent in one worktree, with Hide's `hide:memory` identity as its origin and no parent or watch.
The reserved attempt durably captures its repository from the confirmed connection and node-owned Git facts.
Repository owner/name comparisons follow GitHub's case-insensitive identity; the URL host, `pull` path kind and PR number stay exact.
Its PR number, branch and repository-qualified URL must match before a catalog result can settle it; a changed connection cannot fail or settle the old attempt from another repository.
Schema 14 adds nullable `card_promotions.repository`; legacy unknown provenance stays unknown, keeps its card state and is shown as unconfirmed with its PR link disabled.
Schema 15 rebuilds `card_promotions` to take the states `building`, `unbuilt` and `kept`, and adds nullable `building_branch`, the branch whose pull request settles a `building` card, kept apart from the attempt's own agent and branch so that releasing or retrying the attempt never touches that work; an earlier build rejects schema 15 with `WrongSchema`.
A failed batch retry preserves that original target and refuses a different or unknown connection; restoring the original connection asks for a fresh catalog before deciding absence.
The fixed prompt reads each card through `hide memory show`, which prints the card's document comparison (which side is newer, the document's excerpt and the operator's words) when it has one, treats it as untrusted reference data, processes cards sequentially with one commit each, reports successful placement through `hide memory placed`, and opens or updates one PR with each card's operator date and source session.
Before correcting a document it checks the card against its checkout's code, since a document that describes the code as it is now is not wrong because a newer decision exists, and it writes every reason in the core's output language (`Runtime::output_language`, [AI_PROVIDERS.md](AI_PROVIDERS.md#the-output-language)).
A failed batch retry reuses its branch, retained worktree and confirmed PR rather than creating another; a duplicate request while its cards are running returns the existing intent.
An unprocessed card reported through `hide memory skipped` returns to To ask with its bounded reason, while successfully placed peers remain in the shared PR.
The tracker requires placement for batch cards and returns unplaced cards when their agent ends, preserving the PR number for retry.
The attempt is written `running` before the spawn and `failed` when the spawn is refused, keeping the refusal (`card_promotions.failure`), which the failed card draws as why its agent never started (`MemoryStartFailure`: the question its new pane's shell stopped at, the agent that never started, or another refusal whose words stay in the log); a start Hide never finished fails after seven minutes with no agent instead of holding the card, a bound past two of Herdr's start deadlines so a resent start has its own; one attempt runs per card, and a retry is the next attempt with its own intent.
A failed attempt gives back what it made: when its branch holds no commit of its own and its worktree no change, its panes are closed and the worktree and branch removed (`memory_promotion::release_attempt`, logged as `promotion.worktree_released`); one that holds work is kept, and the retry continues on its branch there, so retries never pile worktrees up.
The release runs on the machine that holds the Project, through its own Herdr and node, and not at all while that machine cannot be reached; a start is released only when its spawn answered that the agent never started (`shell_starting`, `agent_not_started`), and nothing is closed while Herdr shows an agent or a held start in the worktree, the branch is the one checked out in the Project's own folder, or a commit or change appears by the time its panes have closed.
A spawn held up by another spawn on this Mac (`spawn_busy`) is asked again for up to ten seconds and then fails the attempt, since nothing was started.
A spawn that answers the agent was started but has not shown its session yet (`native_identity_unavailable`) leaves the attempt running with no agent while the core binds the agent once its session shows, and the tracker asks the same intent again on each check (a busy spawn lock then only waits for the next check), which continues that spawn rather than starting another, until the agent shows (its id is then recorded on the attempt) or the seven-minute grace ends; the start it asks with lives in memory, so after a restart an unseen agent is left to the grace.
Every write after the spawn, the tracker's moves and `placed` are conditional on the row still being the attempt (and, for `placed`, the agent) they read, so none of them undoes another.
The tracker follows every `running`, `pr_open` or `building` row on this Mac on a worker: after each GitHub answer and about once a minute while an attempt is running, it reads the PR catalog for the attempt's branch (this repository's own branch, never a fork's, opened no earlier than the attempt started, and once seen pinned by number) and the coordination ledger for its agent under the lock, and writes each move off it only if the row is still the state and attempt it read, so a `placed` or a retry in between is kept: an open pull request is `pr_open`, a merged one `merged`, a closed one `closed_unmerged` (drawn as an ordinary card and never suggested again), and an agent that ended with no pull request `failed`, which the tab asks about with 다시 시도.
When the tracker first finds a running attempt's agent gone with no pull request, it asks the PR catalog for a new read and remembers the generation it asked for; `failed` is written only once an answer to that ask or a later one is a current read, or says the repository has no GitHub remote, so a pull request opened just before its agent ended, or missing from the catalog restored at launch, is never missed.
An answer that cannot say (gh missing or logged out, or a failed read) leaves the attempt running and followed, drawn as `unconfirmed` with GitHub's reason (`memory.project.rows[].promotion.github_failure`): there is no timer to `failed`, nothing can start a second attempt, and the next answer GitHub can give settles it.
The ask lives in memory and is made again after a restart; like every GitHub read, its answer comes only while a window is attached, so an attempt whose agent ended with no window open settles when one attaches.
A pull request that falls out of the 200 the catalog reads stays `pr_open` until it comes back.
`hide memory skipped` uses the same attempt and agent checks as `placed`, records a reason up to 500 characters, and leaves the card in the failed state for retry on the shared branch.
`hide memory outdated` is answered on the same conditions as `placed`, to the attempt's own agent only, and ends the attempt as `outdated`: no pull request, no `failed` question on the tab, the card unverified and its reason (at most 500 characters) kept for the next check.
`hide memory building <card> <branch> <path>` is answered on the same conditions as `placed`, for a branch of the repository other than the attempt's own, one the worktree catalog lists when it has read the repository's branches, and a path that exists in that branch's checkout when the catalog lists one; it ends the attempt `building`, with no pull request of its own.
A `building` card is still given to agents and is drawn as a progress row with the agent of the worktree on that branch; the tracker follows that branch's own pull request, opened at any time since that work began on its own, so its merge makes the card `merged` with the path the agent named, and its closing makes the card `closed_unmerged`.
`hide memory unbuilt <card> <reason>` is answered on the same conditions and ends the attempt `unbuilt` with its reason (at most 500 characters): the tab asks about it once, and the operator's keeping it writes `kept`, an ordinary card that neither the promotion nor its document disagreement asks about again until its text changes.
The tracker follows only `running`, `pr_open` and `building`, so an `outdated`, `unbuilt` or `kept` attempt is never moved again.
A merged card is left out of every prompt's cards, before the candidate limit so it takes no live card's place, and stays in the index as one line pointing at the file `placed` named (or at its pull request when none was named), so the agents read it in the repository.
`hide memory placed` is answered only while the card's promotion is running or its pull request open, only to that attempt's own agent (while Hide has not confirmed the agent yet it answers `memory_promotion_agent_pending`, to ask again), and only for a repository-relative path that exists in the caller's checkout, outside `.git` in any letter case, with no invisible character (bidirectional controls, zero-width and format marks, separators, variation selectors, Unicode tags).

The shell holds no authority, but the core does not hand all of it to Herdr either.
Herdr owns pane existence, split geometry, zoom, cwd, agent lifecycle and the PTY; the core owns the focused project and checkout, each checkout's visible tab, the keyboard focus pane, panel visibility and text scale.
The focus anchors among them (`ui_state.selected_pane_id`, `focused_checkout_id`, `focused_device_id`) move only on the events that ask for them; `ui_state_update` does not move them, and the shell leaves them out of it.
A restore whose saved pane or checkout the first local session does not hold opens on Herdr's focused pane and the checkout holding it (`consume_restore_hint`), since the core tells Herdr every focus it takes; a Herdr focus no catalog checkout holds is no place to open, and the catalog's first checkout is the start then.
A core move is that case even when nothing was saved: the other core held this machine as a device, whose selection is its Herdr's focus and is never saved, so the move's rekey leaves `focus_from_herdr` in `core-state.json` (`node_migration::reown`), which the first session takes and the next save drops; with nothing saved and no move, the catalog's first checkout is the start.
That start passes over a checkout whose folder the node found gone (`resync_navigator_focus`, `PathIndex::gone`): a pane restored onto a folder moved away while Hide was closed keeps a row with no activity, which sorts by its id, so the window would otherwise open on that empty project by chance.
That save is built from the shell's last snapshot, which can be older than a click the core has not echoed yet, and taking its copy once put the keyboard back on the pane the operator had left (issue #672).
The core also owns the checkouts last brought to the front (`ui_state.recent_checkouts`, ⌘K's Recent, PRD cmdk-recent): ten records of device id, checkout id and the project and branch names a dimmed row is drawn from; the row's device is a listed one, which the shell names from its row, so no device name is kept, and a record an older build saved with a `device_name` loads with that field ignored.
One pass at the head of `sync_workspace_view` (`runtime/recent_checkouts.rs`) records a front change on whatever path caused it, compares borrowed strings with the head of the list and writes nothing when the front is unchanged, and saves through the coalesced off-lock UI-state save.
It stores no project id, because a device project's id changes when the helper's facts group it, and the checkout id (`device_catalog::checkout_id`, the folder's) does not; `catalog.checkout_ids_renamed` migrates only ids an older build saved under a Herdr-workspace key, which this list never held.
A record leaves with an unregistered project, a removed worktree or a removed device, never because a catalog is empty or still resolving, since that would empty the list on every restart; the shell leaves out a record a connected catalog does not list.
`recent_visible_tabs` is a different thing, the tab-keyed attach window.
The core also keeps the Agent cycle's pane order (`ui_state.recent_pane_ids`, issue #301, `runtime/recent_panes.rs`), so it survives a reload and a restart: the page decides what a visit is, since only it knows whether the keyboard is in a terminal or a View area of the same checkout, and reports each with `pane_visit`; the core moves a pane a listed tab holds (this machine's or a connected device's) to the front, keeps fifty and saves them with the UI state. The page reads its own report back before the echo (`web/src/recent.ts`): until the core's order moves, the reported pane leads it.
It prunes nothing against the panes it lists, because they arrive after the first frame of a restart; the shell leaves out a pane no listed tab holds.
The core also owns which Claude and Codex panes show their conversation ledger in place of the terminal (`ui_state.conversation_pane_ids`): a pane opens on its terminal, enters the set only through `toggle_conversation`, and leaves it with the pane, so a refresh never turns a pane back into a conversation the operator did not ask for.
A core-owned value changes on the event that asked for it and Herdr is told afterwards, so the canvas and the focus ring never wait for a round trip.
Selecting a pane first moves the core-owned project, checkout and visible tab to the context that owns that pane, then sends one `pane.focus` request to Herdr; splitting that user action into project, tab and pane requests would expose intermediate frames and make partial refusal possible.
The shell sends physical terminal-focus events even when its latest snapshot already names that pane; core coalescing decides whether Herdr needs another operation, while focus applied from a snapshot sends no operator echo.
Every control Hide sends on this machine's Herdr socket for a tab or a pane focus travels one ordered lane (`runtime/control_lane.rs`): tab focus, tab creation and owner open, tab move, tab rename and pane focus.
Each request is queued, in flight or settled.
A queued request has not left, so a newer one that replaces it on the lane leaves nothing of it to remember; an in-flight request is applied by Herdr whatever Hide decides next, so its answer can arrive after a newer request and is Hide's own.
One socket operation runs at a time and the rest wait in arrival order, at most 16 (`CONTROL_LANE_LIMIT`; a request past it is refused where it was asked and logged as `control.lane_full`), so a tab focus, a creation and a pane focus sent moments apart reach Herdr in the order they were asked, where a thread per request let them land in either order.
Only a focus coalesces: a queued tab focus is replaced by the newer one, and a pane focus keeps one turn on the lane whose target is the latest intent, so rapid clicks cannot leave older requests running after the last click has been confirmed.
Creation, move and rename are distinct intents and are never replaced.
A replacing tab focus, and a pane click that joins the waiting turn, take their place at the back of the queue, so a control accepted between the two still leaves first.
A tab move on this machine carries no deadline while it waits: its five-second wait starts when it leaves the lane, and a move whose pending record was replaced, is no longer transmitting, or belongs to an earlier Herdr connection is not sent (`tab.move.stale_not_sent`).
A worker that panics inside a socket call settles its job as an unknown result (`control.worker_panicked`), so the lane never stays busy behind a dead worker.
A device's controls keep their own connection and generation and are not on this lane.
The running control carries a serial, pane id and connection generation; only its matching latest intent can be settled by its result, and a reconnected host runs the latest intent through its new connection.
After `pane.focus` responds, the worker reads `pane.layout` and the owning `workspace.get` outside the runtime lock and requires both the target pane and its active tab to agree before confirming the request.
Each of those three responses uses the existing five-second absolute read deadline and 64 KiB frame cap; connection setup retains the connector's own bound.
While a pane control is running, session layouts update topology without settling or changing Hide's keyboard focus; the socket operation's bounded transport wait owns failure, rather than a separate view timer releasing the latest intent while an older operation is still running.
If the mutation answer is lost or malformed, its external effect is unknown: the automatic burst ends without sending the queued successor, and the latest correlated caller receives a retryable failure explicitly naming that unknown result, without claiming Herdr confirmed its focus.
Hide keeps the latest local selection at that outcome, but the pinned API supplies no mutation cancellation or fence: the lost tab move waits out the three-second deadline, a late Herdr focus after it is followed, and a later user selection or Retry starts a new burst rather than resuming the aborted queue.
Which Herdr tab move is Hide's own answer, and when one Herdr makes is followed, is read from one list (`tab_focus_requests`, `runtime/tab_focus.rs`): the tab moves that have left the lane and not been answered, in the order they left, each a tab focus, the tab of a pane focus, or a tab created with focus.
