A request still waiting on the lane is not in it: the lane holds it, and nothing of it can reach Herdr until it leaves.
The order carries meaning because of two facts: the lane sends the next control only after Herdr has answered the last, so Herdr applies them in the order they were sent, and Herdr delivers events "in order within each subscription" ([Socket API](https://herdr.dev/docs/socket-api/), Event subscriptions), so their moves arrive in that order.
A Herdr move is one `tab_focused` event, and the event replica keeps each as it applies it (`SessionTabMoves`, `sidebar.rs`): a session folds several events into one state, and one that ends on t2 cannot say whether Herdr went t2 or t2, t3, t2.
Each session the replica publishes carries its count of moves and the latest 16, but a move alone never publishes while a workspace is held for its layout, because that publish would take back a workspace the creation worker already projected; the move rides on the next publish.
The core reads the moves it has not read yet, oldest first; each answers the earliest request on its tab, and every request sent before that one leaves with it, because Herdr applied it earlier.
A session alone answers nothing: until the moves of earlier requests have arrived it shows Herdr from before them, and taking one that still showed the first of quick clicks t2, t1, t2 as the answer to the second t2 let the later moves be followed as Herdr's own and left the screen on t1 after a last click on t2 (#653); taking a session that showed the last request's tab as the answer to all of them would have followed t3 out of t2, t3, t2 arriving one session each.
A snapshot read outside the event stream (a close's or a creation's fresh projection, the editor's status answer) has no place in Herdr's order and answers nothing.
When moves were dropped between two sessions while a request waited, because more than 16 arrived or a new replica started after a reconnect, the tabs newly active since the last session and a fresh focus event stand in for them, and `tab.focus.moves_gap` is logged; with no request waiting there is nothing to answer, so a reconnect logs no gap.
A move answers by its tab alone, whichever workspace holds Herdr's keyboard, because a checkout is keyed by path and can hold tabs from several Herdr workspaces.
A request for the tab Herdr shows moves nothing, and the pinned Herdr publishes no event for it, so its answer settles it; Herdr does publish one for any other tab, another workspace's active tab included, so which tab Herdr shows is one value, recorded on each request as it leaves: the tab of the request before it, or with none outstanding, the focused tab of the last session.
A refused request leaves at once and moved nothing, so the request after it takes over where Herdr was; one whose answer was lost, and one Herdr never reports, wait out the three-second deadline, which for a pane focus's tab move starts at Herdr's answer to the focus and does not run while that pane control is running.
A refusal is reported when nothing was asked after it, also when its request had already passed its deadline.
At most 16 requests are kept (`TAB_FOCUS_LIMIT`, the same number as the moves); a burst faster than the deadline evicts the oldest and logs `tab.focus.request_evicted`.
While any request in a checkout is outstanding, sent or waiting on the lane, Herdr is about to be moved again and its moves there are not followed; a move that answers a request Hide has since left is logged as `tab.focus.late_answer`.
The list replaced one pending slot that rules were added to a failure at a time: a superseded list for a request replaced in flight (#413), a pane focus's tab wait (#594), superseding on leaving the lane (#629), and confirming only sent requests (#635); each had followed a Herdr move that was Hide's own answer.
A pane focus is confirmed only after it has left the lane, by a session whose layout of its tab focuses it while that tab is active in its workspace: a session that arrives before it leaves shows Herdr from before it, and taken as the answer it kept a queued pane focus from ever being sent, leaving Herdr on another tab (#629).
After a tab focus or pane focus whose answer was lost, the focus waiting behind it is not sent, because the lost one may still land after it and put Herdr back; Hide keeps the tab it shows, records `tab.focus.unknown`, and the next click starts a new request.
A tab Hide created shows from its acknowledgment only when no focus was asked for after the creation was sent; otherwise the operator's later choice stands, the tab waits in the strip, and `tab.create.focus_superseded` is recorded.
With no request outstanding, a move of Herdr's focused tab or pane to another value is followed and a diagnostic records the ids and the origin.
A pane focus that differs from the selected pane within its layout is not adopted from the stream alone: the pinned stream has no cursor, so a queued layout cannot undo a completed last click until a fresh read of `pane.layout` and the owning `workspace.get` agrees.
That read never gates the session update that carried the move.
The update is ingested at once, with the layout, the panes and every other part of it taken and the differing focus (and the zoom of the pane it names) left as Hide's; the read then runs on its own worker, outside the runtime lock, with the same bounded reads.
Exactly one read is outstanding (`pane_focus_readback`, a slot the runtime takes under the lock as it ingests and the worker frees on every way out, including a panic), so a slow Herdr delays only that focus: an earlier version read before ingesting, and a pane close or zoom behind a slow answer reached the screen up to ten seconds late (#424).
A confirmed answer applies the layout the session already stored, with its focus and zoom, after the identity is rechecked under the lock against the current pane-request serial and connection generation; an answer whose identity is stale, or whose move the stored layout no longer shows, moves nothing and hands the worker the read the stored layout does call for.
An unconfirmed answer keeps Hide's keyboard focus and is asked again with the next session update, never in a loop.
Herdr's focus on another pane is not a failure: it is the read refuting a layout older than Herdr's focus, most often older than Hide's own last focus, and it ends quietly.
Only a read Herdr could not answer while it was still the latest one is logged, as `pane.focus.stream_readback_failed` with Herdr's reason; a read a newer focus, selection or connection overtook ends quietly whatever it answered (`only_the_latest_readback_herdr_could_not_answer_is_a_failure_line`).
An update that called for a read while the slot was taken is that next update: the answer may have been read before Herdr made the move it shows, so the worker reads again for it, where it used to be dropped and an external move landing during a read of a stale layout was never followed.
A refusal or timeout keeps the core-owned context at the requested target, records a diagnostic and resolves the correlated shell request as a retryable failure; the shell presents Retry and Dismiss, and Dismiss clears only that notice.
A non-focused workspace's active tab is that workspace's memory, never a focus to follow: folding every workspace's active tab into one value per checkout let the last one overwrite the rest, and every tab focus on the other workspace timed out and snapped back.
Herdr moves that memory silently: a close that removes a workspace's active tab is followed by `tab_focused` only when the workspace holds Herdr's keyboard focus, and by nothing at all otherwise.
The replica therefore asks `workspace.get` for the replacement the moment such a close is applied and on each operation tick while the answer names a tab the stream has not delivered yet; a workspace still waiting after a bounded number of reads is a replica that cannot converge, and it is rebuilt from a fresh snapshot.
If the close retired the focused pane, the replica restores it from the confirmed layout of the focused workspace's replacement active tab before publication, whether `tab_focused` or `workspace.get` confirmed that tab.
Herdr can leave that tab's layout unchanged and emit no pane focus, so waiting for another layout would publish a remote session with no focused checkout and send the shell to Overview despite surviving tabs.
An unconfirmed replacement or layout keeps the previous projection, and a background workspace's layout or replacement never takes a surviving global pane focus.
Waiting for the focus event alone froze the whole workspace's projection, so a closed pane stayed drawn as `closing…` for three to five seconds, until the operator's next tab focus or the close deadline's status check.
The focused checkout always draws the tab that holds the selected pane: a tab action moves the pane into the tab, and a pane action, a restore, or a retirement moves the tab to the pane (`align_visible_tab_with_selected_pane`).
A retired selection lands, in order, in the tab it was in, in the tab the same session pass has the checkout showing, and only then where Herdr's keyboard is, because Herdr's keyboard can sit in another Agent area.
A reopen's seed tab shows why: its events arrive after the reopen answered, so its focus can be followed after the restored tab was placed, and when the seed went the selection took the other area's tab Herdr named and the restored tab lost the screen (#425).
The pending model covers the visible tab and the focused pane; zoom, splits, closes and resizes keep their own operation records below rather than the lane, and their layout is drawn before Herdr confirms it while the PTY keeps its size until Herdr has applied it (Drawn ahead of Herdr below); a close that removes a whole tab is drawn gone at once as well (Reopening locally closed work).
Every Herdr-owned mutation has one core-owned operation record with a host connection generation, target and conflict-scope IDs, phase, stage, start time, absolute deadline, caller-visible message, and retry policy; the records are exported as `status.async_operations`.
Transport success is not topology truth: split, zoom, resize, move, and close remain pending until an authoritative session event or fresh snapshot identifies the exact created, changed, or absent topology.
The session update that shows a pane operation's effect can arrive before the answer to its request (they travel on different threads), so the answer also checks the layout the core already holds; Herdr sends nothing more once the layout stops moving, and an operation left waiting would reject every later zoom, resize and close in its tab until its deadline.
A zoom on the only pane of an unzoomed tab is never sent (`pane_alone_unzoomed`, and `remote_pane_alone_unzoomed` for a device): Herdr answers it unchanged with `single_pane`, so no topology would confirm it, and its record, unknown once the deadline passed and then without one, refused every later split, zoom, resize and close in the tab until hided restarted.
A device's pane and tab operations follow the same rule: when one begins to wait (its answer, an ambiguous answer, its deadline), the core checks the session it last observed for that device (`status.remote[..].session`, one per configured target, so nothing grows with use), through the one decision function the session pass also uses.
That held session counts only when it belongs to the connection the operation was sent on, and a device's report judges only that device's operations.
An expired or otherwise unconfirmed mutation becomes unknown and is never resent when doing so could repeat a destructive effect; it drops the geometry operations waiting behind it in its tab without holding the next request, and other scopes continue.
Each tab has one geometry line: one zoom, split, resize or pane close is with Herdr at a time and at most `GEOMETRY_QUEUE_LIMIT` (8) wait behind it, so a fast gesture cannot create an unbounded queue; a resize joins the last waiting resize of the same pane and axis, and the request past the cap is refused with `pane.operation.queue_full`.
A refused, failed or unknown operation drops the waiting operations behind it, because each was asked against the layout the failed one would have made, and the confirmed layout is drawn again; an unknown one does not hold the next request.

### A card an agent reports stale

A decision the operator reverses in conversation leaves the card and the files it cites unchanged, so nothing above catches it until the reversing session is analyzed (PRD memory-stale-cards D-01).
An agent a prompt gave a card to reports it with `hide memory stale <card> <reason>`, and the card's current revision is held: it leaves the session-start index, the prompt match, the re-verification queue, the evidence walk, the due-check count and the promotion suggestions, and stays in `search`, the comparison set new cards are checked against, and `show`, which says it is held, why and since when (D-05, D-11).
Only a session whose authenticated prompt receipt (a turn other than `show`) names that very revision can report it; a session with none is told to retry once its conversation is read, a session that only read the card with `show` is refused, and a Project whose receipts are at their cap and recorded none for the session is refused apart (D-04).
A revision has one report, which `stale_reports` keeps apart from the hold: the analysis, the deadline and an agent's release leave it spent, so that revision is not held again, and a changed text is a new revision with a report of its own (D-09).
Only a person's release gives the report back (the release below), so one false report does not leave the revision unreportable for good (D-10, amended after PR #1300).
A session holds at most 3 cards and a Project 10; a report past either is refused (D-09).

The hold (`card_holds`, in `hide-memory/src/store/hold.rs`) stores the reporting runtime and session, the time, the reason folded to one line without control, direction or invisible characters, masked by the redactor, cut to 500 characters and then escaped, a deadline seven days out, and where the session's conversation ended when it reported, read from the node holding it within 5 s, in the unit its analysis cursor counts: a file's length (`session_stat`), or an OpenCode session's message count (`session_activity`); a node that cannot say leaves no place, and that hold lasts to its deadline (D-13, D-14).
It ends in four ways, and its row, reason included, is deleted with it (D-08, B17):

- Analysis of the reporting session saves its cursor past the report's place, with every group before it written (`save_cursor`, one transaction). That analysis put the reported card first in its comparison set, marked `reported: true` and charged against the same card and byte bounds (one that does not fit is left out with one `stale.hint_left_out` record per pass, and saving the cursor keeps that card's hold, because the analysis never saw it), and the extraction prompt asks it to look for the reversal; the reason never goes to the provider, so the Hide AI disclosure is unchanged (D-06, D-08, D-15). If the operator's words reversed the card, the conflict rule already replaced its text; if not, the card is given again. A session with fewer than three operator events asks no provider and its cursor still moves, which ends the hold the same way (B10).
- `hide memory release <card>`, which any caller with the Project may run, the operator's way back (D-10). A person's release also deletes the revision's `stale_reports` row in the same transaction, so any session a prompt gave that revision may report it once more; any other release leaves the report spent. The core reads who the caller is from its credential and its pane (`MemoryCaller`, decided in `Runtime::prepare_memory`; the two credentials are in [hided and the WebSocket boundary](#hided-and-the-websocket-boundary) and [delivery.md, Commands and caller identity](delivery.md#commands-and-caller-identity)): a pane-bound caller is a person only while neither Herdr's agent rows nor the delivery ledger name a live agent in its pane, and an agent otherwise, its session known or not. A checkout-bound caller is never a person, because a terminal outside every pane and a tool shell of Codex's shared app-server daemon arrive the same way; its release ends the hold, keeps the report spent, and its answer says that a release run in a Hide shell pane with no agent gives the report back. An agent that opens a shell pane of its own and releases there reads as a person, since Hide does not record who opened a pane. The answer and the `stale.released` record carry the caller (`person`, `agent`, `checkout`) and the report (`given_back`, `spent`), and the record names the caller's session when an agent's is known.
- The deadline. The hooks and every list above compare it with the time they read at, so a Project no pass reaches frees the card on time; the 60 s pass deletes the row (`stale.released`, cause `expired`) (D-07, B9).
- Any change to the card's text or state (edited, replaced, forgotten, undone): a trigger on `memory_items` deletes the hold whichever write path made the change (B12).

A report needs a card a prompt gave the session, so it reaches the agents with a Memory hook (Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, Pi, omp and agy); Grok and Cursor are given no card by a prompt, and their report is refused like any other session's without a receipt.
An OpenCode report's place is the session's message count, which includes the step still being written that ran the command, so analysis passes it only once that step is finished and a later message is read; a pass that stops at the unfinished step leaves the card held.
OpenCode 2's sessions are read from its own tables like 1.x's, and its prompt gets its card from the terminal half of Hide's plugin ([agent-hooks.md](agent-hooks.md#opencode-2)), in the pane that shows the session.
The reason is masked, folded to one line with no control character and its markup escaped before it is stored, and `show` prints it quoted as the reporting agent's unchecked words, since another agent reads it back.
A report never changes the conflict rule, and the reason is never a card's source (B8).
The pass, the command and the release write `stale.held`, `stale.refused`, `stale.released`, `stale.hint_left_out` and `stale.offset_unavailable` records with the card number, session and a reason code, never the reason's text (B16).
There is no screen, snapshot field or protocol change for a hold (D-11).

Schema 16 adds both tables and the trigger; the writer keeps the v15 store as `.v15-backup` first, which a Project's delete scrubs like the v7 and v8 backups, and a failed move leaves the store at 15 to retry.
A read-only hook refuses the store until the writer moved it and answers without Memory meanwhile, and an earlier build refuses a v16 store with `WrongSchema` (D-13, B14).
Regression owners: the tests of `hide-memory/src/store/hold.rs`, `a_card_a_prompt_gave_the_caller_is_held_shown_and_an_agents_release_spends_its_report` and `only_a_release_from_a_pane_with_no_agent_gives_the_report_back` in `herdr-core/src/memory_command.rs`, `hide_memory_reads_a_person_only_in_a_pane_no_agent_runs_in` in `herdr-core/src/runtime/tests/workspace_control.rs`, `a_reported_card_is_named_to_its_sessions_analysis_and_comes_back_after_it` and `an_opencode_session_is_analyzed_once_by_message_and_its_report_ends_by_that_analysis` in `herdr-core/src/runtime/memory_analysis.rs`, and `hook_memory_context_with_a_trust_attribute_is_injected_in_every_message_shape` in `hide-session/src/lib.rs`.

### Drawn ahead of Herdr

A tab creation, split, zoom, resize or pane close Hide asked for reaches the screen before Herdr confirms it (PRD instant-pane-topology D-07): Herdr's answer takes tens of milliseconds, but its layout event arrives hundreds of milliseconds later.
The prediction is drawn onto the session payload after every confirmation has read Herdr's own layout (`runtime/drawn_ahead.rs`), so the operation records, the close pipeline and the focus readback judge Herdr's layout and never their own drawing.
A zoom, a resize and a close are drawn at the request; a split and a created tab are drawn at the answer, because only the answer names the new pane, and a created tab waits for its pane's layout as a one-pane tab of the checkout's owner workspace (at most `PROVISIONAL_TAB_LIMIT`, each dropped at the close stage timeout).
Opening an owner can acknowledge the new tab before its workspace is known; the same bounded provisional record holds that creation without inventing a workspace or treating its missing layout as a retired pane.
Herdr's layout, the existing timeout or connection loss ends that provisional record.
Geometry is laid out with the same split arithmetic that projects Herdr's layouts (`pane_prediction.rs`), so the confirmed layout replaces the drawn one without a jump when they agree; when they do not, Herdr's wins and `pane.prediction_mismatch` records both shapes.
A close is drawn on the canvas only: the closing pane keeps its sidebar row, session and read state until Herdr confirms the close.
Whether a close waits in its tab's line is Herdr's layout's answer, not the drawing's: once a close draws its sibling gone, a ⌘W on the pane left drawn alone still closes one of two panes in Herdr's tab, so it waits behind the close ahead instead of meeting it in progress, stays drawn until it runs, and leaves with its tab.
A waiting close starts only after a settling close ahead has left the tab's records (one that failed or whose result is unknown stays for the operator, so the waiting close starts and meets its refusal, which drops the line) and after the session update in progress has been applied, because it reads the tab's panes from the navigator, which shows the previous session until then; a close started from it would see its tab change before its effect and cancel itself.
A drawn change republishes through `RepublishWaker`, which wakes the coordinator once per burst, so the drawing costs one publish and nothing per frame.

A terminal's grid follows the drawn rectangle the moment it is drawn (PRD grid-ahead-of-herdr D-03, which replaces instant-pane-topology D-08): the shell fits each terminal when its host rectangle changes and sends the changed grid as `terminal_resize`, and the core sends it to the pane's node at once, confirmed layout or not.
That is safe because the pinned Herdr never sizes a PTY from its layout: the PTY is the size the controller client attached with and last sent as `terminal.resize`, through a split, a sibling's close and a zoom alike (D-02, measured on a private 0.9.3 server and asserted by the client's contract test against the pinned binary, [The Herdr wire boundary](#the-herdr-wire-boundary)).
So a grid sent on a prediction is right whenever the prediction is, Herdr confirming the same layout moves nothing, and a layout Herdr draws differently moves the host once more, so the view reports and the PTY takes one more size (`pane.prediction_mismatch` records the shapes); a refusal draws the confirmed layout and the grid returns with it.
The grid hold this replaced waited for Herdr's layout before sending a size, which put Herdr's close answer (0.7 to 4.3 s measured) and its layout event between a pane close or zoom and the terminal's reflow; the "tug of war" that once motivated it (9570a2a) was Hide itself sending two sizes as the optimistic and confirmed layouts alternated, which drawing the prediction over every publish until it settles does not do.
A `pane_op.timing` record's `first_frame` for a resized pane is the first frame after that pane's resize went out, so it may precede `applied` (D-06); the stage names and fields are unchanged.

Keys typed before the pane that should take them exists, or while its terminal session is opening, are held rather than dropped (D-11), by the pane's node (`hide-node/src/terminal/input.rs`), since keys reach it without the core (see The terminal path).
After a new tab or split the shell sends keys against the request; they wait until Herdr's answer names the pane and its shell reads keys (below), and are then written to it in order, and a refused creation discards them.
A new tab's or split's pane runs a shell, and until its line editor reads, its terminal is in canonical mode on macOS and Linux: the kernel echoes the keys on a line the first prompt then starts after, and its erase removes one byte, so a Backspace over a Hangul syllable left a broken character that zsh drew as `<ffffffff>` and read together with the next key.
Herdr's answer therefore holds the request for the new pane (`held_for`, as a fork's start does) and a worker reads, every 50 ms off the lock, whether the shell reads keys itself: Herdr's `pane.process_info` names the shell and the node of its machine answers `Call::LineInput`, which is `Keys` while the shell alone holds the terminal, or `Console` for a Windows console, which keeps keys as input events, so there the first read that names the shell ends the wait (`runtime/terminal_input.rs`, `ShellWait`).
The wait ends at most 2 s after the request opened, about when its first key reached the node, with every read cut to what is left of it, so the second left inside the 3 s a held key is still written at covers the core's own lag and a local socket's connect, which has its own bound: a shell with no line editor or still in its startup files then gets the keys as before, and a read that fails (a shell Herdr has only just started) is read again until then and named in the end's diagnostic.
A creation Herdr answers after that bound (a split queued behind another operation of its tab, a Task's tab after its worktree) gets no wait, since its oldest keys would be too old to be written by the time the shell reads, and a repeated answer for the same pane starts no second wait.
Each end leaves one `terminal.input_shell_wait` diagnostic with its outcome (`reading`, `timeout`, `unreadable` or `late`) and how long the wait took.
Meanwhile the shell's mark keeps sending keys against the request, and a key typed into a pane a pending request is held for goes against that request even after the mark ended (a pointer press elsewhere), so no key overtakes the held ones or skips the wait; a ready request ends the mark only once its pane holds the keyboard, so keys typed where the creation was asked for still follow it there (`web/src/keyTarget.ts`).
A pane a pending request is held for counts as the request's pane for its layout and close rules below, so keys waiting for a pane that closes or leaves are discarded too.
The core keeps each request's row and tells the node what became of it (`runtime/terminal_input.rs`: `RequestOpen`, `RequestResolve`, `RequestDiscard`); keys for a request the core has not named yet are held as pending for up to ten seconds, because they can reach the node before the core's word does.
Each holder keeps at most `INPUT_HOLD_LIMIT_BYTES` (64 KiB) and at most `INPUT_REQUEST_LIMIT` requests are held at once; crossing a cap discards what was held with a diagnostic, and nothing reaches the screen.
Held keys are discarded, never written elsewhere, and each discard leaves one `terminal.input_discarded` diagnostic naming its reason.
The creation made no pane for them: `refused`, `failed`, `not_sent`, `no_pane`, `unknown` (Herdr's answer was ambiguous), `unchanged` (Herdr answered that the split changed nothing), the reason of the operation ahead when a refusal drops the line behind it, or the agent phase for an agent tab that never started.
The pane was made but will not take them: `not_shown` (created in the background, so keyboard focus never goes there), `pane_gone` (the pane is closing, or left the layouts after it was laid out), `released` (its attach was released), `session_failed` (its terminal ran out of retries and waits for a manual reconnect) and `read_only` (its session opened as an observer).
A cap was crossed: `limit` (64 KiB), `evicted` (a waiting request pushed out by a newer one) and `reused` (a request id taken by another creation).
The keys are too old: `stale`, for each key the pane could take only more than `HELD_INPUT_MAX_AGE` (3 s) after it was typed, because keys typed that long ago are no longer what the operator means to run; the diagnostic names the pane and the byte count, never the content.
Each key keeps the time the node received it from the request into the pane's hold, and its age is checked when the core resolves the request to its pane, when the pane's hold takes a newer key (so stale keys never push fresh ones over the cap) and when the session opens; keys arrive in order, so what is discarded is the oldest part and the rest goes in, in order.
The rule is per key, as D-11 states it, so the fresh part can act without the stale part: an Enter typed a second ago runs whatever the prompt holds after the command typed four seconds ago was discarded, and the shell sends each key, a whole escape sequence or a whole paste, as one chunk, so no sequence is split by the discard.
Only Herdr's own layout marks a request's pane laid out, never a pane Hide drew ahead, so a provisional tab that expires before Herdr lays its pane out keeps its keys; the cost is that a pane Herdr names and then removes before any layout lists it keeps its request and held bytes until a newer request evicts it or Hide closes the pane, bounded by `INPUT_REQUEST_LIMIT` rows and 64 KiB each; the bytes reach another pane only if Herdr gives that pane the same id within `HELD_INPUT_MAX_AGE` of their being typed, which the restore worker below records the pinned Herdr doing for a just-closed pane.

Every tab creation and pane operation Hide starts leaves one `pane_op.timing` line in the diagnostic log with each stage's milliseconds after the request: Herdr's answer, the first drawn snapshot, the first and the first layout event, the confirmed layout ingested, its snapshot sent, and the pane's first frame (D-14, `runtime/op_timing.rs`).
Herdr's share is `layout_event - herdr_ack`; the rest is Hide's.
The record carries ids and times only, and at most `OP_TIMING_LIMIT` are open at once.

The notifier announces once per burst rather than once per change.
`Core::snapshot_delta` (`herdr-core/src/handle.rs`) clears the announcement flag **before** it takes the lock; clearing it after the read would swallow a change that landed during the read.
Launch creates the core once, immediately: the core only needs a herdr binary lazily, when a pane attaches (see hided and the WebSocket boundary), so nothing blocks its creation on Herdr.
The first attach uses the size the view reported, or the size persisted from the last launch, and never a placeholder; a pane with no known size is held back and says it is waiting.
An attach asks for the grid the view itself reports whenever one is known, because that grid is the one the frame guard accepts: starting at a settled size the view had already left held every frame until a resize, and a pane that is not drawn cannot send one through the display tick, so the shell samples a settled size once its layout is ready.
A frame at a foreign grid is diagnosed once per grid, not once per frame; the held frames themselves are the ordinary signal that the resize has not landed.
An observed pane is the exception, because Herdr draws an observer at the grid it attached with and an observer has no writer to resize: a view size the observer was not attached at, once it has held for 150 ms (the async-operation tick acts on it), or its frame at another grid than the view's, attaches the observer again at the view's grid, so a view that changed size after falling back to observing neither holds every frame from then on nor keeps an idle pane's old frame reflowed.
A window drag costs one new observer, not one per intermediate size: frames at the old grid are held while the size settles, and one attach is in flight at a time because the pane has no session until it lands.

A clicked path is one event, not a sequence.
The shell resolves the token on the filesystem and decides which registered checkout owns it by the longest symlink-resolved prefix.
A file of a checkout other than the one in front opens where the operator is and moves no checkout: an HTML file is one `browser_open` naming the front Workspace, and another file or Open source is one `file_open` naming the file's own checkout and the front Workspace as `view`.
The core accepts that `view` only for the Workspace in front and a checkout of its device (`file.invalid_context` otherwise); the file stays its own checkout's editor tab, so hided checks its path against that checkout's root, the read and the save use that root, and the checkout's own Workspace binds the same tab, while the display belongs to the front Workspace.
A display therefore reads from the checkout whose root holds its path, its Workspace's own when it does (`display_source`): a restart's restore, a Retry and the binding of a restored display use it, a checkout adds no display of its own for a tab another Workspace shows, and closing a display keeps its document while any Workspace still shows it.
The Explorer selection follows only a file of the checkout in front, since its tree holds no other.
A file of the checkout in front, an HTML file of another checkout while the Overview is on screen, and a folder go through its checkout as before.
An HTML file opens with one `browser_open` event naming that checkout and `reveal: true`; after the page is admitted, the core uses the existing Workspace reveal operation to bring its checkout and Browser View forward together.
The active Agent tab remembers that page before the checkout comes forward, so a restored tab's previous View bookmark cannot hide the requested page.
Another file, a folder or the terminal menu's Open source sends `reveal_path`; the core then decides the focused checkout, the tree's expanded set and selection, the editor tab and the lane together: a file opens the lane on Files, and a folder, which no tab shows, opens it on Files with the tree on (PRD right-lane B16).
A window on a node resolves the path against its own machine's checkouts, which the core lists in that node's session rather than its navigator, so the `file_open` or `reveal_path` names the node in `device_id`: hided then leaves the path to the node's helper instead of the core's machine's roots, and the core finds the checkout in its catalog (`catalog_checkout`).
A checkout of a device's session is that device's Herdr's to bring forward, so the core moves no focus of its own for it: one in front there is revealed at once, and one behind, as a path clicked on the Overview names, is asked of the device's Herdr with the device brought forward (`Runtime::reveal_when_in_front`), held on the Workspace choice that waits for the device's front (`PendingChoice::reveal`), and revealed once the sync sees the front land there; nothing is read before, and the reveal dies with the request: a refusal or a lost answer, a later choice, the device leaving, the device's front moving to a third checkout, or its deadline passing, `CLOSE_STAGE_TIMEOUT_MS` (5 s) for the answer and, once the device accepts, `CHOICE_LANDING_TIMEOUT_MS` (15 s) for the landing, which waits on the device's Herdr and then its session's publish over the link (`Runtime::expire_pending_choice`), drops the choice and all it carries, a Memory visit as a reveal, so a later visit of the operator's own to that checkout opens nothing for the old click; the expiry reaches only the log (`workspace.choice_expired`), since the operator has nothing to act on.
Dispatch is fire-and-forget, so four separate events would arrive as four frames and a refusal partway would leave the screen half moved.
A path outside every checkout never reaches the core: the shell hands it to macOS, opening a file in its default application and a folder as a Finder window, and revealing rather than opening anything whose default application is the operating system running it - an executable file, an application bundle, an installer package - because link detection is a guess over arbitrary agent output and one wrong click must not start a program.
In the web shell the desktop host is that filesystem: `hideHost.probePaths` answers each path's physical path in the wire spelling (The platform layer, `path`) and its kind, taking at most 64 absolute or `~/` paths per call, and `hideHost.openPath` takes a path outside every checkout, or any path on a ⌘-click, and decides open or reveal in the main process (`desktop/src/main/localPath.ts`), so the page never chooses to run anything.
What a type's default application does differs from Mac to Mac, so the host opens only an allowlisted document type or a folder without an extension, reveals everything else and any document with an execute bit or a program's header, and refuses a path that is not its own physical spelling when it is judged or opened, so a symbolic link swapped in after the hover is never followed.
The line a link names travels page-local (`web/src/editor/lineRequest.ts`) to the editor that opens the file, because the core's file events carry no position.
A page without the desktop host, and a pane on an SSH device, resolve no paths and link URLs only ([UI_BEHAVIOR.md: Terminal links](UI_BEHAVIOR.md#terminal-links)).

A spawned child does not split the operator's pane.
Herdr owns split geometry and the PTY size, so a delegated child pane is really moved out - `pane.move` to a new tab in the workspace it is already in - rather than left undrawn; a tab holding nothing but delegated children then stays out of the tab strip while remaining in the checkout.
A move stays in flight until the published layout shows the child in its own tab, not only until Herdr acknowledges it: the move's events arrive after the answer, and asking again before they did moved the pane out of the tab the first move had made, so no layout ever landed and the moves repeated; `RELOCATION_RETRY_INTERVAL_MS` bounds a move whose events never come.
Detection is the same on every pass, so a child that arrives while Hide is running and one already split when Hide started take the same path, and a refusal is retried on a fixed interval rather than assumed to have worked.
Herdr reports a refusal as an unchanged move with a reason rather than as an error, so the decision reads `changed` instead of trusting a successful request.
Machine-qualified lineage is resolved before tab ownership is projected; local snapshots, remote snapshots and machine identity changes all update the tab strip and Agent area membership in the same transition.
A disconnected parent restores its unresolved local child to normal placement, and reconnecting removes it from the bar while preserving sidebar selection as a delegated canvas.
If normal placement is full, the unresolved child waits and the core moves its keyboard and visible-tab identity to a shown tab in that same transition; a local catalog rebuild preserves known delegation until canonical lineage resolves it.

Ownership is the fifth derived status axis and it is read off the lineage, never stored.
Ordinary delegated rows can only be Working or Seen; their changes turn ancestors unread without changing the ancestors' own groups.
The six core-owned escalation causes in `docs/status-model.md` are the explicit exception: the child's lineage root enters Needs You and Sessions carrying the child's ask, and the root's pane band opens the child.
The lineage is therefore built before the read axis is applied on every ingest, and there is no clock, timer or second store for it.
Before the lineage, each pane's saved completion is laid back on a pane a restored Herdr server no longer reports finished (`agent_state::apply_completions`), so an ancestor sees the descendant completion it saw before the restart; [status-model.md](status-model.md#across-a-herdr-restart) owns the rule.
The single state owner is `herdr-core/src/agent_state/`: `axes.rs` derives lifecycle, completion, ownership and read state, `turn.rs` derives groups, request verbs and runtime action gates, `sessions.rs` derives the Sessions tool's groups and task tags, `escalation.rs` derives the six raise causes from delivery facts, `header.rs` derives quiet pane bands, `work.rs` associates PRs and issues and assigns each duty, and `tally.rs` owns marks, representative ranks and scope counts.
Its scope projection also carries list sections and checkout trees; the web resolves their pane IDs and physical occurrence indexes and applies locale-aware typography and alphabetical placement without choosing status priority, membership or counts.
The `agent_scope` projection carries physical totals, Overview membership and request membership as separate values because their existing screen rules differ.
The scope projection reads Factory worker panes from the current summary’s column cards, excludes them from requests and the Overview attention count, and keeps them in physical agent lists.
A changed Factory summary refreshes the scope in the same publication; unchanged worker membership reuses the cache.
Device scopes also carry connected row listings and pane places, and the PR projection carries the tile's turn breakdown, so the shell does not reconstruct ownership or sum attention groups.
`agent_state/tally/scope.rs` owns their calculation and one bounded cache of the current input and output, refreshed on agent or catalog reconciliation, with no worker, timer, subprocess, I/O or serialization.
Existing close targets also carry `tally/close.rs` consequences: confirmation priority, stop-work rows, and outside-descendant rows and counts.
`tally/tasks.rs` derives the Overview's tasks (PRD overview-device-tasks D-04, D-05, D-18): a task is a lineage root and every descendant it has on any device, with this project's checkouts they work in, the pull requests the link record joined to their sessions and the four-step stage `작업 → PR → 검토 → 머지` (absent for a task that is only a conversation).
Each project's `agent_scope.tasks` carries its cards in the section's order (needs you, blocked, working, waiting, done, then resting and cleanup, which the shell folds), each card's members root first, the devices and other projects it delegated into, and its Needs You count.
A device a root's child was started on carries that work as an `incoming` entry naming the root's device and project, which the shell draws dashed and never counts; a child whose parent's machine the core cannot resolve is a root of its own (B15).
A pane is placed once, at its first placement across the local and last-known remote catalogs, so the same task is never counted on two devices.
The tasks are derived, never stored, and rebuilt only with the scope cache's compared input; the shell draws titles, rows and PR marks from the agent rows it already holds and resolves the projected project and device IDs for navigation without regrouping or recounting.
`work.rs` also publishes the request row’s selected PR and ordered issue keys, including folded-chip recency, while `tally.rs` owns local and remote device and removal totals.
`tally/relations.rs` supplies command-palette lineage groups and their issue keys, and `tally/cleanup.rs` supplies checkout-use counts, preserving last-known disconnected descendants separately from live close targets.
Text search and pixel geometry stay in the web; filters select from the core's priority order without deriving a new status.
The cache includes pane close flags and labels so a pane-only change refreshes an open sheet; it adds no close action or runtime enforcement path.
Catalog rebuilding restores cached scope fields before equality checks so an unchanged source does not create a publication merely by constructing new project objects.
`sidebar.rs` retains payload and snapshot assembly, `request_view.rs` retains request block assembly and timestamp persistence, and `pet.rs` selects a pose from the local summary.
These functions operate on the facts already projected by the core, including remote rows; they introduce no reader, timer, worker or I/O under the runtime lock.
The existing `sidebar::tests`, `request_view::tests`, `pet::tests` and `runtime::tests::lineage` exercise these shared rules through their original projection callers.
`runtime/session_state.rs` owns durable live resolution: a successful UI-state save acknowledgement publishes the saved resolution, an activity fingerprint rejects stale acknowledgements, and new input, delivery or working activity restores a row.
The same coalesced writer acknowledges dormant conversation and live resolution records from the exact saved frame, applying both results before announcing a change outside the runtime lock.
The owner-thread clock checks the end of the 24-hour Resolved window through the existing tick; it does not create a timer or per-frame publication.
Closed-session PR links are read outside the runtime lock, and Sessions deduplicates them against live PR ownership.
An unchanged remote raw session reuses its complete published projection, including resolution and machine-qualified lineage; applying read fields to a freshly built copy is not itself a runtime transition.
Pane bands travel in `terminal.headers` through the terminal metadata wire and the rest-section stamp.
The stamp compares the core-owned header map, so an unchanged band publishes no rest delta; header changes are copied with the changed owned snapshot and serialized outside the runtime lock.
`docs/status-model.md` owns both rules.
The Agents `My Work` view filters only the core-final Delegated answer and leaves visible orphans in operator-owned groups; `All` changes only the shell's session-local visibility projection.
`agent_start_in_checkout` creates a tab in the checkout's owner workspace (see Checkout owner workspace below) through the task-operation slot that `create_worktree` already uses.
It is the one start path for every surface (⌘N's start panel, New worktree, an issue's Start, a pull request's hand-off, the phone; PRD home-device-rail D-20): it names a checkout by path, or `home` for the device's Home (see Home below), and a `device_id` for a device's checkout, which opens its tab on that device's Herdr through the checkout's owner there (`worktree_control::TabTarget::device`; a tab needs no file helper, so a device whose helper is not ready still takes one).
A start may name a `model` only for an agent whose start declares one, the one rule of the shared adapter (`LaunchDialect::options()`): Claude Code, Codex, Grok, Pi, omp, Cursor and agy take `--model <id>`, and OpenCode, whose CLI lists no model option, takes none, so a model for it is refused with that reason rather than dropped.
The id must pass `hide_agent_adapter::valid_model` (`[A-Za-z0-9._:/\[\]-]`, at most 80, never starting with `-`), the one check every start and restart uses; the CLI judges whether it knows the model, and a refusal stays on its pane, never replaced by another model.
Herdr's `agent.start` types each argument into the pane's shell single-quoted, so an id such as `opus[1m]` reaches the CLI as written (measured on Herdr 0.9.5 with a stand-in agent that recorded its argv, 2026-10-18).
The model and effort an agent was started with are kept for every restart Hide makes of it, as fields and never as a replayed argument list: `ui_state.pane_launches` (`runtime/agent_choice.rs`, saved in `core-state.json`) holds, per pane, the kind, the Herdr name Hide started the agent under, the `LaunchChoice { model, effort }` and the conversation it describes, written by the task start, by `hide agent spawn --model --effort` and a Factory worker's start (`Command::Spawn`'s `launch`, kept once the start is typed and before the agent shows its session, which Codex CLI reports only at its first turn), and by every restart below.
A record describes only the agent Hide started under that name: it follows the conversation that agent reports (a `/clear` moves it along), describes an agent that came back from a Herdr restart without its name only while it reports that conversation, and is dropped when the pane shows another name, another known kind or an unnamed agent on another conversation, or leaves its machine's topology, so an agent started by hand in a reused pane id never inherits it.
A removed device's records leave with it, and the map holds at most `PANE_LAUNCH_LIMIT` (1024) records; a start past that is logged as `pane_launch.limit` and its restarts run on the CLI's defaults.
The record covers only agents Hide itself started: an agent the operator typed in a pane (`claude --model opus --effort high`) restarts on the CLI's defaults, and ⌘N, New worktree and an issue's Start carry a model but no effort, so a Claude Code agent keeps its effort across a restart only when `hide agent spawn --effort` or a Factory worker started it.
A restart names the recorded choice explicitly, so a model the operator switched to inside the conversation (`/model`) gives way to the one the agent was started with.
A wake (in its pane or dormant), a fork, a pane's Reopen and a closed tab's or pane's reopen each copy the choice into their own record (`SleepRecord`, `DormantRecord`, `ForkRequest`, `ReopenRequest`, `ClosedAgent`) and add it after the resume arguments, because a bare resume leaves it to the CLI's configured default: Claude Code 2.1.302 resumed and forked with the configured effort, and Codex CLI 0.163.0 forked with the configured model and effort (measured 2026-10-18).
The words come from the same declaration (`LaunchDialect::choice_arguments`): the model as the agent declares it and the effort in the kind's declared spelling, `--effort <e>` for Claude Code and `-c model_reasoning_effort=<e>` for Codex; a saved choice the kind does not take fails the restart with that reason, a fork before any pane is made for it, rather than starting it on another model.
A pane with no record (an agent started on the CLI's defaults, or by a build before the record existed) restarts on its resume arguments alone, as before.
Every start supported by the shared adapter writes that kind and the kind's model into `ui_state.agent_start`, which every start surface preselects next; `terminal` is never remembered, and a store written before this choice existed seeds the kind once from the retired `issue_settings.default_agent`, which the next save drops.
A start carries the surface's `request_id`, stamped on the task receipt and on a refusal's `last_error`, so each surface reads only its own answer.
A start may also carry `stay_behind` (the phone's, see The mobile companion): its tab is made without moving the desktop's visible tab or keyboard focus.
A first prompt goes as the CLI's own argument: `--prompt <text>` for OpenCode, whose positional argument is a project path, and a positional prompt after `--` for Claude Code, Codex, Grok, Pi, omp and Cursor.
Nothing is ever typed into the pane, where a startup question would take it.
Home permission roots go as repeated `--add-dir` arguments only to Claude Code, Codex, omp and Cursor; a checkout cwd remains available to every start independently of those optional roots.
Start support grants no session reader, resume, fork, sleep, letter or bell capability.
Herdr passes every argument to the shell verbatim, one argv entry each, and refuses a line break or a tab as `invalid_agent_argument`, so `worktree_control::prompt_argument` writes each line break as U+2028 (LINE SEPARATOR), which Herdr passes through and the model reads as a line break, and a tab as four spaces, and refuses any other control character and a prompt over 64 KB, since Herdr types the command into the pane's shell and macOS bash took 27 s for 76 KB and did not finish 300 KB in 60 s (zsh: 5 s), against the 120 s start wait.
Each start event checks its prompt with that encoder before anything opens, so such a prompt is refused as `agent_start.invalid_prompt` (`worktree.create_invalid_prompt`) with no tab, worktree or Home sync left behind; a refusal from Herdr fails the agent start with its reason, and in both cases the surface that sent the prompt keeps the text.
Herdr types the command into the pane's shell, so the first prompt can stay in that shell's history and shows in the process list on the machine that runs the agent (D-26); no Hide log carries it.
The provider catalog the model menus list is read while a start surface shows one (`ai_settings.start_observing`, a second hided demand beside the Settings tab's `observing`), without the Settings-only hook diagnosis.
The worker that created the pane then starts the chosen provider in that exact pane with `agent.start` (`worktree_control::start_task_agent`), after the creation is published, so every request shares one executor and no client starts an agent itself.
The agent is named after its pane (`hide-<provider>-<pane>`), because the pinned Herdr refuses a name another agent already holds and two tasks often run the same provider.
Every `agent.start` Hide sends, a task's, a fork's, a restored tab's and a wake's, goes through `agent_start` (`start_at_shell_checked`, or `start_at_shell_reusing_name` for a Reopen and a wake): the pinned Herdr types the command only while the pane's shell is available, refuses with `agent_pane_busy` otherwise, does not wait, and announces no event when the shell gets there.
On macOS and Linux the shell is available while it alone holds the terminal (its foreground process group is the shell's own and has no other member, `available_pane_shell_from_job`); on Windows while no process in the system's process table names the shell, or a process below it, as its parent, compared by pid alone (`available_pane_shell` in `src/platform/windows.rs`).
Windows can give a shell the pid of an exited process that a running one still names as its parent (csrss.exe names the smss.exe that started it), and Herdr refuses that pane for as long as the process runs.
A pane just made is still running its shell's startup files, so the worker reads `pane.process_info` until that holds and only then sends the start; a busy refusal after a good read (a prompt hook took the terminal in between) goes back to waiting, since Herdr typed nothing.
The shell holding the terminal does not say its startup files are done: a startup file that asks a question (oh-my-zsh's `[Y/n]` update prompt reads one key with `read -k 1`) leaves the shell alone in the foreground too, and a start typed then answers the question with the command's first letter and runs the rest (`laude: command not found`).
A tab Hide makes for an agent, a spawn's tab, a fork's own tab and the new-agent tab in an existing checkout, therefore runs the account's shell from Hide's own command, the way VS Code starts its terminals: the node of the machine that runs the pane answers that command and its environment (`Call::ShellIntegration`, `hide_host::shell_integration`, a `ShellLaunch`), and the tab is made with Herdr's `layout.apply`, whose pane runs the given command whatever Herdr's own shell settings are (`live::create_tab_in`, `agent_start::shell_launch`).
zsh starts with `ZDOTDIR` naming Hide's files, which read the user's own in zsh's order with `ZDOTDIR` as the user has it (the user's own value comes in `HIDE_SHELL_ZDOTDIR`) and give it back, unset included; bash starts with `--init-file` naming a file that reads the user's files in bash's own order; fish runs a `-C` command after its configuration; on Windows, Windows PowerShell runs a `-Command` after the user's profile that carries Herdr 0.9.3's own prompt wrapper (the OSC 9;9 folder report Herdr gives its own PowerShell panes) inline, as Herdr does, since a script file would need an execution policy that allows scripts.
Each reports the Herdr pane token `hide_prompt` once the user's files are done, the mark a start in such a tab (`ShellStart::Integrated`) waits for inside the same wait; Herdr reads no OSC 133 prompt mark, so the token is the transport.
A spawn records the launched shell with its pane, so a later ask of the same intent waits for the mark too; a shell in the foreground that is not the one launched (a `.zshrc` that runs `exec fish`) marks nothing, so its start is not held for a mark and is confirmed after typing (`shell_wait.other_shell`).
The screen line a refusal carries is the operator's terminal output: it is cut to 120 characters, stripped of control, zero-width and bidirectional characters, passed through `hide_memory::redact`, and never written to the diagnostic log.
The shell starts as Herdr's `auto` mode would start its own, a login shell on macOS and not on Linux; bash cannot read an init file as a login shell, so its login file reads `/etc/profile` and the first of `~/.bash_profile`, `~/.bash_login` and `~/.profile` itself, and `shopt login_shell` stays off.
Hide-made agent tabs therefore run the account's shell (Windows PowerShell on Windows) even where the operator set Herdr's `default_shell` or `shell_mode`, since Herdr's API does not answer its shell settings and Hide does not read a config file it does not own; after a Herdr server restart the tabs come back with their labels running Herdr's own shell, without the integration, as any `tab.create` tab does.
The zsh and bash files live under `~/.hide/shell-integration/<digest of the files>/`, and the node removes another build's folder (only a digest name or a staging folder) once no pane has been started with it for an hour, since a shell reads its later files only after a question in an earlier one is answered.
Every shell Hide starts runs those files, so the folder is synced to disk before its rename into place, a root another account can change gives no integration, and a version folder whose files differ from the build's text, or that another account could change, is written again before any shell is pointed at it.
`worktree.create`, `worktree.open` and `workspace.create` take no command, so a newly opened workspace's first tab is replaced through `layout.apply`'s `tab_id` by one that runs it (`live::replace_tab`); the web's new-worktree start keeps its first pane, started plain.
`pane.split` takes no command either, so a fork into a split starts plain, as do panes whose shell is another (sh, nu, cmd), and a spawn there confirms its start after typing it instead (`docs/delivery.md`).
On Windows `pane.process_info` does not show the parent condition, so there the busy refusal is what waits, and a pane refused for good fails at the cap below.
Holding the terminal is not yet reading it: until the shell's line editor (ZLE, readline, fish) starts, the terminal is in canonical mode, where the kernel keeps a typed line itself and drops every byte past its limit, the Enter included (1,024 bytes on macOS, 4,096 on Linux).
A Factory first start passes a prompt of several KB as the CLI's argument, so typed into a pane whose `.zshrc` was still running it arrived cut at about 1,022 bytes and the agent never started.
A start line that can pass the shortest limit (`wire::agent_start_line_bytes`, the quoted arguments and the executable) therefore also waits, within the same wait, until the node that runs the pane says the shell's terminal takes keys (`Call::LineInput`, read off the lock by `hide_platform::process::line_input` from the shell's controlling terminal, since the pinned Herdr's `process_info` names no tty); a terminal still in canonical mode whose limit fits the line, and a Windows console, which has no line discipline, start at once.
A shorter line keeps the shell-holds-the-terminal rule alone and asks no node, so a shell without a line editor still runs it; a long line whose terminal cannot be read is refused with that reason rather than typed on a guess.
A start Herdr typed whose agent never showed (a cut line before this wait, a program that exited before Herdr saw it) keeps its name held (`launch_pending` with no agent) until that start's own deadline, the `timeout_ms` of 120 s Hide passes, and the pinned Herdr settles a passed deadline only when that agent is next read, which a server with no screen never does by itself, so every later start under the name was refused as `agent_name_taken` for as long as the server ran.
On that refusal a start reads the name once with `agent.get`, which lets Herdr settle it, and sends the start again only when the answer is `agent_not_found`; a name still held is an agent's or a start that may still show one, and the refusal is the answer.
`pane.release_agent` cannot free such a name: Herdr matches it against the pane's detected agent, which a start that never showed does not have.
Herdr binds that held name to whatever agent appears in the pane before the deadline, one started there by hand included, so a spawn asked again for the same intent waits while Herdr holds it, types the start once more only after Herdr let go and the shell is alone, and otherwise answers `agent_not_started` (`docs/delivery.md`); a new attempt closes the earlier pane while it still runs the terminal that spawn recorded, which frees the name with it.
The wait is capped at 30 s, Herdr's own default readiness wait; past it nothing was typed (a terminal still in canonical mode is named, with its limit and the line's length, in the failure and in the diagnostic), the start is a failure the task banner offers to retry, and the diagnostic `agent_start` / `shell_wait.timeout` records the pane's last process state.
A shell that held the terminal but never marked its first prompt stopped at a question: its start fails as `shell_starting` with the screen's last line and nothing else of the terminal, which the spawn's answer, the task banner and a Memory card's failed status name.
The answer lands on the task receipt's own axis (`agent_phase`: `starting`, `started`, `failed` or `unknown`, with `agent_message`): a failure keeps the worktree and the pane, a provider missing from the PATH of the node that would run it fails before Herdr is asked (the `agent_installed` call; a device's own Herdr answers for its PATH), a transport failure is `unknown` because the agent may be running, and only a definite failure is offered again (`task_agent_retry`, which reuses the recorded pane and refuses when it is gone).
The receipt cannot be acknowledged while the agent is still `starting`.
Worktree deletion first asks the checkout's host for a non-mutating authoritative recheck (`Call::WorktreeRemovalCheck`, `hide_host::worktrees::check_removal`) on the action worker, before any chosen descendant or checkout pane closes.
The check refuses a locked worktree with its name, Git lock reason and manual `git worktree unlock` action; no Discard choice unlocks it.
It also rechecks registration, HEAD, branch, protected base, nested worktrees, dirt and the exact ignored-repository names confirmed by the operator.
Only success admits the existing close sequence, whose worker rechecks the same contract before `git worktree remove` and the chosen branch removal (`Call::WorktreeRemove`, `hide_host::worktrees::remove_confirmed`).
That close sequence (`close_checkout_panes`) reads each pane's shell and foreground pids with `pane.process_info`, and every process under the shell from the process table of the node that runs the panes (`process_descendants`), before it closes any pane, and after Herdr's snapshot confirms the panes gone it waits, within the same `CONFIRM_TIMEOUT`, until those processes have ended, identified by pid and start time and named by their program, all read on that node (`process_starts`).
Herdr confirms a pane closed before its shell or agent has exited, and reports no process between the two, while on Windows a pane's `cmd.exe` runs the agent through `powershell.exe`, which can outlive both; a running process holds its working folder there, so the folder cannot be moved aside or deleted until it is gone; the wait therefore runs only for a folder on this Windows machine (`ProcessWait::for_folder_removal`), and a device's panes, whose pids belong to the device, and a project removal, which touches no folder, skip it.
On Windows the pinned Herdr ends a pane's processes only by closing its pseudo console, which ends the processes attached to that console: its later `TerminateProcess` pass over the shell's descendants opens each one without the right to end it, so that pass ends nothing. A descendant with a console of its own (a new or hidden window) or none (detached) therefore outlives the close, and the deletion waits for it like any other.
A process still running at the timeout fails the close with its program name and pid, which the delete dialog shows so the operator can end it and delete again, and the worktree is not touched; a refused move into `hide-removed` is logged as `set_aside_refused`, and the removal goes on to Git's own removal at once; on Windows a second line, `set_aside_holders` with the same `attempt`, lists the open handles on the folder or inside it (the owning process, the path, the access, and this process's own id beside them), because the error (32 or 5) names no one. The scan reads the system handle table on its own thread, one at a time (a scan still running makes the next record `scan_busy`), duplicating only handles of the file type, because a handle of some kinds can block the questions asked of it and the removal never waits for the scan.
The phases are `checking`, `closing`, `removing`, `finished` and `failed`; a failed preflight keeps every pane and file.
While the panes close, the core fails the removal if the worktree catalog shows the checkout moved (another HEAD or branch, a deletion gate, or a missing row) only while the reader still answers for its repository: closing a checkout's last pane can end the only Herdr workspace that asked for that repository, the next answer then has no project for it, which says nothing about the worktree, and the host checks the registration, HEAD and branch again before it removes anything.
Git is not left to delete the folder: when Git would remove it (registered, not the main worktree, not locked, clean unless forced), `hide_host::worktrees::remove_worktree` renames it into `hide-removed/` under the repository's shared Git directory, so `git worktree remove` finds it missing and drops only the registration, and a background thread deletes the files; a refusal moves the folder back, and a folder that cannot be renamed (another volume) is removed by Git in place within its own 60-second `REMOVE_DEADLINE` rather than the 15-second read bound.
A trash entry is deleted only once Git no longer registers it, and every removal sweeps its repository's trash, so an entry a stopped process left behind goes with the next removal, including a retry of its own.
A finished local removal takes its row out of the worktree catalog in the same frame and counts itself in `WorktreeRequest::removals`: the coordinator rebuilds the rows on its next wake, a read that started before the removal cannot bring the row back, and the reader re-reads only the removed worktree's repository, because Git changed that repository's own directory; a failed removal re-reads every project, since what stopped it is news.
The deletion gate (`worktrees::deletion_gate`) refuses the main worktree, a locked worktree and an unavailable ignored-repository scan.
Other risks are warnings, and a loss the folder would take (dirt, a nested worktree, an unreadable status or named ignored repositories) is a `discard_label` the operator must accept, which the core rechecks against its current row when the event arrives.
The host reads lock reasons from NUL-delimited Git porcelain and measures each ignored repository boundary, including `.git` directory, gitfile and bare forms, with 2,000,000 scan steps, 30 seconds and 1,024 names as hard caps.
It does not follow directory links or traverse Git metadata; unreadable, invalid or over-budget scans report why review cannot proceed instead of claiming an empty list.
An accepted discard allows nested-worktree and dirt checks through and removes with one `--force`; it never skips the lock or ignored-repository scan.
The event and `ConfirmedRemoval` carry the exact measured repository names the confirmation showed; a changed set requires a fresh review and Discard choice.
A branch the gate warned is unmerged goes with `git branch -D`.
An unsupported host check refuses before closing panes, with no fallback to unchecked deletion.
Git and Herdr have no shared atomic reservation: a change after preflight can still stop the final removal after panes closed.
No shell reports a removal's completion, so there is no event a client could send to claim one.
Only a pane outside the set Herdr confirmed closed counts as one that appeared during the confirmation, because the navigator still lists the closed pane until Herdr's close events are applied.

### Checkout owner workspace

Every checkout has one owner Herdr workspace, read from Herdr and never inferred from where its tabs sit (`checkout_owner.rs`, PRD checkout-workspace-binding).
A Git checkout's owner is the workspace Herdr binds to its path (`WorkspaceInfo.worktree.checkout_path`, which only `worktree.open` and `worktree.create` set).
A plain folder cannot be bound, so its owner is the live workspace carrying Hide's `hide_owner` workspace token, whose value is a digest of the device and the folder; Herdr drops workspace tokens on a server restart, and the next tab after one makes and marks a new owner.
The owner is decided from the session Hide already receives (`CheckoutSnapshot::owner_workspace_id`, never sent to the shell), so a snapshot costs no Herdr call.
Every tab Hide creates in a checkout goes to its owner: Add Tab, New agent, agent start (including a pull request handed to an agent), Reopen Closed Tab, registering a project Herdr has no workspace in (`live::registered_owner`), and opening a registered project that has no workspace, on this machine and on a device alike (`TabHost`).
A reopened tab takes back its old place in the strip only when it lands in the workspace it was closed from; one closed outside the owner, or in a plain folder whose owner closed with its last tab, keeps the place Herdr gives it.
A checkout with no owner open gets one first, on the control worker with the runtime lock released (`live::open_owner_tab`): `worktree.open` on its path from the repository's main worktree (Herdr 0.9.1 refuses a linked worktree opened from anywhere else with `linked_worktree_source`), which answers with the workspace Herdr already binds there (`already_open`), so a repeated or racing request converges on one owner; a plain folder takes a process-wide lock, lists the workspaces, reuses the marked one, and otherwise runs `workspace.create` at the folder and writes the mark.
When no workspace is bound to the checkout but an unbound one already sits in it, Herdr binds that one and answers `already_open`, so it becomes the owner; that is Herdr's decision, and Hide follows it.
A newly opened owner's first tab is the new tab; an owner already open gets `tab.create`.
A workspace Herdr newly opened is renamed as the sidebar names the checkout (a linked worktree by its branch, the primary checkout and a plain folder by the project) with `workspace.rename`; `worktree.open` carries no label, because Herdr applies one to a workspace that was already open too, and an operator's workspace is never renamed.
A refusal fails the request through the existing tab-creation failure and Retry, with the checkout and reason in the diagnostic log (`tab.control.failed`, `owner.mark_failed`), and nothing is created in any other workspace.
A tab Hide created belongs to the checkout it was created for, never to the checkout its pane cwd names: a new pane reports the cwd it was born with until its shell has entered the start folder, and Herdr sends no event when that settles, so a pane-cwd read put a new tab under another checkout until an unrelated publish (#400).
The core keeps the checkout from the creation's answer, `CreateTab` and `OpenOwner` acknowledgments and the task slot's agent start and Home tab (`WorktreeTaskOutcome::created_tab_id`), for at most 64 tabs with the crossing reported to the diagnostic log.
A record lives until the session has carried its tab and then lost it, or until the session becomes unreachable, since the next Herdr server numbers its own tabs; when the session placed the tab before the answer arrived, the runtime wakes the coordinator to republish at once (`RepublishWaker`).
The record is runtime only, so after hided restarts a tab Hide created is placed like any other; a recorded checkout no longer in the catalog is reported once (`created_tab.checkout_missing`) and its tab falls back to that rule.
A new worktree's first tab is recorded from `worktree.create`'s own `tab` (`created_tab_id`), under the worktree's path, on this machine and on a device alike.
Until a pane of a recorded tab reports a cwd inside its checkout, the core reads that pane's cwd, and its agent's, as the checkout's own path, before any reader of the session sees it (`clamp_created_tab_cwds`; the coordinator applies the same clamp before it builds the catalog off the lock).
Every reader then agrees: the project and checkout rows and the purpose mirror (`session_spaces`), the pane's `cwd` and the listeners attributed by prefix (`project_layout_panes`), and the tab's own placement.
Without it a birth cwd in a parent folder made a project row for that folder with no tabs, claimed the listeners of its sibling projects, and could give another checkout's purpose to the worktree's workspace.
Once a pane has reported a cwd inside the checkout the record is `settled` and the cwd is read as reported, so a later `cd` out of the checkout is not hidden.
A folder in another worktree of the checkout's own repository, listed below the checkout (`CreatedTabClamp`), is that worktree's, not the outer checkout's: a pane born there is still on its birth value and stays read as the outer checkout until it reports a cwd in the checkout proper.
A repository that merely sits below the checkout is not one of its worktrees, so a folder in it is the checkout's, as before.
The checkout is read once, when the tab is recorded, and the catalog's worktree paths once, when the catalog arrives, both as the file system names them (`PathRules::Local`); a session then costs one read of each pane cwd and compares the nested worktrees by names, so their number adds no lookup under the lock.
Known limit: the nested worktrees are the catalog's, so a tab whose pane is born in a nested worktree before the catalog has listed it settles at once and its cwd is read as reported from then on, and the settle is not undone when the list arrives.
The reason is that a settled record is a latch (a later `cd` out of the checkout must not be hidden), and a worktree is listed within seconds of the `worktree.create` that makes it, a window a tab created for the outer checkout rarely lands in.
A tab is recorded once: a second acknowledgment of the same tab keeps the first record, its window and its settled state.
A tab that reports no such cwd for 30 seconds (`CREATED_TAB_BIRTH_WINDOW_MS`; the birth value lasted about 20 on a loaded runner) is settled too and reported once as `created_tab.cwd_unsettled`: the clamp is bounded, never a permanent override of the shell's own folder.
The window is measured on the runtime's monotonic `birth_clock`, so a wall-clock step cannot end it early, and a test moves that clock instead of waiting.
Measured against a private pinned Herdr (0.9.1) server, `workspace.create` and `tab.create` through a link and a trailing `/` answer the pane's cwd resolved through the link and without the separator, and the schema only types `cwd` as a string.
On this machine the comparison reads both sides as the folder they name (`normalized_for_comparison`), so a checkout recorded in one spelling settles from a cwd reported in another.
The coordinator clones the payload for the catalog only when a recorded tab is in the session.
A tab Hide created on a device is kept the same way, by target and the device Herdr's own tab id (`created_device_tabs`, at most 64): the device's helper groups a tab by its first pane's cwd, so before the device's projects are grouped the core reads a recorded tab's panes as in the folder it was created for until one reports a cwd there.
A device folder is compared with the device's own strings unchanged (`PathRules::Device`, `hide_platform::path::wire_relative`), never on this machine's files or spellings, where the same name may be a link or another folder; the cost is that a device checkout recorded through a link the device resolves does not settle by path and is believed when the window ends, reported as `created_tab.cwd_unsettled`.
The worktrees nested below the checkout are those of the repository the device helper's worktree answer lists the checkout in, so before that answer arrives a folder in one reads as the checkout's, the same limit as above.
A device tab with no pane in the session is never settled by the window, as on this machine.
An acknowledgment that arrives after the device's session already carried the tab clamps that tab in the stored session and regroups it at once, rather than at the device's next publish; the stored session already shows the other recorded tabs as their folder, so only the new record reads it.
Its records leave with the tab, when the device's session fails (a later connection numbers its own tabs), or when the device is removed.
Readers of the cwd that need no clamp: the agent row's fallback name (`project_agent`) runs only when Herdr's workspace label is blank, which the projection never leaves blank, and the label worker (`labels/worker.rs`) uses the cwd only as the first directory to look in for a session it already has an identity for.
Any other tab, one made outside Hide by Herdr, a shell or another tool, belongs to the checkout its first pane's cwd names.
A tab already in a workspace that is not the owner stays in that workspace and is never moved (`pane.move` would change its pane id and cut its lineage, read and sleep state); the protected primary close's replacement shell and a delegated child's own tab stay in their workspace.
Tools outside Hide, such as sasu dispatch, choose their own workspace; `hide agent spawn` uses the core's checkout and device start paths.

What an agent has spawned in-process is not on Herdr's wire at all.
The hook helper reports it through the `pane.report_metadata` socket method, which Herdr defines as display-only pane metadata, and the core reads it back out of the pane tokens its ordinary snapshot already carries; `herdr-core/src/agent_hooks.rs` is the only place that reads those tokens.
A count Hide cannot read is reported as unknown, never as zero.
Herdr drops its pane tokens when its server is handed off or restarted, so the local coordinator hands each listed agent that lacks `hide_hooks` to a worker thread (`coordination/hook_tokens.rs`, the lineage writer's shape), which reads that pane's record under the helper's lock and sends the helper's own report, with the counts only when the record's agent and session are the ones Herdr reports for the pane; neither the file read nor the report is made on the session-sync thread or under the runtime mutex ([agent-hooks.md](agent-hooks.md#after-herdr-hands-off-or-restarts)).
Only the local coordinator does this, as it alone sweeps the records of panes Herdr no longer lists: the records are files of the machine the core runs on, and a device's records would need a node call, which is a node-link protocol change that is not made yet.
