A pane's parent travels the same channel, and it is the only lineage there is: Herdr records none.
hided is the sole writer of `parent_pane`, optional `parent_machine`, `child_session` and `parent_session` through `pane.report_metadata`; the unchanged value and lifetime contract is described in [status-model.md](status-model.md#where-a-parent-comes-from).
A fork is the operator's root, so its pane carries no parent token: hided writes `fork_pane`, `fork_session` and `fork_source_session` instead, which name where it came from without making it anyone's child ([status-model.md](status-model.md#where-a-parent-comes-from)).
Those tokens die with the pane, not with the agent, so a relationship holds only while both panes still report the sessions it was written for: `wire.rs` compares the child's, `agent_state::apply_lineage` the parent's, and an agent that took over a pane is a root.
A spawn without `--parent` is an operator handoff: `AgentRecord.parent` is empty and its separate optional `origin` records the spawner.
Delegated origin is derived from parent, and all CLI agent answers include nullable origin.
Origin never feeds lineage tokens, sidebar ownership, ancestor unread state, descendant badges, subtree close or the links worker's parent edges; a handed-off session remains linked to its own branch without a delegation line.
The one thing the screen reads from it is whether Hide itself started the agent: a handoff whose origin is Hide's code-owned identity (`docs/delivery.md`).
That is a third ownership value, `HideAi { operator_turn }`, beside the operator's own and a delegate's: such a row is a member of no operator list (checkout trees, Working, Done, Idle, badges, rail counts, Overview, the phone's list, notifications), and it is listed only while the operator has something to do, which `agent_state/turn.rs` derives as an ask of its own or a home turn (`PR #n 리뷰` while the attempt's pull request awaits the operator, `CI 실패` once checks failed and the agent stopped), never from the screen ([status-model.md](status-model.md#work-hide-ai-does-for-a-home)).
The core derives that from the ledger alone (`coordination::started`) and lays it on each live agent row as `hide_ai` (with the task Hide gave the spawn) and on the snapshot as `navigator.hide_ai_checkouts`, the checkouts Hide made, keyed by device and path.
The checkouts are a list beside the catalog and not a field on a checkout row because a row is rebuilt from Git and Herdr at several places and would lose a value laid on it, and a checkout stays listed after its agent ends.
The derivation runs inside `publish_delivery` and is compared by value with the last one, so a letter, a watch or another agent's registration stamps no new revision; only an agent or checkout Hide started appearing or ending does (`runtime/tests/lineage.rs`).
The home turn is computed from the attempt's pull request the Memory tracker already reads (`runtime/memory_promotion.rs`, `PromotionTracking.homes`), keyed by agent, and applied to the rows only when that table or the agent set changed, so a pass whose facts did not move adds no work to the snapshot path and `lay_hide_ai` touches no operator row.
When the tracker finds the attempt's pull request merged or closed it retires the attempt, off the runtime lock and capped at 16 attempts per check: the pane of the agent the promotion recorded (its coordination id, resolved to a pane through the ledger when the check is planned; an attempt with no recorded agent ends none, and no agent is chosen by its name) closes, and the worktree is removed with its branch kept when its tree is clean and no other agent or running program is in it, otherwise it stays with a `promotion.worktree_retired` diagnostic; a retire that could not be completed stays in the table and is tried again on the next check, and the same attempt retires once, so a repeat has no second effect.
The spawn receipt's `parent` continues to name the caller that authorizes every creation phase in both modes, with an explicit `SpawnMode` distinguishing responsibility and watch behavior.
Only delegation installs an automatic watch; handoff retains normal mail and explicit watches.
Version-1 receipts lacking mode default to delegation, and legacy `no_watch` fields are ignored while completed receipts and existing watches survive unchanged.
Caller plus intent is still the receipt key, and a mode change is an `intent_conflict` before side effects.
Both paths create a separate tab without focus, including a tab in an already-open checkout; interactive product tab creation retains its existing focus behavior.
Hide's fork and delegated `hide agent spawn --parent here|<self id>` register the relationship only after Herdr produces the real pane, agent and session; a registration failure preserves that pane and records a diagnostic instead of inventing an edge.
A registration whose agent has not shown its session yet (Codex reports it at its first turn) waits in the ledger, and the lineage writer finishes or ends it from the agent lists the session sync already reads (`coordination::identity_wait`, [delivery.md](delivery.md#a-registration-that-waits-for-its-agents-session)).
Hide's fork registers the fork with no parent and the source as `origin`, which only the source's own live registration may ask for: `coordination::link_fork` registers the source and records the wait, and the wait registers the fork.
A fork runs on a worker (`live::run_agent_fork_with_registration`): it makes the pane where the operator asked, a tab in the source's workspace whose pane runs the shell integration's launch, as any agent tab does (`live::create_tab_in`: `layout.apply`, or `tab.create` when the node has no launch), or a split right of the source (`pane.split`, which takes no command), both without focus, and hands the pane to the core under the lock (`Runtime::ingest_fork_pane`) before it starts the agent with the fork's resume arguments and the launch choice the source was started with (Agent start above), which the fork's pane then keeps.
The core then puts a fork tab right of the source's tab in the source's Agent area, through the same `agent_placements` queue a restored tab uses when Herdr's session has not carried the tab yet, and moves the keyboard to the fork with an ordinary operator pane focus, unless a view was chosen on the control lane after the press (`last_view_intent_serial` above the serial at the press), the rule a new tab follows, or the Agent area has no room to show the tab (`agent_can_show_created`).
The core's own move for one fork is not a view the operator chose: it records it (`ForkFocus`), so of two forks starting at once the later press keeps the keyboard, and a pane a replaced Herdr made is neither placed nor focused by the fork (`PendingFork::generation`); a pane focus already queued on the control lane when Herdr is replaced is the lane's, as any queued focus is.
Keys typed from the press until the agent starts are sent against the press's input request (`InputOrigin::Fork`): the row names the fork's pane as `held_for` once it has the keyboard, so the shell keeps marking keys for the request there, a click into that pane included, and the node holds them until the agent's start resolves the request, under the caps every held key has (64 KiB per holder, 3 s per key, so keys typed more than 3 s before a slow start are dropped and logged); a fork that yields the keyboard or fails discards them, and a failure closes the made pane, drops its placement and gives the keyboard back to the source.
`wire.rs` is the only token conversion boundary.
The consented helper supplies `hide_platform::host::machine_id` once in its connection greeting; the device worker applies it only to the established connection generation, outside `Mutex<Runtime>`, before resolving machine-qualified lineage.
A failed identity read logs a lineage diagnostic and leaves the parent unresolved while the helper stays usable; there is no SSH-alias fallback or extra identity SSH command.

Every shown Agent tab stays attached; recently shown tabs fill the remaining retention window.
The total is `max(ATTACHED_TAB_LIMIT, shown_count)`, at most six tabs, so an unseen attach never displaces a shown tab.
Herdr renders a pane for every attached client, so the core releases tabs outside that bounded set through the ordinary session drop.
A released pane keeps its projection entry carrying `released`; the shell disposes its terminal and redraws from Herdr's frame on the next visit.
Terminal metadata belongs to each session pane, independently of which pane is selected.
A selection that moves, including a switch to another checkout, an acknowledged new tab awaiting its layout, or a selected pane that retired clears only the selected pane's presentation and cannot erase other retained tabs' metadata; each authoritative layout still prunes panes that actually closed.
The shell disposes a parked terminal whose entry is missing, so a single list without a retained pane throws its renderer away even when the next list brings the pane back.
The attach window continues to release its oldest tab normally, and returning to an evicted tab restores its PTY content with a new renderer.
The idle tick attaches every shown tab's panes, while focus, read and sleep-visit state follow only the active area's tab.
All shown tabs count as on screen for sleep prevention.

Hide owns local Agent area membership and tab order.
Moving, reordering or splitting a tab changes the persisted Agent tree without `tab.move`, workspace creation or pane movement, preserving pane identity and lineage.
Herdr remains authoritative for tab existence; its reorder does not rearrange the saved tree.
Device strips retain Herdr-confirmed reorder (`place_checkout_strip`, `place_device_strips`): a move of a tab within its owning Herdr workspace sends `tab.move` to that device, and an offline device refuses it.
The legacy host without `workspace_views_path` retains its mixed file/Herdr strip compatibility path; the web host refuses its obsolete local `reorder_tab` event.

A pane that is going away ends its attach quietly.
Herdr closes the PTY before it reports the pane gone, so the attach child ends while the pane is still drawn; projecting that as `ended` is what flashed "terminal attach ended" over a pane the operator had just closed.
A close Hide asked for, or a pane Herdr has already stopped listing, projects `closing` with no notice chunk and keeps the pane's last frame until it is removed. Every other reason still reports `ended` with its message.

### Local agent delivery and inactivity watches

Receipt-proven spawn names use the existing lineage worker and delivery writer.
An append-only receipt count and placement revision guard naming admission, so unrelated letters and unchanged native observations build no naming plan.
Each batch admits at most 32 receipts, with one active and one pending batch and at most 4,096 attempted receipt ids per connection.
Each receipt needs at most four one-second Herdr calls and a five-second durable checkpoint wait, all outside Runtime and the session-sync thread.
The worker checks shutdown between receipts; stopping can wait for the current receipt's bounded calls.
Workspace and tab outcomes are persisted separately from spawn completion, and completed replay does no naming I/O.

The core's delivery worker owns a separate private ledger and returns mutation success only after atomic persistence outside `Mutex<Runtime>`.
Runtime holds an immutable ledger projection; one bounded writer queue and separately owned doorbell/watch producers stop and join with Core outside the lock.
The existing pane input path adds only timestamp assignments (the last input, from a key, paste, phone write or agent-find key; a submission is stamped only by a phone reply or a prompt hook of the pane's own session); the observation adds the time the pane entered `working`; watch snapshots publish start, warning and end transitions.
The daemon exposes typed `hide request`, `inbox` and `watch` commands through the existing caller-bound Workspace boundary without requiring a renderer.
The doorbell decides from hide-owned facts only (`doorbell::judge` over the pane's observation, never a screen read) and writes only to a pane at rest, and prompt-hook stdout flush precedes durable intake confirmation.
The observation carries what the pane's session read says it waits for in Herdr's current state (`doorbell::Turn`), taken from the label overlay the coordinator computes before it observes the payload; an agent whose read reports turns is held while a native question or plan waits (`awaiting_operator`) or while no read settled the current state (`session_unread`).
The doorbell's pass also decides which panes show letters waiting on a possible draft (`doorbell::letters_waiting`), in memory under the short lock it already takes to publish its holds and bounded by the open letters, and publishes only when a mark appears or goes; the shell's Deliver now is one event that releases the draft and leaves the ringing to the next pass ([delivery.md](delivery.md#delivering-a-held-letter-now)).
Herdr's readiness is three-valued at the wire boundary (`wire::Readiness`): only a launch in progress or an explicit not-ready refuses, and the unreported readiness of an agent started by hand is decided by those facts; a refusal after the verdict is retried on a capped backoff off the lock ([delivery.md](delivery.md#safe-intake-and-manual-fallback)).
Pending delivery becomes undelivered after 60 minutes; watch warnings use 20-minute inactivity, one tick for a target its own row shows stopped on a question or held at a blocked prompt, and first-warning time plus 60 minutes, with persistent counts and activity reset.
The helper's metadata-only session activity uses the same native ownership proof as the local reader.
A confirmed report to its parent ends the sender's watch immediately; unanswered parent warnings and overdue undelivered letters use the existing human notification paths once per cause.
Each letter keeps a durable `hook_confirmed` receipt separate from cancellation.
Acknowledgement can come only from the recipient's own pane and session, so it is that receipt; it still counts as checking a watch warning and does not schedule another doorbell.
A letter an earlier build acknowledged with `hook_confirmed: false` stays in the bell turn's batch after the pending letters and counts as open until its delivery deadline, when `Ledger::expire` ends that wait.
Automatic doorbells remain limited to pending letters; the delivery deadline ends the intake wait of a pending letter (it becomes undelivered) and of such an acknowledged one.
The first receipt ends a matching report watch; an actual post-flush confirmation still does when cancellation or the delivery deadline arrived between pull and confirmation.
Cancellation and the undelivered state prevent a new pull; an already-printed in-flight confirmation remains valid, preserving cancellation while changing undelivered to delivered.
Replayed confirmation after restart preserves a watch explicitly started after that first receipt; a report whose acknowledgement left no receipt does not suppress spawn's initial watch.
Legacy letters with an absent or null field prove confirmation only in `delivered` state; an older `acknowledged` record cannot distinguish manual acknowledgement from confirmed intake.
Older acknowledged records remain excluded from pull and keep their existing closed-state retention rules unless they still await a reply; the missing receipt never becomes delivery proof.
The same core owns registration, spawning, lineage, the mailbox and inactivity watches; no external coordinator or conflict probe remains.
A registration ends when its host's in-sync replica stops listing its pane: each publish hands the published pane set to `Runtime::observe_delivery`, which compares it with the previous one only when it moved (`coordination::gone_registrations`), and the delivery store ends what it found in its next transaction; a connect forgets the host's panes and notes the ledger's next id before asking for the snapshot, so a snapshot judges only registrations made before it.
[delivery.md](delivery.md) owns the CLI, capacities, privacy, completion reports, remote delivery, manual fallback and the residual external-input race.

### Explicit terminal attachments

The browser cannot name a dropped file's or a pasted image's path, so hided's own staging (`hided/src/attachments.rs`, see hided and the WebSocket boundary) is the only source of a `terminal_attachment` intent: it writes the uploaded bytes to disk and sends the event carrying the original pane ID, bracketed-paste mode, a generated request UUID and the staged paths.
hided reports `terminal_attachment_ready` itself once a clipboard image's bytes are staged at the exact path the core reads it from.
No clipboard image bytes enter the JSON event or the render mutex.

`runtime/attachments.rs` owns admission, captured terminal and remote connection generations, one active worker, retry, cancellation and an ordered 64 KiB input reservation.
Subsequent input to the originating pane is held by the pane's node until file preparation and transfer succeed, then the complete quoted path payload and held bytes enter the node's terminal writer together (`TerminalControl::AttachmentHold`, `AttachmentDeliver`).
No Enter is synthesized, and an upload failure never forwards held input, because it may contain an Enter that would submit an incomplete prompt.
Retry keeps immutable prepared bytes; cancel discards held input explicitly.
Missing, closed, released or reconnected targets retire the intent and cannot forward its data to a replacement session or fall back to the local host.
A local pane with no terminal session yet is still a target: the web shows a pane once its layout arrives, before its session attaches, so a drop can reach the core first (issue 735).
That intent waits for the pane's session, taking the first session the pane starts as its own, with the paste and typed input held under the same 64 KiB reservation, and both are written when the session attaches.
The wait is bounded by `COMMIT_GRACE` from admission (`hide-node-link/src/attachments.rs`, how long hided keeps a committed file from eviction, so the pasted path still names a file); the coordinator's operation tick checks the deadline with no event needed and fails the intent as unavailable, with a `terminal.attachment.terminal_wait_expired` diagnostic.
Only admission, completion and actionable failure transitions publish a notice through `status.async_operations`; terminal-specific actions render in the originating pane.

The node that holds the picked files reads them (`hide-host/src/attachments.rs`, the `read_attachments` call, asking the core before each file whether to go on, so a cancel stops the read between files): only explicitly selected regular files, refusing final-component symlinks, special files, control-character paths and files that change while read; `terminal_attachments.rs` keeps the limits' admission and constructs ordered quoted terminal input.
The bounds are eight files, 20 MiB per file and 40 MiB per intent; clipboard decoding additionally allows at most 16 megapixels.
`hide-node/src/ssh/attachments.rs` transfers the immutable bytes over the device's authenticated SSH client (`DialedTransport::stage_attachments`), not a shell command or a subprocess, and returns only remote paths.
Remote staging lives in the remote user's private `.hide-terminal-attachments` directory, with generated exclusive filenames, 0700 directory and 0600 file permissions, ownership checks and same-byte verification before adopting a completed upload on retry.
Connection setup is bounded at 15 seconds and transfer work at 45 seconds; cleanup has its own bounded connection and operation timeouts.
Neither filesystem nor network I/O runs under `Mutex<Runtime>`.

Both staging locations admit at most 128 files and 256 MiB.
Cleanup scans only their immediate generated entries on the next explicit attachment intent and expires entries older than 24 hours; there is no background janitor and successful paths may therefore survive longer while idle.
Local clipboard PNGs remain available after a successful local paste, while remote success releases the local PNG after upload; the core's own node removes it (`Call::RemoveClipboard`, refused for any path that is not a clipboard image), so the core touches no file.
Cancellation, retirement and destruction attempt to remove only that intent's exact generated files; failed remote cleanup is diagnostic and remains bounded by the next-intent expiry policy.
Original user-selected files are never deleted.
This boundary has no provider-specific draft, composer or attachment shelf.

### Reopening locally closed work

The core owns one session-local, twenty-item LIFO stack for file tabs and local Herdr pane or tab closes initiated through Hide.
Remote closes and topology changes reported by another Herdr client never enter it.
Every item names its device: a file tab closed on a device is that device's, a pane or tab close is this machine's.
Reopen restores the newest item of the device in front and `recent_closed` counts only that device's items, so a reopen on one device never restores work on another and never takes another device's newer close off the stack; a device's file reopens through that device's host.
A device's Herdr pane and tab closes are not recorded, so a device has nothing of that kind to reopen (PRD S5.5 D-21 exception).
Recording them needs the capture and marker-adoption protocol below run against the device's own Herdr and its agents' resume on that machine; that stays open until the device control path carries the layout export and the `HIDE_REOPEN_INTENT` adoption, and until then a closed device pane is reopened by making a new one there.
Before sending a Herdr close, a background worker exports immutable layout facts and the core records the close intent in its target scope; it enters `closing` immediately and starts the external effect after capture succeeds.
A close that removes a whole tab (its tab close, or its only pane's) takes that tab out of the Agent areas at once: `shown_agent_layout` publishes and attaches the stored layout less every tab whose close is preparing, transmitting or awaiting topology (`PendingClose::leaving_tab`), and the stored layout keeps the tab, so a refusal, failure or unknown result puts it back where it stood with no layout write to undo.
It is a projection over a close that still waits for Herdr like any other; no surviving pane's size depends on the closed tab, which is why it alone does not have to stay drawn.
When the closing item holds the keyboard, the operator moves to what that projection now shows, through the same tab focus a click sends: Herdr 0.9.1 focuses the closed tab's left neighbour (or its right one, for the first tab) on its own, and with Hide's focus pending, that move is read as its answer rather than followed; a refusal or failure focuses the original tab again when nothing has moved since.
When the projection shows nothing because every tab of the checkout in front is leaving, the operator's close (`retain_for_reopen`, which a sleep's own close is not; a close that opens a replacement shell keeps its tab, so its checkout is never empty) brings back the most recent other checkout of this machine in `ui_state.recent_checkouts` that still holds a tab no close is taking, through `focus_checkout`, marks it the Workspace a reload opens on, and records the move in the close's selection restore, so a refusal or failure brings the operator back as it does the tab (`move_from_closing_target`, `leave_emptied_checkout`).
A checkout whose worktree or project is being removed (`worktree_removal` checking, closing or removing, or `workspace_removals_in_flight`) is passed over too, because its tabs can still be open while the removal closes the agents outside it first.
The move is part of the close event, not a second event the shell sends, so a refusal cannot leave the screen half moved; with no such checkout or another device in front, the front stays and the shell draws the empty Agent area.
A close that first opens a replacement shell keeps its tab until Herdr confirms the close, so the area never stands empty while the shell is made.
A named tab's last agent pane gets its shell inside the same tab instead (`ClosedContext::replacement_in_tab`, decided by `Runtime::keeps_named_tab`; named means the operator's Rename gave the tab its current label, which the core records by tab id in `UiStateSnapshot::operator_tab_names` because Herdr keeps no author for a label, so a spawn's name or a store from before the record never counts): the close worker splits it from the closing pane with `pane.split`, unfocused, at the checkout and stamped with the close's `HIDE_REOPEN_INTENT` marker, and the pane joins the close's approved pane set before the agent pane's `pane.close`, so the precondition that refuses a changed tab still holds for every other pane; a retry adopts only the pane carrying that marker and makes a new one only when it is gone, and a failure after the split (a lost answer, a snapshot that does not show it yet) names the marked pane (`ReplacementFailure::shell_in_tab`) so `Runtime::note_shell_in_tab` adds it to the approved set and the retry is not refused as a changed tab.
The keyboard moves to that shell from the worker's own session read, which Herdr's event stream can still be behind, so while the close is sending or awaiting its topology an update that lacks the shell, or shows it before its folder is known, keeps the keyboard on it (`Runtime::close_shell_in_flight`) rather than retire it to the tab's focused pane, which is the pane being closed and would hold the keyboard after it is gone.
The context keeps the tab's name apart from the label Hide displays, and whether the operator gave it (`ClosedContext::tab_name`, `Named` against `Labeled`, so a reopened or woken tab is recorded as the operator's again under its new id), and a reopen or a wake that opens a tab sends Herdr only that name (`ClosedContext::herdr_tab_label`), because Herdr keeps any `tab_label` a `layout.apply` carries as the tab's own name; a pane reopened into a tab that still exists sends none, so a rename made after the close stays, and a record saved before the field reads as `Unrecorded` and sends `tab_label` as it always did.
A pane closing beside other panes in its tab stays drawn until Herdr confirms it, moving only the keyboard to a surviving pane, because Herdr decides the neighbours' geometry.
The captured item is a reservation separate from the twenty confirmed entries, so a failed or unknown close cannot evict older undo history.
A definitive Herdr refusal releases only that reservation; a transport failure or malformed acknowledgement keeps it available, starts one read-only status check, and reports the uncertainty inline.
The status check applies its fresh session snapshot to the navigator before classifying the reservation, so a target confirmed absent cannot remain drawn until another unrelated event arrives.
Only an authoritative absence promotes a reservation into the confirmed LIFO stack, in original user request order; the newest unresolved reservation blocks reopen from silently selecting an older item.
A reopen asked for while that newest close is still settling is held (`reopen_after_close`) instead of refused, because its tab has already left the screen: it runs when that close is promoted with a captured item and this machine is in front, and is dropped when the close ends any other way or a newer close starts, so it never reaches an older item.
The snapshot exposes the count, top label, pending reservations, in-flight state, async operation records, and inline notices; the shell routes the menu and shortcut and renders those values without keeping a second stack.
Unknown agent activity is a separate close guard: the core refuses local and remote destructive close until a fresh status is available, while ordinary working or unresolved demand uses the existing one-time confirmation.

A subtree close (`close_tree`, PRD close-agent-subtree) is one event naming the target pane or tab and the descendant panes the sheet showed at the press, and `runtime/tree_close.rs` runs it as one operation made of the ordinary closes above.
Admission happens once, as the event arrives: the listed panes are intersected with the live descendants outside the target, an absent one counts as closed, a pane that now needs a status check or an unconfirmed risky pane refuses the whole close, an ordinary close of a listed pane that ended refused or failed is dismissed as the retry it is (only once the close is admitted, and never one whose replacement shell may already exist), a tab held by something that needs the operator refuses the whole close, and the close slots must hold every local node, so a refusal closes nothing.
At most 64 panes per tree and four trees run at once; the admitted tree's local nodes reserve their slots against `RECENT_CLOSED_LIMIT`, so ordinary closes cannot take them halfway through.
Each node starts only when every descendant it waits on is confirmed absent, as a local `close_pane`/`close_tab` through the same capture path (reopen entry included) or as a device's `remote_control` close; the ingest paths and the async-operation tick advance the trees, so nothing waits or blocks under `Mutex<Runtime>`.
A node waits for its tab only while what holds it goes away by itself: a running close or pane operation, or a finished close queued behind a running one; a close that needs the operator (an unknown result, a failed replacement-shell close, a failed pane operation, a device close whose result is unknown) fails it at once, a tab still busy after the pane timeout fails it too, and a device that leaves the status list fails its node rather than reading as a confirmed close.
A refused, timed-out or disconnected node fails itself and every ancestor, which never start, while other branches finish; the tree publishes one `tree.close` async operation per node and writes `tree_close.started`, `tree_close.pane_failed` and `tree_close.finished` diagnostics with the tree and pane ids.
Delete worktree and Remove project take the same tree as a prelude when the operator chose to close the agents outside: the removal is recorded first, the tree runs, and only a tree with no failure hands over to the ordinary pane close and removal; a failed tree fails the removal with its reason, and a retry lists only what is left.

Recreation also runs outside `Mutex<Runtime>`.
Pane restore uses the captured parent path, direct neighbor, split direction, original first-child ratio, and cwd, falling back to the tab's current pane and then the checkout root when the original facts no longer exist.
A direct pane sibling uses `pane.split`; a sibling subtree is wrapped at its captured nested parent with `layout.apply`, preserving the surrounding tree and every existing ratio.
Tab restore resolves each captured pane's cwd and label, applies the remaining exported layout, restores its workspace position, and starts each captured agent in the new pane with the captured session id when the agent kind supports resume, followed by the launch choice it was started with (Agent start above); the pane the reopen made keeps that choice for the next restart.
The create, split, and applied-layout requests stamp their panes with a reserved `HIDE_REOPEN_INTENT` environment value containing the closed-item key and mutation stage.
On a retry, the worker adopts only a pane or layout carrying that exact marker; a new id, matching cwd, or matching workspace or tab label is never ownership evidence, so an unrelated agent cannot be interrupted as part of restore.
Both a recovered layout and the first `layout.apply` result must contain the exact expected terminal-pane count before any agent starts.
An under-count is repaired once by reapplying the fully tagged intended layout to the owned tab and revalidating the returned count; an over-count is never adopted or overwritten because the extra pane may have another owner.
The pinned create and split mutations have no operation context, and the request envelope id is correlation only, so this marker is the topology evidence that makes an acknowledged-late mutation converge without guessing.
The pinned Herdr can reuse a just-closed pane id while its old PTY still owns the agent process, so the restore worker interrupts that reused process before `agent.start` applies the explicit resume arguments.
A failure before a pane or tab exists retains the same closed item for retry; once a tab exists, failed agent starts degrade to a shell or fresh session and publish a pane-local notice rather than offering a partial pane retry.
File reads use the filesystem worker, and missing, unreadable, already-open, and successfully reopened files each have an explicit result.
A file close carries its matching debounced save in the same core event, writes that exact path and contents outside the runtime mutex, and removes the tab only after the current draft saves successfully.
No reopen notice uses the shell's modal interaction alert.

### Agent sleep

Claude Code and Codex agent sleep (PRD agent-sleep) ends an agent that has sat seen and untouched past the operator's chosen hours and resumes its conversation in the same pane when the operator comes back; it exists because Herdr has no hibernate of its own.
Three owners keep it replaceable when Herdr gains one (D-20): `herdr-core/src/agent_sleep.rs` decides and remembers and makes no call, `herdr-core/src/agent_sleep_herdr.rs` is the one module that touches Herdr and processes, and `herdr-core/src/runtime/agent_sleep.rs` connects them to the runtime.
The local coordinator's tick runs the decision at most once a minute; a tick inside the minute is one integer comparison, and the minute pass reads the agent rows and makes no call under the lock.
Each decided end runs on its own worker, at most four at once: it reads the agent again with `agent.get`, refuses unless it is still idle or done and the same kind, has the pane's node send SIGTERM to the pane's foreground process group (`terminate_group`, which the node refuses for a group of 1 or less) only when `pane.process_info` names a group that is not the shell's, and waits up to ten seconds for the shell to hold the terminal again.
It never escalates to SIGKILL; a refused or timed-out end leaves the agent awake and the pane alone for an hour or until its state changes.
The pinned Herdr then drops the agent from `agent.list` with its session reference and answers a prompt to the pane with `agent_not_found`, so nothing reaches the shell and nothing guards against it; the sleep record, persisted in `core-state.json` beside the read records and off the wire, is what draws the row until an agent is back.
A wake is Herdr's own `agent.start` in the same pane, under the agent's Herdr name when it had one (orchestrators address agents by name) or `wake-<pane>` otherwise, with the provider's resume arguments from `recent_closed::resume_arguments`, or none for Start new session, followed by the launch choice the sleep record kept from the agent (Agent start above).
A committed visit that changes the tab on screen, Wake agent and Retry resume; a wake asked for while the end is in flight runs when it lands; a second wake while one runs starts nothing.
A failed wake shows a plain reason (`The working folder … no longer exists.`, `The conversation couldn’t be resumed.`, `The agent did not become ready in time.`) and keeps Herdr's code and message in the `agent_sleep.wake_failed` diagnostic.
The last look is stamped when a visit commits and once a minute while a pane is in the tab on screen, and the stamps are saved through the coalesced state save, never published.
A relaunch forgets an end that was in flight, because it cannot know whether it landed, and reads a wake that was in flight as still asleep until an agent appears in the pane.

The panes of a node that dials the core (A core on another machine) sleep as the core's own do (PRD core-host-node-remote-core B13); a device the core dials does not, and `agent_sleep::sleep_refusal` says so from the pane's `SleepMachine`.
That node's coordinator's tick runs the same minute decision for its panes, with its own minute; the four ends in flight are counted over every machine.
The records and stamps keep a node's pane under its `remote:<node>:pane:` id, and each machine's session settles only its own (`SleepScope`): a node's records are settled against its session as its Herdr names the panes, before the session is scoped to the device, and its rows are marked and stamped when the core takes that session.
An end or a wake for a node's pane runs on that node's Herdr connection and its link (`SleepTarget`), with the pane id its Herdr knows; the agent's Herdr name comes from the row's `herdr_name`, because a device row's `id` is the core's own.
A visit is that node's Herdr bringing a tab forward while the node is in front, which the core sees in the node's next session: the tabs that came on screen wake, and the last look of those and of the tabs that left is stamped.
Removing the node forgets its records and stamps; its panes are not touched.
A sleep that closes its pane (Pi) is refused on a node, because the dormant record reopens it from the core machine's catalog.

A provider whose implemented sleep dialect declares `closes_pane_when_sleeping` uses a separate dormant conversation record under the same persisted `agent_sleep` store.
The common mechanism does not grant a provider sleep support; its adapter must declare an implemented reader and resume dialect first.
The record's `SleepId` names the durable intent, while the captured native session, node, connection generation and execution sequence fence the old execution.
The existing coalesced state writer acknowledges the exact record only after private atomic replacement and persistence barriers; a failed or stale write cannot authorize a close or start.
Close admission reuses the existing geometry reservation and capture owner, saves the bound close key before sending its effect, and waits for authoritative disappearance before publishing a sleeping row.
The captured close carries an immutable non-reopenable purpose through its worker replies, so a dormant conversation has only its SleepId wake route and never enters ordinary Reopen Closed.
After that reservation retires, a late sleep capture or close reply cannot recreate an ordinary close operation.
The archive holds at most 256 conversations and four pending or uncertain transitions; a full archive or transition budget refuses admission without closing a pane.
An uncertain result retains its admission and offers explicit status inspection, with at most one inspection worker and a bounded intent-marker scan.
A recovered intent with no saved close key sent no close and is released by that status action without a pane effect.
A wake saves the intent before creating its separate tab, saves the owned tab before starting the provider with the saved native session and cwd, and clears the archive only after current reader facts confirm that conversation in that tab.
The record keeps its checkout's owner as the project list names it when the session sleeps (`DormantOwner`: the repository's main worktree or the plain folder, whether it is a git checkout, and the name a workspace opened for it takes), and the wake opens the checkout from that owner rather than from the list.
The list may not show the checkout then: a linked worktree with no open workspace is listed only once a window has read its repository, which no window may have done since hided started when the Wake comes from a phone, and an unregistered project is listed only while one of its workspaces is open.
A record an older build saved has no owner, so its wake finds the checkout in the list as before.
A Wake on a session whose project the operator removed opens its checkout and lists the project again, by decision: pressing Wake on that session is the operator asking for it.
The row hides Wake for every lasting reason a wake would be refused, and one function (`dormant_wake_blocker`) decides both: a missing reader, an invalid record, another machine's record, no Herdr connection or, for a record with no owner, an unlisted checkout hides it and says why, while a failed wake keeps the reason it failed; a spent transition budget or a busy checkout passes and keeps Wake.
Its stable intent marker permits reuse of only its own tab; no pane address, input or terminal capability is copied into the new execution.
A session that held a delivery registration when its pane closed saves that registration (its id and the execution that held it) in the same record, from the delivery ledger at admission, and the wake continues it; the core started the wake, so it knows which registration the new pane takes over and nothing is matched by name or guessed afterwards.
While the record holds a registration, the closed pane's absence ends neither the registration nor the watches on it: `observe_registration_panes` queues no end for it and the watch readings do not call its target gone, so letters sent to it stay pending instead of failing with `target_unavailable`.
The wake starts the agent under the Herdr name the registration was made with, waiting out Herdr's release of the closed agent's name (`agent_start::start_at_shell_reusing_name`); a session with no registration is named as a fork.
An agent whose CLI runs no session hook on resume (`LaunchDialect::resume_reports_session` is false: Cursor, whose `sessionStart` hook is Herdr's only source for its session and does not run on `--resume`, issue 938) would leave the woken pane with no session in Herdr, so no reader fact, no observed session and no lineage token could ever confirm it.
For such an agent the wake worker, once Herdr has started the agent, says which session the pane resumed with `pane.report_agent_session`, under the source its Herdr integration uses (`herdr:<kind>`) and a nanosecond sequence, which is exactly what the missing hook would have sent; everything after it is the ordinary confirmation below.
The report is fenced on the saved wake intent like every other write of the worker; a report Herdr refuses ends the wake as `WakeUnknown` with `agent_sleep.session_report_failed` logged, and nothing is sent again.
The report names only the id the core resumed; when Herdr later reports another session for the pane (the agent's own hook after a new chat, which carries a later sequence from the same source), Herdr's record wins, the pane's `child_session` token stops matching and the relationship ends as for any pane that hosts another session, while the registration stays with the pane.
Once current reader facts confirm the saved conversation in the wake pane and the pane's observed execution is the same native session, kind and device, the runtime queues a `coordination::Rebind` and keeps the record, and its sleeping row is withdrawn; the delivery store's next pass moves the registration, its letters, its watch and its spawn receipt to the new pane in one transaction, and the lineage writer then writes the parent tokens there because its reconciliation key includes where each registration's pane is.
The record is cleared only after the published ledger shows the move.
A woken pane that is another conversation, kind or device, a registration the operator ended meanwhile, or a pane another registration already holds is not continued: `agent_sleep.registration_refused` is logged with the reason, the hold is released and the registration ends as any whose pane is gone.
Restart converts pending work to Unknown and never resends creation, close or start automatically.
These transitions live in the `dormant` child modules of the same three sleep owners, and all persistence, Herdr and folder I/O stays off `Mutex<Runtime>`.
A Factory worker's sleep (`docs/factory.md`, The worker lifecycle) is the same record with the Factory's worker bound to it.
The host publishes its sleeper panes, each with the worker in it and the kind and start time of its agent, and the core binds the intent to the Factory, the worker's agent record, name and worktree when the pane and the agent kind match, whoever asked for the sleep.
A record saved before the host published its worker is unbound, and a wake accepts it only for a worker of the same kind that started before the sleep.
A close the Hide sleep made is not an operator close: a confirmed dormant close skips the pane-closed report to the Factory, while any other close still pauses the Task.
A Factory wake binds the saved conversation to its worker as it begins, so the wake is asked only for a record that is the worker's own; a record whose close has not landed answers "ask later", and an agent started after the sleep or of another kind answers "awake".
When current reader facts confirm the conversation in the new tab, the record is kept as `Woken`, with that tab's pane, instead of being cleared, because the pane is the only way back to the worker.
The host reads these records each tick, registers the new pane under the Factory outside the runtime lock, has the engine rebind the Task's worker, and only then asks the core to release the record.
A `Woken` record no one binds within an hour is dropped with the `agent_sleep.woken_unbound` diagnostic, and a record whose wake failed or stayed unconfirmed stops the Task once.
`Woken` is never published in the snapshot, so no screen draws it.
The directory-chain barrier is the platform layer's on every system (The platform layer, below); a chain whose barrier fails refuses dormant persistence rather than returning a durability receipt.
The chain is a fact about the state folder, not about one save, so the core makes it durable once per folder per process, by the folder's file identity, and again only for a folder replaced since (`persistence::establish_folder_once`); every save still flushes its file and that file's folder.

## The link record

The link record (PRD link-graph) keeps which sessions made or worked on which pull request, which issues each pull request is linked to and from where, and which worktrees each branch had, after the panes, the branches and the provider files that said so are gone.
Owners: `herdr-core/src/links/` (`store.rs` the SQLite store, `worker.rs` the one writer, `query.rs` the `hide links` answer), `herdr-core/src/runtime/links.rs` (the facts handed in and the panel applied), `hide-session` (the shared line parser the link facts come from), and `hide-host/src/serve.rs` (`link_files`, `link_read`).

It lives in its own file, `links.sqlite3` beside `session-search.sqlite3` (`hide_kit::layout::links_store`), so the two have separate failure, write-lock and size boundaries; it reads each project's retention from the old search file's `policy` table through a read-only `ATTACH` (`LinkStore::policies`), and nothing writes that file any more (see [Project sessions and Memory](#project-sessions-and-memory)).
One worker thread, `hide-links`, spawned in `Core::create` and joined in `Drop for Core`, owns the only write connection; the core hands it owned facts (each GitHub project's pull requests with their issue links and sources, the navigator's worktrees, live panes' session ids and branches, and the core's resolved parent relationships) when they change, and it applies them as idempotent upserts.
The schema moves by `PRAGMA user_version`; a newer version than this build knows is refused (`links_store_newer`), and a file that fails `quick_check` is set aside once as `links.sqlite3.corrupt` and rebuilt from the session files still there, with a diagnostic for what could not be rebuilt.
The file is private, `secure_delete` is on and `max_page_count` caps it at 64 MiB; crossing the cap refuses the write (`links_store_full`) and the panel says so.

Session files are read only through the worker's own cursor, one bounded read per turn (20 ms of work, then a 30 ms rest), `hide-session`'s shared line parser, and never under `Mutex<Runtime>`; the core's own node lists and reads them (`link_files`, `link_read` with one file), so the worker opens no session file itself, and a call that fails ends the listing as failed, to be listed again from its start; the first start backfills the last 90 days once (meta `backfill_done`), then lists files changed since the last list every 15 s, every 3 s while a panel is open (B29), with a 60 s overlap.
A listing is read page by page: a page is the newest 2,000 files, a full page is followed by the next older one (a `listing.capped` diagnostic says so), and the listing's time is recorded (meta `listed_at`, and `backfill_done` the first time) only once its last page has drained with every write kept, so a failed write is listed and read again rather than skipped.
Only a file that is not there marks a line's file gone (`session_file_missing`); a file the system refuses to open is `session_unreadable`, recorded against its stamp and retried when the file changes.
A line keeps the request text (500 characters at most), the branch spans, the pull request addresses it printed (20 per session) and its continuation and parent links; no conversation body is copied.
A session is linked to a pull request when it printed the address within -2 s to +30 s of GitHub's `createdAt` (`created`), or when a branch span inside the project's folders overlaps the pull request's life, from the previous pull request on that branch closing to this one closing (`worked`); a non-interactive run (Claude `entrypoint` of SDK or print, Codex `exec`) makes no line.
A link is closed by an end time, never deleted, and only when its source says it ended: a closing reference GitHub no longer lists, a Hide branch link that was cleared on a branch a checkout here holds; a failed GitHub read closes nothing.
Retention follows each Project's Copied history: Off keeps a line only while its file exists, which the own node answers for all of them in one `real_paths` call (a call that fails removes nothing; an OpenCode session, which lives in OpenCode's database, until its read finds it gone), otherwise a line stays while it ended within the period, or within a year while its pull request is still open; the prune runs every 30 minutes on the worker.
A repository is keyed by GitHub's repository id when `gh repo view` gives it, so a rename keeps its rows.

A connected device's sessions come through its helper: `link_files` lists the device's changed session files and `link_read` reads up to 8 of them, each answer only link facts (helper protocol 32 and later include bounded request and issue facts); the worker asks each ready device at most once a minute, a device whose call failed is left alone for a minute before it is asked again, and a device that is gone keeps every row it gave.
The owning node converts the native cwd in each `LinkRead` answer to the platform layer's wire spelling before sending it, so Windows agent paths relate to catalog paths with the same separators without the core interpreting another device's native path.
The panel's read is one request at a time, the latest only, answered as `link_panel` on its own delta revision beside `rest`; `links_open` names the pull request or issue, a generation fence drops an answer for one no longer shown, and a failure keeps the lines read before.
A store that cannot open is tried again every 15 s, and while it is closed, or while the worker's writes keep failing, the panel's answer is that failure rather than a record that stopped growing.
The worker re-reads an open panel after it writes, and publishes the small per-Project summary (`link_summaries`: session counts by pull request and issue, each session's pull request chips, and whether the first fill of this machine or of a device is running; a later listing turns no spinner) at most every 2 s and only when it changed.
The summary also names, in the core only and never on the wire, the checkouts whose work landed: each session line that made its pull request or worked on its branch (an address it only printed is not work) is given to the deepest of the Project's current worktrees that holds its folder on its own device, counted only if it started there since that worktree was added (`WorktreeSnapshot.created_at_unix_ms`, so a worktree made again at a used path inherits nothing, even through a resumed or still-running session, and one whose age is unread has nothing); a checkout on a branch weighs only that branch's pull requests, so a branch switched in place starts clean, and a checkout landed when those include a merged one and no open one.
The runtime stamps that onto `CheckoutSnapshot.landed` (`runtime/links.rs`, `associate_landed`) when a summary arrives and on every catalog or GitHub change, only while Git also counts the checkout's HEAD in its base; with no summary nothing is landed, so a checkout with no record of its work, which Git ancestry alone reads as merged, is neither drawn merged nor folded as settled.
`hide links pr <number> | issue <number> | branch <name> | session <id> [--all-projects]` is read-only and answers the panel's record as one JSON line (the target, the repository, each pull request with its state, issue links and sources, worktrees and whether each was removed, and each session with its ids, device, role, times, request, file path, whether the file is there, and parent).
It answers through hided's own core without a renderer, scoped by default to the Project of the calling checkout, which may be a device's (a pane on a device reads that device's Projects, and `--all-projects` reads this machine's and every connected device's, the same catalog the link worker records): a target in another Project fails with `other_project` and a hint to pass `--all-projects`, a missing one with `not_found`, an unreadable record with `links_unavailable`, each with a non-zero exit.

### Session work records

The link store's version 2 adds the session work ledger, keyed by device, agent kind and native session id.
Version 3 retains one immutable nullable original project and repository for each retained session, including descendants.
An observed connection can identify only a session that started after that connection was known; first discoveries of older history and records migrated from versions 1 or 2 keep unknown repository identity.
The original project is the deepest connected project root or recorded worktree on the session's own device whose folder holds its cwd, chosen by one SQLite query (`capture_session_origin`).
The record keeps every worktree a project has had, so their number never bounds that choice: loading them all to choose in Rust, behind a 512-row refusal (`ledger_project_capacity`), stopped recording every new session on a machine that had made 572 task worktrees, and each record opened then read as failed.
The writer resets its connection observation window on restart, and an accepted repository edit advances that project's window even when changes return to the same repository before the worker observes them.
The save acknowledgement posts the new connection epoch to the writer for both local and device Projects, with an unknown repository until its new provider read answers.
A retained cursor's same session identity cannot certify a new birth when the native file is replaced or truncated.
Both local and device intake certify only a birth strictly before the node read began, with no newer project facts pending when its answer returns; otherwise ordinary reader data is kept with an immutable unknown origin.
A device clock ahead of the writer may therefore leave its original repository unknown rather than assign a current connection without a valid observation window.
These rows share the store's size cap, device conversion and Copied history pruning; no later catalog lookup fills an unknown original repository or rewrites a known one.
Version 4 adds the backlog's durable add attempts and its missed table (below); a version 3 store keeps its session origins when it arrives.
`links/ledger.rs` owns its values and bounds, `links/ledger_store.rs` its transactions, `runtime/backlog.rs` the backlog's events, and `web/src/SessionRecord.tsx` its read surface.
Only `hide-links` writes it; record reads are latest-only mailbox requests, with separate delta revisions and generation fences.
A backlog GitHub create records the project's `github_place` target and connection epoch when it starts, and a missed line's create first compares the session's original repository with the one resolved now.
An unknown original repository or a confirmed connection to another repository refuses creation with `repository_changed`; a connection edit cancels the creates in flight for that project (`invalidate_backlog_repository`), which check before reading the repository, before reserving and right before `gh issue create`, and a late answer whose target or epoch moved is not published.
Every external create remains pinned to the original reserved repository, and a retry reconciles that durable attempt before another effect; a retry the ledger refuses because the repository changed fails with `repository_changed` rather than make an issue in the new one.
The helper's version 32 supplies canonical request senders and letter kinds from every supported native reader; an older helper keeps its existing raw requests and PR facts until updated.

The record retains requests of at most 500 characters, goal chapters, assigned tasks, END judgments, created outputs and missed work.
A Claude Code command record (`<command-name>`) is a request only when what Claude Code wrote after it shows the command went to the model: a command it answers itself, such as `/model` with its `<local-command-stdout>` or a `local_command` system record, is neither a request nor a Sessions last request, a search hit or label context.
The incremental reader holds a command record that has no reply in the bytes it read, rereads it with the next poll and so neither loses nor repeats it; a reader of a whole page or file with no reply after the command counts it as a request.
Requests already stored from before this rule are not filtered on read.
It bounds requests and turns at 2,000 per session, chapters at 200 and task groups at 200 (a split request's tasks are dropped whole), missed work at 200, new tasks at six and new missed items at three per END and 80 characters each, and continuations at two task groups.
Oldest entries beyond a retention cap are removed with a diagnostic, and the existing 64 MiB file and Copied history policy govern the whole ledger.
A record tree has a 2,000-node, 32-depth and 2,000-request/output budget; an overflow is a failed read, rather than a larger snapshot.
The update queue and label context cache each hold 512 entries, worker calls have 16 pending slots, and the context reader handles at most 16 misses per pass outside the runtime lock.
A cold label context requests a worker read and postpones END until authority and open work are known; it never substitutes a root author for unknown lineage.
The record calls a session what its reader calls it, the id in the session's own records, and a lineage edge is keyed the same way.
Herdr reports a Pi or omp session as its file, so a registration names such a session by a path; the store names it by the reader's id for that file, which the file's cursor holds once the reader has read it (`session_name` in `links/store.rs`).
Before that read the edge stays under the file, and the read that first names the file moves it to the reader's id in the same transaction, as it would have been recorded had the read come first; Pi 1.0.4 writes its file only at the first message, so a just-spawned Pi child has no file and no record yet (`a_pi_or_omp_child_spawned_by_its_file_is_in_its_parents_record`).
Edges an earlier build left under a file the reader had read move once at open, recorded in `meta` as `session_names` rather than a schema version, since the tables keep their shape and an older build still opens the file (`edges_left_under_read_files_move_to_the_readers_names_once`).
A lineage edge or a file's cursor can still name a session the ledger holds no row for: a spawn known before its file is read, a session retention removed while its file's cursor stays.
Such a session has no record of its own: a tree lists only the children the ledger records, and its label context carries its parent's name and no work, never a failed write (`a_session_the_ledger_never_recorded_fails_no_write_and_stops_no_context`).

START keeps its existing goal lock and nullable-goal behavior; both boundaries use `context_label.v14`, and END adds new/continued/no work, new missed items, resolutions and one output task choice in the existing call.
A new judgment names one task per independent deliverable of the request, one to six (PRD task-split-checklist D-01): the ledger stores them as one group, the first as `task-<offset>`, the id every task stored before splitting has, and the rest as `task-<offset>.<n>` (`links::ledger::task_group`).
The judgment's context lists the open tasks and the tasks of the newest `FINISHED_TASKS_IN_CONTEXT` finished groups, each group whole (`LinkStore::ledger_context`), and the prompt tells it that IDs sharing the part before the first dot are one request. A continue of any task of a group writes the turn's END to the whole group, the group counts once against the continue limit, retraction and the task cap remove a group whole, and a group's output and done verdict are the group's: Done only when its last END is Done and every PR assigned to any of its tasks is merged.
A live pane's chip reads `link_summaries.progress`: in the summary pass, and only when the store's revision (`LinkStore::revision`: this connection's changes and SQLite's `data_version`) or the set of pane sessions moved since the last read, the link worker reads the record tree of each session `PaneFact` names, as the label context already does, and derives its tasks, their PRs, `<done>/<total>` and its direct children's `ChildProgress` (`links::ledger::WorkProgress`); a session with no task has no row, a read failure keeps the row it had and is read again on the next pass, and the runtime publishes only a changed list, so nothing is read or built under `Mutex<Runtime>`.
A child counts done when all its own tasks are done, or with none when its last END is Done; it is working on a working or waiting task or status, other otherwise, and unjudged with neither a task nor an END, which hides the fraction (`ChildWork::of`); the record carries the same `child_progress`.
An unassigned session has no goal chapter; an accepted goal creates its first chapter without inventing a title for a greeting.
Roots count operator requests; a delegated record counts its initial prompt and canonical work requests from its named parent, never reports or other letters.
Late lineage retracts invalid judgments, restores the last valid END and recomputes output attribution in the same transaction.
Learned lineage comes from the core's proven parent pane relationship across local and connected-device snapshots, keyed with the owning device's native session identity; retained coordination registrations take precedence and keep ended parents available.
Project, pane and lineage facts use the same combined snapshot set, and a project's GitHub answers are selected by device as well as path.
The borrowed lineage fingerprint costs one pass over current local and device panes, agents and registrations; only a change allocates parent facts or posts them to the existing worker mailbox.
Past requests from summaries Off, failed analysis or a pre-ledger session stay raw and unjudged, without background reclassification when summaries return.
Completion requires the last assigned END to be Done and every assigned PR to be merged; no assigned PR makes the latter condition true.
Only a tool-printed address within -2 s to +30 s of GitHub creation makes an output, and descendant outputs are attributed once to the parent's spawn-turn task with their originating session retained.

The backlog's one add (`runtime/backlog.rs`, PRD overview-device-tasks D-10, D-13, D-21, D-34) is `backlog_add {intent, workspace_id, title, missed?}`: the shell names each add with an intent key, and the core makes the item in the project's own source with no form.
An add with no valid intent key is refused with an error and no row; every other add ends in a row of `issue_work.backlog`, made, on its way, or failed with why.
A memo is written to `local-issues.json` at once; a GitHub item is a title-only `gh issue create` on a worker, one at a time per device with at most eight waiting (`WAITING_LIMIT`), and an add past them fails at once as `queue_full`.
When one waiting item fails before its worker starts, the next one in line starts, and a worker that panics still answers as `unconfirmed`, so a device's queue never stalls behind a failure.
A GitHub item is pinned at its add to the repository target and connection epoch its project names then; when either differs as it starts or is tried again, it fails as `repository_changed` and nothing is made, in the old repository or the new one.
A GitHub add is a durable attempt in the link ledger, recorded before the external effect, so `backlog_retry {intent}` (even after a restart) first looks for the issue that attempt made and names it instead of making a second one; that search never names an issue number another unfinished attempt in the same repository already claims; that search adopts only an issue the signed-in account wrote (`viewerDidAuthor`), so a same-titled issue someone else filed is never taken for Hide's, and the create itself names the reserved repository outright (`GH_REPO`), so a remote changed after the reserve cannot move it.
The ledger keeps the newest 256 finished typed items and never evicts an unconfirmed one, since its row may still retry it; it holds at most 32 unconfirmed at once (`PENDING_BACKLOG_LIMIT`), all of which a restart offers back, and a new create past them fails as `queue_full` until one is confirmed or put away.
No request, answer or body leaves the device: the issue carries the title alone, and the missed line it came from is kept only in the ledger's missed table (`backlog_key`), which also marks the item's row (`issue_work.missed_keys`).
One missed line is one item: a second add for the same session and line makes no row while the first is shown, and a memo whose line the ledger no longer has open (another view put it away) is deleted again and its row fails as `refused`.
Each row carries its phase (waiting, creating, created, failed with one of the fixed failure codes) and `retry`, true only on a failed row `backlog_retry` can act on: one whose job is kept, a GitHub create or a memo that could not be saved, which the retry makes again.
An add refused before anything was made (an invalid title, an unknown project, summaries off, a missed line of another device, no link record, a full queue) is a failed row with `retry: false`, `queue_full` for a full queue and `refused` otherwise; at most eight such rows are kept (`REFUSED_LIMIT`), the oldest leaving with a `backlog.refused_evicted` diagnostic.
At most 32 retryable failed items are kept, past which a new add is refused as `queue_full`; `backlog_dismiss` forgets a failed one, and the intents of the last 64 made items no longer shown are remembered, so a late resend of one makes nothing; on a missed line's row it also puts the line away in the same event, and a retry of a missed line's row is refused while summaries are off, as its first press is.
`backlog_undo {workspace_id, task_key}` deletes a memo or closes the GitHub issue (`gh issue close`, on the allowlist) only when Hide made that issue and its repository is the one the project names now, and the missed line it came from opens again; an undo answered after a later one still takes its item out and opens its line, and only the `issue_work.undo` slot follows the newest undo.
Hide made an issue when a typed item's or a missed line's create in the link ledger completed with its number (`MadeIssue`); the worker asks the ledger before anything reaches `gh`, an issue the ledger does not name fails as `refused` with a `backlog.undo_not_made` diagnostic, and without a ledger the undo is refused outright, so Hide never closes an issue someone else made, across restarts and a core move, since the record travels with the ledger.
An undone typed item's create is marked `undone`, so it names the issue no more, and `issue_work.made_keys` lists the issues the ledger names (newest first, at most 1024), which is all the panel reads to offer `되돌리기` on a GitHub issue.
`backlog_origin` reads where an item came from for its panel through the node that holds the session (`session_work_turn`), asking only links that are up and starting no reconnect, and starting an item from missed work puts that turn's request and answer in the agent's first prompt on this device only.
Each item has its own slot in `issue_work.origins` (at most 32), and a slot carries that turn's request and answer only while some window shows the item's panel or Start dialog: a window sends `backlog_origin_close {task_key}` when the last of its own holders closes, the daemon (`OriginHolds` in `hided/src/demand.rs`, at most 8 items per connection) passes it on only when no other connection holds that item, and drops a closed connection's holds the same way, so one window's close never empties another's panel and a later client never receives the turn.
The daemon sends that close while its holds are still locked, so another window's hold and read of the same item reach the core after it, and refuses an empty key or one longer than 256 bytes; a window reads its items again whenever its connection becomes live, since a reconnect is a new connection whose old holds were let go.
A read for an item whose slot is reading or ready leaves it as it is, only a failed slot is read again, and a read past the 32 open items is logged (`backlog.origin_capacity`) and shows no origin rather than an error.
Whether a GitHub repository is public is read with its other GitHub facts and carried as the source's `public`, unknown until GitHub answers.
The removed missed-issue draft (`session_issue_draft`, its templates and form) and the path that always made a GitHub issue whatever the project's source (`issue_create` and the `issue_work.create` slot) are gone.

## Historical usage accounting

`Call::UsageFiles` lists native candidates and `Call::UsageRead` returns one bounded, content-free usage step through the node boundary.
Candidate and read paths use `hide_platform::path` wire spelling, converted to native paths only by the node that reads them.
Both require the live link's independently advertised `ReaderFeature::Usage`; the node repeats that check before dispatch.
This optional reader adds no protocol-version requirement: an older peer grants no usage reader and is refused before a call is sent.
Cursor grants none, and protocol 24's frozen reader facts remain unchanged.

The usage worker owns one writer and reads only its supplied own-node link, alternating agents so a large history cannot starve another.
Each turn performs one bounded read and transaction, then rests for 20 ms; native JSONL parsing itself yields at 10 ms and never exceeds one MiB including its checkpoint witnesses.
OpenCode's usage read sizes each row from its record header (`octet_length`) before loading it, so a message or part past the 64 KiB row bound is skipped as `record_capacity` and the cursor moves past it; measuring with `length(CAST(data AS BLOB))` loaded the value, and one row past SQLite's 1 MiB value limit failed every read and held the cursor on it.
OpenCode 2.x writes one assistant message per step and sets its tokens and cost once, when the step ends, so a completed assistant message is one usage event, keyed by its message id and placed in its session's checkout; a step still running, or one that failed without usage, is read again or passed, and a completed step with no usage is skipped as `usage_missing`.
The row holds its whole content, which a step that ran a long tool can make several MiB, so SQLite's JSON functions read the step's few fields in place and only those come back, each text cut where it is taken.
A row is scanned up to 8 MiB, and one read scans at most 8 MiB of rows in all: a row that would pass that total waits, the cursor still before it, and the next read takes it, while the first row of a read is always scanned so a read moves on.
A row over 8 MiB is skipped as `record_capacity`, which the worker logs as `usage_records_skipped`, so its step is reported as unread and never vanishes.
A database 2.x migrated from a 1.x one holds both sets of tables, with every migrated message in `session_message` under the id `message` still holds, so one cursor walks the messages of both in time order and the table that holds a message decides how it is counted.
A message the 1.x `message` table holds is counted from its step-finish parts, keyed by part id exactly as before the upgrade, so a ledger filled by an earlier read gets the same keys again and counts nothing twice.
A message only `session_message` holds was written after the upgrade and is counted the 2.x way, keyed by its message id.
A 2.x database with only one of `message` and `part`, or either without a column Hide reads, is no state 2.x makes and is refused as `usage_read_Schema`.
A database of neither OpenCode schema is refused as `usage_read_Schema`.
A failed listing or read is logged as `usage_list_failed` or `usage_read_failed` with the node's `reason` (its refusal code, such as `usage_read_Database`, or how the link failed), and the agent is listed again 30 seconds later.
Two replaceable visibility flags control its work: without a window it rests; candidate discovery runs every 30 seconds, or 3 while usage is open, and changed reports publish every 30 or 5 seconds respectively.
Its owner signals the condition variable and joins it on shutdown, including startup failure and an idle wait.
Complete uncapped listings retire deleted file checkpoints without deleting their totals or recent dedup authority.

`usage_ledger::store` owns `usage.sqlite3`, named by `hide_kit::layout::usage_store` under the core state directory.
Normalized usage events contain native call identity, model, time, session, working folder, provider, tokens and recorded cost, with no conversation fields.
An ingestion transaction adds a new call and its daily `(native node, agent, local day, model)` totals together with the file checkpoint; repeated calls add nothing, and changed completed calls keep the first accounting value and report a count-only conflict.
Claude streaming records retain each token field's maximum for that native call, replacing its daily contribution and whole-call pricing band in the same transaction without another call.
A persisted checkpoint distinguishes a yielded read from EOF, so a restart cannot freeze an unread suffix.
Discarded capacity-overflow events leave a durable per-agent limited marker; a future timestamp is counted as skipped without poisoning its valid batch neighbours.
The day is assigned in the core's time zone at first ingestion and stays assigned after the time zone changes or the core moves.
New calls use the current core time zone.

Each agent's first backfill may fill older days within the 400-day retention window, and its event keys stay until that backfill's durable completion marker is written.
After completion, whole days before the 45-day event horizon are frozen: replayed or late calls before that boundary are counted as skipped and never change those totals.
The boundary is retained as a monotonic instant so a clock rollback or time-zone change cannot admit a call whose dedup key already expired.
Maintenance removes at most 512 expired events and 512 expired daily rows per turn; the event bound is one million per native node, the candidate bound 10,000 per agent, the daily-row bound 100,000, the native-node bound 32 and the database bound 512 MiB, with each refusal reported rather than silently growing storage.
The store uses SQLite WAL and full synchronous commits, refuses a damaged or newer store without rebuilding it, and travels with `MOVES_WITH_CORE` through the existing consistent SQLite copy.
Native physical node identities and their qualified source paths stay unchanged when the core's owner changes.

The same store retains the public price cache and the last network-attempt time; that state moves with the ledger so a core move cannot bypass the daily fetch bound.
Daily totals hold four fixed token-only context bands, not another table of call identities, preserving tier-aware repricing across event expiry within the existing row and database caps.
The separately owned price-fetch thread asks the own node for `Call::UsagePriceFetch`, refused for devices.
The node owns the bounded public HTTP read; the core parses the returned body and the ledger's sole writer persists the table before publishing it.
Shutdown closes the own-node link before joining the ledger and price threads, so those joins do not wait for an in-flight HTTP answer.
Closing also releases the node's label-generator locks, even while unfinished calls retain node references; a lock acquisition completing after close releases its file instead of retaining the role.
The teardown-only `AiRelease` and `LabelUnlock` requests remain callable after close under the existing call cap and timeout, because their owners may drop later; closing does not wake or replace their cleanup answers.
The node's unfinished HTTP work retains its capacity slot and five-second deadline until it ends.

`usage_ledger::ai_usage` is the other writer's input: routers tell its bounded `AiUsageQueue` after each attempt, and the same ledger worker drains it at most once a second into `ai_daily`/`ai_state` of `usage.sqlite3` (`store/ai.rs`).
It reloads those rows and publishes only when a usage page is visible, so a hidden window costs the queue push and the batched write only; `docs/AI_PROVIDERS.md`, Counting Hide AI's own usage, owns what counts and the caps.

`usage_ledger::query` prepares both the 20-week and 53-week calendars on the worker, including every agent selection; the shell formats and draws their prepared values.
Each range sends inclusive `calendar_start` and `calendar_end` civil dates and only days with recorded calls, including calls with zero new tokens.
The mounted calendar fills omitted dates as level-zero cells, pending while that range is reading; it does not recalculate totals, prices, quartiles, streaks or recorded-day contributions.
This keeps empty history from repeating hundreds of empty amount records on every attach or reconnect.
Only the core's own native-node rows enter these calendars.
New tokens include input, output and cache writes, never cache reads; reasoning is already part of output.
Nonzero closed days determine nearest-rank quartiles, or quarter-of-maximum thresholds below seven active closed days, so today's unfinished spike cannot recolor history.
The calendar starts on the first record's week, capped to the surface's week count, and stays anchored 45 days back during first backfill; agent selection preserves the calendar and the other agents' shares.
