Empty days during backfill remain pending, zero-call ranges remain no data, and cost reasons carry at most eight distinct unpriced model names plus the omitted count, together with price-loading, stale-price and incomplete-usage facts.
Agent hover costs cover retained history even when its first days precede the displayed calendar.
Provider provenance is bounded to 32 distinct native ids, and crossing that bound refuses the projection rather than omitting an id silently.
Calendar arithmetic uses civil dates across daylight-saving transitions, and a current streak may include yesterday while today is still empty.

## The Herdr wire boundary

The bundled Herdr release is pinned in one place, `contracts/herdr-bundle.json` (the macOS asset, and the Linux and Windows assets that only CI runs), and `contracts/herdr-api.schema.json` is derived from it: it is what that exact binary answers to `api schema --json`, never a copy from a Herdr checkout.
`herdr-core/build.rs` turns the five sub-schemas into Rust modules at build time; generated source stays in `OUT_DIR` and is never committed.
The modules are included into a private `generated` module of `herdr-core/src/wire.rs`, so that file is the only place that can name a generated type, and an import from `runtime`, `domain`, `sidebar`, `session_sync` or any other crate does not compile.
`wire.rs` converts generated values into the core's projection and event inputs and builds the request parameters, including the `events.subscribe` object; none of its `pub(crate)` signatures carries a generated type.
Another crate that needs a typed answer gets it from the few `pub` functions in the "what `hided`'s phone routes need" block of `wire.rs` (`pane_rows_params`, `pane_rows`, `pane_reply_params`, `pane_key_params`, `pane_session`), which take and return plain values; hided's phone routes are their callers.
`hide-herdr-client` is transport (connectors, framing, timeouts, `ApiError`, the protocol revision) plus the plugin registry entry below, and generates nothing.
The one answer a crate that cannot depend on the core reads is the plugin registry entry (`plugin.list` and Herdr's offline `plugins.json`, read by `hide-kit`), which `hide-herdr-client::plugin` states by hand, and `entry_matches_the_pinned_schema` fails when the pinned schema's required fields or source kinds stop agreeing with it.
Do not write new wire deserialization structs in `session_sync/{projection,replica}.rs`.
One fact about the pinned Herdr that no schema states is asserted the same way: the PTY size is the controller client's, the `--cols`/`--rows` it attached with and the `terminal.resize` lines it sends on the control stream, and a split, a sibling's close or a zoom leaves it where it is (PRD grid-ahead-of-herdr D-02).
`the_pinned_herdr_keeps_the_controllers_pty_size_through_a_zoom_and_a_siblings_close` in `hide-herdr-client/tests/it/real_herdr.rs` runs it against the pinned binary on every system (the `os contract` lane), so a pin bump to a Herdr that sizes the PTY from its layout fails there, and that pull request decides what the drawn-ahead grid does on such a Herdr ([Drawn ahead of Herdr](#drawn-ahead-of-herdr)).
The event envelope is consumed as generated (`event` and `data`); the stream carries no protocol, host or sequence, and the snapshot names no host, so a remote snapshot's identity is the host Hide reached the socket through, stamped by the caller of `wire::remote_snapshot`.
Request envelopes still name their method explicitly because generation does not discriminate method constants; use generated parameter types inside them, built in `wire.rs`.
The envelope `id` is request correlation, never retry identity; mutation convergence must use an operation context on a method whose pinned schema actually carries one, or reconcile the resulting topology before retrying a method that does not.
`live.rs` and `remote.rs` also use this boundary for response decoding and generated request parameters.
The boundary preserves remote protocol diagnostics before decoding the complete generated snapshot, and the isolated pinned-server probe checks the control responses.
Terminal input, scroll, resize and release messages and the parameterless snapshot request remain boundary-owned schema gaps, with tests that require migration when their parameter types appear.
Behavior the schema does not state: the pinned Herdr publishes no `tab_focused` or `pane_focused` event for a `tab.focus` on the tab it already shows, the active tab of its focused workspace (checked on a private server: a move to another tab publishes both, a repeat publishes neither, and a `tab.focus` on another workspace's active tab publishes both with `workspace_focused`; a `pane.focus` in another tab, a tab created with focus and `workspace.focus` each publish `tab_focused`; a burst t1, t2, t1 publishes three in order), so a focus that lands there is confirmed by its answer (the control lane paragraphs above).
Behavior the schema does not state: the pinned Herdr leaves an `agent.start` `launch_pending` in `agent.list` until the agent's state moves again or it is read with `agent.get` (herdrdev/herdr#3853), and a read past the start's deadline makes it clear the agent's name; the session sync works around both by reading only Hide's own starts, inside their deadline, while their agent rests ([The core, the shell and Herdr](#the-core-the-shell-and-herdr), which lists what the workaround deletes).


## Agent adapters

`hide-agent-adapter` is the data-only leaf shared by the hook helper, session readers, kit, core, daemon and device helper.
One static row per supported agent declares its canonical id and aliases, labels, programs, skill location, installation and hook dialects, session format, launch/find/usage selectors, links and logo id.
Prompt intake, spawn refusal, subagent counts, Memory and bell eligibility are independent fields: gaining a prompt hook does not make an agent a bell target or make its session readable.
The common lookup trims borrowed input and compares ids and aliases without case, allocation, I/O, a lock or a cache.
Every start normalizes a known kind to its Herdr name at the shared wire boundary; captured unknown kinds retain their original bytes and gain no capability.
Lifecycle identity checks normalize known kinds while keeping their existing unknown-kind comparisons, and bell identity keeps unknown spellings exact along with pane, name, native session, readiness and sequence checks.
Delegated and handed-off starts use the same known-kind identity for live detection, native readiness, child binding and same-intent replay, preserving captured spellings and every other ownership and receipt check.
Herdr target names are references to the pinned Herdr contract, never a second protocol vocabulary.

The leaf selects dialects but does not implement them.
`hide-agent-hooks` still owns installed hook bytes and command arguments, input/output formats and the only writes to operator hook files.
`hide-session` owns session location and parsing, including whether a format reports user turns; the delivery gate conservatively holds a letter when such a reader cannot establish the current turn.
`hide-kit` projects the shared rows into pieces and owns installation policy, while core feature gates read the relevant independent capability.
OpenCode's letters, Memory context, spawn guard and subagent count come from Hide's plugin ([agent-hooks.md: OpenCode: Hide's plugin](agent-hooks.md#opencode-hides-plugin)), not from its sessions.
Pi's and omp's letters, Memory and spawn guard, and omp's subagent count and `ask` guard, come the same way from Hide's extension ([agent-hooks.md: Pi and omp: Hide's extension](agent-hooks.md#pi-and-omp-hides-extension)).
Pi 1.0.4 and omp 18.7.0 extend the shared file reader through `hide-session::native_file`: the first version3 session header proves the native ID and exact cwd under the respective default `.pi/agent/sessions` or `.omp/agent/sessions` root.
The native default cwd directory is additionally required because Pi's ID lookup outside it prompts to fork instead of resuming the same session; lossy folder encoding still never proves ownership.
Filename suffixes never supply an ID, and links below home and custom session directories confer no read authority.
Native IDs ending in `.jsonl` are refused locally because this CLI treats that argument as a path.
omp's default bucket uses its native temp-first, home-relative and external-path spelling; a nondefault bucket never authorizes a launch.
Before a native-file close, fork, wake or archive resume, the current Identity/Activity-capable node audits its default directory for one safe route to the exact proven ID and source.
omp's selector also matches case-insensitive ID prefixes, filename prefixes and the filename suffix after the last underscore, so any second matching candidate refuses the action.
The label proof retains the resolved source path even for an ID reference; it stays within authenticated reader answers, internal row facts and local durable intent, never the UI snapshot.
Fork, sleeping records and queued archive resumes retain that original path with the expected native ID and checkout, and re-prove it instead of discovering a different same-ID file after a wait.
The action-only audit shares the discovery entry cap and a 1 MiB aggregate header budget; duplicate IDs, linked entries and uninspectable native-resolver candidates refuse the effect.
Queued starts repeat the route proof after shell readiness and before each actual start attempt; fork execution and task/helper identity must still match their admitted intent.
Archive resume also checks the actual current control connector, local connection generation and file-node identity before creating topology and each start attempt; a retained retired connector cannot acquire authority from a replacement reader.
The shared session connector repeats the captured current control, file-node and exact task/fork-intent check after each potentially waiting connection, before archive start or native-file fork tab/start requests can be written.
An already-created failed fork child is still cleaned up through its original connector; that cleanup cannot target a replacement server.
Search, archive detail and Memory reads carry the catalog row's expected native ID and checkout, and refuse a replacement rather than deriving their requested owner from the new file.
Labels, activity, project catalog, content search, archive detail, link sightings and local phone pages use the same before/after native proof; a phone idle poll and a cloned older-page reader cannot retain a replaced owner's history.
Same-length reads compare the physical file stamp, failed native-file label reads revoke cached native identity and turn authority, and link identity comes only from the proven first header even across incremental chunks.
The parser reads raw recorded history, including messages on earlier branches, without reconstructing active model context or following a fork's `parentSession` file.
Pi `session_info.name` has no manual/automatic source and is read as an automatic title; an explicit empty name clears it, while absence on an incremental read preserves the prior name.
omp's physical first title-v1 slot, or its legacy first session header, supplies the current title and auto/user source.
The cursor samples that bounded prefix through its existing file descriptor on each omp poll and charges its actual bytes to the existing read allowance, including a refused prefix.
The label worker rechecks current native-file metadata through its single serialized reader no sooner than three seconds after a successful read, even when Herdr's state is unchanged; a refused or unavailable native read waits fifteen seconds before retrying.
This adds bounded file reads on the existing worker, with no I/O under Runtime or session-sync, no extra provider request for unchanged events, and no publication or store write for unchanged facts.
It applies the current title, including an explicit clear or manual-to-auto change, without rewinding the event checkpoint or replaying question marks; `title_change` entries are audit history, not current title authority.
Only recorded message times and actual tool-result or bash-execution output supply PR sightings; thinking, custom extension context, compaction and branch summaries supply no human request.
Pi has no structured native wait/content or subagent grant, and authenticated protocol24 facts never acquire its new reader.
omp's native `ask` calls carry bounded question text and option labels into the existing turn tracker; only correlated results, a later human turn or a native abort clear that pending question.
Nested child artifacts never enter root discovery or conversation, and no child identity or task completion is inferred from text.
Grok 1.0.46 joins the same native-file proof through `hide-session::grok`, though its session is a folder: `.grok/sessions/<url-encoded cwd>/<id>/`, whose `updates.jsonl` is the append-only conversation and whose `summary.json`, replaced whole by Grok, owns the native ID (`info.id`, which must also name the folder), the cwd (`info.cwd`) and the current title.
The group folder must be the URL encoding of the proven cwd; a cwd whose encoding exceeds 255 bytes, which Grok names by a slug and a hash instead, is refused rather than guessed, and a `GROK_HOME` elsewhere grants nothing.
A subagent child (`session_kind` starting `subagent`), a headless run or a hidden session is no pane's root conversation, so it is refused as an owner and never enters discovery, its title or the root conversation.
`grok --resume <id>` takes the folder named `<id>` in the launch cwd's own group before any other group, so the route proof requires the proven session to be that folder; Herdr's Grok integration reports the native ID, and a fork or wake is routed from the proven `updates.jsonl` the label overlay hands it, as for Pi and omp.
Grok writes one record per prompt block (sharing `promptIndex`) and one per contiguous answer text run (tagged with the turn's `promptId`); readers join a prompt's blocks and a turn's runs into one message, and the phone resets its page when an append continues the message it holds.
Grok writes a pasted image inline and a tool's output twice, so a record longer than the line cap is ordinary: `grok::LargeLine` scans it in bounded memory across polls and keeps only its update kind, ids, prompt index, flags, times and block type, so the record still starts, asks, answers or ends a turn without its body and a checkpoint never carries one.
What such a record loses is its body: an oversized question waits without content, an oversized typed prompt starts its turn with no message, and an oversized answer or tool output supplies no text and no PR sighting, each counted as `body_capacity`.
A record the scan cannot follow (a malformed line, nesting past 64 levels) is skipped as `conversation_capacity` with an unreadable turn mark, as in the Claude and Codex readers (What the last turn waits for), rather than stopping the read.
Within one poll the blocks of a prompt are joined; a poll boundary between them leaves two label events, which the phone joins again when it pages.
Record times come from `agentTimestampMs`, which a fork keeps while rewriting the envelope's whole seconds; a `hideFromScrollback` prompt is Grok's own wake, and only a terminal `tool_call_update`'s printed text supplies PR sightings.
On each Grok poll the cursor also reads `summary.json` (`generated_title`, with `title_is_manual` marking the operator's `/rename`, and absence clearing both) and `plan_mode.json`, charging their bytes to the poll's allowance; neither moves the event checkpoint.
An unanswered `ask_user_question` call (`x.ai/tool` kind `ask_user`) carries its questions and option labels into the turn tracker until its terminal update or a cancelled turn; plan approval is `plan_mode.json` saying `Active` and `awaiting_plan_approval`, the only native record of that wait, with `plan.md`'s text bounded as its content.
A `/rewind` leaves the dropped turns in `updates.jsonl`, and Hide reads them as recorded history like Pi's earlier branches.
Cursor's ordinary local chats use `hide-session::cursor`: `HOME/.cursor/chats/<MD5 of native resolved cwd>/<UUID>/store.db`, with the adjacent schema-v1 `meta.json` owning the cwd and root-session flag.
The directory UUID, database `agentId`, sidecar creation time and cwd bucket must agree before canonical checkout comparison; custom configuration roots, ACP stores, subagent stores and derived JSONL transcripts grant no reader or exact resume route.
Before Cursor Sleep, Wake or archive Resume, the owning node refuses a non-default effective `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` or `XDG_CONFIG_HOME/cursor` from its own startup environment, and refuses a UUID present in more than one distinct known root.
This action-only metadata audit shares the 64-directory, 10,000-entry and 8 MiB path budget, opens no alternate-root database, and records a stable refusal reason without environment values.
It does not certify the later pane shell's environment: an unknown or later export can make native resume fail or select a different conversation carrying the same UUID; default-only proof and argv do not exclude that residual risk.
The node owns the stable account HOME; reads fence its native descendant directories, no-follow single-link files, retained database descriptor and still-current cwd identity before and after publication.
SQLite opens read-only under a transaction with bounded length, SQL and VM work; WAL requires an already initialized read-only shared-memory companion, and recovery journals refuse the read without repairing native files.
Only the native content-addressed protobuf root's ordered agent turns enter conversation, decoded by Prost after their SHA-256 references have been verified; tools, thoughts, shell turns, root prompt scaffolding and child maps supply no conversation or PR sightings.
An incremental read admits at most 1 MiB of native payload, 512 blob queries and 200 events; metadata is at most 64 KiB, a blob 256 KiB, a graph 10,000 turns and a turn 256 steps, and each database, WAL or SHM file is at most 64 MiB.
The graph checkpoint binds native owner and file incarnation, closed-turn reference hash, current user reference and consumed-step prefix hash; appended last-turn steps continue, while a changed prior prefix or replacement resets the view.
Native message ordinals encode turn and unit, never SQLite byte offsets, and the same unit decoder serves labels, search, archive pages and the phone's bounded reverse pages.
The archive requests authenticated `session_conversation` pages with mandatory native ID and checkout scope, capped at 256 pages, 10,000 events and 64 MiB total read and text; raw `session_text` refuses Cursor SQLite.
Cursor advertises identity, labels, conversation, search and activity only; native titles, Memory extraction, links, structured waits/content and fork remain unavailable.
Its generated goal remains title authority, optional recorded message times are rounded down to minutes, and missing times stay unknown rather than adopting file modification time.
The adapters enable conversation, title, exact resume/fork and the common durable distinct sleeping-session lifecycle; omp uses `--resume <id>` and `--fork <id>`, Grok `--resume <id>` and `--resume <id> --fork-session`, and Cursor enables exact `--resume <id>` and Sleep without title or fork.
Authenticated reader facts distinguish an intermediate Pi-only protocol25 helper from one implementing omp, Grok or Cursor; matching protocol numbers alone grant nothing, and protocol24 never gains these readers.
OpenCode 1.18.30 and 2.0.29 are read from their own database through `hide-session::opencode`, with no session file: every read opens it read-only and proves, inside the read's one transaction, that the id names a root session (no `parent_id`) whose recorded `directory` is the exact checkout.
All OpenCode reads, including link discovery and usage, share that guarded connection.
OpenCode 1.x keeps a session in `session`, `message` and `part`; 2.x, a rewrite, keeps it in `session_v2` and `session_message`, a message's whole content in its one row, and a database 2.x migrated from a 1.x one holds both sets of tables.
OpenCode takes its data folder from `XDG_DATA_HOME` when that is set (its `xdg-basedir`, in 1.x and 2.x; proven live on 2.0.29), so every Hide read of its database follows the node's own `XDG_DATA_HOME` the way it follows `CODEX_HOME`, captured and validated once at startup by `hide-session::environment` and named by one function, `opencode::database_path`.
A read is confined to that folder: `$XDG_DATA_HOME/opencode` when the variable is absolute, free of parent components and not blank, otherwise `~/.local/share/opencode`.
An unusable value (relative, a parent component, a control character) refuses every OpenCode read as `opencode_data_home_invalid` (`usage_read_Environment` for usage), never a fall back to the home folder, and the value is never in the message.
Every read names the schema its database holds once, inside its transaction, from the tables and columns Hide reads (`Schema::detect` in `hide-session/src/opencode/schema.rs`), and runs that schema's queries; when both are there the 2.x tables are read, because 2.x holds the migrated sessions and keeps writing only them.
A database that holds neither schema fails at that point as `opencode_db_schema_unknown`, never as an empty one: the catalog lists it as a refused store, usage as `usage_read_Schema`, a node answers it as unsupported, and a label, conversation, activity or sleep proof read carries the reason.
A 2.x conversation lives in `user`, `synthetic` and `assistant` messages in `seq` order; the other kinds (agent, model and location switches, `system`, `skill`, `shell`, `compaction`, `idle`) are counted in the message index and passed unread, and the queued prompts of `session_pending` and `session_inbox` are not messages yet.
A person's message can carry a screenshot inline, so no 2.x row is loaded whole: SQLite's JSON functions scan it in place and hand back only the person's text, the number of image files, or an assistant's text and tool items, each within the 1.x limits (a text or item over 256 KiB is `part_capacity`, 256 items a message and 512 a read as in 1.x).
A row over 8 MiB is not scanned (`message_capacity`), one read scans at most 8 MiB of rows (the first row of a read is always taken), and the newest message of a session, when it is an assistant's that is too large to show it finished, is waited for until another message follows it.
Reads of the same canonical database path are serialized from admission through handle destruction, so closing a validation handle cannot release a peer SQLite connection's process locks; contention is refused as busy, and the gate registry retains at most 64 live paths.
Its private SQLite VFS allows only existing main and WAL files opened read-only, refuses deletion and temporary or journal files, and keeps shared memory read-only; SQLite cannot create a missing WAL even if the provider removes it during admission.
A nonempty WAL requires its existing shared-memory index and normal locking; a checkpointed WAL-mode main with absent or empty WAL uses an immutable read, with mmap disabled, while a rollback-mode database retains its normal locks.
The reader holds the original source handles and records identities and change stamps for the main, companions and bounded ancestor chain; it closes the transaction and connection, validates every handle and name again, and only then returns the buffered result.
A journal or concurrent source change refuses the read without repairing, copying or writing the provider's files.
An OpenCode transaction or changing companion returns a typed pending read, which retains established session proof but clears current-turn authority.
The label worker rereads a pending answer, or a refusal whose session file is not there yet (`session_file_missing`), after 15, 30, 60 and 120 seconds (`READ_RETRY_BACKOFF`); the fifth failure logs `read.retry_capped` and waits for a real state/reference change, while a state change, a new reference or a successful read resets the allowance.
The file is missing for a pane Herdr lists before its agent has written it (Claude writes it with the first message), so a child another agent started gets its label from the retry rather than from its first finished turn; a refusal of any other reason is not retried, and a native-file agent keeps its own fifteen-second retry.
Definitive invalid-source refusals still revoke proof, and a changed requested owner never inherits it.
OpenCode is referenced by id only; a path reference is refused feature-locally, and no path is ever turned into an id.
A subagent's child session is never cataloged, read into the root conversation or titled as it; the link graph alone reads it, as its own line under its parent (`Owner::Recorded`).
`SessionFormat::requires_native_proof` (every native-file reader, and OpenCode) is the one predicate the core's lifecycle gates read: launch confirmation, durable sleep and wake, the proven-id sidebar rule, the fork's route proof and current-execution check (for either placement), task resume scope and resume arguments; `proof_reference_kind` says whether that proof names a path (Pi, omp, Grok, Cursor) or an id (OpenCode).
Resume is `opencode -s <id>`; an id that begins with `-` is refused before it reaches an argument list.
A fork is decided on the session's node by the schema of the database holding it, in the read that proves the session (`hide_session::opencode::fork_route`, NodeLink `opencode_fork`): a session in 1.x's tables is forked by the pane itself, `opencode -s <id> --fork` (1.18.30, `cli/cmd/tui.ts`), and one in 2.x's tables through OpenCode's server, because 2.x's interactive command has no `--fork` (2.0.29, `packages/cli/src/commands/commands.ts`: the root command takes `--session`, `--continue` and `--prompt`, and only `mini` and `run` take `--fork`); given `--fork`, 2.0.29's interactive command prints its help and exits at once, so the fork's pane held only its shell and Herdr never saw an agent there.
For 2.x the node runs `opencode api POST /api/session/<id>/fork` (the server's `session.fork`) on the account's `PATH`, bounded at 20 s, and the pane opens the session it answers with `opencode -s <new id>`, so the fork is made once, before its pane exists, and every later step of that fork, a retried start included, carries the same id; a refusal, a timeout or an answer that names no valid id ends the fork with that reason before a pane is made.
A fork whose tab or start then fails (the parent gone, the tab refused, the agent not started) leaves the session OpenCode made in OpenCode's own list, as OpenCode's own fork would; nothing deletes it, and the fork's reason and an `opencode_fork_left` diagnostic name it for the operator to find.
A run of `opencode api` that ends without an id the node could read (a timeout, a stop, an answer it cannot parse), or a node whose answer never reached the core, says a fork may exist in OpenCode's list.
OpenCode's activity is its newest write (the session row or any of its messages) and its message count, the same answer whose change wakes a sleeping OpenCode row's route proof.
The project catalog lists root sessions by their own row's directory under the shared entry cap, with the locator `opencode/<id>`; search reads and stamps branch on that locator inside the existing calls, and a stamp is the session's creation, message count and newest write.
OpenCode's database failing never takes the other agents' history with it: a database Hide cannot open or list contributes no rows, a session whose proof cannot be read is listed unavailable with its reason, and each session's proof has its own SQL work cap.
A database that is there but cannot be listed rides the node's session answer as a row of its own (`{"agent", "store_refused"}`), which the core logs as `sessions.store_unreadable`; a missing database is no OpenCode sessions and logs nothing, and a core that does not know the row counts it as one refused row.
Memory's analysis pass reads OpenCode through the same guarded reads, so a database it cannot list gives it no OpenCode rows and a session whose proof fails is listed unavailable; neither fails nor pauses the pass.
Archive detail reads OpenCode through bounded label-transcript reads under the Conversation fact instead of `SessionText`; a session rewound or replaced between those reads refuses the detail.
A message's offset is its index, and a checkpoint keeps the creation time and a digest of the last message read, so a session an OpenCode revert rewound and grew back is read again rather than continued.
An OpenCode `question` tool part supplies the same structured question as Claude's: running asks, completed or error answers, pending (its input still streaming) asks nothing yet, and an unknown state refuses the read; in 2.x the part is a content item named `question`, whose streaming state is 1.x's pending.
More question parts on the unfinished message than the question cap refuse the read rather than miss the one it waits on; a part over the row limit is never parsed to learn whether it is a question and is skipped as `part_capacity`, as a finished message's is.
Question parts that fit the row limit but not what is left of the read's budget leave the unfinished message to the next read, which starts at it.
OpenCode keeps the asking assistant message unfinished while it waits, so that unfinished tail is folded without settling it (`TurnTracker::fold_unsettled`), and its completion folds again and clears the wait.
The phone pages OpenCode by message index with the same page bounds, through the same proven reads.
OpenCode's Memory reader is the same read shaped as the provider-neutral page (`hide_session::opencode::read_conversation`, answered for `session_conversation` by the scope's id): an event's offset is its message's index, a receipt in Hide's synthetic part (a `synthetic` message in 2.x) is provider-injected context at that index, where the label read reports it, so either reader records it under the same turn, and a person's message is classified as the file readers classify one (injected context and an interruption are not the operator's words).
Hide AI retains its separate provider registry; its adapter id only associates a provider with an agent identity and logo.

The web shell reads `contracts/agent-adapters.json`, generated by the leaf's `export_web_contract` example, for support order, aliases, names, Docs/Install links, logo ids and start/resume eligibility.
Its normalized lookup drives marks and canonical resume requests; desktop and phone start choices share that projection.
The leaf contract test compares that file with the current projection, checks independent hook/session fixtures, links and logos, and rejects a bell target with no prompt hook.
Pre-migration byte fixtures pin Claude Code and Codex commands and complete installed files, separately from feature-gate and isolated pinned-Herdr Settings tests.

Each row explicitly declares six Factory capabilities as available, unavailable or unconfirmed: direct questions and their refusal dialect, user turns, turn end and final answer, startup guidance, resume and next-prompt letters.
These declarations activate no new guard, transcript reader or Factory policy.
`hide-session::turns::UserTurnFact` carries a turn kind and optional bounded structured content when Claude Code, Codex, Grok or omp native records supply it.
Missing content remains absent; a failed or incomplete read certifies no structured fact for the current Herdr state.
The content constructor bounds text to 8 KiB, choices to eight and each choice to 256 bytes before copying, preserves UTF-8 boundaries and marks any truncation.

A device report with an unknown adapter id contributes no Settings row rather than a row with an empty feature table.
The core logs the id and stable device id once per exact pair in a runtime, keeping at most 128 pairs whose fields are at most 512 bytes each, without eviction or relogging.
Exhausting that resource or receiving an oversized identity is a reported failure domain: the row stays omitted, and one limit diagnostic identifies the first triggering device and agent with UTF-8-safe prefixes of at most 128 bytes, original byte lengths and truncation flags.
An identity outside those bounds is never retained or individually logged, and the limit diagnostic is emitted only once.
After an oversized identity is rejected, later admissible pairs remain eligible while capacity remains; once-per-pair identity logging is guaranteed only inside the admitted domain.
This adds bounded work when a kit report arrives, no new polling, notifier fan-out, process or persistent file.
The adapter lookup on projection and hook paths performs only a scan of the fixed static rows and their aliases.

## The platform layer

`hide-platform` is the one place code that depends on the operating system is written, so that macOS, Linux and Windows differ in one small crate rather than in every crate that touches a socket, a process or a file.
It is a leaf: no hide dependencies, no state beyond what an open endpoint holds (a Windows listener's accept thread and its backlog) and the one cache `programs` keeps (below), nothing under the runtime mutex, and no policy; a caller decides what a save or a request means, and the crate only answers the operating-system question under it.
Each function answers the same way on all three systems or says `ErrorKind::Unsupported`; none guesses a default.
`hide-platform/tests/` states each module's contract as what a caller observes (bytes both ways, a timeout that fires after the time and not before, a shutdown that frees a blocked read, a live bind refused and a dead one replaced, an endpoint only the account can reach) and runs unchanged on every system in the `os contract` lane (`.github/workflows/os-contract.yml`, with its macOS leg planned for a change to `hide-platform` or `hide-herdr-client` and run by the nightly).
The crate is small on purpose, so that lane builds in minutes on a runner with no workspace.
The install policy stays in `hide-kit`: on Windows its ASCII `hide.cmd` forwards arguments and the exit code through `%~dp0.hide-kit\hide.exe`, while `fs::link` makes the sibling directory junction into the package's resources without symlink privilege.
The junction handles Unicode package paths without relying on cmd's code page; the kit replaces only its exact shim and owned junction, and leaves competing commands and foreign entries with a reported failure.
The kit record keeps its last installed CLI destination, so a removed old package can be replaced without treating every `resources/hide[.exe]` path as owned.
PATH and registry edits remain the operator's decision.
Windows is a host for the app (hided, a local Herdr, the desktop window), and a Windows machine as a remote device is the goal after that: the device helper, the host probe that replaces `uname`, and the kit and hook install on it are later slices, and this crate is shaped so they need no rework (every function takes a path or an open handle and keeps no state of its own, except the login shell's `PATH` that `programs` remembers, and answers for the machine it runs on; what it reads from the process is the account it runs as, which `private` and `lock` compare against, and the variables `host` lists in `host::VARIABLES`, each of which a caller with its own registry can hand in through a `_from` form instead).

`ipc` is the first module: a Unix domain socket on macOS and Linux and a named pipe on Windows behind `LocalStream` and `LocalListener`, built on `interprocess`, the crate Herdr itself uses.
Its read-only `is_endpoint` tests the entry itself without following a link, using the same platform-specific socket or marker predicate as stale-endpoint removal; callers need no operating-system branch to distinguish an absent endpoint from an unsafe entry.
The address is a path everywhere, because that is how Herdr names its socket; on Windows the path is also the pipe's name (`\\.\pipe\<path>`, as Herdr's client reaches its server) and a marker file stays at the path while the listener lives, so "the path exists" means the same on every system.
A Windows pipe has no read or write timeout, so a read with a timeout looks at the pipe before it reads, and the Unix side waits for readiness with `poll` instead of `SO_RCVTIMEO`, which macOS refuses to set once the peer has closed even though its bytes are still waiting; the contract tests pin both.
A Windows pipe has no write timeout at all, and says `Unsupported` where a Unix socket keeps one; `hide-herdr-client` treats that one answer as no timeout, because a request is one line that fits the pipe's buffer and never blocks.
A write a peer can stall is bounded the same way on every system: it runs on a thread of its own, its caller waits for it only until a deadline, and a `ShutdownHandle` then ends the held write (the contract tests pin that a shutdown frees a blocked write); a node's link streams (`hide-host/src/link_bridge.rs`) give each chunk 5 s and then end the stream, so an end that stops reading never holds a worker of the link's control lane.
A Windows connect waits at most two seconds for a pipe whose instances are all busy (`TimedOut`; the default would wait for ever), and `bind` reads that answer as a live listener; `bind` also removes only a socket (Unix) or the marker file (Windows) and refuses anything else at the path.
A refused connect cannot say whether a listener lives, because macOS answers `ConnectionRefused` for a dead socket and for a live listener whose 128-connection queue is full alike (#403), so `bind` decides by a lock and uses the connect only as a fallback.
A listener holds an exclusive lock on `<path>.lock` (`fs::lock`) from before it binds until it drops, and `bind` takes that lock first: a held lock is `AddrInUse`, a free one means no listener of this build lives, and only then is a leftover socket or marker removed.
The lock file is opened as the account's own private regular file (`private::open_own_file`): a link at its name, a file of another account or one with a second name is refused with the file's name in the error, never followed or locked.
The kernel ends the lock with its holder however the holder ends, so a killed listener never blocks the next bind, and the drop removes the lock file while still holding the lock, but only while the file at the path is still the one it locked, so a lock file a cleaner replaced is left to whoever holds the new one; `bind` checks the same identity after it locks, so a file opened just before a removal is opened again, and any answer but "not there" is a failure of the bind.
The connect that follows the lock is a transition path for a listener of a build that took no lock, to be removed once no supported build binds without it; `a_listener_with_a_full_backlog_is_still_live_to_a_second_bind` pins the full-queue case.
A client that connects and leaves never holds up the next one (#315): a Unix socket queues it until `accept`, while a pipe instance stays taken by a client that came and went until the server clears it, and `interprocess`'s listener keeps one instance and clears it only inside `accept`, so the 33rd of 50 quick connects timed out.
The Windows listener therefore keeps four instances listening with an overlapped `ConnectNamedPipe` each, served by a thread of its own that clears an instance its client left, puts a fresh instance in the place of a connected one before queueing it, and queues at most 64 connections for `accept`, like a socket's backlog; the contract tests connect and leave 40 times with nobody accepting and 200 times against a running accept loop, never more than 32 ahead of it.
Past the backlog the systems differ, and the contract names each answer rather than one: macOS refuses a connect at once with `ConnectionRefused` once 128 (`SOMAXCONN`) wait for `accept`, Windows says `TimedOut` after its two-second wait, and Linux's connect waits for room; the listener serves on once `accept` catches up.
A burst that outruns a starved accept thread is therefore a load past the contract, not a lost listener (#396: a macOS runner refused the 129th connect while the accept loop was still alive), and `a_connect_past_the_backlog_is_refused_and_the_listener_serves_on` pins the overflow answer apart from the burst.
A Windows end is the crate's own `Pipe` (overlapped `ReadFile` and `WriteFile` on the handle it was given) rather than `interprocess`'s stream, because `interprocess` wraps a handle only by reopening it, and a reopen relative to a pipe handle is a new client connect: with one instance listening it fails and the original is kept, with several it takes another instance and the original end closes, so the peer's next write fails with "the pipe is being closed" (the contract test `one_connect_is_one_accepted_connection` pins it).
Closing a pipe end throws away what it wrote and the peer has not read yet, so an end that wrote is flushed on a thread of its own before its handle closes, as `interprocess` does; the pane bootstrap writes its answer and drops the stream at once (`an_answer_written_just_before_the_end_is_dropped_arrives_whole`).
A blocked read is freed by `ShutdownHandle` from any thread on every system: `shutdown(2)` on Unix, `CancelIoEx` on Windows, and a blocked `accept` by `ListenerCloser`: a wake socket polled beside the listener on Unix, an event the accept thread waits on with its instances on Windows.
`hide-herdr-client`'s `LocalSocketConnector` and its `ApiStream` for `LocalStream`, the test fake Herdr and session tests in `herdr-core`, the `hide` CLI's connect to the pane bootstrap and the bootstrap's own listener in `hide-node/src/pane_proof.rs` use it, and so do a device node's pane service (`hide-host/src/panes.rs`) and the device `hide`'s connect to it; the opener supervisor's channel is a Unix socket pair because only Unix needs that supervisor (Windows owns the helper through its job object).
The bootstrap accepts on a thread of its own and hands each connection to the async runtime, because `accept` blocks, and closes the listener through its `ListenerCloser` on shutdown.

`process` is the second module and the one place a child is started or a pid is asked about.
`OwnedChild::spawn` starts a child that leads its own group (Unix) or lives in its own job object (Windows, started suspended so it cannot start a process before it is inside the job); `kill_tree` and the owner's `Drop` end the child and everything it started, and the Windows job also ends the tree when the owner dies without a destructor.
No Unix system offers that for a group, so an owner that must outlive its own crash keeps a channel the child watches (the codex app-server's stdin is one; the opener supervisor's socket is another), which is why `OwnedChild` promises the drop and not the crash.
`detach` starts a daemon that no signal to its parent's group reaches and that holds none of the parent's standard streams, nor any descriptor or handle a supervisor lent the parent beyond them (a debugging pipe, a readiness pipe): the child marks every descriptor above the standard three close-on-exec on Unix and the parent clears the inherit flag of its other handles on Windows, because a daemon that kept a lent pipe would keep a supervisor waiting for it as long as the daemon lives (`a_detached_child_holds_none_of_its_parents_extra_descriptors`).
The opt-in `OwnedChild::spawn_guarded` uses that same ownership helper and waits within an absolute deadline for a cooperative Unix child's `OwnerWatch::from_launch`, which retains an independent socket until all work finishes and ends its still-owned tree on owner loss, and whose reader polls that socket beside a wake channel, so closing the watch wakes the reader by data and end of stream rather than by a socket shutdown or a receive timeout (#446); the runtime descriptor is restored close-on-exec before acknowledgement, and downstream starts remove its metadata.
It admits one watch per process, preserves stdin for the caller's payload, and uses the existing kill-on-close job on Windows; detached daemons and arbitrary uncooperative Unix executables retain the legacy launch contract.
On Windows guarded launch names its job and the child checks both its membership in that exact job and the kill-on-close limit before returning startup proof; its query handle closes before return, leaving the parent the sole persistent job-handle owner, and missing metadata reports a standalone invocation.
`LOGIN_CHILD_VARIABLES` and `restrict_to_login_environment` are the one rule for a child that must find the account's login but must not inherit the rest of the environment (the weekly `/usage` read): `HOME`, `PATH`, `USER`, `LOGNAME` and `TMPDIR` on Unix, and `PATH`, `PATHEXT`, `SystemRoot`, `USERPROFILE`, `USERNAME`, `TEMP`, `TMP`, `APPDATA`, `LOCALAPPDATA`, `ComSpec`, `windir`, `SystemDrive`, `ProgramFiles`, `ProgramFiles(x86)`, `ProgramData`, `HOMEDRIVE`, `HOMEPATH` and `CLAUDE_CODE_GIT_BASH_PATH` on Windows, where Node takes its home from `USERPROFILE` and its start-up needs `SystemRoot`; a caller such as `hide-ai` names no variable itself, and a hook or nested-session marker stays out by not being listed.
`capture_until` drains only currently available stdout/stderr bytes without reader threads or joins, under the same absolute deadline and a caller-selected combined byte limit capped at 1 MiB, and ends at the child's exit by the same rule as `run_to_end`.
Failure retains bounded partial output and reports cleanup uncertainty separately; a failed guarded launch retains its unconfirmed child in `GuardedSpawnError` for explicit recovery, and no timeout asserts that an unobserved exit occurred.
Kernel calls have no universal cancellation guarantee, and Unix ancestry cannot identify a helper that has both escaped its group and been reparented before cleanup observes it.
`run_to_end` observes exit without reaping its leader (a stopped leader has not exited, though macOS's `waitid` answers for it with `WEXITED` alone, so only an exit, a kill or a core dump counts), ends the owned tree while its identity is retained, waits until nothing of the tree is left running, then reads both pipes until they are empty and answers; it does not wait for every write end to close.
A write end can be held outside the tree for as long as its holder lives: a helper that left the group and was reparented before the walk, or a child another thread of the same process started while the run's pipes were being made.
The second is a macOS fact: Rust's standard library makes a pipe there with `pipe` and then marks each end close-on-exec, where Linux has `pipe2(O_CLOEXEC)`, and a child `posix_spawn` starts in between inherits both ends (#729: a hide-kit test's long-lived lock holder kept a `launchctl print` run's pipes open past its 5 s deadline, so a login agent that was never loaded failed retirement).
Windows has neither: the standard library makes a child's inheritable pipe ends and calls `CreateProcess` under one process-wide lock, and a job has no breakaway, so there the pipes reach their end with the tree; the rule is the same code on all three.
Nothing of the tree is left running when every member of the leader's group is ended by `is_alive`'s answer on Unix, and when the job counts no active process on Windows; signal 0 to the group cannot say it, because macOS refuses it with `EPERM` both when only zombies are left and while a killed member is still exiting, and a member that left its group is signalled but not awaited.
It reads only available bytes, keeps at most 64 KiB from each stream, and observes the original deadline and stop flag through draining without reader threads or blocking joins.
After ending the tree, the end is confirmed by the leader's observed exit (on Windows a wait on its process handle), because Windows ends a job's processes after `TerminateJobObject` returns and a busy machine can take longer than one poll interval; a stopped run is confirmed the same way, since a process the kill reached is seen ending at once, so only a pathological end delays a quitting owner. A leader still running 10 s after the kill counts as one the kernel could not end and is logged as `process.end_unconfirmed`; that unconfirmed cleanup returns `RunCleanupFailure` inside `RunFailure::Wait`, retaining the original outcome and child owner for recovery without a blocking destructor.
An escaped helper reparented before the process walk cannot be attributed by Unix ancestry, so it is not ended, and its open pipe no longer holds the answer; a kernel call has no universal cancellation guarantee.
`kill_tree(pid)`, `terminate`, `is_alive`, `parent_of`, `descends_from`, `start_time`, `cwd_of` and `measure_tree` answer for a pid from the kernel's tables (libproc on macOS, `/proc` on Linux, Toolhelp and job accounting on Windows) and fork nothing, which is what lets the git path and the process cap use them; a start time is an opaque number that only tells a pid from a later process that reused it.
`is_alive` asks whether the process has not yet ended, and every system answers it the same way: a Unix zombie still answers signal 0 until its parent, or init once it is orphaned, reaps it, so the answer also reads the process state (`Z` in `/proc/<pid>/stat`, `SZOMB` from the `KERN_PROC_PID` sysctl, because libproc has no answer for a zombie), as Windows reads the exit code of a process a handle still keeps open.
Before that, a helper `run_to_end` had ended still counted as alive on Linux until init reaped it (#595).
Windows has no process group to signal, no polite `SIGTERM` and no readable working directory, so `terminate_group` and `cwd_of` say `Unsupported` there and `terminate` ends the process at once.
`image_of(pid)` names the program file a process runs: where that file is now, and the identity (`FileId`) of the file the process was started from, which stays its answer when the app around it is renamed aside or a new file is put at its path.
macOS reads the identity from the process's own mapping (the lowest file-backed region whose file the kernel names at the program's path, `PROC_PIDREGIONPATHINFO`, whose structures libc does not declare and `process.rs` states with their SDK sizes), because the path alone still names the old program after a new file took its place; Linux follows `/proc/<pid>/exe`, which reaches the running file even once it is deleted.
Windows names a process's image by the path it started from and keeps no identity of the file that follows a rename, so `image_of` says `Unsupported` there.
The one start helper, the pane bootstrap's process identity (`hide-host/src/pane_peer.rs`, the single copy `hided` now reuses) and the AI process cap's measurement (`hide-ai/src/process.rs`, which only maps the answer) are the callers of record; a raw `Command::spawn` that outlives its call is a review rejection (practice `process.md`, rule 1), except the ones that are deliberate: `hided`'s detached daemon (`spawn_owned`), the default-app handoff and, on Unix, the opener helper's own CLI (`hide-node/src/opener.rs`, the node's, which hided's node role runs), whose owner is a socket the supervisor watches (on Windows that helper is an `OwnedChild`), the Tailscale probe that tokio kills on drop, and the Herdr server a core's login item starts when Herdr says none runs (`ensure_herdr` in `hided/src/login_item.rs`), which is the machine's server as one the operator starts is, owned by Herdr and left running so a core that restarts finds it; no grep gate enforces it yet.
Dropping an `OwnedChild` after the child was waited for names nothing on Unix, because the child's pid may be another process's by then, so a caller that wants a finished child's leftovers ended calls `kill_tree` at the moment it exits (the kit and the codex shutdown do); a Windows job ends them when it closes.
A Windows job has no breakaway, so a helper an owned child daemonizes dies with the job; the daemons the app starts on purpose (`hided`) start through `detach`, which on Windows also makes this process's standard handles uninheritable first, since Windows hands a child every inheritable handle and a daemon holding the CLI's output pipe would keep `hide connect`'s caller waiting for the end of that output as long as the daemon lives, and a Windows start that must outlive its owner is the next slice's to settle with the port.
`set_output_aside` is the one place a process redirects its own standard streams: a login agent's output is a file launchd opened once, at the job's start, so renaming it there leaves the process writing on under the new name, and a core that runs for weeks grew it without bound.
Asked at the start of `hided core-login` and then every minute (`hided/src/login_item.rs`), it renames the file to `core.log.1` once it passes 1 MiB, replacing the one before, and moves standard error, and standard output when it went to the same file, onto a new private file at the old name with `dup2`; output that is not that file is left alone, and Windows, which starts no login agent with a file for its output, answers `Unsupported` (`output_appended_to_a_file_is_set_aside_past_its_limit_and_goes_on_in_a_new_one`).
A child started before a set-aside and given the core's standard error keeps writing to the file set aside; the Herdr server the login item starts is given none.
`fs` is the third module and the one place a file is made private, locked, replaced, linked or identified.
It takes a path where a caller holds a name and `Handle` (`AsFd` on Unix, `AsHandle` on Windows) where it holds an open folder or file, so `hide-host` passes its `cap-std` handles straight through and nothing in the crate depends on `cap-std`.
`private` makes a folder or file only the current account can use (0700 and 0600 on Unix; a protected access list naming the current account alone on Windows, set at creation so there is no wider moment) and answers `is_private`, `others_can_modify` and `owned_by_current_user` for the checks that decide whether a folder may hold code the account runs; `Access` names the three cases a writer needs (private, private and runnable, keep what the replaced file had).
Every folder Hide makes under `~/.hide` and in its state folder is made private (`private::create_dir`, `create_dir_all` or `own_dir`), never with `std::fs::create_dir_all`, whose folders take the umask's mode: a device helper's bridge folder once made `~/.hide/state` 0755 that way, and a core moved onto that machine later refused its delivery ledger, whose parent must be private.
`private::own_dir` makes a folder this account's private one whatever an earlier program left there: it makes a missing one and every missing folder above it private, restricts one the account owns that others can reach, and refuses a link, a file or another account's folder without changing it; it opens the entry itself (`O_NOFOLLOW | O_DIRECTORY`, or a reparse point opened as itself on Windows) and judges its owner and access and sets its mode or access list through that handle (`fchmod`, `SetSecurityInfo`), so a link swapped in after the look cannot redirect the change; the daemon's state folder is opened through it ([hided and the WebSocket boundary](#hided-and-the-websocket-boundary)).
It judges the last name only: a link among the folders above, such as a `~/.hide` that leads elsewhere, is accepted and followed, as the delivery ledger's start follows it when `establish_dir_durability` resolves the chain from the filesystem root, because the folders above are the caller's to trust and an operator may keep `~/.hide` on another volume.
`lock` is an advisory lock on a file or a folder that ends with the process: `flock` on Unix and `LockFileEx` on Windows through the standard library, with a wait that gives up after a time or when the caller cancels.
Dropping a lock releases it before closing its descriptor: under `flock` the lock belongs to the open file, and a child another thread starts in that moment keeps a copy of the descriptor until it starts its program, so a lock left to the close stayed held and a one-try lock elsewhere read it as busy (#820); `fs::a_dropped_lock_is_free_while_a_child_started_meanwhile_still_holds_its_descriptor` holds such a child in that moment.
`cap-std` opens its folders with `O_PATH` on Linux and the kernel answers `EBADF` to `flock` and `fsync` on such a descriptor, which refused every save on Linux once (#285), so a folder is always locked and flushed through a fresh readable descriptor of the same folder; `hide-host/tests/it/save.rs` and the contract test that locks through an `O_PATH` handle are the proof.
Windows refuses a byte-range lock on a folder (`ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED`) and a lock file inside a checkout would put a file in someone's work, so a folder is locked through one of 65536 empty files in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\hide\locks`, picked by the folder's identity: two folders that pick the same file only wait for each other, the count cannot grow, and the folder is trusted only when the account owns it and nobody else can change it.
That lock is one account's, so two accounts working in one folder do not exclude each other on Windows as they do under `flock`.
`atomic` replaces a file whole or not at all (`write_file`, `replace_file`), exchanges two names (`exchange`: `RENAME_SWAP` on macOS, `RENAME_EXCHANGE` on Linux; on Windows a `ReplaceFileW` that parks the old file under a `.hide-swap-` name and a rename, so the replaced name never shows a partial file, but it names nothing for the moment `ReplaceFileW` moves the old file aside and the other name is empty until the rename, which `EXCHANGE_IS_ATOMIC` says) and renames without replacing (`rename_no_replace`: `RENAME_EXCL`, `RENAME_NOREPLACE`, `MoveFileExW`).
The legacy writers provide whole-file visibility with best-effort parent sync, which on Windows never reaches a folder flush, since they open the folder without the backup semantics a folder handle needs.
The opt-in `write_file_durable` shares the bounded temporary pipeline and checks file sync, a same-folder rename (`MoveFileExW(REPLACE_EXISTING | WRITE_THROUGH)` on Windows, the form the `atomicwrites` crate uses) and a fallible open and flush of the parent folder on every system.
Its file is always the account's alone (`DurableAccess`: private, or a private program): it never keeps the access of the file it replaces, because Windows keeps an access list only through `ReplaceFileW`, which has no write-through form.
Its errors distinguish `BeforeReplace`, `ReplacementUncertain` and `ReplacedNotDurable`; the last retains the installed `FileId`, all retain the original I/O cause, and failed owned-temp cleanup reports its own path and cause separately.
Neither uncertain outcome restores old bytes or deletes the destination: the caller must suspend assumptions about the old version and validate recovery before using it again.
The parent and its ancestry must already have an established durability boundary; this writer does not create or sync ancestors, `private::create_dir_all` still provides no parent-chain barrier, and OS acknowledgement promises neither universal power-loss protection nor a kernel-call cancellation deadline.
A folder's barrier is `atomic::sync_dir`: `fsync` on Unix, and on Windows `FlushFileBuffers` on the same folder opened again with the right to add an entry (`NtCreateFile` of an empty name relative to the folder's handle, since `ReOpenFile` refuses every folder, and with no backup intent, so a privilege never grants what the access list does not), since `NtFlushBuffersFileEx` refuses a handle with neither write nor append access and its normal flush, unlike the data-only ones, takes a folder and writes its data and metadata through the storage cache; etcd's `fileutil.OpenDir` flushes a Windows folder the same way.
PostgreSQL and SQLite skip a folder's sync on Windows instead, which this layer does not do: a folder that cannot be flushed is an error, never a quiet success.
The opt-in `private::create_dir_all_durable` establishes a descendant chain from an explicitly supplied already-durable trusted ancestor, preflighting at most 64 normal components and syncing each existing or newly created child and its containing parent; every one of them is the account's own, so a refused flush there fails on Windows too.
The opt-in `private::establish_dir_durability` first establishes an existing account-owned trusted directory's canonical ancestor chain from the filesystem root, with at most 64 components, checking its identity and each fallible directory barrier without creating or removing entries; its returned canonical path can anchor sibling state and home directories without treating existence as durability.
That operation requires a caller-trusted stable ancestor namespace, permits shared system ancestors and resolves their existing aliases, and does not infer privacy or protection from another account for those ancestors.
It refuses links and directories another account can modify, leaves existing permissions intact, and reports the last completed and uncertain component with the original I/O cause while retaining the partial chain for explicit recovery.
On Windows an ancestor the account neither owns nor may add a file or a folder to, which `AccessCheck` answers for the process's token before any flush, is passed over: it cannot be flushed by this account, and no entry in it can be one this account made (`C:\Users` for a standard account); every other folder of the chain, the leaf always among them, is flushed, and a failure there is an error (`hide-platform/tests/it/fs.rs`, `an_ancestor_is_passed_over_only_when_the_account_neither_owns_nor_may_add_to_it`).
The document save in `hide-host/src/save.rs` is exchange-and-verify on top of these and keeps its contract on every system; its checks (a regular file, one name, owned by this account, writable) are answered by `identity::link_count`, `private::handle_owned_by_current_user` and `permissions::Permissions`, so they mean the same on Windows.
`link` makes a link a caller can replace in one step (`replace_link`, the kit's `current`) and ask about (`is_link_to`); the name is never missing while it is replaced, and a path opened through it at that moment finds the old target or the new one on macOS and Windows but can answer "not found" on Linux, where the path walk that follows the link can meet the swap.
A link is a symbolic link on Unix; on Windows a junction for a folder, which needs no privilege and leads only to an absolute path on this machine, and a symbolic link for a file, which needs the privilege or Developer Mode and otherwise fails with `NeedsPrivilege`, a typed answer (`needs_privilege(&error)`) that a caller with an alternative takes.
`identity` says which file a name leads to (`FileId`: a device and an inode, or a volume serial and a 128-bit file id), whether two names are one file (`/tmp` and `/private/tmp`, a link and its target), the id of one entry of an open folder (`entry_id`, which the Explorer's "replaced while the prompt was open" check compares on every system), a canonical spelling with the `\\?\` prefix removed where the short form means the same, the path an open file or folder is at now (`path_of`: `F_GETPATH`, `/proc/self/fd`, `GetFinalPathNameByHandleW` with its `\\?\` prefix kept, so hided's root boundary compares a file and its root by prefix at any length), a `Stamp` (length, write time, change time) that tells whether a file changed between two looks, and whether a folder tells `Name` from `name` (asked of the folder at run time, because a Mac or Windows volume may be either).
`private::open_own_file` opens a file only when the name itself is the account's own regular file with no other name (a symbolic link at the name is not followed, a hard link is refused, a pipe does not block), which the label generator's lock, the daemon's locks and the pane credential reads stand on; `fs::open_regular` is the same refusal of links, pipes and devices without the ownership, for a file the operator picked to attach; `private::create_dir_all` makes a chain of private folders.
`private::handle_others_can_modify` checks the permissions of an already-open descriptor, so retirement decides from the same ledger, progress or run-state file it reads; the kit also refuses links at each owned directory before reads and removal.
`private::inspect_directory_entry` authenticates a real directory or returns an inspected system-owned alias target without following it.
It follows the sticky rule: a system folder others may add entries of their own to is still safe to rely on, since only their right to delete, rename or re-permission it or what is in it (on Windows `DELETE`, `FILE_DELETE_CHILD`, `WRITE_DAC`, `WRITE_OWNER`, or rewriting it) takes away a name a caller depends on; Unix states it as a root-owned sticky folder such as `/tmp`, and Windows as a folder owned by SYSTEM, Administrators or TrustedInstaller whose access list gives others no more than `FILE_ADD_FILE` and `FILE_ADD_SUBDIRECTORY`, which a drive's root gives every signed-in account (`inspection_lets_others_add_to_a_system_folder_but_not_delete_from_it`).
A drive's root also hands that group the right to change any folder made in it, so such a folder is refused, while Hide's private folders cut inheritance (a protected access list) and pass; retirement validates every component of an external state override and each alias target namespace before treating a missing ledger as empty.
`space` answers what an entry takes on its volume (allocated bytes, links and identity, from one `lstat` on Unix and by opening the entry on Windows, which has no call that answers from a name) and how much the volume has left, for the disk usage walk and the cleanup's free-space line.

`watch` is the fourth module: a `Watcher` watches folders, each with everything under it, and puts what changed on one `Changes` queue, which the caller drains on its own schedule.
A change is the path that was created, written, renamed or removed, once however many times the system said so while it waited, under the spelling the caller watched (FSEvents answers with the real path); reading a file is never a change (inotify's access events are dropped, and Windows is not asked for them).
On Linux a folder made under a watched one is watched only once its creation is read, so what is made in it before then is reported as that folder alone; a caller that waits for one exact path makes it in a folder that existed when the watch began.
The queue holds at most 4096 paths; past that, or when the system's own queue overflowed or it stopped watching, the waiting paths give way to one `Change::Overflow` that says anything under a watched folder may have changed, so what waits is bounded however long a burst lasts and a lost change is never silence; once the reader takes the overflow, what changes after it queues again.
`Watcher::keeping` takes a filter on the path relative to the watched folder, applied where the system reports it, so a burst no caller asked about neither fills the queue nor overflows it.
macOS and Linux are watched through `notify`; Windows is watched with `ReadDirectoryChangesW` directly, one thread per folder, because `notify` drops the answer Windows gives when its buffer overflows (no entries, or `ERROR_NOTIFY_ENUM_DIR`, on which it also stops watching); `watch` returns once that thread has started its first read, because a folder is watched only from then and a change made just before would be lost.
The Git watch is the caller, on the node that holds the repositories (`git_watch`, `hide-host/src/git_watch.rs`): one call holds one watcher over the projects' common directories, keeps only Git's facts (`HEAD`, `index`, `packed-refs`, `FETCH_HEAD`, `refs/`, `worktrees/`), and reports the repositories that changed, an overflow, or that nothing did, at least once a second so the caller can end it; the node ends the watcher when its caller stops answering or the node closes.
The core's side in `herdr-core/src/worktrees.rs` starts that call on a thread of its own and drains its reports on the coordinator's tick outside the runtime lock.
The thread keeps at most 256 reports for the tick and never a heartbeat; a report past that is dropped and kept as one re-read of every project (`git_watch.mailbox_full`), so a tick that falls behind costs reads, not memory.
With no renderer attached the coordinator ends the watch (`git_watch.rested`) rather than leave its reports unread, and the first read after a renderer attaches starts it again and reads every project once it is watching, as after any gap.
A stopped watch's thread ends on the node's next report and the reader joins it; the reader's end waits up to 3 s for those threads and names any still running (`git_watch.stop_unconfirmed`).
When the watched set changes it starts a new watch and keeps the old one, read after the new one in each pass, until one pass after the new one reports that it is watching, so a repository in both is never unwatched and a change the old one reports as the new one starts is still read.
An overflow becomes a `git_watch.overflow` diagnostic and a re-read of every watched project, where an inotify overflow used to be dropped without a word.
A watch that ended (`git_watch.ended`) or could not start is tried again after 30 s, and once it is watching every project is read again, because anything may have changed in the gap; a set change before then passes that re-read on to the watch that replaces it.
No watch event wakes the core: the coordinator's tick reads the queue, as it read the callback's map before, so the notifier's fan-out is unchanged.

`host` is the fifth module and answers where the account's things live and what the machine is called: `home_dir` (`HOME`, `USERPROFILE` on Windows), `state_dir` (`~/Library/Application Support`, `$XDG_STATE_HOME` or `~/.local/state`, `%LOCALAPPDATA%`) and `state_dir_under` for a caller whose state follows a home it was handed, `herdr_socket_default` (Herdr's own order for the pinned release: `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/herdr`, then `%APPDATA%\herdr` on Windows, then `~/.config/herdr`, each with `herdr.sock`, which `real_herdr.rs` checks against what the pinned Herdr reports), `login_path` (this process's `PATH`, which starts no shell) and `find_program` (an execute bit on Unix, `PATHEXT` on Windows), `login_shell_path` (the `PATH` the account's login shell sets up: `$SHELL -ilc` prints it between two marks, past whatever the startup files print, under a deadline and a stop flag; `Unsupported` on Windows, whose processes already get the account's `Path`), `default_shell`, `trash` (`NSFileManager` on macOS, the Recycle Bin, the freedesktop trash), `primary_language` (the first of `defaults read -g AppleLanguages` on macOS, the account's `LocaleName` on Windows, `LC_ALL`, `LC_MESSAGES` or `LANG` on Linux, as a BCP 47 tag; `NotFound` for the `C` locale), `tailscale_cli` (the app's CLI on macOS and Windows; `Unsupported` on Linux, which has no fixed place), `name` and `machine_id` (`IOPlatformUUID`, `/etc/machine-id`, the `MachineGuid` the Windows registry keeps).
`programs` is the one search for an agent's CLI: the login shell's `PATH`, this process's own `PATH` and the usual install folders, as `search_path`, `account_path`, `find_cli` and `cli_path_with`.
The install kit's "installed" and Hide AI's backends both read it, so there is no second program search (`hide-platform/tests/it/programs.rs`).
The login shell's answer is the one thing the crate remembers between calls, and it stays here on purpose: it is a fact about the account (what `$SHELL -ilc` prints), not about a caller, so a cache per owner (the kit's target, Hide AI) would start the operator's shell once for each and could disagree about the same program.
It is keyed by the shell and home it was read for and capped at eight pairs, kept until one of the shell's startup files changes, and asked again after sixty seconds when the shell did not answer, which is logged as `platform.login_shell_unread`.
Each shell and home pair has its own lock, held across the ask, so callers for the same pair that arrive cold together wait for one shell and read its answer (`callers_that_arrive_cold_together_start_one_shell`) while a caller for another home never waits behind it (`a_slow_ask_for_one_home_does_not_hold_another_home_behind_it`), and a stop raised by the caller that is asking ends the shell and remembers nothing.
A CLI found by this search is run with it too, never with the daemon's `PATH` alone: the Memory version probe and every Hide AI child take `cli_path_with` or `account_path` (`hide-ai/src/program.rs`).
Every program a node starts by name is started the same way, through `programs::command` (found on the account's search and run with it): `git` and `gh` (`hide-host/src/{git,git_command,worktrees,clone,gh}.rs`) and the Factory's commands and its checks' shell (`hide-host/src/factory.rs`).
Only a process's first `command` waits for the login shell: until a first read of the search (`account_path_now`) has finished, the caller waits for it, at most the shell's deadline and a second (`FIRST_READ_WITHIN`), so a fresh process such as the `hide` CLI finds on its first command a `gh` that only `.zshrc` puts on the `PATH` (`a_fresh_process_runs_what_only_the_login_shell_finds_on_its_first_command`); after that a caller takes the search as last read while a thread of its own reads it again once it is ten seconds old, so a caller's own deadline (`GIT_DEADLINE` for a git read) bounds its run, and the per-command cost is one lock and a clock read, the startup files' stats moving to that thread.
A daemon starts that first read as it comes up (`warm_account_path`), so its own first command seldom waits, while a device's node role starts none until a command asks, since a link that runs no command needs no shell; a read that panics is finished all the same, so it holds off neither its waiter nor the next read.
The two callers that must have the shell's current answer ask `account_path` and run `command_on` with it: a move's `gh` check and the Herdr server a moved core's login item starts (`ensure_herdr`), whose panes' shells start with its environment, so a core whose machine's Herdr server is down starts up to the shell's ten-second deadline later.
A daemon an SSH exec or a login item started has the system's folders alone on its own `PATH` (`/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin`), so a core a move put on a Mac mini found no Homebrew `gh`, and Git there ran its hooks, filters such as `git-lfs` and credential helpers without the account's folders; run with the account's search, a child finds what it starts by name where the operator's terminal would (`a_command_runs_what_the_login_shell_finds_with_the_path_it_was_found_on`).
The search is asked again only when a startup file changes, so a tool the operator installs into a folder those files name is found by the next command without a restart.
A test that shims one of these programs on a child's `PATH` therefore also controls the login shell, by naming a stand-in shell or removing `SHELL`, since macOS's `path_helper` puts the system's folders, and its Git, ahead of the folders a shell was handed.
Each refuses with `NotFound`, `InvalidInput` or `Unsupported` rather than guessing; hided's environment registry hands its own values to the `_from` forms, so the variables it depends on stay listed in `hided/src/env.rs`.
The Windows check builds the whole workspace, tests included.
`listeners` observes Windows TCP owners through the native IPv4 and IPv6 listener tables and reads each owner's cwd from bounded process-parameter memory, while the node's Unix reader (`hide-host/src/ports.rs`, the `listening_ports` call the core's `ports.rs` makes) retains `lsof`.
Its `ObservedWorkingDirectory` is attribution data, never the kernel proof `process::cwd_of` promises, and cannot authenticate a pane or checkout caller; that API and its Windows `Unsupported` answer are unchanged.
Each observed cwd is resolved with `fs::identity::canonical` before checkout ancestry is compared, so alternate case, junction and safely shortened verbatim spellings name the same directory.
A resolution error other than `PermissionDenied`, or a retained verbatim form whose prefix cannot compare reliably with a shortened checkout path, makes the sample unavailable instead of making the checkout idle.
The reader requests only query, memory-read and exit-observation rights, checks process birth identity around the read and after a final table sample, and refuses unknown architectures, unnormalized or changed records, invalid UTF-16, unreadable live owners other than permission-denied cwd observations, and exceeded limits.
At most 4096 rows, 512 owners and two 1 MiB tables are considered, with three allocation attempts per table and a ten-second work budget checked between native calls; each owner costs six bounded memory reads, including at most two 65534-byte cwd strings, and one filesystem path resolution, aggregate cwd text in either the observations or the returned sample is capped at 1 MiB, and every handle closes within its call.
An owner whose cwd observation returns `PermissionDenied` is excluded because protected owners are outside the current user's cleanup targets; an owner absent from the final listener tables is also excluded after an unreadable cwd.
Other incomplete observations and any owner or identity change become `unavailable_reason`, so destructive checkout cleanup remains refused.
The ports reader reads on its own `BackgroundRead` thread, outside `Mutex<Runtime>`, at the existing five-second refresh, and the coordinator picks the answer up on its next wake and publishes only changed state through the existing notifier.
One read is in flight at a time and the refresh interval starts after that completed sample, so a slow read cannot trigger immediate repeated work and never delays a Herdr event.
The work budget is checked between bounded local kernel calls and does not claim to preempt one; there is no retained read job to cancel after return.
Cleanup reads a fresh sample on its existing worker before removal; discovery starts or stops no server.
What has not moved yet: the `O_NONBLOCK` open that keeps a FIFO from stalling a reader (`hide-host` document, bytes, index and save, `hide-session`), the `readlinkat` in `hide-host/src/list.rs`, hided's root opens in `boundary.rs` (`openat` beneath the pinned root on Unix, a final-path check on Windows) and its Windows `ShellExecuteW` opener, and the stop signals in `hided/src/lib.rs` (tokio's per-system handlers).
What stays Unix by design until a later slice: the one-time move of a Mac's old state folder (`hided/src/state_move.rs`; no Windows build ever wrote that folder, so `Env` names none there).
hided starts on Windows, and `hided/tests/it/handshake.rs` runs there except the tests that reach a checkout through a folder link, which on Windows is a junction judged by the boundary's final-path check, and the FIFO test.
On Windows a pane bootstrap caller is bound to its pane when it descends from the pane's shell; the checkout binding needs the caller's working directory, which `cwd_of` cannot read there, so a caller outside a pane is refused `caller_unavailable`.
