`path` is the sixth module and decides how a path is spelled between machines: UTF-8 with `/` between names, the spelling Git's index, LSP and VS Code's remote URIs use.
A path below a root (a checkout, a History scope) is a `RelPath`: no leading `/` and no empty, `.` or `..` name, so it can only name something under the root it is joined to; the host protocol's paths, the file index's rows, Git's changed files and every relative path in a frame are this.
An absolute path keeps its root as the owning system writes it and only its separators change: `/repo/src` on macOS and Linux, `C:/repo/src` and `//server/share/src` on Windows, with the `\\?\` prefix gone where the short spelling means the same path and the drive letter upper case, so one folder has one spelling.
On macOS and Linux both spellings are the native ones, so the wire there is what it always was; `hide-platform/tests/it/path.rs` holds it to that and states the rest on all three systems (one relative spelling for a file below a checkout, a `\\?\` root relating to its short spelling, `..`, absolute input and another drive refused with a typed `PathError`, case following the root's volume, which `relative` asks of the folder only when two spellings differ).
Only the machine that owns a path converts it, once per direction: `to_wire` and `relative` on the way out (the catalog, hided's boundary answers, the CLI's caller path, the file index, and `wire.rs` for what Herdr reports), `from_wire` and `RelPath::to_native` on the way in (`relative_path` in `hide-host/src/root.rs`, what Hide sends Herdr; hided's boundary still opens the wire text as given, which is the native spelling on macOS and Linux and one Windows reads with `/` too), and `to_native` refuses a name the system cannot hold as written (a `\\` or `:` on Windows, a device name, a trailing dot) rather than reading it as something else.
Everything else relates wire spellings by their names alone (`wire_relative`, `wire_join`, `RelPath::parent`), which is correct whatever system owns them, so the core, hided and the web shell join and compare a device's paths with `/` and never ask their own filesystem or their own system's rules about another machine's path.
This differs from treating an absolute path as an opaque value only the web displays: the shell, the core's persisted state (`expanded_paths`, editor tabs, drafts, `workspace-views.json`) and the snapshot already key and join absolute paths with `/`, and one spelling keeps all of that and the Unix wire unchanged.
`to_wire_lossy` is for a path that was always sent and is shown or compared rather than read back; one with no wire spelling keeps the system's own text, which on macOS and Linux is the same.
The desktop host is the other place this computer's paths meet the page: `desktop/src/main/wirePath.ts` states the same rules in TypeScript, letter for letter, answers the folder picker and the terminal-link probe in the wire spelling and reads every path the page names (a reveal, an open, a probe) back with `from_wire`'s rules, so a native `C:\repo`, a rooted `\repo` or a drive-relative `C:repo` from the page is refused on Windows; `wirePath.test.ts` holds it to the Rust tests' expected spellings, the Windows ones on every system.
Not spelled yet: a Windows device's paths seen from a Mac (its helper and Herdr report native ones, and `wire.rs` leaves a spelling this machine does not read as absolute as it came), and the daemon frame's state folders, which the shell only shows.

`user_agents` answers what a core move needs from the machine taking the core, named for what the caller asks rather than for launchd: whether the system keeps login agents (`supported`), what an agent needs of the account's login (`login`: not needed, logged in or logged out), how long the machine waits on power before it sleeps on its own (`sleeps_on_power`), and the install, one-shot start, start, last exit, loaded, installed, unload, remove and forget of one agent named by its label.
launchd is the one implementation (`user_agents/launchd.rs`, with `/bin/launchctl`, the `gui/<uid>` domain, the property list under `~/Library/LaunchAgents` and `pmset`); every other system answers `Unsupported` with the reason and holds no agent, which is what makes a move to it fail its session and power checks, and a Linux or Windows implementation is added beside it without reshaping its callers.
`UserAgents::fixture` runs the stand-ins of a folder in the programs' place (`HIDE_PREFLIGHT_PROGRAMS` names one), and a fixture HOME's kit gets `UserAgents::none`, so no test reaches the account's real login agents.

`stdio` ends a command-line program quietly when the reader of its standard output leaves, as `hide agent list | head` does.
Rust turns a failed `print!` into a panic whose message ends in the operating system's error code, so `hide` installs one panic hook first thing in `main` (`exit_quietly_when_stdout_closes`) instead of every print site handling the failure: when the code is `BrokenPipe` by the standard library's own classification (`EPIPE` on Unix, a closed pipe on Windows, so no table of codes is kept) the process exits with status 141, 128 plus `SIGPIPE`, which is what a shell reports for a command a closed pipe killed, and prints nothing; any other panic, including a print that fails on a full disk, goes to the hook that was installed before.
`hide-platform/tests/it/stdio.rs` writes to a pipe whose reader is already gone on every system, and `hided/tests/it/closed_stdout.rs` shows `hide version` doing the same.

`hide-platform/tests/` and `hide-herdr-client/tests/it/real_herdr.rs` (the client against the pinned Herdr, ignored unless `HIDE_E2E_HERDR_BIN` names the binary) are where a change to the transport is proved; the subscription's reader is the high-frequency path, and on Unix it is the same blocking `read` as before.
`hided/tests/it/real_herdr.rs` is the same for the daemon: hided starts against a private pinned Herdr, answers `/health`, opens a Workspace through the shell's `/ws` events, and a line typed into its pane comes back on the terminal stream through `herdr terminal session control`; the Windows check runs it on Windows, where it is the proof that hided starts there.

## Resident process ownership

The process that starts a resource and the lifetime owner are not always the same: the desktop host deliberately starts session services that outlive its window.
That product lifetime is the local exception to treating host quit as the end of every child; it does not exempt the service's own workers from engineering principle #14's cleanup rule or #15's caps.
The following table describes Hide's current macOS startup path, not an operator's separate service configuration or a promise of restored live processes.

| Process | Starter and lifetime owner | Stop condition | App quit | Reboot and login |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Herdr server | The packaged desktop host checks server status and starts a detached bundled server only when status confirms none is running; explicit overrides/unpackaged hosts supply their own server. Herdr owns PTYs, panes and agent processes. | Explicit Herdr server stop, session stop targeting that server, or process/OS termination; stopping a different named session leaves this server running. A bundled `hide connect` hands a server that runs another Hide app's Herdr to its own app's Herdr, and a device's node hands the server on that device's registered socket to the pinned copy when it runs an older release, panes and processes kept (see The bundled Herdr runtime); neither stops, restarts or replaces anything else. | Continues independently; the host does not signal it. | The old process is gone; the next packaged app discovery can start a missing server. This startup path installs no login service for Herdr. |
| hided | `hide connect` serializes discovery in its state folder, returns a matching healthy daemon or starts the sibling `hided`; the daemon owns the core and its background work. | `hide stop`, a stop signal or idle expiry; default idle expiry is 600 s with no clients and an unreachable Herdr, unless daemon keep-alive or paired-phone Mobile keep-alive holds it. A bundled connect also replaces a healthy daemon of another build in that same state folder, except a core its login item runs, which it updates or refuses (Updating the core); an unbundled CLI refuses it. | Continues; no-window screen readers pause, while label work and the daemon's own lifetime policy remain active. | The old process is gone; `hide connect` starts another on demand. These discovery paths install no hided login service. |
| Device node (`hided node serve`) | The core opens an SSH exec channel on the device's one connection for a consented device; the node serves that channel's stdin/stdout and, for the channel's life only, its panes' bootstrap socket (`hide-host/src/panes.rs`). | EOF, unusable request framing or output failure ends admission and closes the pane socket, its streams and its folder; scoped workers finish before node exit. Core close/revoke closes the channel, not the connection; consent revocation drains admitted work up to its bound. | A helper may continue while the surviving daemon retains its connection; window closure alone is not helper shutdown. | The process/channel is gone. A later consented connection starts another helper; this helper path installs no login service. |
| Node-role hided (M) | `hide connect` on a machine whose state folder has a placement record starts the sibling `hided`, which runs the node role; it owns its one SSH connection to C, the attach channel, the relays, the local forward and the watch thread, all ended with it. | `hide stop`, a stop signal, or idle: no screen open and its Herdr not answering, by the same rule as the core role (`server::watch_idle`). | Continues; the host does not signal it. | Gone; the next `hide connect` starts it. Nothing is installed to start it. |
| Attach role (`hided attach`, C) | C's sshd starts it on the exec channel M opens; it starts no core and pipes the channel to C's running core. | Its channel ends, C's core socket closes, or 30 s pass without a byte from M. | Not the app's. | Gone with sshd; M dials again. |

The local executable authorities are `desktop/src/main/{host,herdr,spawn}.ts`, `hided/src/{cli,env,lib,server}.rs`, `hide-node/src/ssh/host.rs` and `hide-host/src/serve.rs` with `hided/src/node_cli.rs`.
The one-release kit retirement stage is `hide-kit/src/coordination_retirement.rs`; it does not start a resident service.
It reads legacy v1 and current Hide-only v2 Sasu registries without importing them, checks their retained run states, and refuses if the authoritative revision disappears after selection.
After reboot, hided and its watches start with the app; no login automatic start is added.
Herdr's [CLI reference](https://herdr.dev/docs/cli-reference/) and [Socket API](https://herdr.dev/docs/socket-api/) define its public stop and session boundary; the bundled contract remains the files named in [The Herdr wire boundary](#the-herdr-wire-boundary).
In the bundled upstream `src/session.rs`, `stop_session_with_timeout` selects the named session's API/client sockets and sends `server.stop` through the same routine as `stop_active_server`; an unnamed session stop targets the default session.
An app exit preserves service ownership, but a reboot ends the prior server, PTY and helper processes; persisted layout/session references are not continued execution or proof that an agent completed its task.
hided's replacement checks the named daemon's health and process identity before signalling it, and leaves Herdr and pane processes alone (`cli.rs::connect`, `cli.rs::stop_daemon`).
The device helper admits 4 running requests and 32 queued requests on the core connection; revocation's drain bound is 250 s, while each call has its own deadline (`hide-node/src/ssh/host.rs`, `hide-host/src/serve.rs`).
A device has one SSH connection with at most eight session channels, the node's link among them, and its pane service holds at most sixteen callers and eight streams; terminals keep their own connections until layer 3, so those bounds are not yet a cap on every connection to a device.
Desktop CLI children, attach streams, provider children and bridge helpers have narrower owners than these session services; their cleanup and budget belong to their existing feature sections and the [resident work ledger](PERFORMANCE_TESTING.md#resident-ticks-timers-and-watchers).

## The bundled Herdr runtime

The packaged desktop app ships the pinned Herdr binary in `Contents/Resources/herdr` and passes it as `HERDR_BIN_PATH` to every `hide` child it starts, unless the environment already names one as an explicit override (an isolated e2e run or a development server keeps its own binary; a value a Herdr pane exported is not an override, see The desktop host).
Neither `hided` nor the core starts a Herdr server, save the login item of a core a move placed, which starts one when Herdr says none runs (`ensure_herdr` in `hided/src/login_item.rs`): `hided` resolves the herdr binary (`Env::herdr_program`, below) for its node's terminal service to spawn `herdr terminal session control` per pane attach, and a missing socket is read as evidence the server is not running (`herdr-core/src/live.rs`), never as something to start.
The packaged desktop host is the one place that starts a server, because after a reboot nothing else does and the operator opens hide, not a terminal (`desktop/src/main/host.ts`, `ensureHerdrServer`, over `ensureServer` in `desktop/src/main/herdr.ts`).
Before each `hide connect` it asks its bundled `herdr status server --json` about the socket its children would use; only `running: false` starts `herdr server`, since a socket file a dead server left behind reads as a live path but answers nothing (Herdr answers `not_running` for it).
The server is started detached, leading its own process group, and waited for up to five seconds; a server Herdr started this way restores the saved layout and resumes agents with a native session reference without any client attaching (measured 2026-09-29 on an isolated server).
It is not the host's child in the ownership sense: its panes are the operator's work, so it outlives the app as a server `herdr` starts from a terminal does; `herdr server stop` or a `herdr session stop [name]` targeting that server ends it.
The server gets the host's child environment without the per-pane values Herdr sets in every pane itself (`HERDR_BIN_PATH`, the pane, tab and workspace ids, `HERDR_ENV`); an app opened from Finder carries launchd's environment, which has no locale, so the server also gets `LC_CTYPE=UTF-8` when no locale variable is set, because a pane shell without one reads Hangul as bytes (`desktop/src/main/herdr.ts`, `serverEnvironment`).
An unpackaged host and an explicit `HERDR_BIN_PATH` override start nothing, so a development or e2e run keeps the isolated server it made.
Nothing here stops or restarts a server that answers, because doing so would end the operator's panes; a server that dies while the app is attached is not restarted.
The one change made to an answering server is a live handoff after the app is replaced, below.

The server outlives the app that started it, so once a newer app replaces that app the server goes on running the old app's Herdr from wherever the old app was put, renamed aside (`hide-previous-<version>.app.bak`) or in the Trash: every pane runs under that program until the server ends, the old bundle stays in use, a newer Herdr the app pins does not run, and macOS names the old app when a process in a pane asks for a permission such as Screen Recording (seen 2026-10-14 with `hide-previous-0.1.0-main.a7f73ac1.app.bak`).
A bundled `hide connect` therefore looks at the server on its socket before it looks at the daemon, under the same `connect.lock` (`hided/src/herdr_handoff.rs`, called from `cli.rs::find_or_start`).
It asks the socket's peer for its pid and the kernel which program file that pid runs (`hide_platform::process::image_of`), and acts only when that file is not its own app's Herdr and sits beside another Hide app's runtime (`hide_kit::holds_package_runtime`: `hided` beside `app.asar`, read from what the folder holds, since the old app's name has changed).
A server the operator runs from their own Herdr is never touched, nor one when the host was given another Herdr (`HERDR_BIN_PATH` naming anything but the app's own).
It then pings the server, asks its own Herdr for its version (`herdr status client --json`), and sends `server.live_handoff` with `import_exe` set to its own Herdr and that version and the pinned protocol as the import's expectations, only when the server says it can (`capabilities.live_handoff`, which Herdr answers on Unix only) and its Herdr is the same release as the app's or an older one, so a newer server is never handed back to an older Herdr.
Herdr spawns the replacement from the given binary, passes it every pane's terminal, and exits once the replacement holds them; the pane processes are not restarted, and a refusal before that point leaves the old server as it was.
Herdr's clients are told to reconnect: the daemon's subscriptions reconnect as after any Herdr restart, its terminal attaches retry on their usual 5 s schedule, and a Herdr TUI the operator attached directly exits with Herdr's "live update in progress; reconnect after handoff completes".
Forty panes with scrollback took about a second on an isolated server (2026-10-14), well inside the request's 15 s bound and `hide connect`'s 25 s.
Herdr restores each screen from what it held, so output written at that instant can lose one line break: a pane printing a line a second showed two of them joined on one row in Herdr's own read of the screen, and every line after it in place (2026-10-14, packaged app over a server its previous build started).
`hide connect` then confirms the server on the socket runs its own Herdr, and every outcome but "nothing to move" is one `herdr.handoff` record on its standard error, which the host writes to its log (`cliRecords`); none fails the connect, because a server that keeps its old Herdr is what every earlier build left.
Herdr calls this live handoff experimental and runs it itself only for `herdr update --handoff`; Hide uses the same request against the binary it ships, and `hided/tests/it/herdr_handoff.rs` (the remote mailbox lane, Linux, and its macOS nightly) proves it against the pinned binary: a server started from one app folder is handed to another's Herdr with its pane's process alive and listed, and a Herdr in a folder no Hide app made is left alone.
A connected device's Herdr follows the app too (PRD device-herdr-update), by a rule of its own, because Hide never starts a device's server: the server on the device's registered socket is the operator's, and it is handed over whenever it runs an older stable release than the pin (D-04, D-06).
A package that carries Herdr for the device's system and processor (the helper rule: the Mac app's own `herdr` for an Apple Silicon Mac, the Linux package's for a Linux x64 machine) uploads it with the helper into `<helper root>/herdr/<digest prefix>/herdr`, beside the build folders and not in one, so a build that changes only the helper finds it in place and a new pin is uploaded once per device (`hide-node/src/ssh/host.rs`, `place_herdr`); the build folder names its copy in `herdr.pin`, which the build's digest covers, and a core update never carries it.
Before each kit apply the device's helper points `<root>/herdr/current` at its build's copy, or takes the link away when the build brought none (`hide-host/src/kit.rs`, `point_herdr`), and Hide's own Herdr calls on the device use that copy first: the status read's `PATH` (`hide-node/src/ssh.rs`, `remote_herdr_path`), the kit's integrations (`hide_kit::device_target`) and the node's terminal attaches (`NodeTerminals::new`); the operator's own `herdr` is never written.
After the apply the node's Herdr updater, which `hided node serve` installs into `hide_host::kit`, runs `hided/src/herdr_handoff.rs`'s `device_update` against the registered socket only (another server on the device, such as a test server on a `/tmp` socket, is never asked): it pings the server, asks the copy its version, and leaves a server on the pinned release (whatever file it runs), a newer one or a preview alone; an older one that cannot hand over is reported, and one that can is handed to the copy with the same `server.live_handoff` and confirmation as this Mac's.
A failed handoff is recorded in `<root>/herdr/handoff.json` by socket, server pid and start, and copy (at most 16), and is not tried again until the server is a new process or the pin changes (D-13).
The outcome travels back in the kit report (`KitReport.herdr`, `hide_kit::device_herdr::HerdrUpdate`); the core writes one `kit` `herdr.handoff` record with the device id for each, and while the server stays older the device row carries `herdr_behind` (`DeviceSnapshot`, cause `cannot_hand_off` or `handoff_failed`), which Settings shows as one line with the operator's step while the device is reachable, and which ends as soon as the live server answers with another release; a success shows only as the version on the row, which the core reads from the server's own snapshot (`RemoteSessionSnapshot.herdr_version`) rather than the status read the connection made once.
A device whose Herdr speaks an older protocol is given the device's step instead of this Mac's advice to stop the session and reopen Hide (`SessionFetchError::for_device`).
A copy stays while any process on the device runs it (`hide_platform::process::running`, which compares program file identities), so a server not yet handed over keeps its program, and while any build folder names it, so a copy another connection uploaded for its build is not taken before that build applies; the next pass, or the device's removal, takes a copy neither holds.
A removal that leaves a copy a server still runs keeps the helper folder and says why, rather than failing.
`hided/tests/it/herdr_handoff.rs` proves the device rule against the pinned Herdr and Herdr 0.9.1, which the remote mailbox lane fetches by digest: an older server moves with its pane's process alive and listed, a server on another socket keeps its Herdr, a server on the pin is left alone, and a refused handoff leaves the server and is not repeated; `desktop/e2e/device-kit.spec.ts` proves the upload, the link and the row on an isolated SSH device.
A Windows device has no node, so it gets nothing (docs/INSTALL.md, What each system supports).
There is no version floor; the pin is exact.
`scripts/fetch-herdr-runtime.sh` downloads the pinned asset into a digest-keyed cache and verifies it against `contracts/herdr-bundle.json` before `desktop/scripts/package.mjs` ships it; `scripts/check-herdr-pin-single-source.sh` fails when any of `scripts/fetch-herdr-runtime.sh`, `desktop/scripts/package.mjs`, `desktop/src/main/cli.ts`, `web/e2e/herdr-fixture.ts`, `desktop/e2e/fixture.ts` or `scripts/web-shell-measure/isolated-env.sh` restates the version, repo or digest instead of reading the manifest.
Move the pin with `scripts/bump-herdr.sh <release-tag>` (a stable `v0.8.3` or a `preview-...` tag), which verifies the asset, writes the contract that binary reports, and rewrites the tag, version and digest tokens in the README, install guide and third-party notice.
A bump also compares the release's own PowerShell prompt wrapper (`WINDOWS_POWERSHELL_SHELL_INTEGRATION_COMMAND` in its `src/pane.rs`) with the copy Hide's Windows integration carries (`hide_host::shell_integration`), and moves the copy with it, since an agent's tab runs Hide's command in place of Herdr's own PowerShell start.
`.github/workflows/herdr-update.yml` polls for a new stable release daily and opens a PR with that bump after running both test suites; it never merges, because the core's Herdr behavior assumptions are only asserted against fixtures this repository wrote.

## hided and the WebSocket boundary

`hided` is the product daemon that owns one `herdr-core::Core` on its creating thread.
Axum workers send dispatch and snapshot commands over a channel; they never call `Core::create`, `dispatch_bytes`, `snapshot_delta`, `on_change` or drop the handle from another thread.
It binds `127.0.0.1` only.
The WebSocket handshake is the client's first JSON frame `{token, schema_version, snapshot_format?, have_revision?, have_terminal_sequence?, have_terminal_epoch?}`.
Schema 2 clients that request `snapshot_format: "semantic-rest-v1"` receive `rest_format: "semantic-rest-v1"` on snapshot payloads: a delta replaces only fields it carries, merging navigator, terminal and status by their direct fields, while arrays replace as complete ordered lists.
Omission retains a value, `null` clears an optional value and an empty array or map clears a collection; `workspace_view` is explicitly null when absent in this format.
Without the capability, schema 2 keeps the supported complete-rest delta, including the omitted absent `workspace_view`; an unknown format is refused with `schema_mismatch` before snapshot delivery.
A mismatched token, Origin, or schema version, or a ninth concurrent client, is closed with a reason code (`invalid_token`, `origin_not_allowed`, `schema_mismatch`, `client_limit`) and a diagnostic log line.
After a valid handshake the daemon reads from the revision the client sent (`have_revision`, the cursor `Core::snapshot_delta_payload` takes), and then one delta frame per core notification burst.
A client with cursor 0 gets a self-contained `snapshot`; a reconnecting client resumes with a `delta` that carries only what changed while it was away.
When the core cannot serve the cursor, because the client's revision is ahead of the core's after a daemon restart, the daemon re-reads from zero and sends a `snapshot` (`server::classify_frame` owns that rule).
Terminal output comes beside those frames from the hub (see The terminal path): a client given a delta resumes every pane from `have_terminal_sequence` when `have_terminal_epoch` names this hub, or, naming none or another hub's, has every pane drawn again; a client given a whole snapshot resets its terminals and is told its new cursor in a `terminal` frame with no chunks.
A frame the daemon cannot produce at all (the core owner thread gone or a transport encoding failure) ends that client's loop, which the browser sees as a bare close and answers by reconnecting; the daemon logs it as `ws.snapshot_failed` with the stage, the only trace of why.
The web shell counts snapshots (`viewGeneration`) and re-requests its terminal view on each one, so a resync redraws the pane instead of trusting what it had drawn.
The token comparison is constant in the token's length (`subtle`), so a refusal does not leak how much of the token a caller guessed.
The Workspace CLI uses the same `/ws` for checkout-scoped commands and results, without giving an agent the shell's unrestricted token or snapshot stream.
Its local bootstrap socket lives in a random private short directory under `/tmp` (the account's temporary folder on Windows, where the path names a pipe and its marker file) so a long state path cannot exceed the Unix socket limit or let another user reserve a predictable path.
A Windows pipe name is global rather than inside that folder, so every client connect checks that the pipe it reached belongs to its own account and refuses one another account holds (`hide_platform::ipc`).
An owner-only record in `pane-capabilities` publishes the socket path atomically; daemon shutdown removes both.
It attests the kernel peer PID and issues a protected file reference, or answers one of the three typed hook reads described below; it accepts no control command, and nothing in the request chooses a Workspace.
Attestation binds a local caller one of two ways, tried in this order (`hided/src/pane_auth.rs`, which issues the credential from the kernel facts the node reads in `hide-node/src/pane_proof.rs`: the pane's shell, descent, the caller's folder, a process's start and the bootstrap socket).
A peer that descends from the shell Herdr runs for the pane named in `HERDR_PANE_ID` gets a pane-bound credential: it records the device, Workspace, checkout, pane terminal identity, and shell process birth, all checked again for each command, so closing or recreating the pane invalidates it (`pane_changed`).
A local peer whose pane attestation does not succeed, because it is not such a descendant, names no pane at all, or names one that Herdr, or a hided started without a Herdr socket, cannot confirm, is bound by its working directory instead: hided reads the peer's cwd from the kernel (`proc_pidinfo` with `PROC_PIDVNODEPATHINFO` on macOS, `/proc/<pid>/cwd` on Linux), canonicalizes it, and the core resolves the longest registered, connected checkout path that contains it.
That checkout-bound credential records the device, Workspace, and checkout, and each command rechecks only that the checkout is still registered and its device connected (`checkout_not_registered` otherwise); an unreadable cwd is refused `caller_unavailable`, and a cwd outside every registered checkout is refused `checkout_not_registered`.
This is what lets a Codex tool shell run by the shared `codex app-server` daemon, whose parent is launchd rather than any pane, drive the Workspace of the checkout it runs in; a caller on a device, through its node, is still attested by pane only, except on the screen machine of a core on another machine, whose node proves a caller in no pane by its working directory as well (`NodeEvent::CheckoutProof`) and the core binds it to that node's own checkout only on the link that node dialed (PRD core-host-node-remote-core D-10).
The checkout-bound credential covers the Workspace commands (file, diff, browser, view, workspace info) and `hide factory`, where it is the operator acting without a pane; delivery and agent commands (`hide request`, `inbox`, `watch`, `agent`) act as one agent and refuse it `agent_pane_required`, because only a pane-bound credential names that agent, and a pane hint never stands in for it; `workspace info` lists their capabilities for a pane-bound caller only ([delivery.md](delivery.md#commands-and-caller-identity)).
A pane on an SSH device reaches the same `/ws` through that device's node link, with no other connection to the daemon (PRD core-host-node B18, B19).
Right after the link's hello, the core asks the node to start its pane service (`Call::PanesStart`, `hide-host/src/panes.rs`), which binds the device's bootstrap endpoint (a Unix socket, a named pipe and its marker file on Windows) in a fresh `bridge-*` folder under `workspace-bridges` in the device's state folder (`~/.hide/state` unless `HIDE_STATE_DIR` or `XDG_STATE_HOME` names another, resolved by `hide_kit::layout` on both the node and the device's `hide`); several daemons may have one device open, so each node keeps its own folder, and a starting service removes the folders of services that ended without removing theirs (a killed node, or the workspace bridge an older Hide ran) once their socket no longer answers.
The device's panes run the `hide` installed beside its helper (see Device file hosts), which bootstraps through each such endpoint when no daemon answers on its own machine.
The node proves each caller with its own kernel, the peer pid descended from the shell Herdr runs for the named pane, and sends the proof up as an event (`hide_node_link::panes::NodeEvent::PaneProof`); hided resolves the pane within that node's own device, whatever the proof names, issues the credential bound to that node and that link (`hided/src/node_panes.rs`), and answers down (`Call::PaneProofAnswer`); the node writes the reference file, which names the node's socket rather than a port (`pane_auth::Route::Node`).
A command's bytes travel the same link as a stream: the device's `hide` opens the node's socket with a `{"stream":true,"token":"…"}` line, the node relays its bytes up as `StreamOpen`/`StreamData`/`StreamClosed` events and writes what comes down (`Call::StreamWrite`, `Call::StreamClose`), and hided serves each stream as a `/ws` connection that accepts only a credential the same node vouched for over the same link.
For that stream's single command the node selects the reference it issued for the prelude's token, reads its pane from Herdr, checks the kernel peer still descends from the pane's shell, and sends the current identity with `StreamOpen` (`hide_node_link::panes::StreamCaller`).
A persistent reference uses the same proof as a one-shot reference; a one-shot additionally requires its holder's pid and start to match the peer, and an older prelude without a token can select only that peer's one-shot reference.
The token only selects the reference, never substitutes for the peer proof; hided takes the identity only for a credential that same node vouched for over that same link and for the pane it names (`pane_auth::remote_identity`).
The proof belongs to one command, never to the reference's eight-hour life: an identity change between stream open and that command remains the same observation window as a one-shot call, and after the core's normal ten-second inspection budget from receipt it asks `PaneInspect` again, including time spent waiting for the handshake or executor.
Ten seconds is the existing scoped first-request and pane-inspection budget, not a new credential lifetime; the scoped connection admits one command and cannot spend that proof on a second one.
The clock starts when the core receives the proof, so an identity change can remain unseen for the node-to-core delivery delay plus less than that budget before validation; this is not a ten-second bound measured on the node's clock.
The node's five-second reference sweep can revoke an ended shell earlier, but it checks file and process survival rather than every terminal identity field, so it is not a substitute for the fresh proof or inspection.
A browser relay uses the proof for its one admission only; after admission its existing claimed connection and display lease govern its lifetime, and retaining a persistent reference does not retain this proof for its next connection.
A stream that carries none, from a peer the node cannot match, is checked against the pane as the node reads it when the command arrives (`Call::PaneInspect`), and a pane whose identity changed is refused `pane_changed` before the core's context is read.
Because the node a credential belongs to is the link it arrived on, never a field of a message, one device's node cannot vouch for another device's pane, and closing or losing the link revokes everything it vouched for at once.
The pane service and that `hide` run on Windows too, and the node reads the account's home there from `USERPROFILE`; the remote probe and kit install still assume a Unix device, so a Windows machine cannot be added as a device yet (issue #293).
The node's paths cross the link in the wire spelling (`hide_platform::path`): the endpoint and each reference it answers with, which the device's `hide` reads back with `from_wire`, as it does the reference hided's own bootstrap answers.
A link carries at most eight streams and holds at most 64 chunks of 48 KiB for one; a node serves sixteen callers at once and hided works on 32 proofs across every device, refusing the rest `bridge_busy`; a starting or failing pane service is a `remote_host` record (`host.panes_started`, `host.panes_unstarted`) and a stream that overflows a `node_panes` record in `Logs/core.jsonl`, since a detached daemon's stderr goes nowhere.
Proofs run on hided's blocking executor with a permit held through queued and running work; every callback returns without waiting on its SSH reader's own link.
Reader-callback refusal and stream-close replies have a separate allowance of 32 queued plus running jobs across the daemon, held until the reply finishes on that executor.
If that allowance is full, hided records `pane.refused` with `control_full` and closes only the offending link through its nonblocking close path, marking its credentials dead immediately and waking normal reader cleanup and reconnect.
A reply that fails records `control_unanswered` and closes its link too; dropping a job releases its permit on every exit path.
Every caller turned away is a `pane.refused` record there with the node, the pane it named and the reason it read (B30): one hided refuses itself (`by: core`), and one the device's node refuses on its own, at its caller or stream cap included, which the node reports up the link (`NodeEvent::Refused`, `by: node`) with its fields cut to a bounded prefix, because a device's stderr reaches no log.
Inside the core both credentials are one caller id in the pane slot (`workspace_control::Caller`): a pane id, or `checkout:<nonce>:<canonical path>`, so action retry records are per capability and no core query or action depends on which binding issued it.
A desktop window's browser gateway acts as `gateway:<key>:<canonical path>`, one key per window, which resolves like a checkout caller; only whether its open comes forward reads the difference (`caller_stays_behind`).
Each checkout-bound issuance and refusal is one `pane_auth` record in the diagnostics log with the device, checkout, reason, whether the credential was persistent, and at bootstrap the peer pid (a command's WebSocket carries none), never the caller's path or the reference bytes.
Direct CLI calls own one-shot credentials and release them after their request, while repeated agent session starts in the same attested pane, or from the same checkout, reuse one reference until its pane ends or the eight-hour limit expires.
Only that persistent reference is claimed (`workspace_claim`, then the `.claimed` marker), which lets it outlive its unclaimed 30 seconds; a one-shot command sends no claim and closes after its answer, since the daemon revokes a one-shot token when its connection ends, and a `hide browser` relay, which the daemon starts only for a claimed credential, claims whatever its credential (`workspace_cli::AfterAnswer`).
A command whose `HIDE_CAP_REF` names a reference the daemon no longer holds bootstraps a one-shot credential once, exactly as a bare command does, and runs on it (`workspace_cli::Credential`): the reference's file is gone because it expired or was revoked, nothing listens on its port because the daemon that issued it crashed, the daemon closes the handshake `invalid_token` because it never issued that token, or it refuses the token `credential_expired` before running the command; a reference file left empty, as a power loss leaves one written without a flush, reads as gone too.
The fallback passes the same attestation, so it carries no authority a bare `hide` would not get, and a bootstrap refusal is the command's answer with the bootstrap's next step.
Once a Workspace action result has reached the CLI, a lost acknowledgement of a persistent reference's claim cannot turn that result into a claim that the action was not applied; the claim failure is logged and a later command rechecks its capability.
Hook reads (`hide inbox --hook`, `hide workspace memory` and SessionStart's `hide workspace info --hook`) use one proof-and-answer exchange through the bootstrap socket on both the core and a node, without a WebSocket upgrade.
`hide_node_link::panes::HookCommand` admits only `LetterPull`, `Memory` and read-only `WorkspaceInfo`; a Workspace action, a letter send or confirmation, and a Factory or browser command cannot be represented on this route.
The caller selects one live route before sending, refuses ambiguous node bridges before sending to any, and never retries a sent request through another transport.
A node sends the typed command with its fresh kernel proof over its own link (NodeLink protocol 31); the core reuses the same credential issuance, validation, delivery executor and Memory lookup as the Workspace route.
Letter and Memory requests own fresh one-shot tokens, held internally and revoked on every return and unwind before their answers are sent; a local reference file is removed with them and a node creates no reference file.
Workspace guidance returns its read-only info and the same persistent reference previously obtained by bootstrap followed by info, with unchanged capabilities and lifetime.
The core claims that reference after the local answer is written or the node accepts its proof answer; a late node answer cannot claim it, and a failed delivery removes only newly issued authority.
The node owns the private file and its claimed marker, removes newly created files on failure, and revokes their token over its own link.
A named reference, when still live, must match the freshly proved pane identity and checkout, and on a node must have been vouched for by that same node and link; it is a constraint on the proof, never a replacement for it.
Letter and Memory reads do not extend a named reference's unclaimed lifetime; an expired reference takes the fresh proof in this same exchange, as a bare caller does.
The same ten-second receipt-age bound applies to a pane proof queued at the core, with a fresh `PaneInspect` after it, and link closure revokes its credentials.
The request and answer are bounded at 16 KiB and 64 KiB, with one answer per local connection, the existing eight local bootstrap workers, sixteen node callers and thirty-two core proof workers.
The two prompt children remain independent and parallel, with their own caller deadlines; the Memory Project is still read from its owning node on every lookup because no existing watcher invalidates all identity facts.
The old scoped Workspace wire still accepts these reads for already-running local clients; the current hook CLI uses only the proof exchange.
The registry has a 64-reference cap, the bootstrap path has an eight-worker cap, and daemon shutdown revokes every reference.
The five-second sweep also drops a checkout-bound reference whose checkout no longer resolves on its device, with a `sweep`-stage `pane_auth` record, so an unregistered project stops counting against the cap before its eight hours end.
A command whose answer no longer matches its credential is refused by the credential's own binding: `pane_changed` for a pane, `checkout_not_registered` for a checkout, each with the next step that binding can take.
The shell handshake marks actual web and desktop renderer connections with `client_kind`; Workspace commands refuse when only the daemon or another CLI client is connected.
Checkout-scoped `hide view select`, `split`, `move`, and `close` resolve the live device and checkout in the core, then apply one existing View layout transition without using the front Workspace as an authorization shortcut.
They leave keyboard pane focus alone; a last View holding an unsaved document refuses close, while a second View of the same document may close by itself.
One request ID carries an action result for up to ten minutes, and the 128-result cap refuses new commands instead of evicting an answer that a retry may need; a second intent uses a new ID.
The CLI returns the request ID on uncertain transport outcomes so the caller can retry that same action or inspect `hide view list`.
File and working-diff opens resolve a caller-cwd path on the calling machine, then use the checkout's existing host channel to read a document or Git status off the core lock.
The core rechecks the caller's checkout membership when it places the result into that checkout's View tree; a missing file, unavailable host, unchanged diff, or layout refusal has a request-scoped CLI outcome.
No open moves the front Workspace, the visible tab or the keyboard pane; an open or select from the operator's own pane, the selected pane of the front Workspace, brings its View forward and opens that Workspace's lane on Files (`AreaIntent::Files`), raising `lane_called` so the page puts it up, and from any other caller the lane keeps its state.
`hide browser open` requires a connected desktop renderer and places a Browser View in the caller's checkout with the same bounded retry result as other actions.
The core checks HTML paths against the caller device's checkout and reads them outside its lock through the consented host channel.
The native host reports page load, completion, failure, and eviction with a load stamp; `hide view status` and `--wait` distinguish page state from View placement.
The desktop host creates pages only when a View has a visible slot, so waiting on a hidden View expires without taking focus.
For a remote Browser View, the desktop host asks hided for the current core-owned address and a route bound to its own process, View, and load stamp.
A remote loopback URL gets an SSH local forward with bounded connections, while a remote HTML file gets an owner-only loopback server whose assets are read through the device host's pinned checkout root.
Neither route falls back to this Mac's same path or port; close, device loss, or desktop process exit releases the route.
Client frames are core events (`schema_version`, `kind`, `payload`).
HTTP serves static assets, `GET /health` (`pid`, `version`, `build`, `schema_version`, `clients`), and token-authenticated Browser route resolution and release for the desktop host.
The same desktop authentication registers and releases its process-bound private CDP gateway at `/browser-control`; the pane's scoped `browser_connect` query discovers a checkout capability without receiving either private token (`hided/src/browser_control.rs`).
`/browser-control/action` accepts only browser open, close and select from that registered desktop process and routes them through Workspace prepare/read/commit, not the shell's unrestricted event dispatcher.
`hide browser` page commands reach a display through the same `/ws`: the CLI's scoped request `browser_relay` names a display, hided runs `browser_connect`'s checkout and display checks, connects to the desktop gateway itself, and answers with the display id and whether it is its area's selected View, so the capability URL never reaches the CLI (`hided/src/browser_relay.rs`).
After the CLI claims its credential, the socket carries CDP text frames both ways until either side closes; the gateway's close code and reason reach the CLI unchanged.
A display shown only in the window of a node that dialed its core is relayed by that node's daemon instead: a caller on that node is answered a one-shot relay URL on its own daemon and dials it after the claim, and the core relays any other caller through the node's link ([A core on another machine](#a-core-on-another-machine)); every relay logs `relay.ended` with where its CDP ran and the bytes it carried.
The relay runs in the connection's own task, outside `Mutex<Runtime>`, and changes no core state or snapshot wire.
At most four relays run at once (`browser_relay_limit` past that), because each holds one of hided's eight client connections and half stay free for the shell and other clients.
A message is at most 4 MiB and a binary frame is refused, both closing with 4009; a relay closes with 4008 after 60 seconds without a CDP text frame in either direction (pings do not count) or after five minutes whatever it carries, and a peer that does not take a frame within five seconds ends it.
A close other than a normal one is logged as a `browser_relay` record with the display and the code.
A device pane reaches the same `/ws` through its node-link stream, so its commands take the same path and refusals as a local pane's.
The CDP client, the frame routing and the command flow live in `hided/src/browser_page/`, and the page-side code it evaluates in `hided/assets/browser/` ([BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md](BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md#agent-page-commands)).
New CDP targets name their existing View area and create a distinct display there; default CLI opens retain URL deduplication and active-area placement.
The native inventory carries each browser's area ID from the core-owned layout, including background Workspaces.
Positive browser area scopes ride the same revisioned projection, including empty areas, and require a currently connected catalog checkout.
The core preserves each area's incarnation only while its authority remains present; a revoke/regrant changes it even if snapshot coalescing hides the intervening absence.
The shell only forwards them; the native gateway permanently retires capabilities whose scope disappears or changes incarnation, distinct from closing a page in a still-authorized area.
No HTTP request dispatches a core event.
The first frame after a valid handshake is `daemon` (`version`, `pid`, `schema_version`, `host_name`, the state paths, the Herdr binary and socket, the idle policy); Settings > About reads it, and it never carries the token.
The daemon owns the core's three observation flags, the Settings one (`ai_settings.observing`, which runs the provider probe and the hook diagnosis), a start surface's (`ai_settings.start_observing`, which runs the provider probe alone) and the Sessions tool's (`request_view.observing`, which re-reads running checks, below): a client's hint is only that connection's demand (`hided/src/demand.rs`, one `ObservationDemand` per flag), each flag follows its first observer in and its last one out, and a connection that closes releases every demand, so a closed tab never leaves a probe or a re-read running.
While Sessions is observed, the local coordinator's GitHub step asks the runtime once a minute (`Runtime::reread_pending_checks`) to raise the generation of each project a row's live pull request has running checks in, so the existing reader reads only those again and a finished run moves its row without a new turn (PRD overview-request-view D-32); a node's project whose listed pull request has running checks is asked for the same way, through the node's read (`reread_node_github`); with no observer or no running checks nothing is asked.
The same step also hands the runtime's request to the advisory poll (`github_poll_reader`, a `BackgroundRead` of its own) and takes back only the paths it saw change (`Runtime::note_polled_changes`), which the runtime asks the existing reader for by the look rule; the poll holds no runtime, runs its conditional `gh api` reads one at a time off the lock (`hide_node_link::gh::conditional_read_arguments` is the only form the node runs for it), and its failures stay in the diagnostic log.
`hide` owns lifecycle: instance lock, `~/.hide/state/hided.json` mode 0600, default-browser open with `#token=`, idle exit ten minutes after the last client once the daemon's Herdr server no longer answers (see The daemon lives with its Herdr), and `hide serve --keep-alive`.
A stop ends the core before it releases the instance lock: dropping `RunningDaemon` joins the core's owner thread, whose `Core` drop joins the layout and state savers, so the last save is on disk before another daemon can start and before whoever owns the state folder can remove it.
The instance uses the platform's explicit-unlock guard, so an inherited file description from a concurrent fork cannot retain the role after its owner ends; another live instance is still refused immediately.
`hide connect`'s connect lock, the instance lock every role and the core a move starts take, and the state record all open the state folder through one opener (`hided/src/state_file.rs`), which makes it private before any store reads it (`private::own_dir`): a folder of this account that an earlier build left open is restricted and `state.made_private` is logged beside it, and one that is a link or another account's is refused with `state.not_private`, so no daemon starts on it and `hide connect` answers `start_failed` naming the folder; each record is written to the folder's `Logs/core.jsonl` at once (`diagnostics::record_now`), since the daemon's log is not open yet and a refused one never opens it, except that a refused link or another account's folder gets nothing written into it and its record goes to stderr only.
A folder of this account that it cannot open (mode 0000 or 0300, or an access list that shuts the account out) is refused with the fix (`chmod 700`, or full control on Windows), since its mode is changed only through an open descriptor; another account's folder that cannot be opened is still named as another account's.
Both the default and legacy connect locks use that same guard, preserving their existing blocking serialization while releasing ownership explicitly.
`hide connect` is `hide open` for a host that loads the shell itself: the same discovery (the live daemon the state file names when it is this build, else one started and waited for), answered as one JSON line (`ok`, `url`, `port`, `pid`, or `reason` `start_failed`/`no_response`/`other_build` with `detail`, `state_refused` with the `file`, `core_newer` and `update_failed` with the `machine`, `core` and `app` builds) instead of a browser; `hide status --json` is the attach-only probe and never starts a daemon.
Daemon startup gets a bounded ten-second health wait, and the desktop host allows up to 25 seconds for the whole discovery command before showing a retryable failure.
The daemon leads its own process group, so an interrupt to the terminal job or host that ran the CLI never reaches it.
`hided` takes `OwnerWatch` before anything else, so a starter that passes an owner channel (only the web e2e fixture does) ends the daemon by ending itself; `hide connect` starts it through `spawn_owned`, which removes the channel's key, so the daemon an app started outlives the app.
Neither the CLI nor the daemon starts a Herdr server; only the packaged desktop host does (see The bundled Herdr runtime).
A release `hided` carries `web/dist` inside the binary (`hided/build.rs`); a debug build reads the directory from disk, so `pnpm build` shows up without a cargo rebuild.
The node's terminal service spawns `herdr terminal session control` for every pane attach and refuses without a binary, so `hided` resolves one (`Env::herdr_program`): `HERDR_BIN_PATH`, the variable Herdr sets in every pane it manages and the app hands its children, then the pinned copy the account's helper holds, found by the account's layout whichever build of `hided` asks (`hide_kit::device_herdr::pinned_for_account`, `~/.hide/host-helper/herdr/current/herdr`: on a device, and on a machine a move put the core on, the Herdr its helper ran), then the `herdr` the account's own terminal finds (`hide_platform::programs`: the login shell's `PATH`, this process's `PATH`, the usual install folders), the order a device's node takes for its terminals; with none it logs `herdr_bin.missing` and no pane terminal can attach.
An SSH exec and a login item start `hided` with the system's folders alone on `PATH` (sshd's and launchd's `/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin`), so a `herdr` searched on that `PATH` alone was missing on a machine whose Herdr ran from `~/.local/bin` or Homebrew, and a move's checks there said it had none (`hided/tests/it/core_move_checks.rs`).
The pinned copy comes before the account's own because a core moved onto a device ran the operator's older `~/.local/bin/herdr` (0.9.1) against the server its helper had handed to the pinned copy (0.9.5): it was found first on `PATH`, and the helper's copy was found only for a program running from a helper build folder.
The search runs where a daemon is about to rely on the binary (its start, a move's checks there, the core's login item), off the async runtime, never on every `hide` command, since it can start the login shell.
A `HERDR_BIN_PATH` that names nothing executable refuses the daemon before it binds, and `hide connect` refuses the same value as `start_failed` before spawning one: Herdr hands every pane the path its server started from, that path dies when the app bundle is replaced under a running server, and a daemon that came up on it would answer healthy and then fail every pane attach one by one (`env::herdr_bin_error`).
The web shell holds no UI authority: it draws the snapshot, writes the hub's terminal frames straight into xterm.js, and sends one event per operator action.
Each terminal instance owns its one WebGL addon and context-loss subscription through `web/src/terminalRenderer.ts`, including while parked.
The pinned addon's own transient-restoration window runs first; persistent loss or failed activation disposes the addon and leaves the same Terminal, buffer, selection and grid on xterm's normal renderer.
Disposal removes the subscription before the addon and precedes terminal destruction; fallback starts no retry, attach, resize or additional work per key or output chunk.
With `probe=1` in the page URL it also installs `window.__hideProbe`, the only way to read the WebGL-drawn terminal from Playwright or a CDP driver; without the query the writer path is the plain `term.write`.

### The daemon lives with its Herdr

`hided` stays up for as long as the Herdr server it follows answers, with or without a window or phone, so labels keep being made when the app is closed (PRD labels-in-hided D-18).
The idle exit still counts ten minutes (`idle_secs`), but only while no client is connected and the Herdr server does not answer a `ping`; the daemon probes it every 30 seconds with a two-second timeout, and any answer from Herdr, even a refusal, counts as reachable (`server::herdr_reachable`).
A Herdr restart or a live handoff, which brings the server back inside the window, never ends the daemon.
A `hided` started without a Herdr socket, and `--keep-alive` and the paired-phone rule, behave as before.
`SIGTERM` still ends the daemon gracefully, which cancels the running label analysis and its provider process before it exits.

A build is the SHA-256 of the `hided` executable's bytes (`hided/src/build_id.rs`), hashed once when the daemon starts and reported as `build` on `/health`.
`hide connect` hashes the `hided` shipped beside it and compares: the same build is attached to as it is, and a live daemon of another build is stopped (`SIGTERM`, five seconds, then `SIGKILL`) and replaced by a new one, so the window and its daemon are always one build after an app update (D-19).
Before it looks at the daemon, a bundled connect also moves a Herdr server another Hide app started onto its own app's Herdr, as The bundled Herdr runtime says, so the daemon it starts or keeps follows a server of the same app.
The state file records the daemon's process start beside its pid (`pid_started`, from `hide_platform::process::start_time`), and `hide connect` and `hide stop` act on the pid only while that start still matches: a pid another process has reused, another account's included, reads as gone and is never signalled, and its stale state is cleared; a state written by an earlier build has no start and is judged by liveness alone.
Each `hide` command checks the environment keys it reads, and the sets live in code (`hided/src/env.rs`), not in the command's body.
The daemon and every command that acts on its whole configuration check every registered key and report all the invalid ones at once.
`hide stop` and `hide status --json` read only the state folder keys (`STATE_FOLDER_KEYS`: `HOME`, `HIDE_STATE_DIR`, `XDG_STATE_HOME`), and `hide status` also `HIDE_IDLE_SECS` (`STATUS_KEYS`), the idle time it prints when `/health` gives none.
An invalid key outside a command's set, such as an opener or a port, therefore never keeps a running daemon from being asked about or stopped.
An invalid key inside the set refuses with that key's name and exit status 2; it is never replaced by a default, since a default state folder would name another daemon.
`hided/tests/it/connect_stop.rs` holds each of these from the command's exit status and output.
A daemon that will not stop is a `start_failed` answer, never a silent attach to the old one, and the replacement is logged as `daemon.replacing` or `daemon.replace_failed`.
Only a desktop package's own `hide`, in macOS `Contents/Resources` or Windows/Linux `resources` (`hide_kit::bundled_kit_dir`), replaces a daemon: any other `hide`, a development build or a copy, answers `other_build` with both builds named and attaches to nothing, because the installed app's daemon is the operator's (`daemon.other_build_refused`).
Only the daemon of the state folder the CLI was given is looked at, so a development or e2e daemon elsewhere is never replaced, and stopping a daemon leaves Herdr, its panes and their agents running.
Each connect holds `connect.lock` in the state folder from looking at the daemon until the one it attaches to answers, so two connects never both replace it; a stop removes `hided.json` only while it still names the stopped pid and never unlinks `hided.lock`, so a daemon started since keeps its state and its instance lock.
A daemon that has ended counts as stopped before its starter reaps it, although Linux still lists an unreaped process's start time.
Before any of that, a connect whose state folder is the default `~/.hide/state` moves the legacy default `~/.local/state/hide` into place once (PRD hide-home-layout D-05; `hided/src/state_move.rs`): under the legacy folder's own `connect.lock`, which an older `hide connect` takes too, it stops the daemon the legacy `hided.json` names, then renames the whole folder in one `rename(2)`, so there is never a copy in two places.
That pid is signalled only once it is proven to be the daemon: a state that recorded its daemon's process start is proven by the kernel's answer for that pid, as `hide stop` judges it, so a daemon too busy to answer `/health` within its one-second request is still stopped and the folder still moves (issue 863); a state an earlier build wrote without a start (before v0.3.0) is proven only by `/health` answering as that pid, because liveness alone also passes a reused pid.
The folder moves only while its `hided.lock` is held across the rename: after stopping a proven daemon, the connect waits at most two seconds for that lock, because macOS can stop reporting a dying process's identity before its descriptors close.
The platform lock helper owns this bounded wait and the acquired lock; an unproven holder is still refused immediately and never signalled, and a lock that stays held answers `start_failed` without moving anything.
A legacy folder that is a link or not this account's is not moved, one other accounts can write to is refused with the `chmod go-w` that fixes it (only when it would move; beside an existing new folder it is just left), the moved folder is made 0700, `~/.hide` must be a private folder of this account, and a daemon that will not stop leaves both folders as they were and answers `start_failed`.
`HIDE_STATE_DIR` naming `~/.hide/state` itself counts as the default, since that is the value `hide connect` hands the daemon it starts.
When `~/.hide/state` already exists nothing is merged: the new folder is used, and each daemon start logs `state.legacy_left` with both paths while the legacy folder remains.
A folder `HIDE_STATE_DIR` or `XDG_STATE_HOME` chose is never moved (D-04).

With no window attached the daemon does only the work that keeps labels current (D-20, B29).
`hided` counts web and desktop renderers, tells the core `ui_attached` when the first arrives and when the last leaves (under one lock, so two windows arriving together cannot leave the core told the opposite of the final count), and refuses `ui_attached` from a client, because a window that sent it would pause the readers every other window draws.
While none is attached the session-sync coordinator rests the readers that only feed what a window draws: foreground processes, provider usage, ports, worktrees, GitHub and disk, and the Background AI probe.
Label work, which runs on the coordinator's own wakes, goes on, and the root follower skips its snapshot reads while no client is connected and catches up with one read when the next client arrives (`RootFollower::resume`).
The readers resume on the coordinator's next wake after a window attaches.
Local hook diagnosis belongs to the existing kit pump, not the session coordinator: one startup read, another after a successful apply, and a refresh due one second after completion while a window observes Settings.
The pump serializes diagnosis and installation on its existing worker, keeps one next-read deadline instead of a tick queue, and publishes only changed answers after releasing Runtime.
Core shutdown stops pump admission before closing its fixed node, rejects replies from that retiring owner and joins the pump outside Runtime.
`hided/tests/it/connect_build.rs` and `hided/tests/it/handshake.rs` (`the_daemon_outlives_the_idle_window_while_its_herdr_answers`, `the_daemon_exits_after_the_idle_window_when_its_herdr_is_gone`, `a_window_cannot_say_whether_windows_are_attached`) own these rules.

### The core's node id

The core names its own machine by a node id, never by the literal `local` (PRD core-host-node D-21; `herdr-core/src/node.rs`).
The id is the machine's own id, `hide_platform::host::machine_id`, lowercased and checked to `[0-9a-z-]{1,64}`, read once when `hided` starts (`NodeId::of_this_machine`) and handed to the core in `CoreOptions::node_id`.
It is the id of the navigator's own device row, whose `kind: "local"` stays as its role, and the `device_id` of this machine's registrations, Workspaces, letters, labels and Workspace views, the device half of every project id (`hide_project::project_id`), and the device half of a folder project's Herdr owner mark.
An event or a `hide` command that names no device still means the core's own node, so the wire keeps its shape and only the value of this machine's `device_id` changed; a `device_id` of `local` now names an unknown device, and `register_device` refuses both `local` and the node's own id.
The web shell reads the id from the navigator's own device row (`localDeviceId` in `web/src/snapshot.ts`); before the first snapshot names it the id is empty, which an event carries as no `device_id`.
A device's agent rows arrive inside that device's status without a `device_id`, so the store stamps each with its status's `target_id` as a frame lands (`stampDeviceAgents` in `web/src/snapshot.ts`); every reader that names a row's session (a pane head's record button, a 태스크 row, `revealTask`) then finds the device on the row, because the session's ledger row is kept under the device id and a session named with the core's own node id for a device's pane finds no record.
The desktop host learns it from the shell's browser sync (`node`) and keys the shared loopback partition and the pages it loads directly on it.
A Herdr workspace whose `hide_owner` mark was written for `local` is still read as this node's folder (`OwnerOpen::on_node`), so a folder project opened before the change keeps its Workspace.

A state folder written under `local` is converted before the core starts (`herdr_core::node_migration::convert`, called from `hided`'s `start_daemon` after it holds the instance lock).
`core-state.json`, `workspace-views.json`, `labels.json` and `delivery-ledger.json` are rewritten whole through a temporary file and a rename; Project Memory and the link record (`links.sqlite3`) re-key their project ids, and the link record its device names and records each replaced project id for the old session search file's retention (`retention_aliases`), in one SQLite transaction each; the old session search file is never written.
The originals are copied under `node-migration-backup/<unix ms>/` first; a start that resumes an unfinished conversion keeps them in that same folder and copies each store once, so retries add no copies, and recording the same replaced ids again changes nothing.
`node.json` is written last, so a start interrupted before it converts again, and a start of an already converted folder writes nothing.
A store that cannot be converted stops the daemon with the file and the reason, written to the Logs file at once with the daemon's pid; `hide connect`, which sees its daemon exit before answering, reads that record and answers `state_refused` with the file, which the desktop host's failure page shows under the reason. A JSON file its own loader cannot read is left to that loader, which already reports it.
A store whose loader has a size limit (the delivery ledger's 16 MiB) is checked before anything is written: one the longer node id would push past that limit stops the start with every file as it was, and one already past it is left to its loader.
The project pairs the session search index is re-keyed with come from the node's Projects in Project Memory and the folders registered under either name, so a start retried after a later store failed still finds them; a row whose new key is already taken is a stale copy and is dropped and counted.
`node.json` records the node that owns every unqualified key in the folder (a pane id, a tab id, a checkout path), whichever machine runs the core.
A folder that names another node is refused today only because nothing yet reads another node's state; a core that does extends this rule without changing the file.
`KEYS` in `node_migration.rs` lists every machine-bound key a store written before this change can hold, with how it is covered, rewritten or owned through `node.json`; that set is closed, since a store written since names the node id or holds unqualified keys `node.json` covers.
Its contract test holds a value at every key the table names and fails when a rewritten key still names `local`, when an owned key changes, or when a converted file still carries `"local"` anywhere.
`UNBOUND` beside it names the rest of a state folder, which holds no machine key, and a test fails on any file in a converted folder that neither list names.
Project ids hash the node id with the root, so a Memory receipt a transcript carries from before the conversion no longer matches a project id and is not honoured.

### The core and its nodes

The core decides and keeps state; it never touches a machine (PRD core-host-node D-21).
Every file, process, socket and session-file read is a node's, asked through one contract, `NodeLink` (`hide-node-link`), addressed by node id (`Runtime::node_link`): the core's own node answers today, and a device's helper answers for its machine.
The contract crate sits below both sides, so the core holds only the trait and the wire types, and the machine side (`hide-node`, over `hide-host`) never depends on the core.
The core's own node is `hide_node::Local`, which answers in hided's process through `hide_host::serve`; the core gets it in `Core::create`, with the Herdr connector the same node opens.
Closing it refuses new calls and wakes pending callers with `Unknown` through their existing one-answer channels; it neither polls nor starts a cancellation thread.
There are at most 64 admitted machine calls, and a timed-out or closed caller does not free its slot until the actual work ends.
The weak answer-sender list is pruned on admission and bounded by those same slots; closing one node affects its clones, never another node.

| The core's | The node's |
| --- | --- |
| Every decision, the runtime and its snapshot | Files and folders: lists, bytes, saves, stamps, path facts (`Call::PathFacts`), new projects (`Call::ProjectCreate`), the project guardrails' policy file (`Call::GuardrailPolicy`) |
| Its own stores: UI state, saved Views, the delivery ledger, labels, the link record, local issues, saved GitHub answers, its log, and their one-time node-id conversion | Processes: git, `gh`, the AI CLIs, ports, agent sleep's process groups, the file opener (`hide-node/src/opener.rs`) |
| Which node a call goes to | The Herdr socket (the connector `Core::create` takes), session files, the install kit, pane credential proofs and the bootstrap socket (`hide-node/src/pane_proof.rs` here, `hide-host/src/panes.rs` on a device) |

`scripts/check-core-touches-no-machine.py` holds the line: production code under `herdr-core/src`, or under `hide-factory/src`, the Factory engine the core runs, that opens a file, starts a process, opens a socket or reads a session file fails it, unless the file is one of the core's stores, listed with its reason, or something a later layer of the PRD moves out, listed with that layer; each listed file carries how many machine touches it keeps, so a new one in a listed file fails too.
The SSH transport is the node's (`hide-node/src/ssh/`).
The core reaches a device only through `hide_node_link::device::{DeviceConnector, DialedTransport}` for a device it dials and `DeviceTransport` with an `Arrived` node for a node that dials it (`hided` builds the connector and hands over each arrival, `Core::create` takes the connector) and keeps the decoding of what a device's Herdr sends (`remote.rs`); the check bans `russh` by name.
No later layer is listed any more: layer 3 moved the terminal attach child and the clipboard image's removal to the node, and layer 4 moved the label generator's lock (`Call::LabelLock`); an entry that no longer reaches a machine fails the check, so none outlives its layer.
The Factory decides on the core's machine and its machine work is the node's of the machine that holds its project (D-01, core-host-node-move Q17): the core's own, or a node that dials the core while its link is up (`Factory::node`, `herdr-core/src/factory.rs` `CoreMachines`), never a device the core dials. One `Call::Factory` (`hide_node_link::factory`) carries its git and `gh` commands, each a typed shape the node turns into one fixed command line so a request names values and never arguments, the project's quick check, its verify bundles, which the node queues one at a time and ends with the engine (`hide-host/src/factory.rs`), the project files its probe and review judgments read, a Task's attached PRD, a worker's worktree removal, and the memory, `hide` program and time zone offset reads (the summary's done-today day is the core machine's local day); free space and installed runtimes reuse `Call::VolumeFree` and `Call::AgentInstalled`.
Its `gh` commands always run on the core's own machine, whose sign-in does the core's GitHub work, so a node answers every `Call::Factory` but `gh` (`FactoryCall::answered_by_node`, the node role's `Services::factory`).
A Factory whose node is not linked waits as for a Herdr that does not answer (`EnvSignal::NodeLink`): a start, a merge or a worktree removal is asked again on the next tick, a verify poll stays pending, and a run the link took with it answers `VerifyOutcome::Lost`, which sends the Task back to waiting and verifies it again rather than keeping it pending.
What an answer means for a Task stays the Factory's (`hide-factory/src/project.rs`), and its own store and the AI choice are core stores.
hided runs both roles in one process, and its core role keeps two readers of its own.
The Explorer watcher (`watch.rs`) asks a node by id, so a second node serves an Explorer on its machine without the watcher changing.
The credential registry (`pane_auth.rs`) reads a local pane's kernel proof through `hide_node::pane_proof` in this process; a device pane's proof arrives over that device's link (`hided/src/node_panes.rs`), and so does the proof of a pane on a screen machine whose core runs elsewhere (below).
The file opener's launcher is the node's (`hide-node/src/opener.rs`), but its safety rule, which refuses an executable or a file a handler would run, stays with the open handler in `server.rs` (`openable`); that handler is unwired on `main`, and the change that wires it moves the rule beside the launcher.

### A core on another machine

Layer 4 of the core-host-node PRD lets the machine with the screen run its daemon without a core, attached to the core on another machine (PRD core-host-node-remote-core).
Nothing the operator does writes the record that turns it on yet: the move of layer 5 and the test fixtures write it, so a machine without it runs exactly as before.
Below, M is the screen's machine and C the core's.

One program takes three roles.

| Role | Runs | Started by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Core and node | The core, its own node, the screens on loopback: every machine without a placement record | `hide connect` (`start_daemon`) |
| Node | No core: the node's link to C, M's screens on loopback, M's terminals and file reads (`hided/src/node_role.rs`, `node_daemon.rs`) | `hide connect`, when `<state>/core-placement.json` names C (`start_node_daemon`) |
| Attach | No core: pipes the node's SSH channel to C's running core, byte for byte (`hided/src/attach.rs`) | C's sshd, as `hided attach` on the channel M opens |

The placement record (`hided/src/placement.rs`) names C's SSH alias in M's `~/.ssh/config`, C's node id, the `hided` C runs as its attach role, and C's state folder when it is not the default.
It is the account's private file, at most 4 KiB, and one that is anything else, names M's own node id, holds a control character or a value over 1 KiB, or names a program or state folder that is not absolute in the spelling between machines, is refused at start with a diagnostic rather than read; `start_daemon` refuses a state folder that has one, so the core role never starts beside it.

M dials, C never does (D-04).
M's node opens one SSH connection to C with the device transport of layer 2b in the other direction, known hosts and keepalive included, and runs `hided attach` on an exec channel; it reads the alias from `~/.ssh/config` again on every attempt, so a fix the operator makes reaches the next dial.
The attach role finds C's core through the attach socket the core records in its state folder (`node-attach-socket`, in a fresh owner-only folder; the accept checks the peer is this account's process, `LocalStream::peer_is_this_account`), and, when it reaches none, answers why and exits, so a node never starts a core (D-07): `no_core` when no core recorded its socket, `core_not_answering` when the recorded core is gone or silent, and `record_untrusted` when the record or its folder is not this account's own and private, which it never follows; the node reports the answer as the reason it waits.
An accept on that socket that fails is logged (`attach.accept_failed`) and tried again after a wait that doubles from 100 ms to 5 s, so the service never ends while its record still names it, and the socket's folder goes with the listener when the core stops.
On the socket the core and the node trade one line each: the core names its node, build, node-link protocol, release and machine name, the node its own node id, build, release, protocol and Herdr socket.
A build is named by its hash, which says only whether two builds are the same; its release (`hided/src/build_order.rs`: the version it ships and its first-parent commit count on main, which `desktop/scripts/package.mjs` passes as `HIDE_BUILD_ORDER`: the count of the last commit it shares with main, so a build packaged off a branch is ordered by where it left main and never past a later main build by its own commits; a shallow checkout names the order or is refused, and CI's `0.0.0-*` builds, which no release compares, name 0) orders two builds by `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` and then that count, and two builds neither key orders, or whose version names no release, are unordered (PRD core-host-node-move D-10, Q3).
The node decides before it says hello and logs both builds when it does not link (`link.build_refused`): only the same build links, a node older than C waits as `core_newer` and C is left as it is (B11), a newer one updates C to its own build first (see [Updating the core](#updating-the-core)), and an unordered one waits as `other_build`.
