C keeps the same rule as its own defense: an older node is refused as `core_newer` and any other build as `other_build`, as are C's own node id, a device C dials, and a node that would be the seventeenth C keeps registered (`nodes_full`); otherwise the core hands back a relay grant and the stream becomes the node's link, the same `RemoteHost` a dialed device's channel builds, marked `dialed_by_node`.
A node whose earlier link still stands is greeted on that link first: when it answers within 3 s the new one is refused as `already_linked`, and when it does not the earlier link is ended and the new one taken, so a node whose connection went half open in a network change is back at once rather than after the attach role's 30 s silence (B9).
A node is attached by one link at a time: from its admission until its link is taken or refused, a second attach of the same node is refused as `already_linked`, so two links racing past the greeting can never both stand.
A grant never bound to a link expires after 60 s, and a bound one goes with its link.
The core registers the node as an inbound device, by its node id, named by its host name as plain text of at most 64 characters (its id when that is empty): it is never dialed and no helper is uploaded to it, its kit is kept through its link as a device's is, from the app it runs ([The install kit](#the-install-kit)), Retry is refused because it reconnects itself, and Remove ends its link, which takes its screens and every credential with it and leaves its kit; it registers again on its next attach.
Each of those answers is a method of the registration's `LinkOrigin` (`Dialed { ssh_alias }` or `Inbound`), which the store keeps in its old spelling (`ssh_alias`, or `inbound: true`); a stored registration with both or neither is dropped with a diagnostic and the rest of the store loads.
The attach role ends with its channel, with the core's socket, or after 30 s without a byte from its node (three missed heartbeats), so a half-open connection or a stopped core leaves nothing running on C (B20).

What crosses the link is the node contract of the other layers, in the other direction (NodeLink protocol 43 adds `opencode_fork`, which forks an OpenCode session for a pane to open, by 1.x's own `--fork` or through 2.x's server, after 42 replaces `antigravity_trust` with `agent_trust`, which names the agent and records or takes out Claude Code's, Codex's or agy's trust of a worktree Hide made, after 41 makes an agent row name its mark (`mark`) instead of a glyph, with four mark counts, a wait on a pull request's checks and the `unfinished` status code, after 40's letter pull that carries `tool`, a hook's ask at a tool event for the letters due while the agent works, after 39's pull request card query that also reads the head commit's checks and reviews; 38 adds agy's three calls, `antigravity_fork_mark` and `antigravity_fork`, which find the conversation agy's `/fork` made, and `antigravity_trust`, which records or takes out agy's trust of a worktree Hide made, after 37's Explorer `copy` and `import`; 36 makes a Hide AI request carry the operator's output language with no default, after 35's `session_work_turn` in place of the missed-issue draft's `session_work_context`, the one turn a backlog item came from, with the volume's size on a disk row and the session search reads dropped, 34's project guardrails' policy write (`Call::GuardrailPolicy`), 33's `feedback` call for View feedback screenshots and letter attachments, 32's bounded session request and issue facts, 31's typed hook proof and answer, 30's credential-free usage fetch and 29's shell integration call).
A node on protocol 42 could not fork an OpenCode 2.x session, so it is refused at its Hello and its helper reinstalled, as every protocol step before it is (`hide-node-link/src/protocol.rs`, `PROTOCOL_VERSION`).
Within 28 a stream's `StreamOpen` may name the caller its node proved (`hide_node_link::panes::StreamCaller`), an optional field: a stream with no proof carries none and its commands are checked with `Call::PaneInspect`. Both ends of a link run one build (a node dials only its core's build, and a core uploads its own build to a device it dials), so the field never meets a peer that lacks it; its serde default only keeps a line without it readable.
C reaches M's Herdr through link streams (`Call::LinkOpen` naming Herdr as the end, `LinkWrite`, `LinkClose` down, `link_data` and `link_closed` up; the other end a stream names is M's browser relay, below), at most 32 Herdr streams per link with 64 chunks waiting each (a browser relay stream, whose chunks are a relay's CDP, 128), so C's session sync, controls, doorbell, find and phone reply reach M's Herdr with no socket file on C (D-18).
A stream C gives up (its reader fell 64 chunks behind, or its open went unanswered) keeps its place until its owner drops it, and the node is always told to close it, so the node's 32 slots never leak.
Link control (`Call::is_control`: a greeting, a pane's proof answer or stream, and the Herdr streams) has its own admission on C and its own four workers on M, beside the four machine calls, so a port scan or a disk walk never holds a Herdr write or a proof reply, whose 10 s wait would end the link.
M's pane proofs go up the link as a device's do, and so do its checkout callers: a process outside every pane whose kernel cwd is inside a checkout C lists for M is proven by M's kernel (`NodeEvent::CheckoutProof`), which C accepts only from a link a node dialed (amendment 6).
While the link is down M's pane bootstrap answers `hide_unavailable` at once, so a hook's `hide inbox` returns within its budget.

M's screens keep the `/ws` contract byte for byte (D-05).
For each screen M opens one relay to C through a local forward on its connection (`/relay?mode=screen`, admitted only by the link's grant header and ended with the link), and passes the screen's events up and C's snapshots, deltas and answers down unparsed.
Each relay is read on its own task whatever the screen takes: the SSH client stops reading the whole connection while one channel's buffer is full, so a screen left unread would hold up every other screen, the panes and the link.
A screen may leave at most 4 MiB of C's frames untaken (one frame is always held, since a file read sends 4 MiB at a time); past it the backlog is dropped, the relay closed with `screen.fell_behind`, and the screen closed with 1013 `screen_fell_behind`, so a read whose answer was dropped fails as the screen's socket ends rather than wait forever, and the screen reattaches from the revision it last applied (D-20, B17).
C's own refusal of a relayed screen (4001 to 4004: `client_limit`, `schema_mismatch` and the rest) reaches the screen with its code and reason, so the screen shows it as it would on C; any other end of the relay closes the screen with 1012 `core_link_lost`.
What a screen sends before its relay stands is dropped and counted as a held screen's is, never delivered late, and the handshake C is sent carries no screen token: M's token stays on M.
A node relays at most six screens (`MAX_RELAY_SCREENS`, two under C's eight-client cap), so C's own windows always keep a place; a seventh is refused with C's `client_limit`.
The answers C runs beside a linked node's screens (a device checkout's file reads and folder listings, and the Browser View sources M asks for) hold one of 64 slots per node while they run; past it a new one is refused with `relay_busy` and logged (`relay.requests_full`).
A file an M screen pastes or drops is staged on M (`hided/src/node_uploads.rs`): for one of M's panes C is told only its path on M (`terminal_attachment` with `staged_on`, accepted only from that node's relay) and pastes it as it pastes any device's, keeping its hold on the keys typed behind it; for any other pane M sends the upload on to C as the screen sent it and keeps no copy, after holding the batch to C's own caps (8 files, 40 MiB) before a byte crosses the link.
C reads M's machine as it reads its own wherever the result is M's: M's coordinator asks M's TCP listeners through the link every 5 s (`ports.rs`, `InboundLink`), and they are attributed to M's panes only, as C's own are to C's; an SSH device's listeners are not read, so its panes carry none.
The size of an M project the Overview shows is measured on M the same way (`disk.rs`, `device_disk_request`): M's coordinator measures the M projects the Projects tab watches (`disk_watch`) and the one a cleanup sheet asks for, and the rows go onto M's own project rows only, never matched against C's paths.
An M checkout's pull request is read on C with C's `gh` login (D-16): M's worktree read answers its repository's `origin` URL from the repository's own configuration, C names the GitHub repository from it (`gh::repository_of_remote`), and M's coordinator asks C's own node to run the same allow-listed `gh` command lines with `GH_REPO` set to that name and no folder (`Call::Gh` `repository`), each repository once and then every five minutes; the answers stay keyed to M, so an M path never matches a C project.
An M project lists every worktree M's worktree read reports, as one of C's own lists its worktrees, a worktree with no pane open included (`device_catalog::apply_worktrees`, D-29).
Such a row has the id of its folder on M (`device_catalog::checkout_id`), the id a move gives that checkout (`node_migration::reown`), so the links, labels and folds kept under it meet the row, and it folds under Inactive by C's own rule (`project_context::inactive_checkout_group`); a device C dials shows only the checkouts its Herdr session opens.
An M project keeps its issues (PRD core-host-node-move D-29, `Runtime::project_node_issues`).
A GitHub project's issues come from that same read on C, which also asks by name for the issues M's checkouts link that the open list leaves out, so no `gh` call is added per project.
A Local project's issues are C's `local-issues.json` under `devices` by M's node id, where a move writes them (`node_migration::reown`), so their numbers continue and the move back makes them M's own again.
A checkout's link is read by the chain a local checkout reads, from M's Herdr tokens (`RemoteSessionSnapshot.issue_tokens`, kept off the wire) and M's branch notes, which M's worktree read asks with one `Call::PathFacts` off the lock.
The projection runs when M's session or one of these answers moves, never on an idle fetch.
An M project's PRs tool lists the pull requests of that same read, as one of C's own does, and `pr_link_issue`, `pr_feedback_read` and `pr_delegate` take an M project as they take one of C's (`Runtime::issue_project`): the issue link is written on M, a new Local issue goes into M's slot of the store, `gh` reads and writes the pull request by the repository's name (`github::Where::Repository`), and the agent starts on M.
A failed read keeps M's previous answer, as a failed read of C's own does (`keep_previous_reads`).
A backlog add on an M project makes a memo in that store, or a title-only GitHub issue with `gh issue create` on C by the repository's name; linking a checkout, or starting an issue in a new M worktree, writes its branch note through M's link and its token through M's Herdr, never C's git.
A device C dials keeps its issues on that device: its projects carry no issue source, so no backlog, Start or link reaches them.
An M checkout in front is read as one of C's is (`Runtime::focused_node_checkout`): the card and the card's refresh ask M's repository again and re-read M's worktrees, and past the 64-repository limit M's repository in front is among those read (`node_github_repositories`, which also decides which M rows say they are reading).
An M project's worktree cleanup runs on M (`live::cleanup::CleanupPlace`): the review and the removal reach M's disk only through M's link, its in-use read asks M's Herdr and M's listening ports, with the checkouts' panes named as M's Herdr names them, and a squash merge's proof is read on C by the repository's name; M's per-row recheck refuses a worktree that changed after the review, and C's own disk is never touched at M's paths.
A Browser View's page shown in an M window is routed by M's own daemon: it asks C for the View's address on the relay port with the same grant (`/relay/browser-source`), and reaches a loopback page of C through the link's SSH connection ([BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md](BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md#a-screen-whose-core-runs-on-another-machine)).
Browser control of a page in an M window follows the same split (B4, B13, B15; `hided/src/node_browser.rs`): the window registers its CDP gateway with M's daemon, which tells C only the window's owner pid on the grant (`/relay/browser-control`), and C keeps it while the link lasts.
C keeps the authority: it checks the caller's credential, decides the scope, and asks M for the gateway's capability over the link (`Call::BrowserGateway`, a machine call).
A caller on M is handed M's own capability URLs, or for a page command a one-shot relay URL on M's daemon, which pumps the CDP to the gateway on M (`relay.ended` with `way: node`); a caller on C or a device whose only window is M's is relayed by C through a link stream whose end is M's browser relay (`way: link`), at most four per link.
Page actions the gateway asks for go from M's daemon to C (`/relay/browser-control/action`), and C runs them as that window.
When the link ends, M drops its tickets, ends its relays as a lost gateway and asks the gateway to revoke every capability (best effort, asked again before a `revoke.failed` diagnostic, the desktop's own lease the backstop), and C stops choosing M's windows ([BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md](BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md#a-screen-whose-core-runs-on-another-machine)).
Terminals take another way.
M's own panes' keys and output never leave M: keys go from M's screen to M's node terminals and output from them to M's hub, and C hears the input facts it always heard.
Every other pane's output comes down one terminals relay per node (`/relay?mode=terminals`) into M's hub, and their keys go up it.
The relay's tap holds at most 4 MiB of base64 output for its node, full frames included (`hided/src/relay.rs`, `RelayTap::output`), separate from the screen-relay backlog limit above.
A full frame replaces what of its own pane waits; output after it that would pass the cap drops the backlog, and a full frame that would pass it drops the waiting output of the panes that waited longest until it fits, both recorded as `relay.terminals_overflow`.
Each pane that lost output gets nothing more until a full frame, asked for when the relay next sends, so a node that reads nothing is asked no redraw meanwhile; a full frame that alone passes the cap is not relayed and asks none (`relay.terminals_frame_too_long`), and a pane's end is never dropped.
Each side writes its relay on a task of its own, so a send the other side is slow to take never stops it reading keys, output or the link's end; a key M took and did not send, or one for M's own pane while its link is down, reaches its screen as not sent.
While that relay is away (C restarted it, or the forward blinked) the keys wait on M in order, 64 KiB and 3 s each with one line of any size always taken, as keys wait for a pane that does not exist yet; a key past the bound is refused to its screen, and one that waited too long or was held for a link that ended is dropped (`terminals.held_dropped`), as is one whose write was cut off (`terminals.held_unsent`).
A screen hears of the keys it lost once per burst: one error frame with the count and the causes, counted per live screen connection rather than queued per line, so a burst larger than any queue loses no notice and a screen that left is only logged.
When C forgets a pane it tells M on the same relay, so M's hub and the screens' pane sizes let it go.
M's panes' output goes up the link only while C has a screen of its own looking (`TerminalControl::Mirror`).
A file read of M's checkout is served by M itself, under the roots C opened on M over the live link (`hide_host::serve::OpenedRoots`, confined by M's `Boundary`, whose roots follow each root C opens and empty when a link starts or ends, so no read sets them); any other path goes to C (B4).

A pane runs at the grid of the screen that last sent it input (D-11, `hided/src/pane_sizes.rs`).
Every screen of the core, its own windows and M's through their relays, says the grid it draws each pane at; input is a key, a mouse report or an attachment's paste, never a view, a scroll or a focus.
The core forwards a screen's `terminal_resize` only while that screen sizes the pane, draws every other screen's view of it at the grid it runs at, and on a switch sends the new grid's view and resize together; with one screen this is exactly the rule before it.
M forwards its screens' views to C with their other events, and tells C at most once a second per pane that a screen of M types into one of M's panes (`terminal_input`), since C never sees those keys.

M waits for C rather than run without it (D-08).
When the link ends M closes each screen once with 1012 `core_link_lost`; a screen that opens while the link is down is held, its frames dropped and counted, until the link is live and its relay's snapshot arrives, so the web shell's ordinary reconnect is the attempt that attaches and its keys typed meanwhile are never delivered later (B8).
A held screen is told only where the link stands, each time that changes: a `core_link` frame with the phase (`connecting`, `updating`, `waiting` or `disconnected`) and C's machine name when its hello gave it, which the window's strip shows (`Phase::link_frame`).
The operator can end the link from M's window (PRD core-host-node-move B16): a `core_link` event {action: `disconnect`} is taken by M's node daemon, never relayed to C, and M records `disconnected` in `core-placement.json` before it shuts the link, so a restart keeps it (`placement::update`, the one way this process changes a record already there, which the core update and the move's commit use too).
Ending the link is what revokes C's reach into M: C refuses M's pane input, file reads and pages as it does for any node whose link ended, and M dials nothing while the record says so.
A held screen's `core_link` {action: `reconnect`} is the one event M takes while it holds a screen: it clears the flag and dials at once, and a core update that failed gets this connection's attempt.
Dials back off from 2 s to 60 s, and the wait starts over after a link that lived 30 s.
A watch thread on M looks every 2 s for a gap of more than 5 s between the wall clock and the monotonic one (the machine slept) or a change in its network address set (`hide_platform::host::network_addresses`, link-local addresses left out, since macOS gives and takes them on its own): with the link up it pings C within 3 s and redials when no answer comes; with the link down it wakes the dial at once only when the last failure was one a move can change: a dial that never reached C's SSH server (`unreachable`), a connection that failed on the way after it answered (a reset or timeout in the key exchange, a channel that failed, a handshake cut off or unanswered), or a link that was lost; a node C refused, or one with a host key, sign-in or alias to fix, keeps its backoff.
While the last failure was `unreachable` and the port has not answered since, the watch also resolves C's name and reads its SSH banner within 1 s on each look, each resolved address with an even share of that second and the name resolved on a thread of its own so a resolver that hangs never holds the watch, and dials as soon as it answers; what the port said holds across the unreachable waits and dials of one outage, so a port silent in one wait and answering at the next wait's first look still wakes M, and one that answered is not probed again until the outage ends or the machine moves, so M is back within seconds of SSH answering again, whatever the backoff had reached (B9).
`hide connect` in the node role first tells the node a window connects (`POST /core-link/connect`, with the daemon's token), which gives a core update that failed on the last connection this one's attempt, then waits up to 20 s for the first link outcome: it attaches the window to a live link, a link down, or an update under way (`updating`), and answers `other_build` for a build C refused, so the desktop host shows the failure it shows for any daemon of another build (B18), and `core_newer` or `update_failed` with C's machine name and both builds (`machine`, `core`, `app`, each the version and the commit's first seven characters, as the node's `/health` names them in `builds`), so the host's page asks for this app to be updated or says the update failed, and names both builds (B10, B11).

The label generator's lock and the human notice follow the machine.
Each label worker asks the node of the machine that runs its Herdr server for the lock (`Call::LabelLock`, see [Agent labels in the core](#agent-labels-in-the-core)), so C labeling M's Herdr takes the lock beside M's socket, held while the link lives.
The human notice's Herdr toast goes to the first connected node that dialed in, the machine the operator works at, and to C's own Herdr when none is linked ([delivery.md](delivery.md)).

| Crosses between M and C (control) | Stays on M (data) |
| --- | --- |
| Each M screen's events, C's snapshots, deltas and answers, through that screen's relay | Keys into M's panes and their output, and files an M screen pastes or drops into them |
| A Browser View's address, and a page of C's loopback, through the link's SSH connection | Pages of M's own addresses |
| The browser authority: a caller's credential check, the scope C decided, M's window's owner pid, and the gateway's page actions; the M-loopback capability URLs it passes on to a caller on M; the CDP of a caller off M, through a browser relay stream | The gateway's registration (its address and control token), and the CDP of a caller on M |
| Output of C's and other devices' panes, and keys into them, through the terminals relay | Bytes of files in M's checkouts under the roots C opened on M |
| M's Herdr streams, pane and checkout proofs, input facts and the label lock, inside the link | M's screens' tokens and the desktop host's discovery (`hide connect`) |
| An M project's issues: the GitHub read and `gh issue create` by repository name with C's login, its Local issues in C's store under M's id, and the branch notes and Herdr tokens C reads through the link and writes there to link a checkout | A checkout's branch note and Workspace token, kept in M's repository and M's Herdr |
| An M project's worktree cleanup: the review, the confirmation and the merge proof C reads by repository name; each Git, folder and removal call and the in-use read go through the link and M's Herdr | The worktrees, their files and the removed folders' trash, on M's disk |
| M's panes' output, only while a screen of C looks at them | |
| A core move's steps, over SSH from the machine giving the core: the checks (`hided core-move inspect`), the receive, verify, place and start on the machine taking it, the retirement; and the first link, whose hello carries the move's intent and commits it (`hided/src/core_move/`) | The move's record (`core-move.json`) and its staging copy, on the machine that drives it |
| The brain state's copy, once: staged, its owner changed, sent by SFTP into the taking machine's `move-incoming/` and checked against the sent hashes before it is placed (`node_migration::copy`) | What a machine held before its core moved away (`moved-out/`), on that machine |
| A core update's build, uploaded by SSH from the newer node or app into a version folder beside the core's, and `hided core-update` run there (`Upstream::install_build`) | |
| Project Memory read and written by an agent hook on M: the `memory` request through M's node to the one store C keeps, and the receipts C's label reads find in M's sessions (`herdr_core::memory_hook`, `Runtime::linked_node`) | M's session files, which M's node reads for C (`ProjectSessions`, `SessionConversation`, `SessionText`) |
| A held letter: C keeps it while M is not linked and its wait counts only while M is (`delivery.md`), and hands it to M's agent through M's node when the agent's hook pulls | |
| The login item: installed, started and removed by `hided core-move` on the machine taking or giving the core, in that account's GUI session (`hided::login_item`, `UserAgents`) | |

### Moving the core

Before a move changes anything, the source checks both machines and names every check that fails, in one answer (PRD core-host-node-move B3; `hided/src/supervisor.rs`, `prepare`).
On this machine: the device is one this core dials and is connected, no other node is linked to this core, its Herdr server runs, Hide AI is set up and on (`ai_setup`, a check of the machine whose core moves, as on a move back, because that setting moves with the core; the window names that machine, not the one taking the core), and no agent of this machine is asleep with its pane closed, since a node cannot wake it (`dormant`).
On the machine taking the core, through the same `hided core-move inspect` the move already runs there over SSH (`hided/src/core_move/target.rs`, `preflight.rs`): it is the same device, it runs this build of `hided`, its Herdr server runs, `gh` is signed in, someone is logged in to its desktop (the session the core's login item runs in), it does not sleep by itself on power, its state folder holds no core and no other move (`target_state`), and the copy can reach it: the machine answers the folder by its resolved path (`reachable`), so a link on the way to it, as `/tmp` is on macOS or `/home` on a Linux whose homes are elsewhere, is no refusal, and what still cannot be reached fails its own check (`target_folder`, `path_refused`: a file on the way, a link that leads nowhere, the state folder itself being a link, which the core's private folder check refuses at its start so only the resolved copy path would be accepted, or a folder that does not belong to the account, so the moved core could not make it private); the folder is judged before the handover record is read (`Folder::look_at`), so an unreadable folder fails this check and not the connection, its Hide AI settings are absent or the same as this machine's, and every agent this machine's Hide AI would ask answers there with that machine's own logins.
The AI check runs on the target, not as a request over the device link, because a device's node answers no AI, GitHub or Factory request by design (`Call::answered_by_device`).
The checks that read the account's logins (`gh` and Hide AI's agents) run the way the moved core will: `inspect` starts `hided core-move check` as a one-shot job in the account's GUI session with the core login item's environment (`UserAgents::start_once`, loaded from a list outside the login agents folder and never restarted) and reads its answer, because a login kept in the login keychain can answer otherwise to an SSH session; with no GUI session no job runs, and the failing GUI session check asks for the login the retry needs.
Nothing on the node link changed shape for the move, so its protocol stays; the attach socket's `Release` line and the node hello's optional `move_intent` are guarded by the attach build refusal and the move's build check.
A failing check says what it found and hide changes none of it: it does not sign in, start a session or change power settings.
A Project's choices (`ui_state.project_settings`: guardrails, repository, issue source, new-work place, base branch) are kept at the digest of the owning machine's id and the project's root and name that id, so the owner change re-keys them with the machines: the records of the machine taking the core become the core's own and the old owner's become a device's, each record naming its new id and moving to the digest of it (`Mapper::project_settings`), and a record of a third machine stays; a record without a root, or two that would land on one key, stops the move with its place, as a key the table does not cover does.
Project Memory (`project-memory.sqlite3`) is re-keyed the same way, by the layer-1 conversion's own operation (`MemoryStore::convert_device`, run on the staged copy by `reown_memory`): a machine the move gives another id takes its Projects, and every card, receipt and cursor row that names them, to the id and the digest the new core reads them under, and a Project already stored under the id a move would give stops the move with the store named, so a Project's Memory is never left beside an empty one the new core starts.
The other stores a move carries need no re-key for a Project or device id: the usage ledger keeps native machine identities, which a move leaves as they are; the session search file is only read and its retention follows the link record's keys; phone pairing, `mobile.json`, the Factory's files and Hide AI's `ai.json` hold neither; and the GitHub snapshot is not copied, since the new core rebuilds it.
The names the operator gave tabs of the core machine's Herdr (`operator_tab_names`) are tab ids and move as every tab id does.
`node_migration`'s contract test holds every field the core saves to the table: a field of `core-state.json` is a row of `KEYS` or is listed there as naming no machine, so a field added without that decision fails the test instead of stopping an operator's move, and the move test holds a value of every shape at every row, project settings included, through the move and the move back.
The machine taking the core keeps the name it was registered under: the owner change drops its registration, since a core does not register its own machine, and keeps that registration's label as `core-state.json`'s `machine_label` (`node_migration::reown`), which the core's own device row carries as its `label`, so a window on another machine names it as it did before the move; a move back to a machine that dialed in, registered only by its host name, keeps none.
When the core moves on from that machine to a third one, the third registers it by that name before its host name (`MoveSource::own_label`), so the name follows the machine across every move.
Hide AI's settings move with the core: the copy carries `ai.json`, which lives outside the state folder, and `herdr-core/src/node_migration/copy.rs` places it, takes it back on a rollback and sets it aside after the commit with the brain stores, never merging it with settings the other machine kept; a move back compares the two machines' settings the same way, and checks on this machine, with its own logins, every agent the core's settings ask (`hide_ai_asks_of_stored`, failing as `ai`), as a forward move asks of its target; settings the core's machine lacks fail as `ai_setup`.
A file name the other machine gives, in the manifest a move back pulls or in the list of files a verify asks for again, becomes a path on this machine, so it is read only as a path below the copy under a store the copy carries (`copy::carried`): a manifest that names anything else is unreadable, a verify answer that names a file the copy does not hold is refused, and both roll the move back before a file is read or written outside the copy.
The copy is loaded with this build's readers before it leaves the machine giving the core (`driver::stage`, as a move back does on the copy it pulled) and again on the machine taking it before it is placed (`copy::check_loadable`).
The delivery ledger is held to its own reader there, because the core refuses a ledger it cannot validate whole and never repairs it, and would otherwise start with every letter, watch, spawn record and registration unavailable.
A failure at either check names the file and rolls the move back with the source core's folder as it was.
The owner change reads a spawn's `machine` against its caller's machine after the move, because the field names the machine of the child only when that is not the caller's own (`node_migration::reown`, `delivery.md`).

A window keeps its address through a move and reattaches by the web shell's ordinary reconnect (B4, W1).
The supervisor mounts the move's screen on the seat before the core or node it replaces stops (`hided/src/core_move/screen.rs`), and the role that ends closes each of its sockets with 1012 (`role_ended` from the core, through `AppState::role_ended`), so the window reconnects to the move's screen, which answers `/health` with `role: "moving"` and sends the move's `core_move` frames; once the next role is mounted the move's screen closes its sockets with 1012 `core_moved` and the window reconnects to that role.
The core's sockets close with the role, not with the core's change notifier, because each socket's own state holds the core and so kept the notifier open: a window left on a core that had stopped heard the move through `done` and nothing after it.
A role's end and the process's stop request are each level-triggered (`hided/src/ending.rs`), because the process outlives its roles: the supervisor is often busy in a move's step when `hide stop` or the idle clock speaks, and a `Notify` dropped what no task was waiting for, so the stop was lost and the old role's listeners and their core outlived the role.
The signal handlers are installed once, before the first role runs, and every wait of the supervisor (a check, a wait on the peer, the first link) and every long step of a move selects on the stop; a move's step a stop leaves is resumed from the journal at the next start, as after a kill, and `hided` waits at most a second at its exit for blocking work a stop left behind.
A move resumed at a start asks its peer only from its waits: the peer's SSH alias is read at the first step that reaches it (`driver::Remote`), so an alias that cannot be read then is the peer not reached, which the wait asks again, while the role the journal's phase names (the node, from its placement) runs.

A core started on a move's copy is pending until the link that carries the move's intent commits it (PRD core-host-node-move amendment 3).
Until then the move may still be rolled back, and the core it replaced starts again on the folder the copy came from, so anything the pending core did outside its machine would be done twice or lost.
`hided` starts it with `CoreOptions::effects_held` while its `core-handover.json` says pending (`hided/src/core_move/gate.rs`), and the core holds every outside effect behind one `EffectHold` (`herdr-core/src/effects.rs`).
No provider is asked for labels (`Runtime::labels_asked`) or for Memory, no bell is typed (`core_pending` in the doorbell's verdict), no watched pane is read, so no warning is raised, no human notice is claimed and GitHub is asked nothing.
The Factory's thread waits before it opens its engine, so it ticks nothing and starts no worker, and the requests sent to it wait in its bounded queue.
No device is dialed and no device helper started, and no agent is put to sleep.
A window on the pending core takes no move of its own, whose checks would dial the device: its `core_move` request is refused as `core_pending` before it reaches the supervisor.
Mobile holds as well: it serves no phone, pushes nothing and runs no `tailscale`, and a pending core that stops removes no serve entry, since the one recorded is the previous core's.
Session sync, the catalog, the attach role and reads that change nothing run, so the link can be proven.
The gate's commit releases the hold once (`Core::release_effects`, called when the gate opens): the doorbell and the watches act on their next pass, the Factory's thread opens its engine, every registered device is dialed and Mobile starts.
The pending lease is a deadline the place records in the handover on the target's clock (`HandoverState::Pending`), which no request to the pending core moves; at it the gate's `expire` and the link's `admit` decide under one lock, so either the move commits or the core gives its copy back, never both, and only the node the move came from (the record's `source`) commits it.
The place records the start's bound (30 s) plus 60 s, and the start is given only what leaves 60 s after it, so a core that started has a full 60 s for its link however long its start took.
The driver learns how long the lease has left from the start step's answer, a span its own clock can count, and before giving up on a target it cannot reach waits that out plus 30 s; with that answer lost it waits the place's full span plus 30 s from the place's answer.
A pending core whose lease ends gives its copy back and then removes its login item's list, though not the item itself, whose `bootout` would stop the process asking: it exits successfully next, which the item never restarts, so no login starts an empty core on the folder; the driver's abort, finding no record, removes the item all the same.
A core is stopped or counted as running only by a pid its state proves by the start the kernel reports for it now (`DaemonState::is_proven_running`), so a pid another process took since is never signalled; a state that proves nothing leaves the instance lock to decide.
A commit, and a rollback, start this machine's role only once nothing of the move says the core runs elsewhere: a placement (or a move back's staging) that cannot be removed is asked again with the window showing Waiting, and the journal keeps its phase meanwhile, so a start in between does the same again.
Two waits of a forward move cannot end on this machine's own: the peer took the move's link but its answer did not come back (`resolve_unlinked`), and the rollback's abort after the link was sent; either can wait on a peer that never returns.
While `core-move-abandoned.json` has room (8 moves) they offer the operator to give the move up (amendment 4): the window asks `core_move` {action: `abandon`}, which `MoveControl::abandon` takes only from the wait that offers it, a node holding its screen included.
Giving up records the move in that file first, which is the decision; then the placement goes, the journal ends `abandoned` and this machine's core starts on its own folder, which the move left as it found it.
A task of the supervisor asks each recorded peer, with the capped backoff, to run `hided core-move abandon` with this machine's build, installed under the peer's helper root first (`Upstream::install_build`), since another build's step would act before its answer is refused as another build's.
That step stops a pending or active core of the move by its proven pid, sets its state aside under `moved-out/<move>/` rather than merging it, and removes the starter, the handover and the copy, so the machine is as it was before the move; another move's handover is left alone and answered `OtherMove`, which ends the entry too, since no core of the given-up move runs beside it.
An entry goes only on such an answer, so a peer that is cut off rather than gone keeps its core running until this machine reaches it.
A move back's Retiring wait offers the same give-up as taking the core here: the record keeps the move with the retirement the core's machine still owes (`PeerStep::Retire`), the move back commits on the copy already placed and this machine's core starts; that machine's core stays `StoppedFor`, which its start (`start.held`) and its gate refuse, until the task reaches it and `hided core-move retire` sets its state aside as a normal retirement.
A move back's rollback before the copy is whole offers nothing: it waits, and a core machine that never returns leaves this machine with no core.
The rollback asks the core's machine to resume until it answers that its core runs, a refusal included (its core cannot start, another move holds it), with the capped backoff and the window showing Waiting with the cause; the copy placed here is taken out only after that answer, since taking it out on a refusal would leave neither machine a core.
A retirement is the commit once its record says retired: what of that folder it cannot set aside or remove afterwards is answered as left over (`StepAnswer::Retired { leftover }`) and logged as `retire.leftover`, and the move back commits all the same, where a refusal would have resumed a core already retired and asked again without end.
A resume is symmetric: it first waits for the core its record says stopped to end (`wait_until_gone`, busy while it has not), since that core exits successfully and nothing starts it again, then drops the record and starts the core whether or not the staged copy could be removed; what could not is answered as left over (`StepAnswer::Running { leftover }`) and logged as `resume.leftover`, and the next staging removes it first.
The core takes a release only while its asker still waits (`ReleaseClaim`): `attach` waits for the answer only `RELEASE_ANSWER_WITHIN`, and a release that waited in line behind a check past that is dropped, or undone with its record and export if the core recorded the stop meanwhile, so a rollback that already gave up on it never finds the core stopped after all.
The machine releasing holds the handover lock for its decision and from the recorded stop to the staged copy, letting it go only while its core records the stop under the same lock.
Labels after the release are the same as after a restart: the release reads to the label worker as the summary switch turning on, so each pane's latest turn that ended while the core was pending is labelled and the turns before it are not.

### Updating the core

A node newer than its core updates the core to its own build before it says hello (PRD core-host-node-move B10, D-10; `update_core` in `hided/src/node_role.rs`, `hided/src/core_update.rs`), over SSH alone, so nothing is asked of the older core.
This build goes into a version folder beside the core's under the same install root (`Upstream::install_build`: the device install's verified, resumable upload without its first-install checks), and that build's own `hided core-update --previous <program>` replaces the core through its starter: the login item is replaced in place, which ends the old job, so a machine that restarts midway starts one of the two builds at login.
The new core has 30 s to take links; one that does not is replaced by the previous build again, which takes links on the same folder.
Either end leaves the install's `current` at the build that runs and keeps the other one for the next connection, so no more than two builds are ever kept there (`hide_host::kit::adopt_build`).
The update holds the core machine's handover record lock for its whole length and is refused as `move_running` while a move's record is there, so an update and a move never overlap.
After an update the node's placement record names the new program and its next dial goes at once; its health says `updating` while one runs.
A node tries once per connection (B20): a failed update keeps its reason, the node's dials go on and are refused as older (`update_failed`) without trying again, and the host shows the update-failed page with both builds; `hide connect`, from a launch or that page's Retry, gives the next connection its one attempt.
Agents keep running in Herdr throughout, and the core runs one of the two builds at every moment but the restart.
No link forms across builds, so a failed update does not reattach the window to the older core: a node's screens run the node's own build of the web shell against the core's snapshot and events, a wire with no version of its own (`SCHEMA_VERSION` has not moved while its shape has), so B10's "the window reattaches" holds after a successful update or once the core's machine is updated, never across builds.
A fixture HOME can name a build whose daemon fails at its start (`hided::env::FIXTURE_FAIL_BUILD_FILE`), which is how the journeys prove the way back.

The app on the core's own machine never stops the core its starter runs (Q13; `update_started_core` in `hided/src/cli.rs`).
A core `hided core-login` started (the login item's, and the fixture starter's, which runs the same command) names its program in `/health` (`starter.program`), and a bundled `hide connect` of another build reads it before it would replace the daemon: stopping that core would leave the login item to start the old build again at the next login beside the app's own.
A newer app updates it as a node does, with its build placed in the same install root on this machine (`HelperPackages::install_here`) and the same `hided core-update`, which runs in its own process group so a host that gives up on the connect never cuts it short; an older app is told to update (`core_newer`), and a build neither orders is refused as another build.
Each connect is one attempt, so a failed update shows the same update-failed page and its Retry is the next attempt.
Under the login item the starter asks launchd once a second whether the new job has exited, so a build that cannot start is known in about a second rather than at the 30 s bound, which keeps the way back inside the host's 25 s connect wait.

### The terminal path

Terminal bytes never pass the core (PRD core-host-node-terminal D-02, D-11).
A key goes from the screen to the node that holds its pane, and the pane's output goes from that node to the screen, while the core decides which panes attach and hears only facts about them.
Three parts carry the bytes, all outside `Mutex<Runtime>`.

- **The node's terminal service** (`hide-node/src/terminal/`) runs one `herdr terminal session control` child per attached pane, against the Herdr the node holds, and keeps the attach rules the core ran before this layer (D-12): an attach waits for its view's size, a pane another client controls is observed, a frame at a grid the view is not drawn at is held until a full frame at the view's grid, an observer whose view changed size attaches again once the size holds for 150 ms, and a failed control attach is retried after 5, 10, 20 and 30 seconds while its pane is shown (this machine's panes; a device's wait for the operator's Reconnect, as they always have).
  It holds keys typed before their pane can take them (below) and writes a paste's text and the keys behind it with the same writer, and tells the core a paste was written only once the session's pipe took it.
  A session's writer holds at most `MAX_UNSENT_KEY_BYTES` (256 KiB) of input its pipe has not taken, a wheel or a resize counting 64 bytes; past it, when the Herdr child stopped reading, the pane's input is refused with `terminal.input_overflow` (reported once a second, like any refusal) until the backlog drains, so the pane never gets the tail of a refused paste.
  At most `MAX_ATTACHED_PANES` (64) sessions run at once; a pane over the cap reads unavailable and attaches when it is shown again, never on the retry clock.
  Besides them it tracks at most 128 panes it was never asked to attach, each named by a screen's view or a core control; at that cap it forgets those only a view named (not on the core's screen, holding no keys, and carrying nothing the core said of them: asleep, closing or a paste's target), and refuses a view of a further one with `terminal.pane_limit` only when none can be forgotten.
  A control about a pane it does not track and that changes nothing (waking a pane never put to sleep, ending a close never begun) creates no entry, while one putting it to sleep or closing it does, so a device's new link told so before the pane attaches writes none of its keys, and the frames the core watches (`WatchFrame`, sent for a pane Herdr just created before it attaches) are kept apart from the panes, at most 64 (`MAX_FRAME_WATCHES`, the core's open operations), the oldest dropped with `terminal.frame_watch_dropped` past it, and ended when the pane is released or forgotten.
  A watch ends with the frame it reports; for a grid change only a frame after Herdr's layout applied it counts, so the core watches when Herdr accepts the change and once more when the layout applies it, never once per frame shown in between.
  A key into a pane it was never told of records no fact and is refused.
  The `/ws` boundary refuses a key or a view naming a pane or creation id over 512 bytes (`MAX_PANE_ID_BYTES`, the longest device pane id), so the entries those caps count are bounded in bytes too.
  One lock guards the service and nothing under it waits on a process, a socket or the core; a session's reader and writer are its own threads.
  Reports are handed to their sink under that lock, so two threads' reports reach the core in the order their work ran and a pane's older state never lands after a newer one.
  Output is decided under the lock and handed to its sink after it, in the order it was decided, by one thread at a time; a session's reader returns once its own frames were handed over, so it is slowed by its own frames, while a control, a key or the clock never waits: each thread hands over its own output and the small notices and forgets such callers queue, never another reader's frame, which that reader is waiting to take.
  So no key or control from the core waits while a large frame is encoded for the screens, and a sink that panics is logged (`terminal.sink_panicked`) and the next delivery goes on.
  Every sink takes what it is given at once and never calls the service back.
  A key the service cannot take is refused with a report at most once a second per pane and kind, whether the service knows the pane or not, so a key flood into a pane that cannot take it costs the core one error, not one per key (B5); the keys refused after that report are counted and logged (`terminal.refusals_unreported`) when the second passes, so none is refused without a record, and past 256 panes a new one is counted with the rest.
- **The router** (`hide-node/src/terminal/router.rs`) is the one `TerminalRoutes` the core and the screen reach: this machine's panes go to its own service, and a device's, which the core names `remote:<device>:pane:<id>`, go to that device's node inside its link, by the device's own ids.
  It holds nothing longer than a map lookup, and refuses a key for a device with no link with a report at most once a second per pane, counting the rest by the service's rule (it has no clock, so a passed second's count is logged on its next key or device line).
  A pane id is split at its first `:pane:`, which always follows the device id because the core registers and connects no device whose id holds `:` (an id holding `:pane:` or ending in `:pane` would read another device's `remote:x:pane:pane:w1` as its own), and every line a device sends is named inside its own `remote:<device>:pane:`.
  A device's node is another machine's program, so the router takes what it sends up only where the core asked for it and only for that device's own panes: output for a pane the core attached on that device, a report about such a pane or one of its panes on screen, and a paste's outcome for a paste held on that device; a device's word that a key moved the keyboard counts only for the pane the last screen key went to and within two seconds of it, and the core ignores an input report for a pane it does not project.
  What it takes is bounded before the core keeps it: every name in a report (a state, a mode, a decision, a kind) is a plain name of at most 64 bytes or the report is refused, a message is cut to 512 characters, and at most 1,024 reports a second are taken from one device.
  A device's error about its own pane reaches the core as a note, a log record, never as the operator's last error, which the screen reads as a refusal of what the operator is doing; and its lines make at most 64 log records and 16 KiB of them a second on this machine (its own diagnostics, its notes and errors, and the lines this side could not read; a pane's state or an input's outcome is never dropped), the rest counted in one record when a later line that makes a record arrives after the second passes, or when the link's proxy ends; a flood still fills the 1 MiB log in about a minute, so a device-wide budget over a longer span is a follow-up.
  Output that arrives for a pane released while it was on its way is forgotten at the hub again.
  What else a device sends is dropped and logged once a second per device and counted after that, a device's diagnostic record keeps only its plain fields, cut to size, and a terminal line past `MAX_TERMINAL_LINE_BYTES` (8 MiB) ends the link.
- **The hub** (`hided/src/terminal_hub.rs`) is the screen side: every pane's output, from this machine or a device, reaches each `/ws` client from here as `terminal` frames.
  Each chunk is rendered once, before the hub's lock is taken, and shared: a pane's ring (at most 512 chunks and 2 MiB) and every client's unsent queue hold the same allocation, so a pane holds at most 3 MiB of chunks plus one full frame waiting for clients, however many of the eight clients are connected (B12; D-16 said 3 MiB, and the waiting full frame is what a frame larger than the cap costs).
  A client that leaves 1 MiB of one pane's output unsent loses that pane's backlog and gets nothing more of it until a full frame, which the hub asks the pane's node for once across clients and asks again every two seconds while no full frame answers it, whether or not the pane draws meanwhile; a client's request ends with that frame or after ten seconds, logged as `terminal.redraw_unanswered`, since a node that cannot answer that long has no session to draw from and its next session's first frame is full; the other panes and the other clients are not touched.
  A full frame replaces what of its pane waits for a client, since it draws over it, and is never counted against the cap, so a pane whose full frame alone passes 1 MiB (a dense screen on a large display) still draws rather than being dropped and asked for again; a full frame larger than the ring's 2 MiB leaves the ring at once, so a client resuming past it is drawn again from a new one.
  A client's unsent chunks wait pane by pane and are taken in the hub's order, so a full frame, an overflow or a forget touches only its own pane's chunks, and what it drops is freed after the hub's lock is released: behind a client stalled with 1,082,315 small chunks across 64 panes, one pane's full frame held the lock 11.5 ms with a single queue and about 10 us this way.
  A chunk past 4 MiB is not drawn: its pane waits for a full frame that fits, asked for only when the long chunk was not itself full, so a pane whose every frame is that long cannot loop.
  A client takes at most 256 chunks or 4 MiB of text per frame, at least one chunk.
  One sequence numbers every pane's chunks, so the one cursor a client names when it reconnects (`have_terminal_sequence`) resumes every pane, on the hub that gave it only: each hub draws a random epoch, every `terminal` frame carries it as `terminal_epoch`, and a page that outlived its daemon names the old hub's epoch and is drawn again whole, however far the new hub's sequence has run; a pane the ring no longer reaches from that cursor, or whose output some client was not sent since the pane's last full frame (a client's cursor passes what it was not sent), is drawn again from a full frame, never left blank or drawn from a broken stream.

A device's terminals ride its one node link (D-10, D-18, D-19, D-20; `hide-node/src/terminal/device.rs`).
The core asks the device's node to start its service with `Call::TerminalsStart` once the link is up, and from then on the link carries terminal lines beside the node's answers: controls and keys down, in the order they were given, and reports and output up.
Terminal lines arrived with node link protocol 26 (the current one is 30); the audited protocol-24 link keeps only its legacy features and is never asked to start terminals, and any other version is refused at Hello and replaced by the payload this build carries.
A device whose link is up without terminals (its helper speaks protocol 24, or its terminal service did not start) tells the router why (`TerminalRoutes::terminals_unstarted`), and its panes read unavailable with that reason rather than that its helper is not connected.
The device sends its reports first and then one line of output per pane in turn, so a pane that floods cannot hold another pane's frames or the core's answers back; a pane whose unsent output passes 1 MiB loses it and is drawn again from a full frame once the link has caught up, by the same rule as the hub (a full frame replaces what waits and is never counted), and past 4,096 unsent reports a report takes the place of the waiting one about the same subject, by the same rule as the core's report queue, so a stalled link loses no pane's state; only past 16,384 reports about new subjects, or 1,024 unsent diagnostic records, is one dropped with a diagnostic.
Output is encoded before the uplink's lock is taken, so a large frame holds up no other pane's output, report or the writer's next line.
On the screen's side at most `MAX_UNSENT_KEY_BYTES` (256 KiB) of one pane's keys wait for the link, counted as the keys' own bytes; plain keys (no carriage return, no escape) for one pane that wait behind each other go down as one line (up to 16 KiB) carrying the last key's time, while an Enter or an escape goes as its own line and a control or another pane's key ends the run, so the node's submit rule reads the chunks it would have read; so a link that falls behind for a moment costs one frame per run of keys rather than one per key, and a 67-byte frame per key cannot fill the cap after 4,000 keys.
A pane past the cap reads ended and refuses keys until it is attached again, and no other pane is touched (B18).
A line the link's writer was too busy with another call to take within 30 seconds was not sent, so it is written again, first, with its keys still counted; only a link that failed mid-line or closed ends the flows.
Keys bind to their pane first, so one pane's keys never end another's flow (B18): besides its 256 KiB of unsent key bytes, a pane may have at most 2,048 runs waiting (an Enter or an escape is a run of its own) and at most 64 panes may have keys waiting, and passing either ends only that pane, as above.
The controls, views and redraws waiting to go down one link are capped on their own at 8,192 lines and 8 MiB; a link that falls that far behind is treated as stalled: what waited is dropped and its keys named in the log as unwritten, later keys are refused with `terminal.device_disconnected`, a `terminal.device_link_stalled` diagnostic names the device and the link's number, and the writer ends the link, so the panes read unavailable and attach again on the next link like any link that ends.
A link that ends takes every flow with it: the device's sessions end with their node, the router reads the device's panes unavailable, and their keys are refused with `terminal.device_disconnected` rather than kept for another link.
The router keeps what the core said of a device's panes that outlives a link, the panes on screen, the keyboard's pane, and which of them hold a sleeping agent or are closing, and tells the next link's node all of it when the link is installed, so keys typed into a sleeping agent's pane are still not written after a reconnect (B9) and a closing pane still refuses them.
Each attach child's stdin is a pipe from the process that started it, so the child ends when that pipe closes, however its starter ended: `hided/tests/it/terminal_children.rs` kills hided with `SIGKILL` and drops a device node's link against the pinned Herdr and finds no `herdr terminal session` left (B20; the remote mailbox lane runs it).
A daemon that stops on its own ends its terminals before the process does: a device's node reports to the router that holds it, so once the core's owner thread is joined the core's handle removes every device's terminals (`Router::remove_every_device`) and closes this machine's terminal service (`Service::close`), which ends its sessions, attaches none again whoever still holds it, and returns only once each attach child it started has exited (or after 5 s, logged as `terminal.close_unfinished`): each child is counted from before it starts until its reaper has seen it exit.
The process goes on after a core that stops for a move, as a node, and a session the core left holding a pane would make the node's attach meet another client (`owner_conflict`), observe instead, and refuse every key as read-only; a node's link that ends closes its terminal service the same way (`NodeTerminals::stop`), and the role that follows starts only after the close returned, so a child still on its way out cannot meet the next attach and the core that starts again on a move back attaches its panes in control (`core_move::a_pane_of_the_window_s_machine_takes_keys_after_the_move_and_the_move_back`).
Terminals follow the link exactly, so a device without the operator's consent, or with consent for an older contract, which today gives no link (`HostPhase::NotAllowed`), shows its panes unavailable and opens no terminal session on the device; withdrawing consent ends the device's terminals at once (`set_host_consent` calls `end_device_terminals`, the link's own end is ignored because its attempt is over), and the pane band reads `helper_not_allowed` (`LinkWait::NeedsConsent`) where a link the core retries by itself reads `reconnecting`.

The core keeps the decisions and hears the facts (D-12, D-13).
It decides which panes attach (every shown Agent tab's, the retention window and `released`, below), which are shown, asleep or closing, the PTY size (the grid the pane's view last reported), a wheel, a click's mouse report, and each creation request keys may be sent against, and tells the router with `TerminalControl` from `sync_terminal_intents` and the events that change them.
The node tells it what changed with `TerminalReport`: a pane's attach state (`terminal_states`, which the snapshot projects), its first frame, a note or an error for the screen, and the operator's input.
A pane the core asked a node to attach is told to be forgotten when it retires, whether or not its first report has arrived, and a paste the core ends, written or not, is released so the router forgets which node held it.
A key is a fact for the core only as its time and whether it submitted (`TerminalReport::Input`): the node reports the first key after a quiet second, an Enter (a carriage return outside a bracketed paste and not after `ESC`, in a chunk of at most 64 bytes) and a key that moves the keyboard to another pane at once, and folds the keys between into one report a second later carrying the last one's time.
A key's time comes from a screen's or a device's clock, so the core takes it as no later than its own now.
The node compares a key's pane with the keyboard's pane as the core last told it (`TerminalControl::Focus`, sent when the snapshot a screen reads shows the keyboard moved, by a key or not: Herdr's own focus move, a close focusing a neighbour, the editor taking it), so the operator's next key into a pane the keyboard left moves it back however long they kept typing there (B1).
Labels' submit time, the doorbell's draft hold and its 30-second quiet wait read the same facts they did before, a second late at most, and no key takes the core's lock or publishes a snapshot (B5).
Reports reach the core through `herdr_core::terminal_reports`: sending never waits for the runtime lock, and one thread takes them into the runtime in batches and notifies once per batch.
Past 16,384 waiting reports a report takes the place of the waiting one about the same subject (a pane's state, first frame, shown frame or keys, a paste's result, a creation's discard), so the core still ends at each node's last word; only a report about a subject none waiting names adds to the queue, and past 65,536 such a report is dropped with a `terminal.report_dropped` diagnostic.

| Goes to the core (control) | Goes between the screen and a node without the core (data) |
| --- | --- |
| Which panes attach and are released (`ATTACHED_TAB_LIMIT`, `released`), shown, asleep or closing; Reconnect | Keys (`key`), from the web socket to the router to the pane's writer |
| PTY size (`terminal_resize`), from the screen that last sent the pane input (`pane_sizes.rs`), the moment that screen's view fits its drawn rectangle | Pane output, from the session's reader to the hub to the client (`terminal` frames) |
| Wheel (`terminal_scroll`) and a click's mouse report (`terminal_click`) | A view's grid and whether it has nothing drawn (`terminal_viewport`), which the node's attach rules read |
| Creation requests keys are sent against: opened, resolved to a pane, discarded | Held keys and a paste's text with the keys behind it, written by the node's writer |
| Input facts: when the operator typed and whether it submitted; attach state, first frame, notes and errors | Redraw requests from the hub to the pane's node |
| A paste's admission, preparation, transfer and cancellation (`runtime/attachments.rs`) | A device's terminal lines inside its node link |
| Every node call (`Call::*`): files, folders and documents (`RootOpen`, `List`, `Stamps`, `Bytes`, `Index`, `OpenDocument`, `Revision`, `Save`, `Create`, `Rename`, `Move`, `Trash`, `Directory`, `RealPaths`, `PathFacts`, `JudgeFolders`, `SetAsideFolder`, `DrainTrash`), Git and projects (`Changes`, `Project`, `Worktrees`, `BranchCheck`, `Registrable`, `WorktreeRemove`, `WorktreeRemovalCheck`, `WorktreesRegistered`, `IgnoredRepository`, `ProjectCreate`, `Repository`, `WorktreeRemoveClean`, `RepositoryClone`, `Git`, `GitWatch`, `Gh`, `HomeSync`), the kit (`Kit`, `HookDiagnosis`, `AgentInstalled`), the AI CLIs (`AiAvailability`, `AiModels`, `AiExecute`, `AiMeasurement`, `AiRestart`, `AiRelease`, `UsageLimitFetch`), processes (`TerminateGroup`, `ProcessStarts`, `ProcessDescendants`, `ListeningPorts`), disk (`DiskUsage`, `VolumeFree`), sessions and the link record (`LabelTranscript`, `SessionActivity`, `LinkFiles`, `LinkRead`, `SessionWorkTurn`, `ProjectSessions`, `SessionStat`, `SessionText`, `SessionConversation`), attachments (`ReadAttachments`, `RemoveClipboard`), the services a link starts (`Hello`, `PanesStart`, `TerminalsStart`), the Factory (`Factory`) and `Cancel` | A device pane's `hide` command streams (`StreamWrite`, `StreamClose` down, `StreamOpen`, `StreamData`, `StreamClosed` up), which hided serves as `/ws` connections, and its pane proofs (`PaneProof` up, `PaneProofAnswer` and `PaneInspect` down), which hided's credential registry answers |
| A device's Herdr socket and the SSH file transfer of a paste (`hide_node_link::device`), through the core's device connector | |

Two terminal facts stay core-routed on purpose: the PTY size, because which screen's view sizes the pane is the core's decision (the one that last sent it input), not any view's, and the wheel, because whether it scrolls Herdr's view or the pane's own mouse mode is a decision about the pane, not a byte for it.

### Agent labels in the core

What each Claude Code, Codex or OpenCode pane is doing (the session's goal, one line for its turn, and how the turn ended) is made by the core, not by a Herdr plugin and not through pane tokens (PRD labels-in-hided, overview-request-view D-08).
The code is `herdr-core/src/labels/`.
Each session-sync coordinator, one per Herdr server (this Mac's and each connected device's), owns a `LabelWorker` it feeds from the agent state it already follows; the core owns one `LabelAnalyzer` for all of them and one `LabelStore`.
Nothing runs under the runtime mutex: conversation reads run on the worker's reader thread, analyses on the analyzer's thread, and the coordinator lays the result onto the session payload just before it hands the payload to the runtime (`LabelWorker::apply`), so the projection is where a label becomes the row's title, sentences and question.

- **When it reads.** A pane's conversation is read only when its agent status, Herdr state sequence or session reference moved, when an earlier read left a backlog, when a provider wait ran out, and once about three seconds after a turn starts whose prompt was not written yet.
  A read is bounded and resumable, from the checkpoint the store kept, so an unchanged pane spends no read, no Herdr call, no request and no publish.
- **What it reads.** `hide_session::label_transcript::read` is the session adapter (PRD overview-request-view D-14): it proves the provider's native owner of the session Herdr's reference names, and returns the same facts for every agent, whatever its format: the session's own title (Claude Code's `ai-title` and the operator's `custom-title`, Codex's newest `thread_name` in `session_index.jsonl`, OpenCode's session `title`), each person's message with its time, its image count and the coordination sender its header names, each assistant message, the pull request addresses its tools printed with the time they were printed (Claude Code's subagent files under `<session>/subagents/` included, the 16 most recently written files and 2 MiB a read, the rest reported as `subagent_files_capped`), and the next checkpoint.
  OpenCode's database is read in the same bounds, each row's size answered from its record header before anything is loaded: a message row over 64 KiB (`message_capacity`), a part row over 256 KiB (`part_capacity`) and a row holding no text (`not_text`) are skipped unread, one read admits at most 1 MiB including metadata, IDs and 64 bytes per part before retaining or parsing payloads, and leaves the rest for the next (a part the first message of a read cannot fit is `read_budget`), and the title is cut to 512 characters in the query (one too long to cut there is no title).
  The part-size preflight visits only the next bounded set: at most 256 parts per message and 512 parts per read, including empty, malformed and skipped parts, plus one header-only cap probe per message.
  Crossing the message cap reports `part_work_capacity` and advances past that message; reaching the read cap defers the next message to the next read.
  A read-only transaction also limits SQLite scans and sorts to approximately one million virtual-machine instructions through its progress handler; missing indexes or excessive database work return `opencode_db_work_capacity` without changing the provider database.
  The ordered queries stream row headers, and metadata, message IDs and parts consume the same cumulative byte budget before their payload query or JSON parsing, including malformed metadata and the first record.
  OpenCode 2.x's `session_message` rows are read in the same bounds, with a content item standing where a part does and an 8 MiB scan limit for a row that holds its whole content (the reader paragraph under Agent adapters above).
  Text an agent wraps around an attachment (Codex's `<image ...>` blocks and file list) and text it injects (hook context, `<system-reminder>`, a compaction request) are no one's message.
  Text the agent itself writes on the person's side is no one's message: Claude Code's hook, meta and system prompts and its compaction summary (`isCompactSummary`, `isVisibleInTranscriptOnly`, which otherwise look like a typed prompt), and Codex's `compacted` record with the earlier requests it replays.
  A Claude Code command is the operator's request only when it went to the model: one Claude Code answers itself with a `<local-command-stdout>` record (`/model`) is not, unless that answer carries `turnOrigin: "human"`, the mark Claude Code writes when it then hands the turn to the model (`/goal`, 2.1.294-2.1.301); `CommandReply::after` in `hide-session/src/lib.rs` reads the mark.
  A Codex user message wrapped in `<codex_internal_context source="...">` is Codex's own text unless its source is `user_goal`, whose `User set the goal: "<JSON string>"` objective is the operator's request (the message stays as written when there is no quoted objective, as for one Codex omitted); `codex_internal_context` is the one place that reads this format (codex-cli 0.163.0, `hide-session/tests/fixtures/adapters/codex-0.163.0-goal/`).
  Only tool output is scanned for addresses (`hide-session/src/sightings.rs`), on the records the read already decodes, at most eight per output; an address in a reply or a request is a mention.
  OpenCode keeps its sessions in `opencode.db` under `$XDG_DATA_HOME/opencode`, or `~/.local/share/opencode` when that is unset, which the adapter opens read-only with a 50 ms busy wait: a locked database is a skipped read (`opencode_db_busy`) retried on the next state change, never a wait OpenCode feels, and an assistant message without `time.completed` is left for the next read.
  The SQLite URI also sets `readonly_shm=1`, so a read never creates or updates OpenCode's WAL index; an unavailable index refuses the read while the live database retains its normal locking.
  `hide-session/tests/it/adapters.rs` is the contract every adapter passes, against a fixture in the agent's own format at the version it names (`hide-session/tests/fixtures/adapters/`); a format the adapter cannot read leaves that pane's row with Herdr's facts only and a diagnostic.
  `hide-session/src/envelope.rs` attributes only the first nonempty line's current `Hide letter <id> from <name> (<agent>) [<kind>]` header, preserving the first sender when several letters arrive together.
  `contracts/delivery-envelope.json` independently pins examples for both this reader and `delivery::mailbox::pull`; older delivery headers have no special sender interpretation.
  It reads only under the agent's own transcript root in its HOME (`.claude/projects` or `.codex/sessions`), after every link in the path is resolved, and takes an id only as letters, digits, `.`, `_` and `-`; anything else is refused with a stable code that names no path (`label_session_outside_roots`, `label_session_id_invalid`).
  For this Mac it runs in process.
  For a device the same function runs in `hided node serve` behind the `label_transcript` call (since protocol 12, with the adapter's facts since 14); the events come to this Mac in memory only and are never stored (only the marks under What is kept are), and the analysis runs on this Mac's provider login.
  A device whose helper is not connected keeps its panes' labels and is retried every fifteen seconds; a helper too old to know the call leaves the provider name, and the device's kit status already offers the reinstall that replaces it.
- **What the last turn waits for.** The same read folds Claude and Codex native turn records into an agent-neutral turn tracker (`hide-session/src/turns.rs`), answering whether an unanswered native question or completed plan waits for the operator.
  Native tool-call ids correlate question results; ordinary prose never supplies structured question content.
  Codex's `request_user_input_async` is the one question whose tool result is not the answer: the tracker keeps its waiting question numbers (at most eight per call) and each `<send_user_message_question_reply>` message names one by `questionItemId`; the message is one of `INJECTED_PREFIXES`, so it is not a person's turn.
  The tracker bounds native call identities to 256 bytes, and a longer id fails the read explicitly.
  It keeps at most eight question calls per turn, and an answered call leaves to make room for a new one, so an interview that asks and answers one question after another never fills it.
  A ninth call while eight still wait makes what the turn waits for not known until the next turn starts or a person writes, and the read goes on: a failed read is read again from the same place, so it would never reach the message that ends it, and the session's labels and doorbell would stop for good.
  Optional `UserTurnContent` keeps at most 8 KiB of text and eight 256-byte choices, preserving UTF-8 boundaries and marking cuts.
  A native record beyond the separate 256 KiB physical-line admission cap is discarded without its body, and the bounded ids its structure carries become the record's turn mark: a tool result answers its call, an `AskUserQuestion` or `request_user_input` call waits with its content absent, a `request_user_input_async` call is not known (its question count decides which replies end the wait), and a Codex plan, start, completion or abort keeps its turn id and mode.
  An id past the native bounds is the same reported failure it is in a retained record.
  A record whose text is past the cap, such as a person's message with a pasted screenshot, keeps the tool marks its structure certifies and loses its text to labels and search, counted as `conversation_capacity` in the read's skip diagnostic.
  For Claude Code the scan also keeps the `name` and `id` of each `tool_use` block, and the `tool_use_id` of each `tool_result`, so a record too large to keep still pairs its calls with their results; the wake reader folds a lost wake mark for a record that may have carried a task's words and one the scan cannot follow, and nothing for one that can carry none (a screenshot), told by the record's kind and the call it answers and never by searching its words (`turns/wake.rs::Record`, [status-model.md](status-model.md#a-quiet-row-that-waits)).
  The marks only text gives, a person's message or an interruption, clear or end a wait and never start one, so losing them can hold a bell but never ring one into a menu.
  A record the scan cannot structure (an envelope it does not know, an escaped discriminator, an escaped or non-ASCII id, nesting past 64 levels) is skipped the same way and folds an unreadable mark: what the turn waits for is not known until the next turn starts or a person writes.
  Neither stops the read, because Claude Code writes a screenshot inline as base64, in a tool result or a person's message, and refusing one record would stop every later read of the session, its labels and its doorbell.
  A checkpoint an older scan took inside such a record kept no ids, so the cursor rereads that record from its start.
  Grok's records are read by its own scan instead (`grok::LargeLine`, above), which keeps a prompt's, answer's or question's turn effect without its text, because Grok also writes a pasted image inline.
  The tracker rides on the read's request and answer beside the checkpoint, so an incremental read continues the turn it was in, a read from the start or a rescan starts over, and a record replayed from the anchor is not folded twice.
  The record keeps the tracker and the Herdr `state_change_seq` the read was asked under (`PaneRecord::turns`, `turns_seq`, set only once the backlog is read), and the overlay answers the wait (`LabelOverlay::waiting`) only for that state and the proven session, so a fact read for an earlier state never stands for a newer one.
  The turn end of an agent whose read reports background tasks is owed that read from the moment Herdr reports the stop (`PaneRecord::turn_end_owed`, not saved); the overlay lays it as `RowFacts.turn_end_unread` for that state and `agent_activity` keeps the row Working until the read lands, fails or the agent runs again, so a stop that a proven task takes back is never announced ([status-model.md](status-model.md#a-quiet-row-that-waits)).
  A failed reread invalidates that proof and publishes no structured fact, while retaining the bounded tracker/checkpoint for a later successful continuation and the existing unavailable-read retry interval.
  A restart resumes without a read only when the wait was read for the stored state; a record from before turns were read is read once, from its anchor, where the turn's mode is not seen and the wait stays not known until Herdr's next state.
  A device helper answers the same field; one that predates it answers without it, which reads as not known.
