  The coordinator takes the overlay before delivery observes the payload, and the doorbell holds on a wait or on not knowing (`docs/delivery.md`, A menu Herdr reads as a stop); the sidebar draws the typed question or approval and carries `user_turn` only for the proven current session/state (`docs/status-model.md`).
- **Who sent a request.** Herdr's input calls name no sender, so the core keeps when the operator submitted to a pane through Hide (PRD overview-request-view D-19): a keyboard chunk of at most 64 bytes holding a carriage return outside a bracketed paste and not after `ESC`, which the pane's node reports as a submitted input fact (`TerminalReport::Input`, for this Mac's and a device's panes alike; see The terminal path), and a phone reply Herdr accepted (`pane_input_submitted`, which hided dispatches after `pane.send_input`).
  An Enter at a prompt Herdr reports `blocked` is not one; an Enter at a plan waiting for approval is, since Codex writes the next turn's message from it.
  Only the moment and whether the agent was running are kept, in memory, at most 32 per pane and 512 panes (`labels/input.rs`), behind a lock of its own that the runtime takes inside its mutex and the workers take alone.
  Each person's message is judged once and the verdict kept by its offset (`labels/facts.rs`): the coordination sender; else the operator when an unclaimed submit came at most two minutes before it (thirty while the agent was running, since a queued prompt is written when the agent takes it up); else, for a message older than this worker's view of the pane, the operator unless it is a delegated child's first request; else another agent.
  The first request is the first one any read holds, recorded once and kept across restarts (`first_request_offset`, set by the first read that holds a request, not by the read from the start), because Grok and OpenCode write the session before the prompt.
  A submit two messages could claim goes to the earlier and writes `requester.ambiguous`.
- **One analysis at a time.** `LabelAnalyzer` takes jobs in arrival order through one `hide-ai` router built from the operator's current provider and model choice, which it re-reads from the runtime before each analysis, so a change in Settings applies from the next analysis and the one running finishes on the choice it started with.
  Each worker hands in at most one job, so arrival order is a round robin between servers.
  Shutdown cancels the running request, which ends its provider child, and joins the thread.
  The prompts and schemas are `context_label.v14` (`context_label.rs`), one pair per boundary, and each asks only what its boundary can know; they name no language, and each request carries the core's output language, which the worker takes with the agent-summary switch on every tick ([AI_PROVIDERS.md](AI_PROVIDERS.md#the-output-language)).
  The turn's start answers `goal` (8 to 30 characters in Korean, Simplified Chinese and Japanese and 8 to 60 in English, what the session is to have made, or null until work is assigned), `goal_changed` and `line` (at most 40 characters, 80 in English, the turn's work), and its end is `working` by construction, never the model's word, because how the turn will end is not known yet.
  A null goal leaves the existing title priority in place; it cannot clear an accepted goal, and an answered greeting with no required next action has an empty line and a stopped end of `done`.
  The turn's end answers the same three, its line the turn's result, what the operator is to answer or what the turn waits for, and `end`: `question`, `blocked`, `waiting`, `unfinished` or `done`, which split a stopped turn by whose move is next (the operator's; the operator's again, on a cause the line can state in one sentence that stopped the turn; something else's, such as a background test, build, CI run or another agent the turn started or handed work to, never a pull request, even when the turn only reported progress; nobody's on unfinished work; nobody's on finished work) and are taken in that order when more than one fits; a question with no line is read as done, a block with no line as unfinished, and a turn is `done` only when it reported its work finished.
  An answer outside its boundary's schema, a turn end that says `working` or a turn start that judges a turn's end, is refused by the router's schema check and by the parser and takes the failure path below; it is never read as another word.
  A record a `context_label.v5` turn end left as `working` keeps it until that pane's next analysis, and needs no conversion: a stopped agent's `working` end moves no verb and no group.
  A `v6` record keeps its end until that pane's next analysis and needs no conversion; the `v6` answers are all words `v8` still has.
  `blocked` names a cause the operator must act on and lands on the existing `error` demand wire value (the English word is Blocked); the background tasks a row waits on are not asked of the model but proven from the session's records (`hide-session/src/turns/wake.rs`, Claude Code only; [status-model.md](status-model.md) on a quiet row that waits), and the reply a row waits on comes from the delivery ledger when the runtime lays lineage (`runtime/agents.rs::lay_reply_waits`).
  The context carries the operator's requests only (D-09): the person's messages whose verdict is the operator's, the session's first request, and any not judged yet (`SessionFacts::feeds_analysis`), the first three and last eight of them or, once a goal exists, the previous goal and the new ones; then the latest exchange, because the line and the end judge the turn.
  The whole context is at most 4,000 characters (`MAX_ANALYSIS_CONTEXT_CHARS`), and each of its two sections is masked and cut on its own (`analysis.rs::bounded_context`): the requests section keeps its head within 2,000 characters, so the first request always stays, and the latest exchange keeps its tail in what is left, so a long exchange (Codex's 6,400-character goal reminder) never pushes the operator's requests out; a cut section ends or begins with `…`.
  The ledger's side of a turn-end request (`work_context`) is appended after that bound, as before.
  Before that context reaches the provider, secrets and emails are masked, the home-account prefix in macOS `/Users/example/`, Linux `/home/example/`, and Windows `C:\Users\example\` or `C:/Users/example/` paths becomes `~` for a bare home or `~/` before a tail, including homes at sentence ends and in `file://` requests, with surrounding prose, sentence punctuation, quote delimiters and the remaining tail unchanged, and other paths stay as written (`labels/analysis.rs`); the goal prompt excludes file paths.
  A turn costs one request at its start and one at its end; the goal moves only on the first analysis or at the start of a turn whose newest message is the operator's, and the end writes only the line and the end.
  A turn is known by the person's message that began it, its time and its words (`turn_key`), so the key holds while the answer grows and moves once at the next message, even one that repeats the last word for word, such as a second letter notice or the same short reply; Cursor stamps that time to the minute, so there two identical messages within one minute stay one turn.
  A failure clears the turn's line and end and keeps the goal, so the row shows its facts and never stops on a guess (B20); a turn end that ran out of time is asked once more (D-33); one the environment can clear (no login, a usage limit, no provider, an over-budget refusal) asks again after the provider's reset time or ten minutes, and one the input settles parks that turn (`AnalysisFailure::retry_after`; [AI_PROVIDERS.md](AI_PROVIDERS.md#retry-policy) owns the router's side).
- **The switch.** Settings › Hide AI › Features › Agent summaries (`AiSettings::agent_summary` in `ai.json`, on by default, D-11) is read from the runtime by each coordinator on every tick and publish (`LabelWorker::set_summaries`).
  Off, the worker asks for nothing, the analyzer answers a queued job as stopped and cancels the running one (`LabelAnalyzer::cancel_running`), and the overlay lays no goal, line or end, so every surface shows the session's own title and the request view its facts; reads go on, because the rows stand on them.
  On again, the stored labels show at once and each pane's current turn is asked for, never the turns that ended while it was off.
- **Only for the session it was proven for.** A record stores the provider's native owner, proven from transcript metadata, and the reference it was proven under.
  A path reference yields a native session id only after the reader confirms it from that file's own metadata and the id hashes to the same provider owner.
  The optional id is retained with that proof and projected only while the current reference still proves it; a switched path exposes no previous identity, and a retained helper answer without the field grants no path identity.
  The projection shows a label only while the pane's current Herdr reference proves that owner, so a new session, a reused pane, a provider change and an A to B to A switch show nothing of the previous session until the new one is proven, and returning to the same session restores its label (`PaneRecord::proven_for`).
  Every read and analysis result carries the pane's generation, which moves with the reference, and a late result for an earlier generation is dropped.
  Nothing is exchanged through Herdr tokens, so there is no token fence or publication guard to keep in step.
- **What is kept.** `labels.json` in the daemon's state folder, beside `core-state.json`, written whole through a temporary file and a rename with mode 0600, outside the runtime mutex, keeps per local pane the proven owner, label, analyzed turns, the read checkpoint, the time the core saw the pane change state and the session's facts (its titles, the last request the operator sent and the last someone else sent with their requesters, the last reply, each cut to 8 KiB, the newest 20 pull request addresses and the subagent checkpoints), limited to panes the server still lists.
  Device records stay in memory, including during later local saves, and the device helper is read again after a restart; loading an older store removes its device records and rewrites the private file.
  The one thing of a device that is written is its `devices` marks: per pane the session proof (a hash token), the hashes of the turns already analyzed, how the last turn ended, the label the model wrote (goal and line, which are its own words and capped) and the time the pane changed state, never a request, reply, path or fact of the conversation.
  A restart restores them so a device turn that was already analyzed is not asked of the model again (75 of 375 label requests in one log window had repeated) and its label is still shown.
  At most 512 panes of a device and 64 devices are kept, the newest first, and a save that changes no mark writes nothing (a pane's state change or a new fact of the conversation alone does not rewrite the file).
  One mark this build cannot read is dropped alone with a `store.device_marks_dropped` diagnostic, and a cap that drops marks writes `store.device_marks_capped`.
  A core move drops the section (`node_migration/reown.rs`).
  A restart resumes from it with no read and no request for an unchanged pane; a file that cannot be read starts empty and records a diagnostic, and nothing in it is shown until a reference proves it.
  The first run with no `labels.json` imports the retired plugin's `display-state.json` once, taking only entries it had proven an owner for.
- **One generator per Herdr server.** A worker generates only while the node on the machine that runs its server holds that server's lock for it (`Call::LabelLock`, `hide_host::label_lock`): an exclusive `flock` on `<socket>.hide-label-generator.lock` beside Herdr's socket, which every daemon reaching that server shares whatever HOME it runs with, so cores on any machine that label one server meet at one file and the core keeps no lock file of its own.
  The node keys each lock by the socket as the core named it, and a node that dialed its core locks only its own Herdr server's file, the one it bridges.
  The core's own node answers at once; a device's or an inbound node's answer crosses its link, so that ask runs on a thread of its own and wakes the worker, because the worker runs on the session-sync thread.
  Until the first answer the worker shows the labels it stored, and an ask that failed (a device still connecting) is tried again after 2 s; only a node that names no server, or one on a protocol that predates the call, has no lock to share, and any other refusal fails the ask.
  A second core on the same server shows provider names, logs `generator.standby` and asks again every thirty seconds, so it takes over when the first one ends; the holder asks again on the same beat, because a node lets its locks go with the link it served.
  The node holds each lock for the worker that took it, so a worker's late give-back never frees its successor's lock, and it unlocks explicitly when the worker or the link ends, so a descriptor inherited by a concurrently starting child cannot extend the role; a node that serves no Herdr server it can name has nothing shared to lock, and its worker generates.
- **Status.** The question state, an unfinished turn and a wait on something other than a pull request come from the analysis's `end`; blocked, working, done and idle come only from Herdr's `agent_status`.
  A `labels.json` written before v5 keeps its `task` as the goal and its `progress` as the line, and a pending question becomes `end: question` with its reply as the line (`PaneRecord::upgrade_v3`); that transition goes when no v3 record is left to read.
  An agent that is working, or whose Herdr state sequence moved while it was stopped and is stopped or blocked again, ran in between and is no longer asking, so its question and requested reply end.
  Nothing reads `status_*` tokens, and nothing produces the Error demand any more.
- **Time.** The core records when it saw each pane's state change and publishes it as `changed_at_unix_ms`; the web draws elapsed time from it on one shared one-second clock (`web/src/components/elapsed.tsx`), so a ticking elapsed time never causes a snapshot, and sorting uses the same time.
- **Diagnostics.** Reads, discarded results, provider waits and standby go to `Logs/core.jsonl` with `component` `labels`, a pane and generation, and never conversation text, secrets or paths; a failure the operator cannot act on never reaches the screen.

`herdr-core/src/labels/tests.rs` owns the behavior and `desktop/e2e/session-labels.spec.ts` proves it end to end; [status-model.md](status-model.md#task-identity) names the regression owners.

### The Factory host

The Software Factory's engine runs on its own thread, `herdr-core-factory`, beside the delivery worker and the label analyzer, its judgments run on a second thread, `herdr-core-factory-judge`, with a router of their own, and worker starts, which wait for Herdr, run on a third, `factory-starts`, so a start never holds a command or a worker's report.
The engine is the `hide-factory` crate, which does not know the runtime or Herdr; the host in `herdr-core/src/factory.rs` gives it a clock, the project's git and `gh`, the verify runner, and a worker port, and `runtime/factory.rs` holds the few places that take `Mutex<Runtime>` to read owned data or hand the core a request.
No file, SQLite, network or subprocess work happens under the runtime lock, and a command reaches the engine through a bounded queue instead of a call into the runtime.
`hided` routes `hide factory` over the pane-capability socket; a caller on the core's machine or on a node that dials the core is checked as delivery checks it, with its machine, and a caller on a device the core dials is refused (`factory_local_only`).
The readonly `factory_question_guard` frame shares pane authentication but has no command role, lazy initialization or publication: one absolute deadline covers admission, pane proof, fresh native identity and an already-open engine's current Task worker lookup.
The lookup joins the Task's accepted worker registration to the current native session and rechecks runtime context/connection before denying; cwd, display names and lineage never establish this authority.
Its queue is the existing 32-slot Factory queue, and an expired request has no effect.
A caller on a device the core dials fails open before an SSH native read, because Factory workers run only on the core's machine and the nodes that dial it; a node's caller is read through that node's link.
A worker starts on its Factory's machine through the spawn-on-machine path (`coordination::Command::Spawn { machine }`), its pane kept as the core names it (`remote:<node>:pane:<id>` on a node), and its rollback holds on to that machine's Herdr and link (`StartControl`), which a new link replaces.
`Core::drop` stops the Factory host first, so its verify runs and external calls end with the daemon.
[factory.md](factory.md) owns the engine, the store, the roles and the read model.

The Factory screens read two snapshot sections, `factory` (the engine's `FactorySummary` and the answers to the screen's own requests) and `factory_task` (the `TaskDetail` of the one Task page a shell opened), each with its own revision.
Both are built and compared on the engine thread and handed to the runtime only when they differ from what it last held, so an idle tick takes no lock and publishes nothing, and the delta carries a section only on its own change.
While a page is open it is read again after every command and tick, since a decision or a running attempt's log tail can change it without moving the summary; an unchanged page is still dropped.
A screen action is the `factory_action` event, whose `command` is the stage-1 `Command` decoded strictly and limited to the verbs a person may send; the core runs it with the operator role relayed by `screen` and puts the answer in `factory.actions` under the event's request id, newest last and at most 16.
`factory_task_open` and `factory_task_close` name the page the engine keeps building, and `factory_secretary_set` records the secretary's pane in the core's UI state.
[UI_BEHAVIOR.md: Factory](UI_BEHAVIOR.md#factory) owns what the screens do.

### The mobile companion

Phone transport, pairing, conversation reads and delivery live in `hided/src/mobile/`; status projection and notification transitions belong to `herdr-core/src/agent_state/`.
`Runtime::remote_herdr_api` lends the Herdr API connection the core already holds for a connected SSH device.
Mobile is off until the operator turns it on in Settings > Mobile, and it is kept in `mobile.json` beside the daemon's state (the switch, the push mode, the serve entry hided added, and the VAPID key), written through a temporary file at mode 0600.

The transport is the operator's own Tailscale, and `mobile/tailscale.rs` is the only file that knows it, so a relay later replaces that one module.
It finds the CLI at `HIDE_TAILSCALE_BIN`, then the macOS app bundle's `/Applications/Tailscale.app/Contents/MacOS/Tailscale`, then `tailscale` on PATH, and reads `tailscale status --json` for the backend state, `Self.DNSName`, `Self.HostName`, MagicDNS and `CertDomains`; every command runs with a fifteen-second bound.
hided binds loopback as before and adds `tailscale serve --bg --yes --https=443 http://127.0.0.1:<port>`, then confirms the entry with `serve status --json` before a QR is shown.
It records the entry before running the add, so an add that took effect and then failed or timed out is still recognised as its own on the next pass.
It refuses to expose while `AllowFunnel` covers that HTTPS 443 name, in the saved config or a foreground session's, because Funnel would publish the address to the internet, and says so on screen; a Funnel turned on after exposure takes hide's entry down and closes every phone until it is off.
Settings > Mobile rechecks every three seconds while it is open, and with no one watching hided reconciles once a minute while Mobile is on, and while a removal is still owed after it was switched off, so a Tailscale that starts late is picked up and a failing command is retried at that pace; an exposed Mobile keeps its exposure through one failed Tailscale read and drops it on the second in a row, and a pairing code is minted only by `mobile_show_code` (Show QR), never by opening the tab, by a background pass or by a phone pairing; `mobile_hide_code`, the last Mobile page closing, and a pass that finds the code expired take it out of the frame.
It owns only the entry it recorded (`{dns_name, port, added_at}`): an HTTPS 443 handler it did not record is shown as the foreign target and left alone, an entry for its old port is replaced, and switching off or a clean exit removes it with `serve --yes --https=443 --set-path=/ off`.
Every start reconciles the record, so a crash's leftover entry is repaired or removed on the next start; `run_daemon` answers SIGTERM and SIGINT with the same shutdown, which removes the entry before the process exits.
A failed command leaves Mobile unexposed with the step that failed on screen, and its command line and stderr go to a `mobile_transport` record; a removal that fails at switch-off stays on screen and is retried until it succeeds, and shutdown waits for a reconcile already running.
While exposed, `https://<Self.DNSName>` joins the allowed WebSocket Origins, and only a phone handshake may use it; a desktop handshake from that Origin is closed with 4002.
A request that arrives through `tailscale serve` (a forwarded-for or Tailscale identity header, or a Host that is not loopback) reaches only `/m`, `/m/*`, `/assets/*` and `/ws`, everything else answers 404, and its `/ws` takes only a phone handshake from the Mac's tailnet Origin while Mobile is exposed, whatever other Origin it claims, with a 64 KiB frame limit.
Every handshake has ten seconds to send its first frame.

The phone app is a static shell and a data channel, kept apart.
`/m/` serves `mobile.html`, and `/m/manifest.webmanifest`, `/m/sw.js` and the icons come from `web/public/m/` through the same static route as the desktop shell's `/assets`; the page, the service worker and the manifest are served `no-cache`.
The service worker caches only that shell, so the app opens without a network and shows its unreachable line.
Pairing, the agent list, an agent's conversation, a pane's rows, replies, keys, starts and the push subscription travel on `/ws` only; the phone calls no other HTTP path.
The first frame decides the client: `{client_kind: "phone", token}` for a paired phone or `{client_kind: "phone", pair, name}` to pair; a phone counts toward `MAX_CLIENTS`, and a refusal sends `{type: "refused", reason}` (`code_expired`, `phone_limit`, `revoked`, `mobile_off`) and closes with 4001.
Every frame to a phone goes through `mobile::phone::encode`, the one place a relay would wrap in end-to-end encryption.

A pairing code is sixteen random bytes, is accepted only while Mobile is on and exposed, lives only in memory for five minutes, is spent by the first phone that uses it, and is replaced only when `mobile_show_code` is sent again; opening Settings > Mobile issues none and voids none (B58).
The QR is `https://<DNSName>/m/#pair=<base64url {v:1, endpoint, code}>`, so the code never reaches a request line or a log.
A paired phone gets a 32-byte credential; `phones.json` (mode 0600) keeps only its SHA-256, compared in constant time, with the name, the last connection, the notification answer, the push subscription and the tailnet name it was paired at (`PhoneOrigin`).
The phone keeps the credential in its storage and in its address fragment (`#k=`), because the manifest has no `start_url` and a Home Screen app starts from the address it was added from, whose storage iOS may keep apart from Safari's.
At most four phones pair at one tailnet name; the limit is checked before the code is spent.
Only the phones paired at the name this core was last exposed at count (PRD core-host-node-move B17): they are listed in Settings > Mobile, receive pushes and are let in, and the limit counts them; a switch-off or a failed check leaves that name as it was.
After a move the phone's Home Screen app still starts at the old machine's address, so each phone scans the new machine's code once; until then the phones paired where the core ran wait unlisted, a credential of theirs is answered `mobile_off` (the phone stays paired and shows the core as unreachable), and a move back lets them in again; the seven-day rule below ends them otherwise, so no more than four phones per address of the last seven days are kept.
A record from before phones kept their address reads as `Unrecorded` and takes the exposure `mobile.json` names at the first load, or the first exposure.
A phone keeps at most two connections, the oldest closed first, a connection that answers no ping for 45 seconds is closed and recorded as `phone.silent`, and one whose frame cannot leave within ten seconds is closed and recorded as `phone.stalled`.
A phone away for seven days is revoked at start, by an hourly sweep and when it next connects, and a revoke, manual or automatic, drops the credential and the subscription in one write and closes that phone's connection.

A phone's whole vocabulary is `open`, `view`, `older`, `more`, `close`, `input`, `start_sheet`, `start_agent`, `wake_agent`, `wake_sleeping_session`, `check_sleeping_session`, `push_subscription`, `push_permission` and `ping`; anything else is answered `refused_request` and recorded as `scope.refused`, and a phone never receives a file, a path, a setting or the core snapshot; the one setting that reaches it is the interface language, which rides every `agents` frame as the core's stored value or null (the phone resolves it, and follows its own system language on null, before the first frame and when unpaired).
A page cannot see the protocol-level ping hided sends, and a Mac that went to sleep leaves the phone's socket open until the phone's operating system gives up on it, which can take minutes, so the phone checks hided itself (`web/src/mobile/connection.ts`, `phone.rs::liveness`).
A slow pane write (a reply or key to a device pane can wait out an SSH channel open) runs beside the socket loop like a start, so a `ping`, an eviction or a revoke is never held behind it: one input per connection is written at a time, a second is answered `in_flight`, and the write is not aborted when the connection ends because its request id must still be settled (`phone.rs::spawn_beside_loop`).
`ping` carries a number and hided answers `{type: "pong", id}` from one in-memory admission check, with no core lock, no Herdr call and no read of the list; a phone no longer let in is sent `refused` with its reason instead, and a `ping` without a number is refused and recorded as `scope.refused`.
The phone keeps one timer per connection and counts any frame from hided as proof it is there, so a phone that is receiving never pings: after 20 seconds of silence it sends `ping` and gives hided 10 seconds to send anything, then closes the socket from its own side, without waiting for the browser's close, shows the unreachable state and retries as for any loss.
A silent hided is therefore shown as connected for at most 30 seconds after its last frame; returning to the foreground or regaining the network sends a `ping` at once with the same 10 seconds, and a hidden page asks nothing.
A new socket that says no `hello` within 15 seconds is given up the same way, so a retry toward a sleeping Mac does not wait for the browser's own connect timeout.
A phone starts an agent through the same `agent_start_in_checkout` event as the desktop (`mobile/start.rs`, PRD home-device-rail D-24, D-25), marked `stay_behind`.
`stay_behind` is the one word that tells the core the start did not come from the operator's own screen: the task slot keeps it (`TaskOperationSnapshot::stays_behind`, never sent to a shell), `ingest_task_operation_result` then leaves the terminal, the keyboard focus and `selected_pane_id` where they were, and the tab request asks Herdr not to focus the tab it makes (`CheckoutTabRequest::focus`, the same `focus: false` a branch migration sends).
The tab is made and listed as for any start, so the new agent appears in the sidebar and in the phone's list, and the phone opens its detail from that list; ⌘N, New worktree, an issue's Start and a pull request's hand-off send no such word and keep bringing their tab forward.
While its start sheet is open (`start_sheet`), hided sends it a `start_catalog`: every device's Home and checkouts as targets named by id, the core's machine's system (`core_platform`, from its device row's `host.platform`) for the phone to name that machine in its language, `claude` and `codex` with the models the provider catalog lists, and the choice the last start remembered; the sheet counts as a start surface for the catalog demand, and the folder a target id leads to stays in hided.
`start_agent` carries a request id, the text, a target id, a kind and an optional model; it is refused before anything is dispatched when the phone is no longer admitted or the target, kind, model or text is not one the sheet could have sent, and otherwise answered with `start_result` once the core's task slot or last error carries that request id, or `timeout` after 90 seconds (`start::ANSWER_LIMIT`, the core's own limit for a start).
A request id the phone repeats is started once: the repeat gets the first answer, joins it while the first still waits, or, after a `timeout`, follows the same start again without sending it; one connection waits for one start at a time, and a second is answered `in_flight`.
The list is `agent_state::phone::project` over the snapshot's `rest` section (`agent_state/tally.rs`, called by `mobile/projection.rs`): the local navigator's agents and every connected device's, in the desktop's four groups, each keyed by device id and pane id with its lineage root.
The core supplies each phone group’s order and count; the phone renders both without recounting.
Sleeping conversations are separate safe rows in that projection, carrying only their `SleepId`, provider, title, canonical group, phase, age and action availability.
They carry no native session, cwd, former pane, close key or lineage, and can never open a pane detail or receive input.
A phone may wake or inspect only a currently projected local sleep intent, with an exact-key payload and renewed phone admission before dispatch.
Its correlated `sleep_result` acknowledges dispatch only; the core-owned row supplies progress or refusal, and a lost answer never triggers an automatic resend.
hided follows the core only while Mobile is on with a phone paired or a desktop window has agent notifications on, reads the snapshot off the core lock with its own cursors once per notification burst, and republishes the list only when it changed.
A detail shows the agent's conversation (`mobile/conversation.rs`) or its terminal, as the phone chooses with `open` and `view`, and hided reads only the one shown, once a second and only while it is open.
Claude Code and Codex draw in the alternate screen, so Herdr keeps no scrollback for their panes and `pane.read` holds only the screen; the conversation comes from the agent's own transcript instead, found through `hide-session` from the session `pane.get` reports for the pane.
Only a pane on this Mac with a reported session whose adapter supplies conversation has one: a device's transcript is on the device, and the newest transcript in a pane's folder may be a neighbour's, so every other pane sends `{type: "conversation", state: "none"}` and shows its terminal, and so does a pane whose session has written nothing yet.
A page is the newest 30 messages, or 256 KiB of their text, before a byte cursor, read backwards in 512 KiB windows (`hide_session::read_page_before`) and never more than 8 MiB a request; the offset of its oldest message is the cursor `older` sends back, and the phone keeps at most 300 messages.
What the agent appends is read the same way down to where the last read ended, when the file's length changed, so a transcript line of any size is passed over rather than stopping the reader; a catch-up past 8 MiB, or a file that shrank, sends the newest page again.
Cursor pages follow native turn and step references backwards under the same 30-message, 256 KiB text and 8 MiB read limits; polling follows its graph checkpoint rather than database length, so a same-length native append is observed and a rewritten prefix resets the page.
An older Cursor pager requires its retained source stamp to remain current, and every page and idle poll repeats native owner and checkout proof.
Only the operator's prompts, the agent's text and an interruption leave hided, each cut at 20,000 characters and marked; tool calls, tool output and injected context stay on the Mac.
The terminal reads the pane with `pane.read` (`source: recent`, `format: ansi`), 200 lines at first and 200 more per pull up to 1000; it sends rows only when their text or the asked count changed, because Herdr's read `revision` counts pane state rather than output, and offers older rows only when Herdr reports it cut some (`truncated`).
A reply is `pane.send_input` with the text and then the `enter` key; the quick keys are `enter`, `esc`, `up`, `down` and `ctrl+c`.
A phone writes to Herdr directly, so the rule that a pane whose agent ends, sleeps or wakes takes no input is the core's input gate (`herdr-core/src/input_gate.rs`), which the runtime keeps equal to its sleep records (an `Ending`, `Sleeping`, `Waking` or `Failed` record closes the pane; a refused end, a woken agent or a closed pane opens it).
A writer that bypasses a node's terminal (a phone's `input`, the agent's find keys) takes an admission from the gate immediately before its write and holds it until the write returns; a closed pane refuses it (`asleep`), never judged from a list read earlier, and the worker that ends the agent closes the pane first and waits for the admissions already held before it signals the agent, so a write admitted before the end lands in the agent and none lands behind it.
A write still held when the worker's six seconds pass is not waited out and not ended under: the worker signals nothing, logs `agent_sleep.drain_stalled`, and the sleep fails like an end the agent refused (the agent stays awake, the pane reopens, the pane backs off for an hour), so that write lands in the agent.
The gate is a flag and a counter behind its own small lock, taken by the runtime without waiting and by the end worker off the runtime's lock; nothing holds `Mutex<Runtime>` across a write.
A node's terminal drops a key, a paste and the bytes a screen answers its terminal with for the same panes (`TerminalControl::Asleep`, which the core sends from the same records); a paste already held for a pane that sleeps waits with the keys typed behind it.
That path is not behind the gate, because the node can be another machine, so a key or paste the node accepted before it heard `Asleep` can still land in the shell once the end has begun.
The window is bounded: automatic sleep never takes a pane on screen (`SleepStore::due` skips every pane of `agent_sleep_on_screen`, the core's shown tabs and a node's `shown_node_tabs`), and a key reaches only the keyboard's pane, which is always shown; so only an operator who picks Sleep agent on a pane they are typing into, or a paste stalled at that moment, can meet it, and only for the time the node's `Asleep` takes to arrive.
What would close it is a node acknowledgement bound to the sleep's generation, which the end worker would wait for beside the gate's drain; it is not built because that window needs the operator's own action to open.
The phone's list still carries the core's `sleep` (`PhoneSleep`: state `sleeping`, `waking` or `failed`, the failure's plain reason, the age and the last progress line), but only to draw the detail.
An `input` is answered from its request id's claim first, so a repeat of a reply that landed, or whose landing is in doubt, keeps its `ok` or `uncertain` after the agent sleeps; only a new write meets the gate, and a refused one claims nothing.
`wake_agent` names a pane the list holds and an optional `fresh`; for a `sleeping` or `failed` row hided dispatches the desktop's own `agent_wake` event for that pane, a `waking` row is acknowledged without a second dispatch, and any other pane is `unavailable`; the answer is the `sleep_result` frame with the pane's `<device>|<pane>` key as `sleep_id`, and the row's next list carries the progress.
A reply is at most 2,000 characters with no control characters, and a request id the phone repeats after a lost answer is written once: a repeat answers `ok` after a confirmed write, `in_flight` while the first is still running, and `uncertain` when the first write may have reached the pane, so the phone asks the operator to check rather than typing it again.
Neither path goes through the attach set or a core key event, so a phone never moves the desktop's visible tab, focus, terminal size or attach window; a device pane uses the core's existing API connection to that device, and a device that is not connected reads as `device_unreachable`.

Push is Web Push that hided signs and encrypts itself with `ring`: a VAPID ES256 key made once and kept in `mobile.json`, RFC 8291 `aes128gcm` bodies, and a POST through `ureq` over rustls on a blocking thread, never under the core lock.
Only the push services' own hosts are accepted as endpoints (Apple, FCM, Mozilla, Windows), parsed as an `https` URL with no userinfo and no port but 443 and posted without following redirects, plus a loopback endpoint in a debug build for the e2e.
`agent_state::push::Transitions` (`agent_state/turn.rs`) owns notification state and the ten-minute disappearance ledger; hided owns subscriptions, mode policy and delivery.
The first list only seeds the transitions; after it, a root agent entering Needs You or Done sends one notice per subscribed phone, a descendant's question, approval or error raises its root, and the notice's tag (`device|root pane`) replaces the one before it.
A push payload carries the root's row title as `title`, `state` (`needs_you` or `done`), its published `status_code` and exact chosen second-line `detail`, the project as `place`, the `tag`, `device_id`, `pane_id` and `clear`.
The sentence is untranslated and never logged; terminal content is still excluded (operator-approved change 2026-10-13, [status-model.md: With and without Hide AI](status-model.md#with-and-without-hide-ai)).
The phone page gives its worker the translated group and row-status words, kept in `hide-phone-words` so a closed app still notifies: with a sentence the body is the row word, sentence and project joined by ` · `; without one the old group word and project remain.
An older worker ignores the new fields; a new worker with an older page's words uses the group word, and with no stored words it draws only the sentence and project.
Encryption accepts at most 3,993 plaintext bytes, so the aes128gcm HTTP body is at most 4,096 bytes including its 103-byte overhead; a payload beyond that returns the fixed size-limit failure through the existing push delivery diagnostic before a POST.
An agent that leaves the list is remembered for ten minutes, so one that comes back in the same state is not announced again; the cost is that the notification of a root that left for good is cleared up to ten minutes later.
The mode decides whether it is sent: never for 끔, only while no desktop renderer is connected for 앱이 닫혀 있을 때만, and always for 항상; a phone viewing that agent's detail is skipped in every mode.
A root that turned Seen rides the next notice's `clear` list, and the app closes those notifications when it opens, because iOS drops a subscription that receives a push it does not show.
A 404 or 410 answer drops that subscription, and the phone registers again on its next connection.

The desktop app's agent notifications (PRD first-run-onboarding D-09, D-10) run the same `Transitions` a second time in `hided/src/desktop_notices.rs`, on the projection the follower already read, so a burst still serializes one snapshot however many consumers it has, and independent of Mobile: the phones' ledger and the desktop's never share a seed.
A desktop window asks for them with the `desktop_notices` event (`{wanted}`), which hided accepts only from a desktop client and never sends to the core; the demand is per connection (`demand.rs`), so a closed window takes its own with it, and the first projection after the demand turns on only seeds, so nothing already waiting is announced.
A judgment that announces or clears something is one `agent_notice` frame (`{notices, clear}`, including the root's `status_code` and `detail`) broadcast to the desktop windows with a backlog of 16 (a browser tab holds no receiver); one function, `judge_pass` in `hided/src/mobile/mod.rs`, decides each pass for both sides, so the phones' and the desktop's switches never change what the other announces; a window that falls behind misses those notifications, which is logged (`agent_notice.lagged`), and the next change is announced as usual.
The core stores separate desktop Needs You (`desktop_notifications`) and Done (`desktop_notifications_done`) choices.
A missing Done choice in an older store inherits the former shared choice; new installs default both off and the wizard enables Needs You only.
Each toggle is one event; Not now disables both in one event, repeated choices publish nothing, and both use the existing coalesced off-lock UI-state writer.
A window asks for notices while either is on, filters each notice by its own group, words it in the interface language and hands it to the host, which drops it while a Hide window has the keyboard and closes the ones `clear` names.
The app icon count follows Needs You only; phone push settings stay independent.
While Mobile is on with a phone paired, hided skips the ten-minute idle exit.
The records are `mobile_transport`, `mobile_pairing`, `mobile_phone`, `mobile_push` and `mobile_store` in `Logs/core.jsonl`, and none carries a code, a credential or a subscription key.
The operator notice for a held letter or an unanswered inactivity warning uses the same delivery: `push::notice_route` judges, from values only and under the Mobile mutex rather than `Mutex<Runtime>`, whether a phone can be reached at all (key, mode against the connected renderers, the Mobile switch, a subscription), and a phone that cannot be reached is not sent to; Herdr is tried next unless this Mac has no socket for it.
Each channel's reason is a code, and when both fail the one `human.channels_failed` record names them (`docs/delivery.md`).

### Panes, tabs and the attach window in the web shell

The center draws the visible tab's `pane_layouts` entry as nested CSS grids (`web/src/PaneGrid.tsx`): a split is a two-track grid sized by Herdr's ratio and every leaf is a pane with its own xterm instance (`web/src/terminals.ts`, keyed by pane id).
An instance lives as long as the core streams its pane (PRD S2 D-05, amendment 1): leaving a tab parks its terminals in a hidden lot in the document, still fed by the chunks the hub keeps sending for every attached pane, and coming back re-parents them into the pane hosts and fits them, so the last frame is on screen in the same tick and only a size change goes out (`terminal_viewport` with `new_view: false`, `terminal_resize`).
A chunk for a pane that has never been shown is dropped; its first show requests a full frame with `new_view`, as does a parked pane's next show after a self-contained snapshot, which reaches the pane's node directly.
The core passes a `terminal_resize` on to the node only when the size changed, so a new view at the grid its session already runs at gets its full frame from the node, which asks Herdr for the whole screen as a redraw does.
`retainTerminals` disposes an instance only when the core reports the pane `released` or stops listing it; the core attaches all shown Agent tabs on its own tick, fills the bounded retention window with recent tabs, and reports `released` for the rest, which the pane header draws as a caption whose click sends `reconnect_pane`.
The store keeps the `rest` section structurally shared across frames (`web/src/share.ts`): the core resends the whole section whenever any part changes, so an untouched workspace, tab or pane row keeps its object reference and its memoized row does not re-render.
A divider drag moves a guide line and sends one `resize_pane` on release, computed from the first subtree's last pane and the travel over the split's span; a change outside the core's `0.001..=0.5` sends nothing.
A zoomed tab draws only the zoomed pane, over the whole canvas, so its fit and `terminal_resize` follow the full geometry Herdr gave the PTY; the other panes' terminals stay parked and fed.
The wheel is Herdr's (PRD S2 B19): the instance has no local scrollback, a wheel event over a pane becomes whole rows in `web/src/wheel.ts` (trackpad pixels accumulate with their remainder, a wheel notch moves at least one row), the rows of one animation frame go out as one `terminal_scroll` with the cell under the pointer and the crossterm modifier bitset, and the core answers with the viewport frame; ⌥ + wheel is left to the browser.
The core routes that event by the pane's session (`scroll_pane` in `runtime/terminal.rs`): a controlled pane's session writes `terminal.scroll`, which Herdr routes to the program's mouse handling or the history; a pane another client on the same server controls is observed, and an observer has no writer, so its wheel moves Herdr's viewport with `pane.scroll` on this machine's Herdr or the device's, one request in flight per pane with the wheels that arrive meanwhile summed into the next; and a pane with no session yet (no size reported, or its attach still starting after a tab switch) keeps the lines for its first frame.
Herdr keeps one viewport per pane, so an observer's scroll moves the other client's view too.
When Herdr moves nothing for an observer (an alternate-screen program has no history in the viewport, and only the controlling client reaches the program), the pane's terminal row carries `scroll_held_elsewhere` and the header says so, until a later wheel moves or this client takes control.
Only Herdr's own answer raises it: a pane with no route to its Herdr, or a Herdr that is not reached, logs `terminal.scroll_failed` and leaves the transport state to say why.
The wheel has one owner: the capture listener stops the event before xterm sees it, because xterm turns a wheel over a buffer without scrollback (ours, and any alternate-screen program) into cursor-key bytes on the PTY and answers a mouse-tracking program with its own wheel report, both of which Herdr already decides from `terminal_scroll`.
Herdr redraws the viewport in place, so xterm would keep a drag selection on the same cells while the text under it moved: the wheel remembers the rows the selection covers (`follow` in `web/src/terminals.ts`), and the frame that moves them is matched against that text (`followScroll` in `web/src/selection.ts`) to put the selection on the rows it went to, clipped to the screen, or, when none is left, to drop its highlight and keep its copy text (`kept`, also held selected in xterm's input textarea so the browser fires a copy event for ⌘C and the Edit menu) until the next click, drag, select all or key; a selection xterm moved itself, or a drag that replaced it, is left alone.
Keyboard focus has one reporter: a pane's textarea gaining focus under the pointer is the operator moving focus and goes out as `focus_pane`, while the focus the shell itself moves to follow the snapshot's focused pane (`focusTerminal`) is never reported back, because that echo, arriving at the core while Herdr's confirmation of the previous move was still in flight, kept two panes trading focus on a slow runner.
A snapshot older than the operator's last click must not pull the keys back, and the page tells such a snapshot from a real move by state, not by time: `web/src/ws.ts` numbers each operator `focus_pane` it sends (`client_id` for the page load, `sequence` per page, counted only once the frame has left the socket), the core records the last number it applied for each page in `focused.operator_focus` of the snapshot, in the same event that moves or refuses the focus, and `PaneView` follows the snapshot's focused pane only while the number applied has caught up with the number sent (`operatorFocusSent` and `operatorFocusApplied` in the store, `web/src/operatorFocus.ts`).
Once caught up, a snapshot whose focus moves onto another pane is a real move (the core followed Herdr, or refused the click after an earlier answer had moved it) and is followed as before.
What the page follows is the focus moving onto a pane, never the catching up itself: the answer to a click on the pane that was already focused moves nothing, so the keyboard stays wherever the operator took it while the answer was on its way (the sidebar, Overview), where following the catching up pulled it back into the terminal (#608).
For the same reason a refused click whose answer leaves the focus on the pane it was already on moves no keys, as before the numbering: the keys stay on the clicked pane and the focus ring shows the core's.
A move that arrives before the snapshot has caught up waits for it once, and is dropped when the focus leaves that pane first or when the keyboard is no longer where it was when the move arrived, because the operator has taken it elsewhere since.
The record is capped at 16 pages, oldest evicted with a `pane.focus.sequence_evicted` diagnostic (a page whose entry was seen and is gone takes everything it sent as applied, because the core reads it as having nothing in flight), and a new connection starts with nothing awaited, because a number lost on a closed socket will never be answered.
A remote pane's focus goes to its host as `remote_control` and is not numbered: the local core never applies it, so it has nothing to acknowledge, and the same guard does not cover a stale device session.
A pointer gesture follows one routing state in `web/src/terminals.ts` (PRD S2 B20): a drag selects locally in xterm, and a single primary click that never left its cell is replayed on release as one `terminal_click` with the pressed cell and the crossterm modifiers, after the `focus_pane` the press already sent; ⌥ + press and a multi-click stay local and send nothing.
The cell is read against xterm's own screen element, which is exactly `cols` by `rows` cells; the pane host around it is larger by the fit's remainder, and a cell derived from the host drifts by up to one row and one column toward the far edge, which is where a full-screen program's one-row hint sits (the first real-session clicks on Claude Code's "Jump to bottom" missed for that reason).
The core decides what the program hears (`events.rs`: the detected Claude agent gets an SGR press and release, anything else nothing), and xterm's own mouse reports never fire because Herdr's frames carry no mouse mode: a `terminal.frame` is a `CSI row;1H` redraw of every row with only synchronized-output and cursor-visibility modes, so xterm's mouse tracking is never switched on.
The same frames pad every row to the full width with written spaces and never soft-wrap, so a copy of the selection is assembled in `web/src/selection.ts` rather than by xterm: trailing whitespace is trimmed from every row, a row whose last cell is non-blank continues onto the next row with no break, and a real line end stays `\n`, and the leading whitespace every non-blank line shares is removed as the program's margin while the indentation between lines is kept (a single line loses its leading spaces entirely, and a drag that starts past column 0 leaves its cut first line out of the margin); a hard line that exactly fills the width is joined too, which a continuation flag in the frame record would close.
An Escape the shell answers (a cycle, a close confirmation, the sheet, the find bar) is stopped at the window capture listener, because the pane's textarea keeps keyboard focus under the sheet and xterm would send the same press to the program as an ESC byte.
Closing follows one flow in `web/src/close.ts`: an unknown activity status asks for `refresh_status` first, a working pane asks once, an idle pane closes with `confirmed: false`.

### Settings and workspace management in the web shell

Project choices live in the additive, versioned `ui_state.project_settings` map, keyed by `hide_project::project_id` of the owning device and its canonical main-worktree root.
The catalog supplies that root; neither the visible workspace id nor the stored primary checkout identifies the settings, and a remote root is never resolved on this machine.
An unset issue or base field retains the legacy path-based choice, an explicit automatic choice shadows it, and a partial patch leaves omitted fields unchanged.
`project_settings_set` admits the exact catalog device/root pair and validates the repository and existing branch before the existing coalesced state writer saves it outside the runtime mutex.
The confirmed record and its consumers move only when that exact save acknowledges the pending record; a failure retains the prior record with a request-scoped failed receipt, and closing a client does not cancel the write.
A core move gives the records of a machine whose id changes the new id and the key made from it (Moving the core), so the choices follow the project to the new core and back.
Records are bounded to 256 projects, and transient receipts to 512 entries so a busy refusal can settle its caller without replacing an active write; receipts are not persisted, and unrelated `ui_state_update` echoes preserve both maps.
Completed receipts displaced by a later caller remain request-scoped within that bound, so a coalesced snapshot still exposes each saved outcome; clients retain an observed terminal outcome when another writer replaces the project's latest receipt.
An explicit repository is a private provider target, not a git remote or credential change.
The existing GitHub reader separates its cached checks by repository as well as root and generation; changing the confirmed connection removes the previous screen data, moves the generation and rejects late remote answers for the old repository.
Before a connection changes, the record retains up to 200 existing checkout issue references by checkout path, branch and bare number, with their original repository.
Bare issue numbers on those unchanged branches keep that identity after restart, while an explicit owner/repository reference names its own target; prior issue creation and PR replies cannot attach to a different repository's same-number resource.
This adds bounded map lookups to link projection and uses the existing readers and save queue, with no polling, subprocess or machine I/O under the runtime mutex.
Remote worktree requests carry the confirmed device/root base choice, and a base change discards the one old in-flight answer before the coalesced reread.
The search slot likewise refuses joins to an invalidated worker and resolves every fresh target from the confirmed connection before a provider read answers.
`project_memory_navigate` scopes focus and the right lane's Memory tool to one checkout and calls that lane forward; a remote lane changes only when the existing pending choice reaches its exact front, and a refused focus drops the choice.
An accepted newer checkout, pane, tab, device, file or local Memory navigation supersedes that pending Memory choice before its late acknowledgment or checkout arrival can move the screen.
Memory projects expose their own hook-preparation and failed-action state, preserving confirmed values and cards for Retry.
Settings retries only its enable or disable action; card deletion, forgetting and promotion retain the existing Memory tool's confirmation and card identity.
Tests in `herdr-core/src/runtime/tests/project_settings.rs` cover the save acknowledgment, failed save, fallback, identity and late-answer boundary.

#### Project guardrails

A project's protections are a field of its settings record (`guardrails`, record version 2; a version 1 record reads as every protection off), saved by the same `project_settings_set` and validated in the core (`hide_agent_hooks::guardrails::Rules::validate`: at most 32 paths of at most 200 bytes, no duplicate, each a pattern the gitignore reader takes); a Git protection on a plain folder is refused `project_settings_not_git`.
The hooks never ask the daemon: each machine holds one policy file its agents' hooks read (`~/.hide/agent-hooks/guardrails.json`), and the core has that machine's node write it (`Call::GuardrailPolicy`, `herdr-core/src/runtime/guardrails.rs`), so the decision stays on the agent's machine and a tool call costs no round trip ([agent-hooks.md](agent-hooks.md#project-guardrails)).
The file names every project of that machine with a protection on, whether or not it is registered, since a project shown from a Herdr workspace is guarded too; removing a project clears its protections, a save still on its way included (`Runtime::forget_project_guardrails`).
A settings record keeps the fields a newer build wrote (`ProjectSettings::unknown`, `Rules::unknown`), so saving a newer record again here loses none of them; a change keeps the record's copy of them rather than its own, since the shell sends back what it was shown, so nothing a change names beyond this build's protections reaches the policy every hook reads.
A save that moves a machine's policy is delivered first and saved after: the record waits outside the frame the state writer saves until its machine answers, so Saved means the machine has the new file, and a refused or timed-out write (10 s) fails the save `project_settings_policy_failed`, keeps the confirmed record and writes the confirmed policy once more.
One worker thread per machine (`herdr-core-guardrails`) writes one call at a time and computes the policy when it takes the job, so a burst of changes is one write of the newest state; each write carries a rising sequence and the node keeps the last it applied, so a write that outlived its 10 s wait never lands over a newer one and is answered with the sequence the node holds, and a core whose sequence fell behind it (another core's write, a clock set back) moves past it and writes again, up to three times in a row before the saves fail; nothing is added under `Mutex<Runtime>` but the map reads that build the policy, and a device that is not connected is written when it is, not on every save.
Only a core on the folder the account's hide picks with no `HIDE_STATE_DIR` (`~/.hide/state`, or `$XDG_STATE_HOME/hide` where that is set: `Env::account_state_folder`, passed as `CoreOptions::account_state_folder`) writes this machine's file: one on a relocated folder, a test's or a verification run's on the operator's account, writes nothing under `~/.hide` (`hided/tests/it/connect_build.rs`), and its saves settle as delivered, since this machine's hooks follow the account's own core.
The core writes its own machine's file after `Core::create` and each device's when it is Ready, so a file changed outside Hide is put right at the next start or connection; a device that goes away is forgotten.
Tests in `herdr-core/src/runtime/tests/guardrails.rs` cover the ordering, the failed delivery, a base branch change, the folder and path refusals, the removal and the start, and `hided/tests/it/node_contract.rs` the node's write.

Interface language is a core-owned UI preference: `interface_language_set` alone changes `ui_state.interface_language`, and unrelated UI saves preserve it.
The existing coalesced persistence queue stores the raw choice; snapshot serialization publishes English for an invalid stored value while keeping that value and logging a diagnostic.
Clients resolve an unset choice from their own primary system language and apply only confirmed snapshot choices.
[LOCALIZATION.md](LOCALIZATION.md) owns the supported languages, translation boundary and current integration coverage.

The color theme is owned the same way: `color_theme_set` alone changes `ui_state.color_theme` (`hide` or `neutral`, Hide by default), `ui_state_update` carries it through, and the current value again saves nothing.
The store keeps it as text beside the mode (`theme`), so a value this build does not know loads as Hide with the `ui_state.color_theme_unknown` diagnostic instead of making the store unreadable.
The shell puts `palette-hide` on the document root beside the mode class, and the phone projection carries the value so a paired phone draws it; the desktop host cannot read the store before it attaches, so its window color before the page paints is Hide Dark, read from `web/src/tokens.css` by `desktop/scripts/build.mjs`.

The agent logo view mode is a core-owned UI preference the same way: `agent_logo_mode_set` alone changes `ui_state.agent_logo_mode`, an unknown stored value reads as `hidden` with a `ui_state.agent_logo_mode_unknown` diagnostic, and unrelated UI saves preserve it.

Settings (`web/src/SettingsSheet.tsx` for the sheet and one module per page under `web/src/settings/`, rules and the list's order in `web/src/settings.ts`) lists its pages in two groups, App (General, Notifications, Devices, Shortcuts) and This Mac (Agents, Hide AI, Mobile, GitHub), with About apart, and opens from the sidebar's gear or ⌥; which page is open is page-local UI state (`web/src/ui.ts`): General (`GeneralTab`, `AppearanceGroup`) writes `accent_hex` and `font_size` through `ui_state_update` (the sidebar's right edge writes `sidebar_width` the same way, 220 to 440, a value outside refused into the diagnostic log), Notifications (`NotificationsTab`) writes the desktop switch the first-run wizard shares and the `mobile` frame's push mode, GitHub (`GithubTab`) sends `github_request` with `refresh` for its Check again, and About (`AboutTab`) reads the `daemon` frame and `status.{herdr,environment,diagnostics}`; its Herdr Version and Protocol rows read `status.herdr`, whose received version and protocol the core takes from the accepted `session.snapshot` answer (`SessionSnapshotPayload.herdr_version`, the protocol revision it was built against), never from the failure path alone. Agents reads each device row's `kit` (its eight `agents` with their pieces, `partial` and `features`, and `sessions`, how many of the agent's sessions run there now), shows one machine at a time, and sends `kit_check` (the core reads this Mac and every connected device, so Check again is one event for all), and `kit_agent_set` and `kit_reinstall` for the machine on screen, Devices (`DevicesTab`, one `DeviceRow` per machine, `DeviceDetails` for what a healthy row does not show) reads each row's `kit`, mentions only a part that needs the operator and sends `ssh_hosts_list` when its Add dialog opens and `register_device` (the alias of the Host chosen from `status.ssh_hosts`, the name, always the kit consent the dialog states, and an optional device Herdr socket; the dialog is opened by the Add device button, or `ui.openAddDevice()` from the rail and the Add project host list), `kit_check`, `kit_reinstall`, `device_host_consent`, `device_host_retry`, `test_device`, `retry_connect`, `focus_device` and `remove_device` (the sidebar's device switcher sends `focus_device` too), Agents also sends `agent_sleep_set` (`after_hours`: null for Never, 12, 24 or 72) and counts this machine's sleeping agents from the navigator, Hide AI reads `status.background_ai` and sends `ai_settings` and the worktree-name switch of `issue_settings_set`, and Shortcuts writes the running host's own set (`browser_shortcut_bindings` in a browser, `shortcut_bindings` in the desktop app).
Every row shows what the snapshot says; an edit is pending until the snapshot carries it, and a refusal is matched to the edit by the core's `last_error` kind and time.
The core clears `last_error` at its next event of any kind, so every refusal it records is also written to the diagnostic log as `error.reported` with its `error_kind` and message; a client that never reads the snapshot in between (a fixture, a headless driver) still finds the reason there.
While a device is selected the sheet shows a device chip saying that selecting it changes where files, Git and panes run and not where Settings live; with This Mac selected no page speaks of ownership (PRD settings-cleanup D-02, replacing S5.5 B35); the Agents page lists one machine's agents from its install kit at a time, This Mac first and then each device in the Devices order, with Reinstall only on an agent whose piece needs it (PRD settings-cleanup B13, B67).
A device row shows only what that device reported (its Herdr version, its helper's platform, and the staged connection test's SSH, auth, Herdr, protocol, PTY, SFTP and Git results), and a refused connection names its step (`problem`: `host_key_changed`, `host_key_unknown` or `authentication`, read off the core's own known_hosts and sign-in words by `remote::connection_problem`) with the action it needs; Hide never edits known_hosts or asks for a password (B36, B38).
Add device lists the account's Host entries from `status.ssh_hosts`, which the `ssh_hosts_list` event fills (`runtime/ssh_hosts.rs` asks `DeviceConnector::ssh_hosts`, answered by `hide-node/src/ssh/hosts.rs`): one worker off the lock reads `<home>/.ssh/config` and the files it `Include`s (at most 16 files of 256 KiB, four levels deep; `~` and a relative path resolve like `ssh` does, `*` and `?` match in the last component), keeps each concrete alias in file order (a `Host` line with several aliases yields each; a pattern with `*`, `?` or `!`, and a name starting with `-`, names none), and resolves up to 32 of them with `ssh -F <home>/.ssh/config -G -- <alias>`, four children at a time, each an owned child under a three-second deadline with a login environment plus `HOME`.
`-G` prints the resolved configuration without connecting, so `address` reads `user@host:port` and nothing is opened or written; `-F` names the account's own config because `ssh` would otherwise read the passwd entry's home.
Each registered device's alias is resolved too (at most 16 more), so `added_as` carries the device's name for an alias a device already uses and for any alias that reaches the same `user@host:port`.
An alias with no address carries `problem` (`ssh_missing`, `ssh_failed`, `timed_out`); a missing or unreadable config is `ready` with no host.
A request while one runs joins it, a dropped runtime stops the children, and the list is read again on each request, never stored: a device is still an alias and a name.
A sign-in refusal is the auth stage's failure alone in the test, since the handshake and known_hosts check had passed.
Copy diagnostics carries versions, paths, states and the core's recent diagnostics, redacts anything token-shaped, and has no terminal output or pane input among its inputs.
`retry_connect` is a new connection attempt: it retires the device's coordinator and transports and connects again from its registration, keeping the device focus, and a connected device refuses it.
`remove_device` removes Hide's own record of a device and nothing on it (PRD S5.5 B26): its registration, its project registrations and pins, its expanded folders, its open file tabs and its closed items go, while its host, Herdr server, panes, agents and folders are left as they are; a dirty tab closed this way leaves its draft in the browser, where a draft no tab stands for is offered to export or discard, and the confirmation counts the projects, tabs and drafts before anything is removed.
Before `remove_device` is sent the page writes every draft of that device's tabs it still has queued, and a draft that could not be stored (B44) holds the removal: the confirmation names it until it is saved or exported (Export releases exactly the text it downloaded, so a later edit holds the removal again, and the confirmation then says that draft leaves only as the exported file, because the page cannot confirm the download was kept), since closing its tab would lose it; once the drafts are being stored the removal can no longer be kept from the dialog.
The web's `ui_state_update` echo omits `workspace_registrations` and `device_registrations`, which their own events own, so a stale echo can never undo a registration.
The folds are owned the same way: `project_checkouts_fold` (a flip, or a set when it carries `expanded`), `checkout_agents_toggle` and `agent_tree_toggle` apply against the core's own sets, and `ui_state_update` neither reads nor writes `collapsed_workspace_ids`, `expanded_checkout_ids` or `expanded_agent_pane_ids`; a fold computed from the shell's last snapshot and sent as a set loses a toggle the core has not echoed yet.
It also omits `sidebar_width` unless the action explicitly changes that width, so an unrelated UI save cannot undo a drag before its snapshot arrives.
The accent swatches read the `--color-accent-choice-*` tokens; the chosen value replaces `--color-accent`, and `font_size` scales the interface text tokens through `--interface-scale` while the terminal and editor sizes stay their own, and so does the sidebar: its nav carries the generated `.interface-scale-exempt` rule, which declares the text tokens again at scale 1, because a custom property resolves where it is declared and setting `--interface-scale` on the nav alone would change nothing.

The sidebar's project, checkout and agent rows open a menu on a right-click, or the menu key or ⇧F10 on the focused row (`EntryContextMenu` in `web/src/components/entry-menu.tsx`, rules in `web/src/workspaceManage.ts`, the items in docs/UI_BEHAVIOR.md); an action a row cannot use is drawn disabled with its reason, and one the host cannot perform (Finder in a browser tab) is not listed.
Each item is one existing event: `focus_checkout`, `create_tab` (a device's `remote_control create_tab`), `set_primary_checkout`, `workspace_pin_set`, `remove_workspace`, and the tab close flow's `close_tab` for the tab that holds an agent's pane.
Pin and Remove are offered on a row Herdr shows without a registration too (PRD sidebar-context-menus D-14): `workspace_pin_set` with `pinned: true` on such a row registers it with the id, label, root and device the row already carries and pins it in that event, with no filesystem read under the lock, and the next catalog rebuild adopts it as the same row; a device's row qualifies only once its helper grouped it under its root. `remove_workspace` on such a row closes its panes through the same close worker and retires nothing, so the row leaves with Herdr's workspace, and every row carries the removal gate's counts.
An agent row carries the session id Herdr recorded (`session_id`, absent when none), which Copy session id reads.
The dialogs (`web/src/WorkspaceDialogs.tsx`) send `create_worktree`, `set_checkout_purpose` and `remove_worktree` and read only the receipt for their own request (`task_operation` by kind, id and target; `worktree_removal` by checkout and id), so another page's or an older request's answer is never shown as theirs.
Add a project's Clone from URL is one core job in one slot, `repository_clone` (`herdr-core/src/runtime/clone.rs`): the core parses the URL again (`hide_node_link::clone::CloneSource`, which the node parses once more when it reads one off the link: https, ssh, the scp form and `file://`; the name has to be the one the URL gives), refuses a second clone while one runs (`repository.clone_busy`), and lets the same URL and folder sent again join the running one, so a double submit is one clone and one registration.
A worker thread owns the run and takes the lock only to write progress or the result; it asks the core's own node for the clone (`repository_clone`), which reports each new stage and, at least once a second, that it still runs, and the worker answers every report with the cancel flag, so a cancel reaches Git within a second; on the node `hide_host::clone::clone_repository` runs `git clone --progress` with `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, `ssh -o BatchMode=yes` and `GIT_ALLOW_PROTOCOL=https:ssh:file` in its own process group, into a hidden `.<name>.hide-clone-<pid>` staging folder beside the target that only a finished clone is renamed from (refusing to replace anything that appeared meanwhile).
A cancel (`cancel_repository_clone`), the runtime going away, or `STALL_LIMIT` (120 s) without a line from Git stops the whole group and removes the staging folder, so a cancelled, stalled or failed clone leaves nothing under the target's name; a daemon killed mid-clone can leave its hidden staging folder behind, since nothing runs to remove it.
Failures are read from Git's C-locale stderr into one plain sentence (authentication, unknown host key, not found, unreachable host, or Git's last `fatal:` line with credentials removed); the URL is kept only in the job, never in the snapshot or a log line, which carry the host.
A finished clone goes through the same local `create_workspace` event Browse folder sends, so it is registered, refused or deduplicated exactly like a picked folder.
A created worktree's pane is focused with one `focus_pane` once the snapshot lists it, because only a focus request is tracked until Herdr confirms it; a late focus event for the pane left behind cannot undo the move.
A dialog or sheet returns keyboard focus to a terminal through `focusTerminal` on the core's focused pane (`restoreFocus`), never by re-focusing the textarea that held it, which would be reported as the operator moving there.

The device rail, the sidebar's full-height left column (`web/src/components/device-rail.tsx`), always shown unless the operator hid it, sends `focus_device` for a device tile; each tile's Needs You and unseen Done counts are read from the snapshot's agents in the web, and whether the rail shows is the core's `ui_state.device_rail_visible`, persisted and sent with every `ui_state_update` beside `left_sidebar_visible`.
Selecting an SSH device makes it the context (`web/src/remote.ts`, the remote Agent area in `web/src/WorkspaceScreen.tsx`): the sidebar lists that host's Herdr workspaces and agents and the canvas draws its visible tab from `status.remote[].session`, the projection the core already builds over SSH with every id scoped to the target (`remote:<target>:pane:…`).
The web follows the host's own focus rather than keeping a selection, because the core attaches exactly the panes of that host's focused tab and, on a node that dialed in, of the tab each other Agent area shows (`node_shown_agent_tabs`); a pane is placed by the rectangle Herdr reports for it, and there is no divider because a remote pane's size is its host's.
Keystrokes and viewport go out with the scoped pane id to the router, which sends them down that device's node link, and scroll to the core, which sends the wheel the same way; focus, split, zoom, close pane, new tab, tab focus and close tab go out as `remote_control` with the device as `target_id` and a fresh `request_id`, and the core checks every id against that host's session before anything is sent, so a stale or local id is refused rather than retargeted.
The pane's own id decides where a click-to-focus goes, and a close confirmation carries the device it was asked about.
A device that is not connected takes no command: the web says so and sends nothing; its sidebar is only the device's name, `연결 안 됨` and `다시 연결`, never its last tree, while the center keeps the last session on screen under a Retry banner while it is `stale`, and draws the connection's own state when there is no session.
The Explorer, file tabs and History run on the device's own helper; worktree creation and deletion, registration, pin and project removal run through the device's Herdr and helper (see Device catalogs); a tab drag and reopen closed act on the device (see Tab reorder ownership and Reopening locally closed work); find in pane reads the device pane through that device's Herdr, and a device that is not connected answers the reason in the find bar; a remote checkout's purpose is written through the core when the host's Herdr is 0.9.1 or newer.
The core also refuses `create_pane`, whose target is implicit, while a remote device is focused (`pane.device_mismatch`), so a split never falls back to this machine.
Typing into a remote pane makes it the core's terminal pane, so `focus_device` back to this machine, or removing the selected device, hands the keyboard back to `ui_state.selected_pane_id` or the drawn tab's focused pane (`return_keyboard_to_local_pane`).
Registering or removing a device refreshes only the device rows (`rebuild_device_rows`); rebuilding the whole catalog there dropped every checkout's tabs until the next session publish.

### Workspaces in the web shell

The shared Overview (`web/src/Overview.tsx`) is window-local state in `ui.ts` (`overview`: the device shown, the project chip, the open panel and each tab's filters), one device at a time with no project scope, and nothing of it is persisted.
It overlays a mounted Workspace or renders as its central fallback page; its modal is not a Recent Panels visit and Agent cycling uses the existing pane order without a Workspace origin.
Project activation uses `projects.ts` to choose this device's recent usable checkout, then primary, then first usable checkout.
The existing `focus_checkout` payload accepts `project_expanded` alongside checkout disclosure, so admission validates the target before either fold or focus changes and persistence keeps the existing collapsed-project state.


The web shell has a Workspace and one shared Overview page, with Overview also available as a modal over the Workspace (`web/src/{Overview,MainScreen,WorkspaceScreen}.tsx`).
The Overview shows one device's `Tasks · 백로그 · Projects` and Usage (PRD overview-device-tasks); its tasks are the core's (`agent_scope.tasks`, `herdr-core/src/agent_state/tally/tasks.rs`), its backlog the projects' sources, and its Projects tab the disk sweep's measurements.
The page, modal visibility, device, project chip, tab and panel are window-local state in `web/src/ui.ts`, never core state, so each window keeps its own.
Recent Panels restores the shared page, while the transient modal does not enter recent history.
Which screen is showing is the shell's own location (`ui.ts`), not core state: the page starts on the Workspace the core has in front only when the core marks it `resumed`, the Workspace the operator last chose, in this process or before a restart (D-11): a `focus_checkout`, `focus_pane` or `focus_tab` the core accepted, or a device `remote_control` focus or Workspace open once the device's front lands there while its request lives (`PendingChoice` names what drops it), so a refusal chooses nothing and a front only Herdr's own focus moved is not resumed; a first run, a last Workspace that is gone, or any other front starts on Main once the device in front has settled (`startupScreen` in `navigation.ts`), and moving between screens creates or ends nothing.
A device Workspace is known only when that device's session arrives, so the first screen waits for the device in front rather than for this machine's Herdr.
