Main and Overview read only the catalog, the device sessions and the agent rows the snapshot already carries; a device that is connecting or cannot answer shows why and leaves its counts unknown rather than zero.
A project's issues and memos ride on its catalog row as `tasks` and a checkout names its item as `task_key` (PRD task-agents-views D-14, `herdr-core/src/tasks.rs`): each source has an adapter there that projects its own answer into one source-neutral shape, GitHub issues and Local issues today, so the web never reads a GitHub shape and a later source (Linear) is one more adapter.
Every local project has exactly one source: `ui_state.project_issue_sources` holds the operator's choice by project path (the project row's Issue source menu, `entry-menu.tsx`'s `choices` submenu, and the same choice on a 백로그 section's source chip and in the Projects panel, sent as `issue_source_set`), and without one `tasks::source_kind` picks GitHub for a repository that reads as a GitHub repository and Local otherwise.
Local issues live in one file, `local-issues.json` in the state directory (`CoreOptions.local_issues_path`, `herdr-core/src/local_issues.rs`): the runtime reads it once when it is created, holds the store in memory, applies a change there under the lock, and hands the whole store to a save thread that writes through a temporary file and a rename, the UI state's own pattern, so no file I/O happens under `Mutex<Runtime>`; a file that cannot be parsed is an error the source reports, never replaced by an empty one, and the store is capped per project and in projects.
A core move keeps the projects of the machine that gives up the core in the same file, under `devices` by the id the folder names that machine by (`node_migration::reown`), and the move back makes them its projects again.
While that machine is a node that dials the core, the core reads and writes its projects' Local issues there, by its node id (`LocalIssueStore::project` and the writes take the machine); every other entry is kept unchanged through every save.
A Local key names only a project path, so `local_issue_update` and `issue_set_open` name the project too, and a key whose path is not that project's is refused rather than applied to another machine's project at the same path.
A checkout of a Local project is linked by the same chain as a GitHub one (the manual `issue` token, a pane token, `branch.<b>.issue`, then the branch number), read as `L-N`.
Creating an issue is `backlog_add` (see [Session work records](#session-work-records)): a memo lands in the store at once, and a GitHub one is a title-only `gh issue create` on a worker thread whose answer is ingested under the lock; `issue_detail_request` reads what an issue's panel shows the same way (the body, and for a GitHub issue its labels, author, assignees, creation time and the latest three of its comments in one `gh issue view --json`; a Local issue answers from the store with its body and creation time at once), `local_issue_update` edits a Local issue's title and body in the store and answers in `issue_work.update` with its `request_id`, `worktree_name_suggest` asks the background AI for a worktree name (`ai::suggest_worktree_name`, feature `worktree_name`), and each answers in the snapshot's `issue_work` slot, which a later request replaces so a late answer for an older one is dropped.
`github_search` (`{request_id, query}`, PRD cmdk-navigation B16-B19) is the one on-demand GitHub search read: the shell sends it when the operator asks, never while they type, and the core runs it on no timer, so an idle daemon searches nothing.
It covers the core's registered GitHub projects (a local Git project whose source is GitHub) and those of a node that dials it, searched by the repository name the node read with the core's login and left out while the node has not named one (`github_search_targets`), at most 20 of them with a `message` on the answer and a `github_search.capped` diagnostic when more were left out; a project the GitHub reader has not read yet is resolved by the search's own worker with the same `gh repo view --json nameWithOwner`, so the search never depends on what was read earlier, repositories are taken once per `owner/name` ignoring case, a project with no GitHub remote is skipped, and a project that fails to resolve for any other reason fails the search, which then cannot say it covered every project.
With no GitHub project it answers `ready` and empty, and a query that is empty after trimming or over 200 characters is answered `failed` in the slot, never as nothing found.
A search is two calls however many repositories there are (GitHub allows about 30 search calls a minute): one `gh search prs` and one `gh search issues`, a `--repo owner/name` for each repository, `--limit 20 --json <fixed fields>`, and the query's words as separate arguments after `--`, so it matches like a search box rather than as one quoted phrase and no word can be read as a flag; the shape is one entry of the node's `gh` allowlist (`hide_node_link::gh::is_search_call`: fixed positions, fixed field constants, up to 20 path-safe repositories, words within the query cap), and `gh search prs` has no head branch, so a hit carries none.
Every call runs under the 15-second `COMMAND_TIMEOUT`, on one `spawn_issue_worker` thread off `Mutex<Runtime>` that makes the repository resolutions and then the two calls one after another, and the answer holds at most 20 pull requests and then 20 issues.
A hit from a repository that was not asked for is dropped, because a `repo:` qualifier typed in the query would widen GitHub's search to it, and a hit's link must be on `https://github.com/`.
It answers in `issue_work.search` (`request_id`, `query`, `phase` `working`, `ready` or `failed`, `results` of `{kind, repository, number, title, state, url, is_draft}` with `state` `open`, `closed` or `merged`, and `message`), absent from the wire until the first search and, like the sibling slots, plain strings.
One search runs at a time (`GithubSearchWork`): a request with the running search's request id or query joins it and starts no second worker, any other request becomes the single pending one and replaces an older pending one, and it starts when the running search ends; the slot shows the latest request as `working` at once, and an answer whose request the slot no longer shows is dropped when it lands, as `ingest_issue_detail` does.
A failed call, any repository's, fails the whole search with the short slot message `GitHub 검색에 실패했습니다.` and a `github_search.failed` diagnostic that carries the request id and the real reason; a worker that panics is a failed search too, so it can never leave the one search slot taken.
`create_worktree` with a `task_key` writes the link into the new worktree's metadata after its identity is confirmed, and a `prompt` goes to the agent as its first argument (the first prompt, above); a prompt that cannot be passed fails the agent start, and the task operation carries why.
The GitHub adapter reads what the existing GitHub reader already fetched off the lock (`github.rs`); every local Git project is read already, so the Overview's 백로그 and the sidebar's 태스크 list only send `github_request` for the projects they show, with `refresh` naming a project to read it again and `look` (sent by `web/src/githubLook.ts` when the window gains focus or a project comes to the front) asking only for a read when the last answer is 30 seconds old, and a read that fails keeps the last tasks with the failure beside them.
The GitHub adapter also reads each open issue's `blockedBy` in one `gh api graphql` query per pass, grouped by repository and at most 20 blockers per issue, and keeps only open blockers as `blocked_by` (a key and the id this repository shows); `gh issue list --json` has no dependency field.
Each issue's labels (name and colour) come in the same `gh issue list` answer and, for an issue read by number, in the same query (`labels(first: 100)`), so a card's labels add no request; they ride the task as `labels` in GitHub's order, a colour that is not six hex digits is no colour, a Local issue has none, and the wire leaves the field off when it is empty.
The same query reads each issue's `subIssuesSummary { total completed }` and `subIssues(first: 100)` (GitHub's limit per issue), so sub-issue progress adds no request and no polling; `total` and `completed` ride the task as GitHub counts them, and the web finds a sub-issue's pull request among the project's `pull_requests` by their `closing_issues`.
A dependency query that fails or answers with `errors` is a failure, never an empty answer: the pass keeps the issues it read, restores the blockers and sub-issues read before, records `issue_dependencies.unavailable` in the diagnostic log, and states the failure on the task source.
A local Git project's catalog row also carries `pull_requests`, the ones the PRs tool and the card show (PRD overview-lenses-prs D-32, D-52, work-tab-pr-lens, sidebar-task-lens): the open ones and the merged ones whose worktree record is still here or that merged in the last 14 days, taken from the `gh pr list` answer `github.rs` already holds, so the view adds no read and the row stays bounded by that list's cap; the field is left off the wire when empty.
The list is two `gh pr list` calls at the same time, every pull request without its checks and the open ones with only their checks, and a merged or closed pull request keeps the checks read while it was open or reads `Unknown` (`github.rs::list_pull_requests`; [status-model.md](status-model.md#github-status-in-the-workspace-row)).
A pull request is tied to a checkout in one place, `github::pull_request_for_checkout`, which every surface asks (the sidebar row, the worktree catalog, an agent row's request block, `pr_link_issue` and `pr_delegate`): an open one by head branch unless it comes from a fork, a merged or closed one only at the checkout's exact HEAD (`CheckoutSnapshot::head_sha`), because a branch name reused for new work must not show the old merge; `refresh_worktree_projection` decides it again when a catalog read moves a HEAD.
`head_sha` is the commit the worktree reader already holds, so no Git process or file read joins the lock.
Until that reader first answers, and for a checkout it never lists, the commit `Repository::head_oid` read from Git's own files stands in (`CheckoutSnapshot::head_oid`, core only), so a merged pull request restored from the saved answer reattaches in the first frame instead of after the reader's first pass; the reader's value wins as soon as it exists, because it reruns on a moved HEAD or ref while the file value is only as new as the row's last build.
`head_oid` and each repository's remotes (`PathFacts::remotes`, the `[HOST/]OWNER/NAME` each `remote.<name>.url` names, unknown when the config includes another file or a remote names a server in a form the node does not read, such as `http://`) are read by the core's own node with the catalog's other path facts (`Call::PathFacts`, `hide_host::catalog::path_facts`), which the coordinator asks off the lock before it builds the catalog, so `build_catalog` and the reconcile under the lock compare names and read no folder; a runtime the coordinator does not feed (a fixture, startup before Herdr answers) asks its own node before it takes anything from the session, and the `packed-refs` read behind it is streamed and capped at 8 MiB, with no process.
The coordinator establishes catalog ownership before its first ingest and runs path facts on a `BackgroundRead` worker, so neither the first answer nor a refresh holds topology publication.
There is one active read; changed paths coalesce to the latest request, whose connection and wanted paths fence the answer.
Agent and PR display metadata cannot invalidate path facts when the wanted paths are unchanged; current registrations and the worktree catalog independently fence the prepared catalog at its final Runtime application.
A completed answer wakes the existing coalesced republish channel, and the catalog is built from the current replica rather than a captured session.
Until facts arrive, an existing tab retains its confirmed checkout and a new directory creates no project row; failure retains accepted facts and goes to diagnostics.
The reader checks owner cancellation between bounded path chunks and joins its current finite node call when the coordinator ends.
A repository's files are written by whatever runs in it, so each (`HEAD`, a ref, a pointer file, the config) is opened only when its name resolves to a regular file, without waiting on a pipe, and refused past a cap (a pointer at 64 KiB, the config at 1 MiB).
Discovery takes only a repository whose own `HEAD` is a regular file, as it always has; the files read after it (a ref, `packed-refs`, the config) follow a link as Git does, so a config a dotfile manager links in still names its branches, and one that resolves to something other than a regular file reads as `branch_notes.read_failed`.
A row the reader never lists keeps the file value for at most the catalog's 30-second refresh after its HEAD moves.
`PullRequestSnapshot::head_oid` and `cross_repository` stay in the core and are never sent to the shell.
Hide writes to GitHub with exactly four commands, all behind the exact-shape allowlist the core's own node checks before it runs `gh` (`hide_node_link::gh::allowed`) and all on a worker thread off the lock: `gh issue create --title T --body B` (a new issue), `gh pr edit N --body B` (a pull request's body, and nothing else of it), and the PR card's two, `gh pr merge N [--merge|--squash|--rebase] --match-head-commit SHA` and `gh api --method PUT repos/OWNER/NAME/pulls/N/update-branch -f expected_head_sha=SHA`.
`pr_link_issue` links pull request N to an issue of the project's source: a Local issue is Hide's link alone (the branch's issue link and the Workspace token, through the same task-operation write as the issue link), a GitHub issue of the same repository is that link plus `Closes #M` written by `github::write_closing_line`, which reads the body with `gh pr view N --json body` just before and writes nothing when a closing keyword already names the issue, so a retry never adds a second line; `new_issue` makes the issue first and keeps it when the body write then fails.
`pr_feedback_read` reads a pull request's body, failed checks and standing change requests in one `gh pr view N --json body,statusCheckRollup,reviews`, for a new issue's fields and an agent's first prompt; `pr_delegate` starts an agent with a prompt on the pull request's head branch, in its checkout with `agent_start_in_checkout`'s path, or with none through Herdr's `worktree.create` on that existing branch, fetched from origin first when only origin has it (`worktree_control::spawn_existing_branch_worktree`, the fetch under the same bounded Git runner), so a new branch is never made; a node project's branch is checked over the node's link and its worktree made by the node's Herdr.
Each answers in the snapshot's `pr_work` slot (`link`: the step, `create`, `link` or `body`, its phase and the issue made; `feedback`), or, for the start, the task-operation slot every start reports through; a later request replaces the slot, and a failure is also a `pull_requests` diagnostic.
The fields are additive and none is a string enum, so the shell decodes the snapshot as before.

The PR card (PRD work-tab-pr-lens, sidebar-task-lens D-43, D-50) is four events and one slot.
`pr_open {workspace_id, pr_number, checkout_id?, open_workspace?}` is the one event that opens a card: it puts the card in front of the lane's 보기 slot (`ui_state.detail`, below) and starts the card's read, leaving the Workspace, its Agent tab and the keyboard where they are; with `open_workspace`, sent from Home, the core also chooses the Workspace (the checkout of the pull request's agent, else of its branch, else the project's primary) and brings the pull request's live agent forward, so the web sends one event and the screen is never half moved.
There is no `PR #N` display in File Views any more; a card a file saved as a View is dropped as `workspace-views.json` loads (`workspace_views::drop_retired_cards`).
`pr_card_read {workspace_id, pr_number, look_again?}` reads what the five-minute list does not carry in one `gh api graphql` query (`hide_node_link::gh::pr_card_query`, an exact shape the allowlist names by owner, name and number): the head commit, the body, the first 100 files with additions and deletions, the first 50 review threads, the repository's allowed merge methods, whether it has a merge queue on the pull request's base, the viewer's permission, the repository's `.gitattributes`, the head commit's first 100 checks and the last 50 reviews, from which the card's timeline takes when the checks completed and when the standing review was given (`herdr-core/src/github/card.rs`; PRD pr-card-and-session-record-redesign D-04).
The helper allows only the query text it knows, so the fields moved the node protocol to 39 and a helper on 38 is refused at Hello and reinstalled.
A part that cannot be understood is named in `failed` and the others stand; files that `.gitattributes` marks `linguist-generated` or that are a common lock file are flagged `generated`.
The slot is `pr_work.cards`, at most eight cards (`PR_CARD_LIMIT`), the least recently opened dropped first, and a read already running is not started twice; the operator's Look again (`look_again: true`) dismisses a failed write notice and reads the list too, while a plain `pr_card_read`, the card's own reads and the read the core starts itself after a moved head keep the notice.
A merge with a method that GitHub accepted, made from this machine's own project, reads as merged at once (`Runtime.merged_here`, `read_merges_made_here`): the list shows it merged with the time it was seen until a fresh read says merged, closed or gone, or ten minutes pass; a read that failed releases nothing. A node's project and a merge-queue ask wait for the list, as before.
`pr_merge {workspace_id, pr_number, head, method?}` and `pr_update_branch {workspace_id, pr_number, head}` are the only GitHub writes the card asks for; each is one worker call, a second ask while one works is the same intent and is not sent again, and the head is the commit the card showed, so GitHub refuses a pull request that moved (`--match-head-commit`, `expected_head_sha`).
The answer is a code, never GitHub's text: `head_moved` (and the core re-reads the list and the card), `conflict`, `not_mergeable`, `denied`, `unreachable` or `failed`, with `already_merged` counted as success so asking again writes nothing twice; the detail goes to the `pr_write.failed` diagnostic.
A card's AI summary rides the same slot (`docs/AI_PROVIDERS.md`).
A project on a connected node reads and writes through that node's `gh` (`github::Where::Repository`), and a project a core does not read opens GitHub in the shell instead.
The reads the list gains for the PRs tool are in `docs/status-model.md`, GitHub status in the Workspace row, and the groups the core decides from them in Pull request groups.

A Workspace's right lane (PRD right-lane D-11: its one `tool`, `memory`, `prs`, `files` or `changes` (History), null while the lane is closed; whether it is `pinned` beside Agent Views; whether the files tool shows its file `tree`; the PRs tool's `prs.only_checkout`, and the widths, `lane_width` for the files tool's documents, `panel_width` for every other tool and the 보기 slot, and `tree_width`) and the arrangement of its View areas belong to the core (S6 D-10, S7 D-11), keyed by device and checkout path in `herdr-core/src/workspace_views.rs` and kept in `workspace-views.json` beside `core-state.json`, a versioned file of its own so neither the old settings nor the single-surface editor state is rewritten.
Only `hided` names that file (`CoreOptions.workspace_views_path`), and it always does; without it the core keeps the earlier single-surface editor, and none of the View area keys below reaches the snapshot.
The shell sends one `workspace_view` event for a tool, the pin, the tree, a width or a reveal (`tool`, `pinned`, `tree`, `lane_width`, `panel_width`, `tree_width` in CSS pixels, and `reveal`, a file of the front Workspace, checked by path components like any file read, whose folders unfold in the Explorer in the same event); every field is the value it should end at, so the same event sent twice lands where it did once, and the snapshot's `workspace_view` is the front Workspace's entry; it is absent from the wire when no Workspace is in front.
A `tool` of null closes the lane, while the pin, the tree and the widths stay (B7, B11), and the Workspace keeps `last_tool`, the tool the lane last showed (set only by `WorkspaceView::set_tool`, published in the snapshot's `workspace_view`, null for a lane that never showed one, read from `tool` in a file written before it), so the shell's reopen returns to it and sends Files when it is null; a `reveal` opens the lane on the files tool with the tree on and opens nothing; a `tool` the core does not know is refused (`workspace_view.unknown_tool`), and a width is held to `1..=8192` (`clamp_width`), the shell keeping each at its minimum.
Whether an unpinned lane floats over the agents now, and whether it is expanded, are the shell's own moment and are never sent or stored.
The widths stay absent until the operator first resizes the lane or the tree, so the default lives in the shell's tokens rather than in the operator's file.
Keyboard width intents also carry an optional `width_request_id` (1 to 128 bytes), which the core echoes in the Workspace snapshot from one bounded runtime slot keyed to its Workspace; it is never stored in `workspace-views.json`.
Each mounted divider retains one pending absolute target, calculating a later key from it until its own acknowledgement arrives; an older acknowledgement from that divider cannot retire the newer target, and another caller, changed geometry, focus loss or unmount rebases it on the core's frame.
The shell still draws only the core's widths and sends one idempotent absolute-width event per key; acknowledgement-only changes publish without saving unchanged widths, and there is no timer, request queue or per-key subprocess.
The files tool with no view draws its tree across the lane, so nothing inserts a page (B12); the operator's close of the last view (`view_layout` close, after the save it waits on) closes the lane when the tree is off and leaves it when the tree is on (`close_emptied_lane`), while an agent's `hide view close`, a cap's eviction or a document closing elsewhere changes no lane field; an open asked while the front lane showed that tree alone, by any route (a tree pick, ⌘P, a page, a `hide` open from the front tab, or a terminal link once it settles in that Workspace), turns the tree on (`front_lone_tree`, read before the event or the link's settle, then `keep_lone_tree` once it is not refused), so a read that lands later stores the same tree and the view opens beside the tree that showed it.
The PRs tool is written to the file as `work`, the name every earlier build reads, and a file's `work` or `agent_sessions` tool opens on PRs, its old `lens` dropped and its `only_checkout` kept (PRD sidebar-task-lens B30), so a downgrade still opens the lane.
The global `ui_state.right_panel_visible` and `right_panel_section` are projected from the front Workspace's lane: Changes, PRs and Memory as their sections, the files tool as the Explorer only while its tree shows, and a closed lane as hidden, so the Changes reader and the device Explorer watch keep the one gate they had.
The PRs tool projects `RightPanelSection::AgentSessions`, never the legacy `Sessions` section that activates Memory and conversation reads.
The lane's 보기 slot (`herdr-core/src/runtime/detail.rs`) is one window-level `ui_state.detail`: `items`, a pull request's card or a session's record, at most `DETAIL_HISTORY_LIMIT` (20) with the oldest leaving first, and `shown`, whether the lane draws it; it belongs to no checkout and no Agent tab, is saved in `core-state.json`, and `detail_open`, `detail_back`, `detail_close` and `detail_show` each start or stop the read of what it shows on the same event.
The sidebar's `ui_state.sidebar_lens` (`projects` or `tasks`) and `task_view` (`status` or `project`) sit beside it; `sidebar_lens_set {lens, show?}` turns the lens and, from ⇧⌘B with the sidebar hidden, shows the sidebar in the same event, and `task_reveal {pane_id, session?}` shows the 태스크 list on that row unfolded and opens its record in the slot.
The 태스크 list draws the device's `agent_scope.tasks` (each task's root, members, checkouts and state, in the core's order, `herdr-core/src/agent_state/tally/tasks.rs`) through `web/src/TaskLens.tsx` and `web/src/taskList.ts`.
Its visible-board demand reuses the existing running-checks observation gate rather than adding a polling worker or clock.
Session escalation, resolve and pane headers are pure core projections over accepted agent, delivery and PR facts (`agent_state`, `runtime/session_state.rs`).
Changed inputs refresh the projection under Runtime and publish only changed values; repeated transport-control frames do not rederive headers.
Resolve uses the existing coalesced state-save worker and publishes its durable result after the save answers; the end of the Resolved window compares one deadline on the existing coordinator tick.
Remembered sidebar folds survive incomplete startup and reconnect catalogs, as recent checkouts do, and leave only with a confirmed project or device removal.

That projection is never saved: `core-state.json` keeps the right panel it held when the process started (`ui_state_to_save`), so an older build started on the same state directory opens with its own panel.
A new Workspace starts with its lane closed, so it shows Agent Views alone.
An unpinned lane floats over Agent Views and a pinned one docks beside them (D-02, D-03): the pin turning on or off, a docked lane opening or closing, a docked divider landing, the tree turning on or off beside a docked lane and a docked lane turning between the files tool and another tool whose width differs resize the agents' terminals once, and a floating lane, a drag in progress, an open inside the lane and an agent choice resize nothing.
The file is schema 3; a schema 2 file (`StoredColumns`), whichever build wrote it, migrates once on load (D-11, B22): File Views on gives the files tool, else Tools on gives its tool, the Explorer becoming the files tool with its tree on (`lane_of`); the pin starts off and File Views' width becomes the lane's.
Its older shapes read through the same path: the side panel (`panel`, `open` and `expanded` as File Views on), S6's `mode` (`legacy_views`), and the two independent `explorer` and `changes` flags (`legacy_tool`), whose widths were shares of a floating panel and start at the defaults.
Such a load records `workspace_views.migrated`, the next save writes schema 3, and a file of a schema this build does not know is kept aside with a diagnostic and the defaults.

The same entry adds a defaulted `agent_layout` key for the Agent areas of this machine's Workspaces and of a node that dialed in (`arranges_agent_areas`), whose Workspaces the core arranged as its own before it moved off that machine; the snapshot's `workspace_view.agent_layout` is null on a device the core dials, whose Herdr shows one tab.
A node's tabs reach its areas when its session arrives (`reconcile_node_agent_topology`, through the same `reconcile_agent_topology`), the tab of the area in use is asked of the node's Herdr (`focus_node_tab`), the node's own focus moves the area in use (`sync_agent_selection`), and the panes of every area's tab attach as the front tab's do (`node_shown_agent_tabs`).
Externally created tabs join the active area's bar without replacing a shown canvas, including when creation focuses the new tab in Herdr.
The replica carries the creation cause and a focus-event revision with the authoritative workspace focus, including during partial publication and bootstrap replay.
One pending creation pairing per live tab is consumed by its first `tab_focused` event; a later `tab_focused` event gets a new revision and is followed.
The pinned Herdr emits no event for a focus command that leaves its current tab unchanged; Hide cannot observe that no-op command.
This metadata remains inside the core projection, adds no terminal-output work, and is removed with its tab; local New and Reopen intents retain their existing focus claim.
Each Workspace stores one Files View list, `view_layout`, with its split, selection and active area, and one bookmark per Agent tab, `tab_views`, a map from a Herdr tab to the display it last put in front.
The bookmark is one display id, never a layout, so it holds no View of its own and no tab owns the list.
The earlier per-tab keys (`view_owner`, `view_presentations`, `view_next_id`, `view_bookmarks` and a schema 3 file's old `layout`) are not read and not written, while the schema 1 and 2 column files still convert through their own `layout`; the schema stays 3 and nothing converts them, so the first run after the update starts with an empty list while the lane's tool, pin, tree, widths, comments and the Agent layout survive.
The shell receives the existing projected layout; none of these fields adds a wire field.
`track_view_bookmarks` compares borrowed shown/active tab ids and the front with the last observation during the existing Workspace sync, and allocates only when one moved.
A tab that was on no Agent area and now is the active one gets its bookmarked display brought to the front of its own area without moving the active area or the keyboard, and a bookmark naming a closed display is ignored; otherwise a change of the front of the area in use is recorded as the active tab's bookmark, and a tab that leaves Herdr drops its bookmark (`prune_view_bookmarks`).
The lane's tool, pin, tree and widths stay checkout-wide, and ordinary focus among already shown Agent areas changes nothing in the list.

Workspace control records the caller's actual tab and native session before an asynchronous read, validates them again at commit, and refuses stale identity without another-tab fallback.
`caller_stays_behind` decides who is a background caller.
On the front Workspace, every caller but the pane the operator is in (the selected pane, `snapshot.terminal.pane_id`, or a device's focused pane) is: another pane of the same tab, and a checkout-bound shell, which no pane vouches for.
On a Workspace not on screen, a pane of a tab other than its active one is (B7); the active tab's panes and a checkout-bound shell change only what that Workspace shows, so a page they open is selected and can be driven.
A desktop window's browser gateway never is, because a page its CDP client creates loads only in front; hided names it `gateway:<key>:<path>` (`gateway_caller_id`), which `Caller::parse` reads as a checkout caller with `gateway` set, so it resolves like any checkout caller and is told apart only here.
No command option makes a background caller foreground.
A background command records the selection first (`keep_front`) and puts it back after the commit (`restore_front`): a new display stays at the end of the area it landed in, in no area's front, and the area in use, the keyboard and the editor's active tab are as they were.
A command records no bookmark itself; the bookmark follows the front of the active tab (`track_view_bookmarks`), so only what the operator had in front is remembered.
GUI events act for the active tab, and a GUI browser open asked in a pane records that pane's tab's bookmark.
An open or select from the foreground caller calls the lane on Files after the commit, decided before the open adds its view so a tree that showed alone stays beside it; a browser page's own area-bound target calls nothing.
Reconcile, preview retirement, close guards, restart hydration and browser inventory read the checkout's one list.
Admission counts the checkout's 32 displays (`MAX_VIEW_DISPLAYS`) in its six View areas.
An open that would pass the cap first closes the display focused longest ago in the checkout (`make_view_room`); the search skips every area's front and never closes a document that holds work or a file still being read back; a display that was its document's last closes that document as `close_file_tab_now` does, and each close is one `view_layout.oldest_closed` diagnostic.
Each layout retains depth three, and authoritative Agent topology keeps the existing 64-tab admission and prunes closed records.
The same admission check covers a caller on an inbound node; an unanswered session or unresolved catalog grouping preserves saved state, while an empty checkout in a fully answered catalog prunes its final tab's bookmark.
Pruning drops a closed tab's bookmark and pending reads and keeps its displays and clean documents in the list, so a tab close never closes a View.
Every tab-valued owner and tab-keyed map participates in `node_migration::KEYS` with `Moves::Tab` for core moves and returns.
Changes use the existing coalesced off-lock save and notification path; identical state creates no additional save or publication, and there is no new polling, subprocess or blocking work under Runtime.
Agent admission counts the shared 64-item limit together with pending creates, placements, reopen and replacement-close effects.
Request-specific claims reserve slots before the worker starts; reconciliation places acknowledged local effects before external arrivals and does not expire uncertain claims by time.
An ambiguous local create gets one bounded marker lookup outside the runtime lock, without another mutation; an unresolved claim remains charged for that runtime session.
Reopen and replacement claims have the same explicit owner, independent of operation phase or Dismiss; retries reuse their claim, and a confirmed tab transfers it to placement before the claim is released.
A close retry that reuses a confirmed replacement carries reuse-only permission to its worker, so a concurrent deletion cannot turn that retry into a new creation.
An authoritative excess tab remains in checkout topology and contributes to the snapshot waiting count; reconciliation admits it when a slot opens without selecting an unplaced tab.
Hide refuses a new external effect at capacity, including a protected replacement close, and uses the existing capacity notice surface.

`split_tree.rs::SplitTree<I: AreaItem>` owns structural repair, ordered membership, focus, move, split, collapse, resize and limits; `view_layout.rs` wraps it with document/preview/browser rules, and `agent_layout.rs` wraps it with stable Herdr tab references and delegated canvas selection.
The shared web `AreaTree` consumes View and Agent adapters over `areaLayout`, `areaDrag` and column-scoped drawn frames, so gestures, geometry, dividers and narrow presentation have one implementation.
`AgentTab` renders the same status/provider/name/Rename unit in every area, and `PaneCanvas` receives its explicit tab.
Agent actions name the Workspace and use the shared stale-frame guard and bounded split-request deduplication; only authoritative topology reconciles persisted references, so a startup placeholder cannot erase the saved tree.
Created and reopened tabs carry a bounded pending placement into that reconciliation; a missing target area falls back to the active area.
The snapshot's `workspace_view.agent_layout` contains only the tree and tab references; `checkout.tabs` remains the source of tab and pane contents, and `checkout.active_tab_id` remains the active area's tab.

A protected primary close uses authoritative workspace `worktree` provenance, including repository identity and linked status, rather than labels or paths alone (`runtime/agent_close.rs`).
The existing close worker first creates or adopts a shell bearing the close intent marker, outside the runtime mutex, reconciles its same-area placement, then sends the original tab or last-pane close.
Creation failure closes nothing; refusal retains the shell and the pending intent, whose Retry close adopts the same marker instead of creating another shell.
Dismiss releases a definite failed intent, and ordinary closes retain their existing guards and topology confirmation.

Schema 2 of that file (PRD S7) stores each Workspace's View areas as a binary split tree (`layout`, rules in `herdr-core/src/view_layout.rs`).
A split node has an `axis`, `row` for first-left and second-right or `column` for first-top and second-bottom, and a `ratio`, the first child's share, clamped to 0.15..0.85; a leaf is an area holding its displays in tab order and naming its `active` display.
A display is one tab showing one file or diff (`path`, `kind`, `committed`, `preview`, `last_focused_unix_ms`) or one web page (`kind` `browser`, with its `url` and the `title` the page last reported), and the Workspace names its `active_area`, the one it last used, where the next file opens.
Area, display and split ids (`a<n>`, `d<n>`, `s<n>`) are minted by the core from the layout's `next_id` and never reused in that Workspace, so an id in an event names one thing for the Workspace's life; a Workspace with no layout has one empty area `a1`.
The same invariants hold on load and after every change: an area has at most one preview display, its `active` names one of its displays or is null when it has none, an empty area exists only as the root, `active_area` names an existing area, and the caps hold.
Loading repairs a file that breaks them in one pass over the tree per rule and reports what it changed as counts in one diagnostic, and a file whose ids or focus stamps come near the end of their numbers, which only a crafted file can, is renumbered, keeping what each area shows and which view was focused last, so minting never overflows.
The caps are core constants, published in the snapshot as `layout.limits`: 6 areas in the checkout's layout (`MAX_VIEW_AREAS`), 3 split nodes on the path from the root to any area (`MAX_SPLIT_DEPTH`) and 32 displays (`MAX_VIEW_DISPLAYS`), which is also the tree's own cap.
A split that would pass the areas or the depth is refused with `view_layout.limit` and its reason, an open that would add the 33rd display when no display can close (see above) is refused with `view_layout.display_limit`, a browser open included, and nothing changes; an open of a document that is already displayed is a focus and is never refused, and a file that carries more than the caps is trimmed on load with a diagnostic, never on an operator's action.
The display cap holds on every path: a preview open beside a preview whose document holds work (which stays and makes the open an addition), Reopen Closed, a file the Explorer creates and a revealed file are refused before anything is read or made, a read that lands after the views filled is refused as it lands (a Reopen Closed then keeps its item), a layout change that adds a display past the 32 is refused, and the tree's own operations refuse a 33rd display, so nothing can pass it.
A schema 1 file (S6) migrates once: its tabs become the displays of one area `a1` in their saved order, its active tab becomes that area's active display, its preview flags are kept, its lane opens on the files tool, and the next save writes schema 3.
Any other version, a file that does not parse and a migration that fails all take the S6 path: the file is renamed aside as `<name>.unreadable-<ms>`, the defaults load with a `workspace_views.unreadable` diagnostic, so nothing is resumed and the page starts on Main, and when the rename fails the store is frozen so nothing is written over the original; saves are atomic and run on a worker, coalesced, never under the runtime mutex.

A display never holds a document.
It binds at runtime to the S5.5 editor tab, the one buffer per device, checkout and document (the tab id, `editor_documents` and the save pipeline), and any number of displays may bind the same tab, so two views of a file share one text, one dirty state and one save queue rather than copies that could diverge (S7 D-03).
Those displays can sit in different Workspaces: a terminal link to another checkout's file binds a display of the front Workspace to that checkout's tab (A clicked path is one event).
Closing a display that is not its document's last removes the display alone, and a `pending_save` sent with it is not needed; closing the last one runs the existing `file_close` path, save-then-close with the carried `pending_save` and the conflict protection, so a document leaves only once its buffer is safe.
The web's frame may still show another view of a document the core already holds in one, so a close of the last view of a dirty, saving or failed document that carries neither `pending_save` nor `discard: true` (the operator's Don't Save) is refused as `view_layout.unsaved`, naming the document, and nothing changes.
A document that becomes dirty, is saving or has a save state promotes every display of it in the same event, because a preview is replaced by the next click and such a document must never be.
In web mode the per-checkout preview slot of `place_editor_tab` gives way to one preview display per area: a preview open retargets the active area's preview display in place, and its old document is retired when no other display shows it, like the preview replacement before it, with no Recent Closed entry; the single-surface editor fallback keeps the per-checkout slot.

A browser display binds to nothing (issue 155, [BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md](BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md)): it is `open` from the moment it exists, the reconcile never removes it for want of a document tab, and a restore reads nothing for it.
`browser_open` from the Explorer or a page's new tab places a display in the named Workspace or the front one, a page's new tab beside the page it names (`beside_display`): in the area next to the page's, else in a new area to its right; pane CLI requests use the separate scoped Workspace action.
`browser_state` records the address, title, and native load state a page reports, while `view_layout` `navigate` loads the operator's own address.
UI `browser_open` receipts remain in `status.browser_opens`, where the shell reads the one an operator's open named to hand that page the keyboard (BROWSER_DISPLAYS.md); pane CLI requests receive their own scoped action result.
A load stamp (`load`, never saved) is how the core asks the desktop app to load a display's address again, and the address, capped at 8 KiB, holds only `http`, `https`, `file` or `about:blank`; loading repairs a stored display that breaks this to `about:blank`.
A browser display with no `url` is the shell’s new-tab page, creates no native page, and is never deduplicated with another empty display.
A file open captures the active empty display with its placement before reading; it replaces that exact display on success only while it is still empty, and a diff selection uses the same placement rule.
A build from before browser displays reads a file that holds one as a file that does not parse, and so takes the unreadable path above: the file is renamed aside and the page starts on Main.

A layout change is one `view_layout` event whose `action` says which: `focus` (a display becomes its area's active display, its area the active area, and its `last_focused` is stamped), `focus_area` (keyboard focus moving between areas), `move` (a display to an index in its own area, which reorders, or in another existing area, where a display of the same document gives way so no area holds one twice; either way the index is the display's final place, counted without that twin), `split` (a display into a new area at one `edge` of an area, taking half of it), `resize` (a split's ratio), `close`, `keep_open`, `retry` (an unavailable display's file read again) and `navigate` (a browser display's address).
Each is one operator action and one frame, and it applies to the front Workspace only; a moved or split display becomes its area's active display and that area the active one.
Every payload names its Workspace as `workspace: {device_id, path}`, copied from the `workspace_view` of the frame the operator acted on, because ids like `d2` repeat across Workspaces and the front can move between that frame and the event; an action naming any other Workspace changes nothing and leaves a `view_layout.stale_workspace` diagnostic with both Workspaces and the action, and no screen error, since the screen already shows the other Workspace.
Every action may arrive twice, so each converges: `move`, `resize` and the two focuses carry their target state, `split` carries a `request_id` whose repeat among the Workspace's last 32 (kept in memory only) changes nothing, and closing a display that is already gone is a no-op with a diagnostic.
An unknown id is refused with `view_layout.unknown_*` and its reason, and so is a split that would change nothing, a display split away from an area it holds alone.
A move or close that empties an area collapses it in the same event and its sibling takes the space; the last area stays, empty.

In web mode `file_open` places a document this way: one already displayed in the front Workspace focuses its most recently focused display, and pins it when the open is not a preview; otherwise a preview open retargets the active area's preview display, or appends one when the area has none, and an ordinary open appends a pinned display to the active area.
`file_open` with `beside: true` is Open to the side: a pinned display of the document in the area next to the active one (right, then left, then down, then up among the tree's neighbours), a new area to the right when there is only one area (within the caps), the empty area itself when no view is open, since an empty area is only ever the root, or a focus of the display already in that area; when that split is refused as the read lands, a display of the document already open is focused, and with none the open is refused with the split's reason rather than doubled up in the area it was asked from; it rides on `file_open` so the request still crosses hided's path boundary like any open.
`changes_select` places diff displays by the same rules, `file_keep_open` pins every preview display of its document, `file_focus` focuses its most recently focused display, and `file_close` closes every display of it and then the document through the existing guards.
`file_draft` and `file_conflict` carry the `tab_id` of their document, which the web always sends, because with several documents on screen the active one is no longer necessarily the one that was typed into.
Opening, restoring, renaming, closing and removing a device create and remove editor tabs by paths the layout does not see, so after every event the core reconciles the two: a display whose document tab is gone is removed, and an editor tab of a restored Workspace that no display shows gets a pinned display in the active area.
Terminal input and output and a document's keystrokes (`file_draft`) skip that pass, since they move no area, and every snapshot read runs it first; a first edit keeps its document's displays open in its own event, looking only at its own Workspace.
Reads land on workers, so two can land with no event between them, and an open reconciles before it places its document: otherwise a preview it replaces could still be unbound from the restored document it shows, which would then come back pinned beside the new one.
The reconcile holds the display caps too: such a document of a Workspace that already has 32 views waits off screen, is reported once with a `view_layout.display_limit` diagnostic, and shows once a view is closed.

With the regions separate, choosing an Agent tab no longer takes the editor off screen: `editor.active_tab_id` is the document of the active area's active display, null while that display is not open (`front_active_view_tab` in `runtime/view_areas.rs`, applied by the reconcile), where before every terminal choice deactivated it.
The core also owns when the lane is called (PRD right-lane D-13, `apply_area_intent_to`): an explicit file open, History selection, page open, Recent Panels display, or a CLI open or `view select` from the operator's own pane sets the lane's tool to Files, and a `workspace_view` `reveal` (Select in File Tree) or a link that names a folder sets Files with the tree on (B16, B17).
Choosing an agent or a tab changes no lane field; a status change, a hover or a menu opening moves nothing, and a refused event moves nothing either.
Each such intent, and every `workspace_view` that names a tool, is numbered (`lane_calls`, the count in any Workspace) and the number is kept as the called Workspace's last (`lane_called`), both on the snapshot's `workspace_view`; the shell reads them to put an unpinned lane up, so a call from that page, the CLI or another page reaches it the same way, and a call into one Workspace never moves another's lane.
The numbers are runtime only and never stored, since whether an unpinned lane is up is the page's alone.
The call history follows the Workspace store's 256-entry retention and is forgotten with a removed device.
Choosing a Workspace or an agent on another device than the one in front is one event: `focus_checkout`, `focus_tab`, `focus_pane` and `remote_control` carry `focus_device`, and the core brings that device forward only when it accepts the request, so a refusal leaves the device in front where it was.
A Recent Panels commit of a display in another checkout is one `focus_checkout` naming it by `display_id`: the core checks the display is in that checkout's Workspace before moving anything, brings the checkout forward, opens its lane on Files, and makes the display the one in use; an unknown display refuses the whole event with `view_layout.unknown_display`.

The snapshot's `workspace_view.layout` is the front Workspace's tree with its `active_area`, `limits` and `display_count`, each display carrying its `tab_id`, `label`, `kind`, `committed`, `preview` and `state` with an optional `reason`; `editor.tabs` still lists every document.
A restored Workspace's tree, ratios, tab order, preview flags, active displays, active area and lane are published the first time it is in front in a process, before any file is read, so the layout appears whole rather than tab by tab.
Each display then moves through its own `state`: `waiting` while its Workspace's root cannot be read yet, with a `reason` naming the device state (`Waiting for <device> to connect` while its helper connects), then `opening` while its read is in flight, a Retry's read included, then `open` once it is bound to a document or diff tab, or `unavailable` with the read failure as its `reason`.
A device helper that cannot be used, one refused for its protocol, unsupported, on a changed identity or not allowed, gives the device's own reason, the one History and Settings show, and its displays keep `waiting` so they open once the device is fixed; Retry on a waiting display is refused with that reason.
The wait is `view_root_ready`: on this machine a read waits until hided has opened the checkout's root, and on a device until its helper is ready and its catalog has moved its checkouts into their Projects (`ingest_host_established` and `refresh_device_catalog` ask again), because a read before the helper is refused and would mark every file unavailable, and a restore before the grouping names a Workspace the device no longer shows.
A restore opens into the Workspace in front, so it runs only while that is still the Workspace the last sync recorded; a front that moved since waits for the sync rather than filling one Workspace with another's files.
The reads land in any order and each fills its own display in place, because the order lives in the stored tree rather than in the order the documents arrive; S6's recording of tabs in strip order, and the reordering once the last read landed, are gone.
A Workspace records nothing into the file until its restore has landed, so a Workspace whose files cannot be reached, or a quit before the reads land, keeps the layout it saved.
An unavailable display keeps its place with Close view and Retry, `retry` reads its file again or, for a diff, opens its diff tab again, and every other display and area is untouched; nothing is started in Herdr, no split or zoom is replayed, and a terminal layout another client changed meanwhile wins.
A diff display stored without its Changes group is repaired on load to the working group, so it binds to the diff tab its restore opens instead of standing beside a second display of it.
Unsaved text returns through the web's per-document draft reconcile in IndexedDB (S5.5), never through this file.
Removing a device forgets its Workspaces' entries, and a pending save is joined when the core is dropped, so a change made just before quitting is on disk.

Two kinds of state stay in the web and are never dispatched.
A drag's preview (the floating tab, the insertion line, the edge overlay with its label) is local, and only a valid drop sends one `view_layout` `move` or `split`; Escape, a drop outside, an ineligible or vanished target and too little room send nothing, and nothing is resized or saved during the drag.
Whether an unpinned lane is up (`laneShown`) and whether it is expanded (`laneExpanded`) are decided in `ui.ts` and never sent or stored: an unpinned lane is up until Escape puts it away, so a restart shows it over the agents, and widening a narrow window restores what the core stores because nothing replaced it.
The core therefore counts a put-away lane as shown: while the lane's tool is set, the files tool's documents stay published and the Changes or Memory readers keep running, the resident cost of a lane that comes back as it was without a read (B4).
The lane is called through the core's numbers alone (`readCalls` in `web/src/workspace.ts`, read on every snapshot by `web/src/laneCalls.ts` whether or not the Workspace screen is drawn): the front Workspace's `lane_called` past the `lane_calls` the page read last is a call, so a call older than the last read never moves a Workspace chosen later, and a lower `lane_calls` is a core that started again; so the page never applies its own open twice; another Workspace in front shows its lane as its stored tool says.
A file a terminal link reveals is numbered on its own checkout's Workspace once it settles there (`settle_reveal_path`), a linked folder too, and an intent that numbers a call announces the snapshot even when nothing else moved.
The page's one dispatch choke point (`laneCalled` in `web/src/actions.ts`) puts the lane up for a `workspace_view` naming a tool or a reveal and for a linked folder's `reveal_path`, so no path can forget it; an agent or tab choice and a press on a terminal pane leave it, and only Escape inside the lane or on its rail (`laneEscape` in `web/src/WorkspaceScreen.tsx`) puts it away.
What the body draws is `laneFrame` in `web/src/workspace.ts`, from the core's entry, the page's moment, the body's width and the tokens alone (`--size-agent-views-min` 480, `--size-lane-min` 360 and `--size-lane-ideal` 520 for the documents, `--size-tree-min` 180 and `--size-tree-ideal` 240): the mode (closed, hidden, overlay, docked or expanded), Agent Views' width, the lane's, the documents' and the tree's, and `field`, the stored width the lane draws from and its divider lands on (`laneWidthField`: `lane_width` for the files tool, `panel_width` for every other tool and the 보기 slot); a pinned lane docks only while Agent Views keep their minimum beside it, else floats with `narrowPin` (B19), and a lane wider than the body gives up document width before tree width.
Agent Views and the lane are drawn in one row under the full-width toolbar (`LaneRow` in `web/src/WorkspaceScreen.tsx`) with the rail after it: a docked lane leaves an empty slot of its width beside Agent Views, and the lane itself is positioned over that slot, over Agent Views' right edge, or over the whole body, so changing between floating, docked and expanded never remounts it; Agent Views stays mounted under an expanded lane, `inert`, so its terminals neither park nor resize, and the page records the keyboard owner from focus and pointer events, including native page focus from the desktop host, to route a chord, the lane's tool owning it inside `[data-lane]`.
A lane out of sight stays mounted, `invisible` and `inert`, so its documents, tabs, scroll and pages keep their state, holds no Escape layer, and hands the keyboard to the focused pane when it held it; the files tool stays mounted under another tool while its tree unmounts, laid out at its own width so its View areas measure what Files will show, and a browser display's slot hidden this way hides its page without closing it.
A divider (`web/src/LaneDivider.tsx`) drags a guide and sends one `workspace_view` with the width it lands on: the lane's divider sends the width of what the lane shows, in the frame's `field` (`laneLanding`), against Agent Views, and the tree's divider trades width between the tree and the documents in one event so the lane and Agent Views keep theirs (`treeLanding`); a browser display's slot reports its rect like any other, and a resize that lands reaches the page through the slot's ResizeObserver.
A lane drag belongs to its starting Workspace and geometry; changing either, or unmounting its divider, releases its pointer capture, listeners, guide and shell-drag mark without landing a width in another Workspace.
The web also owns the pixel rule, because it has the geometry: an edge takes a drop, and a Split item or an Open to the side from the only area is enabled, only when the target area's size along the split axis is at least twice `--size-workspace-area-min` (a `row` split) or twice `--size-view-area-min-height` (a `column` split) plus the divider, and the split stays within `layout.limits`; the core owns the counts and the depth and refuses whatever passes them.

A pane's delegated children and ancestors come from the global machine-qualified pane rows the core projects (`children.chips`, `lineage_path`; rules in `web/src/lineage.ts`).
The remote replica preserves a declared raw pane and machine id, and the runtime scopes it only after the consented helper greeting supplies that device's immutable machine identity.
A parent's header opens the tree popover from its tree button, a child's header draws its ancestor path, and the pane menu lists parent, siblings and children.
Each explicit Open follows the same tracked navigation as the sidebar and Sessions; Escape returns to its badge, and the tree popover's `Overview에서 일감 보기` opens the Overview at the agent's task.
Each of those moves is one `focus_pane` carrying a `request_id` (on a device, `remote_control` with `report_pane_focus_outcome`), and the core's `status.pane_focus_request` is the only answer the header shows: pending until Herdr's layout confirms, or failed with the core's reason and Retry when it can be retried; a second click on the same target while one is in flight is dropped, and a failure never splits the parent or makes a pane.

### The `$HOME` filesystem boundary

`hided/src/boundary.rs` is the one place the boundary is enforced, in the dispatch path before an event reaches the core (`server::apply_boundary`).
A `create_workspace` whose path does not resolve under `$HOME` (a `..` segment, a symlink whose target leaves home, a file, a missing directory, a relative path) is answered to that client as a `path_refused` frame with a reason code (`outside_home`, `home_root`, `not_found`, `not_a_directory`, `invalid_path`), logged as `path.refused` with the path capped at `LOGGED_PATH_CAP`, and never forwarded.
The filesystem is only ever asked about paths under home: a path written outside home gets `outside_home` before anything is read, and a path under home is resolved one component at a time from home, where a symlink's target is tested as written the same way before it is followed (`SYMLINK_HOPS` bounds the chain).
So the reason a client reads never says whether a path outside home exists, not even through a symlink it planted under home: an escaping symlink answers `outside_home` whether its target exists, is a file, or is missing, and `not_found`, `not_a_directory` and `invalid_path` only ever describe a path under home.
An accepted workspace path is forwarded as the canonical path that was checked.
A `remote_file_list` names a path on a remote machine, which the boundary knows nothing about, and is forwarded untouched, a `local` target included, which names no device and which the core answers as an unconfigured target; the core lists it through that device's helper (`node_access::list_folder`), the same confined listing the web Explorer gets, though a remote target's own registration file panel has had no shell sender since the native shell that carried it was removed.
A device with no consent answers that panel with the `not_allowed` file state and a message naming what allowing installs and runs; the panel shows it with an Allow control that sends `device_host_consent`, and the core lists the panel's root again once the helper is ready.
A `file_list` is the Explorer's line for one folder inside a checkout the user already registered: the path has to resolve under that root (a folder that leaves it, or a root that is not registered, is answered `outside_checkout`) and the listing carries files as well as directories, keeps dotted names, and drops `.git` alone.
Those rows follow `hide_host::list::natural_cmp`'s order - directories first, then a case-insensitive name comparison in which a run of digits compares by value - and are capped at `LIST_CAP` with `truncated` set.
The `$HOME` line lists nothing: Add a project picks its folder with the desktop app's native picker (`hide:pick-folder`), so hided only judges the path it is handed, and the one `directory_list` it answers is the Explorer's, named in `kind`.
Create new project names a folder that does not exist yet, so a `create_workspace` with `new_folder` is judged as a parent on the `$HOME` line (home itself allowed) and one folder name (`Boundary::resolve_new_project`), and refused as `already_exists` when anything other than an empty folder, or one holding only `.git` and `.DS_Store`, stands at the target (`hide_host::project::folder`, read without following a symlink); the canonical target is forwarded.
The same check answers a client's `project_target` probe as a `project_target` frame, which the dialog shows before anything is sent; the probe is never forwarded and changes nothing on disk.
The core's creation worker, off `Mutex<Runtime>`, asks its own node to make the project (`Call::ProjectCreate`, `hide_host::project::create`): the node makes the folder at the literal path hided checked and runs `git init` in it before it resolves the path, judging the target again without following a symlink at it because the check and the make are not one step, and answers the real path the core registers; a failure after the folder was made keeps it and says so, and a retry continues into it.
A `new_folder` create that names an SSH device is refused by the core.
A `clone_repository` {url, parent, name} passes the same line through `Boundary::resolve_clone_target`: `parent` may be home itself or a folder under it, `name` has to be one plain folder name, and nothing (a folder, a file, a dangling symlink) may already hold `parent/name`, which is refused as `already_exists` (unlike Create new project, not even an empty or `.git`-only folder is accepted, since a clone never writes into a folder it did not make); the event reaches the core with `parent` rewritten to its canonical spelling, or is answered with `path_refused` naming `parent/name`.
Clone from URL asks the same question while the operator types with a `clone_target` {parent, name}, which hided answers with a `clone_target` frame (`path` when the clone may land there, `reason` otherwise) and never forwards or logs.
The boundary root is read from `HOME` at boot and is not configurable; widening it is a security-policy decision the web Settings does not take, and registering a folder outside home stays with the existing CLI path.
Both frames and the reason codes are in `contracts/hided-ws.schema.json`.

### The checkout-root boundary and the Explorer frames

`hided` widens the file line from `$HOME` to the registered checkout roots the core snapshot carries (PRD S3 D-01, B11).
Every Explorer path - listing, open, reveal, save, create, rename, move, trash, index and file bytes - is checked against the root the event names, or the focused checkout's root, on the real path.
A path under a root is walked one component at a time from that root, so a symlink whose target leaves the checkout is refused as `outside_checkout` before its target is read, and a sibling whose name merely starts with the root's path is not inside it.
A browser display's `file:` address (`browser_open`, `browser_state`, and `view_layout` `navigate`) is a path too: hided decodes it, refuses one that names another host or does not decode as `invalid_path`, checks the path against every registered root the same way, and forwards the URL of the checked path (`browser_url`, `hided/src/file_url.rs`); any other address is the core's to judge.
Roots come from retained `navigator.workspaces` (a local workspace whose checkout exists) and device roots also include `status.remote.session.workspaces`.
One `RootFollower` keeps an independent revision cursor behind one lock and reads complete typed state; `roots_changed` renews the boundary and watchers only when their catalog, remote session, selected root/device, readiness or UI scope changed.
A normalized reset cursor reapplies current authority; unrelated semantic deltas retain the accepted roots.
Its readers are the boot seed, the notify path and a refused event (`spawn_root_refresh`, `RootFollower::catch_up`).
A checkout the core learns after boot, from Herdr's first sync, reaches a client's snapshot and the notify path on separate reads, so a client can name it before the boundary holds it: an event refused as `outside_checkout` (an Explorer event, `file_index`, `file_bytes`, or a device root) first brings the roots current and is checked once more, and only then refused (`admit_event`, `device_root_known`).
The boundary pins each registered root's physical path and file identity across repeated snapshots and refuses an Explorer request when the root's current spelling no longer resolves to that pin.
On Windows, active traversal handles deny delete sharing so a root cannot be renamed during file work, and directory rows are enumerated from the verified handle.
The root identity pins retained by the core allow delete sharing, so an unused worktree can be moved and removed while its original identity remains alive; their file identity and final path are checked against the registration before they are supplied.
`file_list` opens relative to a verified root handle and enumerates that handle, then filters each child against the same pin, so replacing the pathname during enumeration cannot expose an outside directory's rows.
The daemon supplies opened checkout-root identity pins to the core's Rust API; editor reads, saves and Explorer mutations reopen the root against that identity when their workers perform I/O.
Trash moves the selected item through its opened parent handle into a private temporary directory outside the mutable checkout path before handing that staged path to the platform Trash API; a failed handoff restores through the retained handles or retains the recovery stage when restoration fails, and an unavailable same-volume move leaves the original in place.
`file_index` retains the checked root handle and opens one directory at a time from it, so a wide checkout does not hold every sibling directory open; ignore files are read only from nonblocking regular-file handles.
Explorer watch stamps each folder by the directory its path names now, from a root opened once per target, so an expanded folder whose path comes to name a different directory has a new stamp and is announced, and a root replaced after it was opened is refused rather than followed.
`apply_boundary` answers a refused path with a `path_refused` frame and a `path.refused` log line, and forwards an accepted one as the canonical path that was checked; a refusal the second check admits is followed by a `boundary.roots_caught_up` line.
`file_list` answers the Explorer's listing for one folder under a root: files and directories, hidden names included, `.git` dropped, directories first then `hide_host::list::natural_cmp`'s order, capped at `LIST_CAP`.
`file_bytes` streams a file's bytes behind the same boundary: the client names a path, an offset and a length, and the daemon answers with one or more binary frames (a 4-byte big-endian header length, the header JSON, then the bytes) of at most 4 MiB, refusing a read past `MAX_FILE_BYTES` (256 MiB) with a `file_bytes_error` frame; the image, PDF and video viewers read through it and build a blob, and no HTTP endpoint serves file bytes (S1 D-03).
The bare browser cannot establish that the daemon is on the viewer's own machine from a loopback URL, because an SSH tunnel can also present `localhost`; its oversized-file action therefore downloads through `file_bytes` even at a loopback URL.
The download asks for 4 MiB ranges and writes each into a browser-selected file when a streaming file writer is available; it does not accumulate an unbounded download in the page.
This S3 stage assumes a browser with a file-save picker for downloads above 256 MiB.
Without that browser API, the existing Blob download remains available for one protocol read of at most 256 MiB and reports a larger read's refusal beside the button; there is no separate unbounded fallback.
The browser WebSocket explicitly refuses `open_external` with `untrusted_client`, even when the page URL is loopback and the socket has its normal token, so an SSH-forwarded browser cannot start a program on the daemon host.
The bounded host opener remains implemented for a future transport that can establish local ownership: macOS `open`, Windows `ShellExecuteW` default-file association, Linux `xdg-open`, or a validated absolute `HIDE_OPEN_COMMAND` CLI helper.
No such trusted transport is present in S3.
For `file_bytes`, hided verifies the path of the opened file handle against the registered checkout root and streams from that same handle, so a symlink swap between path validation and open cannot redirect the read outside the checkout.
On Unix, the byte source is opened with nonblocking flags before its handle is checked as a regular file, so a FIFO cannot stall the WebSocket while waiting for a writer.
The Windows association preserves the default-app action without passing a checkout filename through `cmd`.
At most four opener requests may run at once and at most twelve launches are accepted per minute; crossing a cap answers `over_budget`.
An explicit `HIDE_OPEN_COMMAND` CLI helper is owned with everything it starts: normal stop, the ten-second timeout, the helper's own exit, or daemon death including `SIGKILL` ends them all (`hided/src/spawn.rs`, `OwnedOpener`).
On Windows the helper is an `OwnedChild` and nothing else: its job object ends the tree when the daemon's handle closes, however the daemon ended.
No Unix system ends a whole process group when its owner dies, so there a private `hided` supervisor (`--open-helper`) holds the helper's process group and ends it when its owner socket reports EOF.
The Unix override must not detach into another session or process group, which this ownership boundary cannot supervise.
On Windows a process the helper starts itself stays in the job and ends when the helper returns, so a helper that starts an editor and exits (`code.cmd` with no editor running) ends that editor too; what the shell or an already running application opens for it starts outside the job.
A `.cmd` or `.bat` helper runs through `cmd.exe`, which receives the file's path quoted by the standard library's batch-file rules; such a script must quote `%1` itself.
The normal `open` or `xdg-open` utility is started directly as an OS default-application handoff, since `xdg-open` can stay attached to the application for its lifetime; the daemon does not signal that process or its descendants after acceptance.
Tokio's process driver attempts to reap a short-lived default utility after the handle is dropped; it does not own the registered application's lifetime.
An opener request is accepted when its utility starts, not when the application confirms it opened the file.
Windows `ShellExecuteW` returns after handing the document to its registered application without a CLI child.
The retained host opener refuses files the handler would run or install - application bundles, installers, terminal sessions, scripts, locators, executable files, and files whose header identifies a program - with `not_openable` instead of launching them.
The bare browser downloads oversized files through `file_bytes` wherever the daemon runs.
`file_index` is the ⌘P index (`hided/src/index.rs`): one lazy walk per root of each device honoring `.gitignore` without a git process (`hide_host::index::walk`), capped at 50,000 files and 50,000 folders, ranked by the web shell's fuzzy score (`web/src/search.ts`, mirrored in `hided/src/index.rs::fuzzy_score`) and returned as at most 80 rows; the first query for a root answers `indexing: true` while the walk runs.
A walked list follows the files that change by age: a query that finds it five seconds old (`STALE_AFTER`) answers it at once with `indexing: true` and starts one new walk, which the palette's own polling waits for, so a file made after the first walk is found the next time the palette is asked, on this machine and on a device, and a root is walked at most once per five seconds however fast the operator types.
A refresh that fails or panics keeps the list it replaced, as if just walked, and logs `index.refresh_failed` or `index.refresh_abandoned`; only a first walk that fails is answered `unavailable`.
The watch (`directory_changed`) cannot carry this: it stamps the Explorer's at most 64 folders, never a whole checkout.
Search by file contents does not exist in the web shell on either machine: ⌘P matches names, and the device reaches the same palette with the same caps (PRD S5.5 B5, D-21 exception).
The reason is that content search was never part of the web shell this parity is measured against, and adding it means a new capped `hide_host` search over the walk, its cancellation and a result view, which is a product addition rather than parity.
The operator's result is that a name search works the same on either machine, and a search for text inside files is run in a terminal pane (`rg`) on that machine.
It is re-reviewed when content search is added to the web shell for this machine; the device then takes the same `hide_host` search through its helper.
A device's root is walked by its helper (`Call::Index`) on the index worker, its rows are that device's paths, the answer names the device so the palette never draws another device's list, and a walk the helper could not make is answered as `unavailable` once and walked again on the next query.
`directory_changed` is the watch (`hided/src/watch.rs`), addressed by node id like every other machine read (PRD core-host-node D-21): the node that holds an Explorer's folders stamps them (`Call::Stamps`, `hide_host::list::stamps`) and hided compares each stamp with the one before. This machine's Explorer is the focused checkout's root and its most recently expanded folders, at most 64 in total, releasing the least recently expanded first; the core's own node answers in this process, so it is asked every 200 ms and a change is announced once the burst goes quiet (200 ms).
This machine's root is stamped by the identity the boundary verified when the checkout was registered, the one every listing of it is checked against, so the watcher never opens the path itself and a root replaced since is refused for the watch as for a listing.
The web's listing cache uses the same 64 (`web/src/watch.ts`) and draws a refresh badge on a folder past the cap instead of a live listing.
A device's folders are its helper's to read, so its Explorer is watched the same way through its link: while the selected device's Explorer is showing and its helper is ready, one request every two seconds asks for a stamp of its front checkout's root and watched folders (the same 64, by the same rule), each folder opened from the pinned root and stamped by its device, inode and modification time, and a folder whose stamp moved is announced as `directory_changed` naming the device.
A device root its helper refuses as replaced is opened again through that helper on the next poll, and every watched folder is announced once so the page re-reads the new folder.
A new target is stamped at once, and a folder that joins the watch set in the same checkout is announced once on its first stamp, because the page listed it when it was expanded, possibly before that stamp; hided admits a device frame only for a folder under a root the catalog carries for that device.
The watch asks nothing for a closed Explorer, another device or a helper that is not ready, so it never starts a helper connection and ends with the surface; the next poll waits for the last answer, and a failed poll is logged once as `watch.failed` and asked again two seconds later, whichever node failed.
Every `directory_changed` names its device (the core's node id for this machine's), and a page drops a frame for a device it is not showing, so the same path on two devices never re-reads the other's listing.
Terminal attachments (`hided/src/attachments.rs`) are the bytes-in path: the browser reads a dropped file's or pasted image's bytes but cannot name its path, so it stages one file per upload in binary frames and commits a batch, and hided writes the staged files and sends the one `terminal_attachment` event, with the staged paths as `paths`.
Both the id and the name are flattened to one path component, one id and one path identify one stage, a stage whose bytes do not match its declared size is dropped at eof, every arriving byte is charged to a 256 MiB staged budget with a 60-second grace on a fresh commit (`COMMIT_GRACE`, which also bounds the core's wait for a terminal), hided's own attachments directory starts empty each run, staged files and their directories stay private (0600/0700), a stage commits only into the root its mode opened, a completed stage leaves the open-upload cap and a connection's uncommitted stages are released when it goes, and the shell's own attachment events are the daemon's to send: a client that sends `terminal_attachment`, `terminal_attachment_ready` or `terminal_attachment_action` is answered with an error frame and it never reaches the core, because those events name arbitrary paths.
A clipboard image stages at the exact path the core reads it from, and hided reports `terminal_attachment_ready` itself.
The caps are 20 MiB per file and 40 MiB and 8 files per batch, checked here and again in the core; a refusal is one line over the pane (B15).
