A `file_list`, `file_open`, `reveal_path` or `file_save` that names a `device_id` other than the core's node id is not a path on this machine, so it skips this boundary: the device's helper judges it against the pinned root, and hided answers that device's `file_list` itself from the helper, or with `directory_unavailable` and a code (`not_ready`, `busy`, `unknown`, `refused`) when it cannot.
A device's `file_bytes` names the checkout `root` beside the path: hided admits only a root the catalog carries for that device and asks the helper for one range of at most 4 MiB at a time (`Call::Bytes`, `hide_host::bytes::read`), sending each as the same binary frame before asking for the next, so a device read holds one range in memory as a local one does; every range names the file it came from, and a file that changes between ranges ends the read as `read_failed` rather than joining two files' bytes.
A helper's range is accepted only as the range asked for (the same offset and file size, never longer, and shorter only where the read ends), so a helper cannot stretch one read into millions of round trips or end it early as if the file were shorter.
One client runs at most two device reads at once: a third ends the oldest, which is answered `file_bytes_error` with reason `superseded` and its own request id, so whatever waited on it settles (D-15).
Every frame and reason code lives in `contracts/hided-ws.schema.json`.
A snapshot section is declared there once the web reads it through the contract (`usageSnapshot`, `usagePreferences`, `uiStateUsageHints`); the type generator emits only what a frame reaches, so `web/scripts/gen-types.mjs` names each such `$def` to emit beside the frames.
Usage has one node row for the core's own node and one agent row per declared adapter (`herdr-core/src/usage/model.rs`); each row separates limit and consumption cells.
A cell is unsupported, loading, unavailable with a cause, or a reading with freshness and observation time; limit readings contain independently typed five-hour, weekly and scoped windows.
Consumption readings carry prepared popover and year ranges for that agent, plus retained totals and native provider ids.
Each node also carries optional all-agent history and share rows; selecting a filter never makes the shell recompute totals.
Limit and ledger publications update only their own cells, so neither worker can erase the other’s values.
The core owns the ledger worker, feeds it only actual window visibility and usage-open hints, and joins it outside the runtime lock on shutdown.
Worker publication builds shared range values before locking the runtime; snapshots clone those references, and serialization remains outside the lock.
A failed start publishes unavailable consumption instead of leaving a loading cell forever.
The UI preferences store pinned `(agent, window)` pairs (at most three) and hidden agents; `usage_pin_set` and `usage_hidden_set` are desired-state events, and a generic `ui_state_update` preserves these fields so a stale window cannot overwrite another window's choice.
The core rejects undeclared windows and account windows reported as not offered; Hide preserves the agent's pins for Show.


### The Explorer, editor and viewers

PRs (`web/src/PrsPanel.tsx`, drawing `web/src/PrList.tsx`) and History (`web/src/HistoryList.tsx`) are the lane's tools beside Files and Memory, with the 보기 slot (`web/src/DetailPanel.tsx`) drawn in the same lane, and the Explorer (`web/src/ExplorerTree.tsx`) is the files tool's tree (`web/src/Tools.tsx`), one tool at a time in the lane, shown while the front Workspace's `workspace_view` says so (see Workspaces in the web shell): the core owns which folders are expanded (`ui_state.expanded_paths`) and hided answers one listing per folder, so an Explorer row is a pure function of those, the checkout's changed-file set and the file's icon (`web/src/explorer.ts`, unit-tested without a browser).
`@tanstack/react-virtual` lays out a 10,000-row folder; nothing in a row runs git or reads the disk (B16).
The core computes a checkout's changed files only while Explorer or History is visible or a diff or empty new tab is active (in the web shell, any area's active display that is a diff or empty browser), and the web renders only a Changes snapshot whose root matches the front checkout's `navigator.changes_root_path`.
Each answer names the device and folder it was read for (`ChangesKey`); the core drops one that no longer matches the checkout in front, and moving to another device's checkout clears the published set in the same frame, so the same path on two devices never shows the other's changes (B22).
An answer lands one pump wake after its request and also names the History row it was read for; in the web shell, an answer for a row the operator has left since publishes its lists and View diffs but keeps the row and the diff in front, because taking that row would make the next request ask for it again and two rows would alternate with a Git read and a frame on every wake while nothing is driven.
History keeps the core's uncommitted and branch groups separate, and a row sends one `changes_select` event with its path, group and preview intent; it starts no Git read from render, scroll or click.
A workspace registration can name a folder below the Git top level while the catalog projects its checkout at the repository root; `navigator.changes_root_path` keeps History on the registered folder for that checkout, on this machine or a device.
The host refuses a root that is not its repository's top level and a scope that is not a real folder of the checkout (a link swapped in is refused), scopes both groups and selected diffs to it, and presents a rename crossing the boundary as an addition or deletion without naming the outside path; a failed or refused read is `unavailable_reason`, never an empty list, unless a list from an earlier read of the same checkout is on screen: that list stays with the failure as `stale_reason`, and the shell's History and Explorer say it may be out of date.
The host refuses only a folder Git finds no repository for as `Unsupported`, and the core marks that answer `not_a_repository`: there is nothing to repair, so the Explorer draws no mark for it and the `changes.unavailable` diagnostic records it; a `git` that cannot run or did not finish is `Io`, a failure the Explorer marks.
Its Git commands run from the root's opened handle (`fchdir` before exec) with `--literal-pathspecs`, and the request names the root identity the channel pinned, so a checkout replaced or moved after it was opened is refused rather than redirecting Git to another repository.
Every Git command the host runs ends within its deadline: it is an owned child that leads its own tree and the deadline stops the tree, so a hook, filter, fsmonitor or textconv it started cannot hold its output open and keep one of the helper's four workers; output still held open a moment after git ended makes the call unfinished, never an empty answer that a dirty or nested-repository check would read as clean; `git worktree remove` is judged by the registration and folder read back afterwards, so an answer cut short after Git removed the folder still reads as removed.
Each requested diff's Git stdout is captured only to the 256 KiB wire budget plus a UTF-8 boundary, then the child is ended with the existing truncation notice; a large patch is never fully buffered before the limit.
A diff is the core's bounded unified patch in the `changes` snapshot, rendered as a read-only CodeMirror 6 view with old and new number gutters; a diff tab has no editor document, autosave or draft buffer.
Several diffs can be on screen at once (PRD S7 A5): the Changes request names every visible diff display of the front Workspace, the area-active displays whose kind is diff and so at most the area cap, and one `Call::Changes` answers them all in `diffs`.
The snapshot carries one entry per display in `changes.diffs` (`path`, `committed`, `text`, `notice`, each within `MAX_DIFF_BYTES`), omitted when empty, and a diff display renders the entry for its own path and group, so two visible diffs never draw each other's patch; `changes.diff`, `selected_path` and `selected_committed` stay as they were for History's selection.
The diffs are taken under the folder History reads, the front Project's registered folder, while a Workspace is keyed by its checkout, so every Project registered in one checkout shares its views; a diff display outside that folder gets no text and a `notice` naming the folder, and shows its diff when a Project that holds it is in front.
The request and its answer changed shape, so the helper protocol version moved with them (`hide-node-link/src/protocol.rs`); unaudited protocol mismatches are refused before use.
Protocol 25 added bounded reader-feature facts to the existing authenticated Hello, retained only for that live connection; the current protocol (30; 26 added terminal lines, 27 the `line_input` read an agent start waits on, 28 a node dialing its core and 29 the shell integration an agent start's tab is started with and 30 typed hook reads in pane proofs) keeps them, and a node on 25 or 26 is refused and replaced.
Structured question and plan content is a separate feature from turn lifecycle facts.
An unsupported content field is removed before typed decoding in both directions, preserving native question identities, answered markers and plan waits for a helper that supports turns alone.
Native session ID projection is independently gated by Identity support; Labels-only answers retain their owner proof while the optional native ID is removed before decoding.
Missing or malformed facts disable the affected reader feature while file and Git operations remain available; the protocol number alone grants no reader.
The audited protocol-24 transition keeps its frozen Claude/Codex and partial OpenCode label/link capabilities, with no new reader or structured user-turn content inferred from the current build.
Reader requests and returned bodies are checked against the live link before serialization and decoding; draining, closed or replaced links cannot lend their retired facts to a checkpoint.
Provider-bearing discovery rows are inspected as bounded raw records before their format enum is decoded, and mixed link batches refuse unsupported members without discarding supported results or advancing the refused member's durable cursor.
Reader header strings decode JSON escapes before provider and requested-path comparison, so a native Windows path keeps the same identity as the request; strings without escapes remain borrowed.
Filtered discovery rows still count toward the original page and oldest timestamp, so an unsupported provider cannot hide older supported files by shortening pagination.
A device normally runs the helper this Hide carries, installed by the digest of its bytes; ordinary payload replacement refreshes the Hello facts on a new connection and remains the recovery for an unaudited mismatch.
The patch wire does not carry complete old and new documents, so `@codemirror/merge`'s two-document view would require an extra read and is not used.
A single click opens the active View area's preview display, a double click or ⌘⇧K pins it, and each area's tab bar draws its own displays, in italics while preview (see Workspaces in the web shell).
An opened document is revealed by merging its ancestors into `ui_state.expanded_paths` (`actions.revealAncestors`), not by the core's `reveal_path`, which promotes the tab and would contradict the preview the palette's Enter promises (B3, B12); the core's `selected_path` then highlights the row.
The editor is CodeMirror 6 (`web/src/editor/`): language packs load per document kind, an edit saves itself after 600 ms of idle and ⌘S saves at once (D-10), a disk change becomes the core's conflict choice, and Markdown Live hides its inline markup (`markdownLive.ts` is a pure plan) while a leading YAML frontmatter block is drawn in its own eight-line pane that scrolls inside itself (`frontmatter.ts` finds the block; the editor then holds one document in two views, and the split and merge never enter either history, D-11). Live also draws an HTML block and an image as widgets from a state field, since CodeMirror takes block decorations only from state: `liveHtml.ts` cleans the HTML (DOMPurify) and decides where an image address may be read from, `liveImages.ts` reads a file image through `file_bytes` (a device checkout through its helper) under a per-view cap and revokes its blob URLs with the view, and a widget keeps its DOM while its source and base are unchanged.
The documents on screen travel in their own delta section, `documents`, beside `editor` (PRD S7 A4): `visible` lists the file documents shown by the front Workspace's area-active displays while its mode shows Views, so at most six, in tree order, and `changed` carries each whose own revision passed the reader's `have_revision`.
Each document keeps its own revision, so a keystroke re-sends only the document it changed, where the `editor` section used to re-send the whole active document on every change; `visible` goes out whenever the section does, a reader with cursor 0 and every full snapshot frame get every visible document, and the section is absent when nothing changed for that reader.
A snapshot read tells a document, and the `changes` section with every diff on screen, moved by an edit number (`model::Edited`) rather than by comparing contents: the value is mutable only through a call that takes a new number, and a landing Changes read replaces the section only when it differs, so per visible document a read costs a few map lookups and one number comparison, and a document is copied only when it was edited.
The web keeps the documents whose ids are in `visible`, overwrites the changed ones and drops the rest; in web mode `editor.document` is always null and `editor.active_tab_id` names the active area's document, and the single-surface fallback never carries the section.
Each display is its own CodeMirror view over its document and keeps its own scroll position and selection by display id.
Displays of one document are kept in step in the page through a per-document channel (`web/src/editor/sync.ts`), which applies the minimal change outside the undo history and marks it as a pending echo, so the other view shows a keystroke before the core's echo arrives and that echo neither moves a caret nor replaces newer input.
Drafts, autosave and close-with-save stay keyed per document (S5.5), so two views of a file write one draft and queue one save.
Image, PDF and video viewers read hided's bytes; the web decides video from the extension because the core has no Video kind (D-09).
An image or PDF viewer's zoom is page-local state of the display that draws it (`web/src/viewers/{viewerZoom.ts,useViewerZoom.ts}`), never a core value: it decides no accepted transition, is a view of the moment that restarts at the fit for each opened file, and a pinch moves it at the input's frame rate, so publishing it would add a frame per wheel event for nothing another client or a restart needs (see State placement and publication).
`actions.textScale` asks the viewer of the keyboard's View area first, through a registry the mounted viewers join (`registerViewerZoom`), and only when none can zoom sends `editor_text_scale` or `pane_text_scale` as before.
A PDF draws only the pages in view and one view either side, at most ten (`MAX_DRAWN_PAGES`, pdf.js's own page buffer), one page at a time, each canvas held to pdf.js's `maxCanvasPixels`, and draws again 200 ms after a zoom or resize rests (`web/src/viewers/pdfPages.ts`).
Unsaved drafts live in IndexedDB (`web/src/buffers.ts`, store `drafts_v4`), keyed by the daemon host, the device, the checkout root and the real path (PRD S5.5 B9-B12).
The host is hided's persistent `host_id` (`hided/src/state_file.rs`, the `host-id` file in its state directory, sent in the `daemon` frame), so a draft written against one Hide host is never opened by another that serves the same browser origin.
A draft for an open document is restored on reconnect, and one whose contents the core already holds is removed only once the core reports the document clean: a dirty core holding the same text is a refused or pending save, and the stored copy is the only one a daemon restart keeps (B13-B15).
A draft no open tab stands for is a recovery item (`web/src/DraftRecovery.tsx`): a status line counts them, and the review sheet offers Open (its own checkout on its own device, after Show device or Show checkout moves there), Export as a `.draft` download, or a two-step Discard; nothing is discarded for its age or because its tab closed.
A rename or move carries the draft to the new identity; a draft that cannot be stored, including when every stored draft together would pass 512 MiB or the browser's quota is full, keeps its tab editable, marks it "kept in this tab only" and offers Export there, and while any open tab holds such a draft every other clean document opens read-only with the reason, so no new edit is made that could not be kept; saving that draft releases the hold, and no stored draft, on any device, is removed to make room (D-14, B44).
The v4 upgrade copies every older draft into `drafts_v4` as an unverified recovery item (no host or device) inside the upgrade transaction, so an interrupted upgrade keeps the old stores and runs again; only an open of this machine's own checkout at the same root and path claims one, and a device's document never does.
Edits are coalesced to one active and one pending committed write per document with a bounded total queue; a move retires the old identity in the same transaction that claims the new one.

### Device file hosts and document saves

`hide-host` is the one file authority for a checkout on any machine (PRD S5.5 D-05): opening a root and pinning its identity, listing a folder, reading a document with its content revision, and saving it.
The daemon's own machine runs it in process (`hide_node::Local`); an SSH device runs the same code as `hided node serve`, this same program in its node role, which speaks one JSON line per request over an exec channel (`hide-node/src/ssh/host.rs`).
Both are a `NodeLink` (`hide-node-link`), so the core's document code (`files.rs`, `runtime/documents.rs`) and hided's listing (`boundary.rs`, `server.rs::device_listing`) make the same call whichever device the checkout is on, and neither keeps a second copy of the root, link or listing rules.
A helper runs only with the operator's consent for that device (D-20, D-23): `register_device` with `host_consent` or a later `device_host_consent` records it on the registration with the consent contract, the install root (`HIDE_HOST_HELPER_ROOT`, default `~/.hide/host-helper`; a consent for the legacy default `~/.local/share/hide/host-helper` with the same command folder is rewritten to it on its next connection without asking, and only when the daemon was not told another root, PRD hide-home-layout D-12) and the command folder (`HIDE_HOST_CLI_DIR`, default `~/.local/bin`), and the first connection binds it to the SSH user, host, port and host key that answered.
A different identity (`identity_changed`) or a build whose contract, root or command folder differs (`outdated`) starts nothing until the operator allows it again.
Revoking starts no new work: the connection admits nothing more, even from a worker that still holds it (a worktree removal between its pane closes and its host call, a facts walk), a request still waiting for a slot is refused unsent, and a save held for the helper to become ready is dropped with its draft kept; a request admitted earlier but not yet written is refused unsent too; the requests already sent answer on the old connection and settle to their real results, and the connection closes once they have (or after 250 s, more than a request's longest wait to be written and answered).
Revoking deletes no draft, remote file or installed helper.
The install root is refused unless it and every folder above it cannot be changed by another account: each is owned by the device account or by root and writable by neither its group nor others, except a root-owned sticky folder such as `/tmp`; the folders are checked as spelled while they are created and again on the root's resolved real path from `/`, home and its parents included, and the helper is then installed under and started from that resolved path, so no link on the spelled path is followed between the check and the launch (OpenSSH's rule for key files, which a device that signs in with a key already meets). A folder whose mode the device does not report is refused, and a group-writable one (a `umask 002` home on some Linux systems) is refused with the folder and the `chmod go-w` fix named.
The helper file itself is reused, or started after its upload, only as the account's own regular file that no group or other account can write, with its mode reported.
The helper is uploaded and replaced only when the device lacks this build's bytes, runs only while its connection lives, and is never started by anything but a connection; the helper itself is never resident and never starts at login.
Consent contract 3 is the whole install kit (PRD device-parity D-12): the install puts this build's helper and every kit part for the device's platform (`hide` and `hide-agent-hooks`) in the helper's version folder, named by a digest over every file's path, mode and bytes (`hide-node/src/ssh/host.rs::Payload`), keeping the folder layout the kit reads.
A contract-2 consent (the helper and the `hide` command only) whose install root and command folder match is carried to 3 when its next connection starts, keeping the identity it was bound to, and `host.consent_upgraded` records it (D-13); a device with no consent is not touched until the operator allows it (D-25).
Every file but the helper is reused by size and mode once a verified upload is in its private folder, because Hide never runs them and reading every part back on each connection would delay each reconnect, so an install cut off part way resumes with only what it had not sent (B18); the helper, which Hide runs, is still checked byte for byte.
A kit part the build does not carry for the device, or one that could not be placed, never stops the helper: `host.ready` records the upload's `sent`, `reused` and `missing`, and the kit on the device reports that part as missing from the build.
The daemon offers a device the `hided` build for its platform (`HelperPackages`): its own program for its own platform, or a `hided-<os>-<arch>` beside it; a device whose platform has no build is `unsupported` with that reason, and another platform's build is never substituted.
Every connection attempt takes its number from one runtime-wide counter that removing a device never resets, so an attempt started before a device was removed and added again under the same id cannot settle, close or bind consent for the new connection.
The product's device platforms are macOS on arm64 and x86_64; the S5.5 verification covered two arm64 Macs, and x86_64 packaging is not finished.
Admission per device is four running and thirty-two waiting requests; past that a request is refused as `busy`, never dropped.
A call has one deadline, its timeout counted from when it was asked: the wait for a slot, the wait for the link's writer, the write and the answer all end by it, so a caller that stopped waiting leaves none of that call's waits behind (`RemoteHost::call`); a call still waiting for a slot or the writer at its deadline was not sent and is refused as `busy`, and one written but unanswered is `unknown`.
A device has one SSH connection (PRD core-host-node b4, `hide-node/src/ssh.rs::Connection`): the node's link, the Herdr API channels, attachment SFTP, the Herdr status read and the browser forwards open their channels on it, the first use dials it and a use after it closed dials it again, and it ends when its last holder goes: the device's client and the node's link each hold it, so a device removed while its link still has a call out (the kit coming off) keeps the connection until that call answers and the link closes; terminals and the capability test still dial their own until layer 3.
At most eight session channels (an exec, a shell or a subsystem) are open on it at once, below OpenSSH's default `MaxSessions` of ten, and the link holds one for as long as it lives; a use past that waits for one to close and, after the SSH operation bound, fails with a `connection.sessions_full` record.
Closing a link ends its channel, so its node reads the end of its input and exits, and leaves the connection to the channels that share it.
A long call (a Git watch, a clone, a disk measurement) reports over the link as it runs, each report its own `{"progress": id, ...}` line; a report the caller answers with false, or its timeout passing, sends `cancel`, which the node answers at once rather than behind the work it stops, and reports already on their way are not the caller's any more (`hide-node/src/ssh/host.rs`, `hide-host/src/serve.rs`).
At most 256 reports wait unread for one call; past that the call is stopped and its caller told why (`host.reports_overflowed`).
This machine's node waits at most a call's timeout too: a plain call runs on a thread of its own, at most 64 at once, and a reporting call stops at its next report once the timeout has passed (`hide-node/src/lib.rs`).
A call its caller stopped waiting for keeps its place until its work ends, so each such call is a `node.call_unanswered` record naming the request, and a call refused past the cap a `node.busy` record, which together say what holds the places.
The Factory reads a verification's progress as such a read: a link that was busy, not connected or did not answer leaves the run pending for the next poll, and only the node's own answer settles it, `lost` included, for a run its node no longer holds (`hide-factory/src/project.rs`).
A link that is lost, or fails to start for a reason that may pass, is tried again on its own two seconds later, the wait doubling to a minute (`runtime/hosts.rs`, `host.retry_scheduled`); a read before then starts nothing, a link that starts begins the schedule over, and the operator's Retry tries at once.
The device's Herdr session answering again says the machine is reachable, so the retry is due at once instead of at the end of a wait that grew while it was away (`device_reachable_again`, `host.retry_due_session_back`); the delay after it keeps doubling, and this happens once per outage (`SessionBack`, ended when the link is ready or the operator retries), so a session that flaps while the helper keeps failing is still dialed no oftener than the schedule's cap allows; a session that comes back while an attempt runs makes that attempt's failure retry at once, the once.
A link that closed while its attempt still ran heard its end in a phase that takes none, so the attempt's answer reads it (`closed_reason` of the host it brings) as a lost link, tried again on its own like any other, and a dead host is never stored ready.
A device's panes read `unavailable` while its link needs the operator and `reconnecting` while the core will try it again by itself (`helper_link_wait`, applied to the pane header's band in `refresh_pane_headers`), so the wait and the dead end read differently from the same transport state.
The operator's Reconnect on a device that still does not answer retires the connection and starts a new one (`Retiring::Retried`): the status row keeps its last session and the time it first stopped answering, and the kit work queued for the device waits for the next link, where a removed device keeps nothing (`Retiring::Removed`).
The words a failed session or helper failed with go to the diagnostic log (`remote.session_unavailable`, `host.failed`) and to the device's row in Settings; a screen that shows the device's last state says only that it is not connected.
Each step a connection reaches (connect, platform, environment, install, start, hello, panes, ready) is a `host.establish_stage` record with the time since it began, so one that stalls names its step.
A link withdrawn while it works (consent removed) sends nothing new but stops each call that reports until told to stop (`Call::runs_until_stopped`, the Git watch) with a cancel, which adds no effect on the device, rather than wait out its bound, while a clone or any other call with an effect settles to its own result; the device's helper reads every cancel and the end of its input at once, since it answers a request it has no worker or queue place for busy rather than wait for one (`hide-host/src/serve.rs`), with its own message, as the node's other busy answers (a save waiting on its folder's lock) keep theirs.

Every document read runs on a worker with the runtime lock released, this machine's disk included (`runtime/documents.rs::start_document_open`), and the tab lands when the read does; a reveal of a file moves the checkout, the panel and the tree only then, and only while the checkout the operator had in front is still in front, so a read that fails moves nothing.
A document is pinned at open to its device, its root path with the identity the host reported, and its path relative to that root (`DocumentPlace`), and every save goes back through that pin: a root that was replaced since is refused, and a save after a reconnect goes to the new helper.
A device root's identity is the one its link pinned when it first touched the root (`node_access::pinned_root`), for opens and Explorer changes as for listings, so a folder made again at the same path after it was listed is refused; an open that is refused this way unpins it, and the operator's next open adopts the new folder.
The draft's base is a content revision (`sha256:<hex>`), not a modification time.
A save is exchange-and-verify, not a compare-and-swap (`hide-host/src/save.rs` states exactly what it guarantees): the file is hashed against the base, the draft is written beside it and exchanged in one atomic rename (`RENAME_SWAP`, `RENAME_EXCHANGE`), and the displaced file is hashed again; a change that landed in between is exchanged back and the save is a conflict, so a change complete at the path is never overwritten and the original is never left truncated.
A third writer that replaces the path during that swap back is not lost either: its file ends up beside the path under the save's temporary name and is kept, and the conflict names the revision now at the path so Keep Editing can adopt it.
A filesystem with no atomic exchange refuses the save and the draft stays, exportable from the conflict or unknown bar.
Saves and revision reads in one folder hold an advisory lock, exclusive and shared, so Hide's own read-back waits for a save still running in a helper whose connection already ended.
One save runs per document and only the newest draft waits behind it.
Reload and a save exclude each other on one tab: Reload is refused while a save is running, waiting, queued or unsettled (`file.reload_busy`), and a save is not sent while a reload of that tab is in flight (`file.save_during_reload`), so neither takes the other's revision or slot away.
A save asked for while the device's helper is still connecting has not been sent: it is `waiting`, goes out when the helper is ready, and is dropped with the draft kept and the reason shown if the connection fails; that is a different state from `unknown`, whose save was sent.
A save whose answer was lost to a timeout or a dropped connection is `unknown`: it is never resent, further saves wait, and when the device's helper is ready the file is read back: the draft's revision means it saved, the base's means it has not reached the file yet (`file.save_not_applied`, and the tab's save state `not_applied`, which the bar words "Not saved yet" rather than "Not saved", because a helper whose connection ended may still finish it; Retry is offered there, and a retry that meets the late write lands as a conflict, never over it), anything else is a conflict.

An Explorer change (`file_create`, `dir_create`, `path_rename`, `path_move`, `path_copy`, `path_import`, `path_trash`) is one `hide_host` request too (`hide-host/src/mutate.rs`, `files::apply_explorer_operation`), made by the host of the checkout in front on a worker: this machine's in process, a device's by its helper.
The event names the device whose tree asked, and a change for another device than the one in front is refused (B34); hided lets a device's change past this machine's checkout roots and the helper confines every path to the opened root.
Changes run one at a time, in the order they were asked, because the page sends a change without waiting for the one before it (a drop, then the key that trashes the folder it landed in).
The core holds the running change in `explorer_operation` and up to four asked meanwhile in its own queue (`request_explorer_change` in `runtime/session.rs`), which never reaches the snapshot; the settle of one change starts the next under the same lock, planned against the checkout in front at that moment.
A change that fails or is refused drops the waiting ones with one `explorer.queue_dropped` diagnostic, because they may rely on it and the operator has not seen the failure; so does a waiting change whose checkout is no longer in front when its turn comes, and a fifth waiting change is refused with `explorer.busy`.
A copy and a drop add items and move none, so they share the slot and the queue but no expanded folder or open tab follows them anywhere.
`path_copy` (`Call::Copy`) names the item and the folder it lands in; the host picks the free name (`name`, `name copy.ext`, `name copy 2.ext`), builds the copy in a hidden `.hide-copy-*` folder inside the destination and renames it into place, so it appears whole or not at all, refuses a link or special file, and measures against 5,000 items and 256 MiB before it writes.
The slot's `destination` is the name the host chose, which the tree selects.
A copy stays inside one checkout: the page refuses a paste from another checkout or device, and the event names one root.
`path_import` (`Call::Import`) is a drop from the operator's file manager, and its owner is hided: a window cannot name a path outside every registered checkout (AGENTS.md), so it uploads each file to hided's staging as an attachment is and sends `path_import` with stage ids; hided checks the shape and the destination, turns the ids into the staged paths and is the only maker of the core's `path_import`, never forwarding the window's event.
The core's worker reads the staged files through its own node (`Call::ReadDropped`, answered only by the node that staged them, so a device helper never receives it) and sends the bytes to the checkout's host with `Call::Import`, which writes them under a hidden working folder as a copy does; a device checkout is written by its helper through the same call.
A drop holds at most 500 items, 20 MiB a file and 40 MiB in all, the staging caps, and the page, hided and the host each check them.
The node protocol was 37 for the two calls, and is 38 since `LetterPull` carries `tool`, a hook's ask at a tool event for the letters due while the agent works ([delivery.md](delivery.md#letters-while-the-agent-works)); a device helper of an earlier protocol is refused with the existing rebuild-or-reinstall line until the app and its helper match.
Trash stages the item in the device's temporary directory before handing it to the Trash, so on a Linux device whose `/tmp` is another filesystem the move is refused and the item stays; Linux devices are outside this stage's supported platforms.
Nothing is replaced: a creation opens exclusively and a rename or move uses the kernel's exclusive rename, so a name that appears meanwhile is refused and both items stay (B16).
A trash names the item by the inode the tree listed (`directory_list` entries carry it as decimal text, because a JSON number reaches the browser as a double and rounds a 64-bit id past 2^53, as an NTFS id is once its sequence number reaches 32) and is refused if another item took that path; the item moves through its opened parent into a private staging folder outside the checkout, is checked again there, and only then goes to that machine's Trash, and a Trash that refuses it puts it back, so nothing is ever deleted permanently (B17).
On success the core carries its own paths in that checkout only: the device's expanded folders, and each open tab with the place its saves go to, so a renamed file saves to its new path; a trashed file's tab keeps its draft and shows the file as removed, and a save does not recreate it (B18).
A change whose answer was lost is reported as an unknown result and the tree reads the folder again.

### The install kit

`hide-kit` is the one list of what Hide puts on a machine: the `hide` command in its command folder (a Unix link or Windows `hide.cmd` and its `.hide-kit` junction), Hide's Claude Code and Codex hook entries through `hide-agent-hooks`, and one release of coordination retirement (`coordination_retirement`).
After the Codex hook part is in place, a pass also has Codex trust Hide's own entries, and the entries the kit recorded Herdr's integration writing, through `codex app-server` (`hide-kit/src/codex_trust.rs`; [agent-hooks.md](agent-hooks.md#codex-trusts-hides-own-hooks) owns the target rule, what is recorded and how, the child's ownership and the failure line).
That check runs once, between `agent_kit::apply` (which puts in the agents' pieces, Herdr's integration among them) and `agent_kit::reports`, so the Codex hook part carries its failure into the agents' rows and a first install is trusted in the pass that made it; `status` starts no process and repeats the last pass's finding.
Each part answers `observe`, `install` and `remove` against a `KitTarget` naming the machine's home, kit and command folders, Herdr socket and binary; a new part is one more entry in that list.
The kit folder is a path that outlives a build, because the hooks and the `hide` link name it: the running app bundle's `Contents/Resources` on macOS, the unpacked desktop package's `resources` on Windows/Linux, and the helper root's `current` link on a device, which the helper points at its own version folder before it installs, as a core that runs from that root does.
`~/.hide/kit/installed.json` records the parts the kit installed; a recorded part that is gone was taken away by the operator and stays away until Reinstall (D-26), a record that cannot be read installs nothing on a guess, and `~/.hide/agent-hooks/installed-once` from before the kit is not such a record (B20).
An apply installs what was never installed and replaces what is outdated, one part's failure never stops the next, and a second apply of the same build writes nothing; Reinstall also restores the parts it names (B8).
Another tool's hook entries, a `hide` that is not Hide's link, and Herdr's per-plugin config folders are never touched.
Every apply also retires the agent labels Herdr plugin `hide.agent-context-labels`, which the core's label worker replaced (PRD labels-in-hided D-12; `hide-kit/src/labels.rs`), before it installs any part: its Herdr link is taken out (a GitHub install with the machine's `herdr plugin uninstall`, any other link with `plugin.unlink`), a watcher found by the lock it holds under `<home>/.local/state/hide.agent-context-labels/watcher.lock` is sent `SIGTERM` and, after five seconds, `SIGKILL` for it and its descendants, and the kit's copy `~/.hide/kit/plugins/agent-context-labels` and the plugin's state folder are removed once the link is gone, so a pass that could not unlink (Herdr down) keeps them and tries again on the next.
What was removed and what stayed is `KitReport.labels_retirement`, which the core logs as `plugin.retired` or `plugin.retire_incomplete`; it is not a kit part, so Settings shows no row for it.
On this Mac the core imports the plugin's state before the kit first runs.
Every child the kit starts (`herdr plugin uninstall`, `herdr integration`, `codex features`, the login shell asked for its `PATH`) runs with a cleared environment, as an owned child that leads its own tree, under a deadline, and ends when the kit's owner raises its stop flag (`hide-kit/src/process.rs`, `hide-agent-hooks/src/codex_daemon.rs`, both over `hide_platform::process::run_to_end`).
The one-release retirement stage first performs bounded, read-only checks of the former coordinator and Hide ledgers, sasu registry and registered checkout run state.
An active run, open request, active watch, unreadable record or capacity failure leaves every installation and folder unchanged and reports why.
For a device, `remote::retirement` reads that account's state and its registered checkout roots through the existing SSH/SFTP connection before any helper upload, folder creation or current-link update, with the existing 15-second operation deadline.
It shares `hide_kit::retirement_inspection` predicates with the local pass and needs neither a new helper nor a remote interpreter to inspect the old state.
The shared authority check refuses relocated legacy homes outside HOME or overlapping the active kit or state, and requires a registered owning checkout for indexed runs outside HOME, before either pass can write.
No old test server is stopped by name without an ownership receipt; the operator confirms earlier test servers before the transition and refuses automatic cleanup when ownership cannot be established.
After preflight it stops the former daemon, unloads the LaunchAgent and deletes its plist, removes only Hide-owned command/plugin links, deletes the kit copy, then renames `~/.hide/hcoord` and `~/.hcoord` to siblings ending in `.retired-YYYY-MM-DD`.
An unavailable Herdr server does not imply a remaining plugin: retirement strictly reads the owned global `plugins.json` at the pinned Herdr's config location, accepting a missing registry or absent coordination entry as already unlinked.
A registered entry uses Herdr's existing offline uninstall and must disappear from the registry before the step completes; malformed or untrusted state and an unavailable removal command remain resumable failures.
`~/.hide/kit/coordination-retirement.json` records the step, reason and completion for idempotent retry.
After a failed retirement step, ordinary parts continue and the retirement row keeps that failure; a preflight refusal precedes the account lock and stops the entire apply.
The old ledger is never imported or deleted and there is no rollback; the operator chooses the merge/install transition time with all work closed and chooses the later release that removes the stage.
Legacy labels and helper folders are retired separately by `hide-kit/src/legacy.rs`, only after account ownership and references are checked; results remain diagnostic records.
The kit runs nothing from and writes nothing into a folder another account can change: `~/.hide` and `~/.hide/kit` are created mode 0700, and a part whose folder is not owned by the account or is writable by its group or others reads failed with that folder named rather than used (`hide_kit::private_dirs`).
One kit at a time changes an account: an apply or a removal holds an exclusive `flock` on `~/.hide/kit/.lock` (`hide_kit::lock_account`), waits up to 90 seconds for another kit holding it, and reports every part unavailable when that wait runs out or Hide is quitting, because two registrations of one machine connect together at launch and a Mac that is another Mac's device also runs its own kit.

A Codex attached to its shared app-server daemon runs its hooks in the daemon's environment rather than the pane's, so Herdr never learns that pane's session and no request, result or label can be read for it (openai/codex#48500).
The kit does not change that setting on its own (PRD settings-cleanup D-14): the earlier `Codex를 pane마다 실행` part (`codex_per_pane`) and its switch are gone, a record that still names it loads and drops it, and a machine where an earlier Hide turned the setting off keeps it off.
`hide_agent_hooks::codex_daemon` stays the one place that writes the setting or stops the daemon, only through Codex's own `codex features disable daemon_auto_start` and `codex app-server daemon stop`, run with the account's HOME and its `.codex` named as `CODEX_HOME`, for the turn-off the operator confirmed; the kit itself only reads the setting and whether a daemon answers, and Codex's `config.toml` is never written by Hide.
The stop (`codex_daemon::stop`) asks `codex app-server daemon version` first and, unless it reads running, asks `codex app-server daemon stop` anyway and takes its typed `status`: `notRunning` means nothing was running, so nothing is stopped and a second request stops nothing again, unless `daemon version` read running, when it is checked again like `stopped`, and any other answer is a failure; the check after the stop is the pane's read, socket rule included, so a daemon whose socket lives elsewhere reads as down once `daemon stop` typed `stopped`; a daemon that still answers after the stop is a failure rather than a stop (PRD codex-daemon-apply D-04, D-11).
Hide supports eight agents, each a row of `hide_kit::agents::ADAPTERS` in this order: Claude Code, Codex, Grok, OpenCode, Pi, omp, Cursor and Antigravity.
They are the agents the pinned Herdr ships an integration for, which is the support policy ([agent-hooks.md: Which agents Hide supports](agent-hooks.md#which-agents-hide-supports)).
A per-agent layer (`hide-kit/src/agent_kit.rs`) sits beside the parts: `Scope` carries `agent_on` and `agent_off`, `KitReport.agents` answers one `AgentReport` per adapter, the record keeps the operator's choice and the pieces installed, and the core's `kit_agent_set` event, `KitSnapshot.agents` and the host protocol's `reinstall` (`agents_on`, `agents_off`) carry the same choice to a device.
Each agent also gets the pinned Herdr's integration for it as a piece of its row (`AgentReport.herdr`, record code `herdr:<agent>`), put in and taken out through the machine's own Herdr CLI, the bundled one on this Mac and on a device the pinned copy the build brought there, else the device's own (`KitTarget::herdr_bin`, `hide-kit/src/herdr_integration.rs`; see The bundled Herdr runtime).
Herdr learns an agent's session id from that integration, which lineage, the mailbox identity, labels and sleep all read, so it is installed when the agent is on, found and has made its own folder.
The record decides what Hide may remove: an integration that was already there when Hide first looked is the operator's and stays, on and off and when the machine leaves Hide, and only one of Hide's own is brought up to date.
A failed install fails that agent's row alone.
Every integration call is one owned child with a deadline on the kit worker or the helper, never under `Mutex<Runtime>`.
The fourteen agents earlier builds supported and this one does not (Copilot CLI, Amp, Factory Droid, Kiro, Qwen Code, Goose, Cline, Kilo Code, Crush, Junie, Augment, Kimi Code, Mistral Vibe and Gemini CLI) are retired once, by the record's ownership (`hide-kit/src/retired_agents.rs`): a piece the record names is taken out only where it carries Hide's own marker, a file the operator wrote or edited stays, and each piece leaves the record as it goes, so a second pass reads nothing and a piece that could not go is tried again.
Their results are `KitReport.legacy_retirement`, logged with the other retirement records and shown on no row.
Claude Code's and Codex's hook parts stay kit parts, because Memory, the pane diagnosis and `device_hook_status` read them, and their agent switch gates the part: off, the part is neither installed nor replaced, and switched off now it is taken out.
OpenCode's hook piece is Hide's plugin, and Pi's and omp's Hide's extension (`hide_agent_hooks::plugin::file` for the adapter's `PluginDialect`, `HookSupport::Plugin`, record codes `hook:opencode`, `hook:pi` and `hook:omp`), an agent piece rather than a kit part: its state is read from the file, and a pane's hook status for those agents reads that piece (`agent_hooks::kit_hook_status`) where Claude Code's and Codex's read the pane diagnosis.
The other agents' hooks are `hide_agent_hooks::guidance`, a smaller module with its own marker and per-agent file shape (Cursor's, agy's named `hide` block, and the retired agents' layouts kept for their removal), so the six-event runtime code and its counters never take a meaningless arm for an agent that does not feed them.
Claude Code's, Codex's and agy's pieces each own the agent's folder trust for the worktrees Hide made (`hide_agent_hooks::folder_trust`, added on the worktree's node before Hide starts the agent there, removed with the worktree, and removed whole when the agent is switched off), recorded so a removal takes out only Hide's ([agent-hooks.md: Folder trust for the worktrees Hide makes](agent-hooks.md#folder-trust-for-the-worktrees-hide-makes)).
agy's piece also owns the trusted, tool-refusing folder Hide AI runs agy in ([AI_PROVIDERS.md: Antigravity](AI_PROVIDERS.md#antigravity)); a pass that finds that folder missing or untrusted reads the piece as Stale and writes it again.
An agent is installed when one of its programs is found on the login shell's `PATH`, the daemon's own or the usual install folders; a folder it creates does not count, and an agent that is on and whose program is gone keeps its pieces, and its switch when the operator chose it, until the operator switches it off ([agent-hooks.md: Installed means the program is found](agent-hooks.md#installed-means-the-program-is-found)).
Detection, and the login shell it asks (once, then again only when a startup file changed), run only on the kit worker, the helper and Hide AI's own worker threads (which use the same search, `hide_platform::programs`), never on the render path or under the runtime lock, and under test the search is the fixture's `~/.local/bin` and a stand-in shell's answer, so a developer's own agents cannot decide a result.
A `KitSnapshot.agents` row keeps four things apart: whether the program was found (`availability`: `available` when it was, `not_installed` when it was not; `unsupported_system`, found but with nothing Hide can put down on that system, is produced by older device builds only: this build's kit never reports it, since every supported agent reads its skill folder on every system, and it stays on the wire as the transition path for a connected device still running an older build), the operator's switch (`enabled`, the record's choice or the adapter's default), whether the record holds the operator's own choice for the agent (`chosen`, `AgentReport.chosen`, absent and so false in a report from a build that predates it), and the pieces' own states; an agent that is on and whose program is gone is `not_installed` with `enabled: true`, and a shell lists it under Installed with its switch only when `chosen` is true, so an agent that is on only by default and was never found is not listed as installed, and one the operator switched on keeps the switch that takes Hide's pieces out.
`KitSnapshot.checking` is true from the moment a read of the machine's kit is queued until it answers, and `KitSnapshot.check_failed` is `read_failed` after a read that could not be made (a device whose helper did not answer) and clears at the next answer; the last report stays in place beside either, and the cause stays in the diagnostic log (`Runtime::set_kit_checking`, `runtime::tests::device_kit`).
`hide_kit::Scope` carries the parts to restore, the two agent choices and the operator's one-shot request to turn Codex's shared server off (`codex_daemon_off`, which merges by OR), and two queued requests for one machine merge with the later choice winning for an agent both name; the device helper receives them in `kit` `reinstall` (since helper protocol 21, whose `codex_daemon_off` field is absent unless the request was made; a helper on another protocol is refused when it connects, so an older helper never receives a turn-off that would leave the running daemon up).
Removing a machine does not touch the Codex setting.
Every Codex start Hide makes passes `--no-daemon` first when that machine's kit read a Codex that has the setting: `wire::agent_start_params` takes the machine's `codex_launch::CodexDaemon`, which every start path (a task's agent, a reopen, a fork, a wake) carries from the runtime, so no path can leave it out; a missing, failed or pending capability refuses the start with an instruction to recheck the target machine’s kit in Settings.
The report carries that capability as `KitReport.codex_daemon` (`KitSnapshot.codex_daemon`), read from the target's Codex binary: `true` when it has the setting, `false` when it is a confirmed older Codex that starts no daemon and so gets no flag, and no answer when there is no Codex or the read failed.
The report also carries whether that Codex starts the shared daemon on now (`KitReport.codex_daemon_on`, read with the capability and never changed by a pass) and whether a daemon answers now (`KitReport.codex_daemon_running`, from `codex app-server daemon version`, which never starts one); either one is what a Codex pane reads as the reason it is not connected (`KitSnapshot::shares_codex_server`), so a machine whose setting an earlier Hide turned off while its daemon kept running still offers the turn-off (PRD codex-daemon-apply D-07, B9).
After a turn-off that answered `stop_failed`, a read that cannot tell whether a daemon answers keeps the machine on the shared server, so the retry stays on offer until a read says no daemon answers (B7).
The control socket decides only the read behind a Codex pane's reason (B9): a failed `codex app-server daemon version` reads as no daemon only while the daemon's control socket under the account's `CODEX_HOME` (`app-server-control/app-server-control.sock`, Codex's observed layout, named once in `codex_daemon.rs`) is gone, and with the socket still there, or an answer Hide cannot read, the read is unknown, so a stale socket a crashed daemon left reads as unknown to the pane until Codex removes it.
The confirmed turn-off never decides from the socket whether to stop, so a Codex whose socket lives elsewhere (another version or platform, a relocated `CODEX_HOME`) is still asked to stop; `daemon stop` answers `notRunning` for a crashed daemon and leaves its stale socket in place (measured on Codex 0.160).
An answer about the daemon Hide cannot read reads as no answer, and the core logs it as a `kit` `codex_daemon.unreadable` record when it appears or changes.
The operator's own request to turn it off (`codex_daemon_disable { device_id }`) is the only change: `Runtime::request_codex_daemon_disable` queues `Scope::codex_daemon_off()` on the machine's kit worker, `hide_kit::apply` runs `codex features disable daemon_auto_start` through `hide_agent_hooks::codex_daemon::turn_off` before anything else in the pass, reads the setting back, stops the daemon that is still running through `codex_daemon::stop`, and answers in `KitReport.codex_daemon_off` (done, or a failure code with Codex's words kept for the log), and `KitSnapshot.codex_daemon_off` is `pending` from the request until that report.
A disable Codex refuses or does not answer leaves the setting as it was and stops nothing; a stop that fails after the setting went off answers `stop_failed`, and the same request again finds the setting off and only stops; `unreachable` (Hide quit or the device's helper went away) can come before or after the setting went off, so its line claims neither.
A call that never ran to a report ends the request as `unreachable`.
A device takes the request only over the helper connection the operator confirmed it on: a request for a device whose helper is not connected is refused into the log (`codex_daemon_off.refused`), a connection that ends before its worker takes the request drops it from the queue as `unreachable` (`codex_daemon_off.dropped`), and each call that carries it is a numbered run whose answer settles the request only while that run is current, so a late answer from an ended connection changes nothing (`Runtime::drop_queued_codex_daemon_off`, `DeviceKitCall::daemon_off_run`).
A Reopen of a pane Hide cannot hear (`pane_reopen { pane_id }`) is core-owned too: `herdr-core/src/pane_reopen.rs` refuses everything knowable first, ends the agent and starts it again in the same pane with its own resume arguments through the same two calls agent sleep uses, on a worker outside the runtime lock, and `Runtime::pane_reopens` holds one entry per pane so a second press while one runs starts nothing (`docs/status-model.md`, Not connected, and what fixes it).
The flag is a transition path that goes away once openai/codex#48500 runs a daemon's hooks in each window's environment.

This Mac's kit runs on `crate::kit::KitPump`, a core-owned thread rather than the session-sync coordinator, because the kit installs whether or not this Mac's Herdr answers: the launch pass first, then a Reinstall, then a re-read every five seconds while Settings is observed and once when a Settings tab showing the kit opens (`kit_check`); it takes its job under `Mutex<Runtime>` and, with the lock released, sends it to the core's own node as the same `kit` call a device answers, so every file write, Herdr call and child process happens on the node (`hide-host/src/kit.rs`, `KitPlace::Bundled`).
The node, not the core, knows where its kit's parts are: hided gives its own node the package's resources folder, and the hook diagnosis the kit and the coordinator publish is the node's `hook_diagnosis` call.
The core's own node, and the node of a machine that dials its core, read where their kit comes from off the program they run (`KitPlace::of_program`): a desktop package's resources (`hide_kit::bundled_kit_dir`), or a whole build Hide placed in the account's own helper root (`~/.hide/host-helper/<16 hex>/`, with `hide` and `hide-agent-hooks` beside `hided`; `hide_host::kit::placed_build`), which is what a moved core's login item runs, on a machine that may have no app at all.
A build copied anywhere else, or a lone `hided` in such a folder, is no build Hide placed and installs nothing into the account, so a development build copied into a folder of that name, or a fixture's build while HOME is the operator's, cannot write the operator's hooks.
From a build folder the kit installs from `<root>/current`, which it first points at the running build, and removes no build and points no Herdr copy, since the move and the core update own those and an update keeps the build it may go back to (`hide_host::kit::run_build`, `adopt_build`).
Any other `hided`, a Cargo build directory among them, has a standalone node, which refuses the kit; the row says why (B11).
The rule is where the program came from, not whether an app is installed: it keeps a development build, whose folder the next build deletes, from writing hooks that name it, and a moved core's program is the build the move placed, the one a device's helper already installed from.
Stopping the core closes its own node, which raises the kit's stop flag before the worker is joined.
A device's kit runs on its helper through the helper protocol's `kit` call (`hide-host/src/kit.rs`: `apply`, `reinstall`, `status`, `remove`), always for the helper root the running helper was installed under; one worker per device makes those calls one at a time (`runtime/kit.rs`, `crate::kit::spawn_device_worker`): the connection pass after every `host.ready`, a Reinstall, and a read when Settings opens.
A machine that dials this core, the one a core moved off, has its kit kept the same way through its link, with no consent and no helper upload: the core asks with the command folder a device gets by default (`~/.local/bin` in that machine's home, which `HIDE_HOST_CLI_DIR` moves) and names no Herdr socket, so the node installs for the Herdr it bridges, from the app it runs (`Services.kit`); removing its row takes nothing off it, since it registers again on its next link.
Each link's kit work ends with that link (`Env.stop`), so a node that dials again starts its kit with a stop of its own.
Two registrations of one account can connect at the same time: the helper upload stages each file under a name no other connection uses, keeps a final file that already holds the expected bytes, and takes a helper folder another connection created first, and the account lock orders the two applies.
An answer a worker brings back after its registration went or its removal started changes nothing (`ingest_device_kit_answer`), so a slow install never brings back a device the operator removed.
A device without consent, with an outdated one, or on a platform this build does not carry installs nothing and its row says why (B17, B21).
Removing a device with its helper connected takes Hide's parts and the helper root off it on that device's worker, after any kit call already running there, and closes the helper connection afterwards; a device added again waits to connect until that is done.
Without a connected helper, the registration goes and a `kit` `device.kit_left` record names what stayed (B24).
The hooks, the `hide` link and the helper root belong to the device account rather than to one registration, so while another registration's consent is bound to the same user and host key, removing one of them leaves the kit for the other, records the same `device.kit_left`, and the device row's `kit.shares_account_with` names that registration for the confirmation.
Each machine's report reaches the shell as `DeviceSnapshot.kit` (`components` with `kit_component_id` and `kit_component_state` from `contracts/snapshot-wire-enums.json`, `busy`, `unavailable`, `offers_reinstall`), and every apply writes one `kit` `apply.completed` record naming each part's outcome.
A device's agent panes are judged by the same `hide_agent_hooks::diagnosis::instrumentation` as a local pane, from the pane's hook tokens (`RemoteSessionSnapshot.pane_hook_tokens`, not sent to the shell) and that device's kit (`agent_hooks::device_hook_status`), in `Runtime::derive_device_session` (D-21).
The kit part read is the one for the dialect that counts for the pane's agent (`agent_hooks::kit_hook_status`): Claude Code's or Codex's hook part, or, for an OpenCode, Grok or Cursor pane, the hook piece of that agent's row (`KitSnapshot.agents`), on this machine as on a device; a change of that piece re-judges the local panes (`runtime/kit.rs`, `pane_judgement_inputs`).
`desktop/e2e/device-kit.spec.ts` proves a device's kit over a real SSH connection: the install, each part's row, the installed hook's SessionStart in a device pane, a part the operator took out staying out until Reinstall, and removal leaving another tool's entries and retained records.
The isolated sshd logs in as the account running the suite, so `desktop/e2e/device-home.ts` proves its private HOME before registering the device; otherwise kit writes would reach the operator's own files.

### Device catalogs

A device's projects are grouped by the same rule as this machine's (`device_catalog.rs`): a project is a repository identified by its main worktree on that device, and each checkout a tab sits in is a row under it, so two Herdr workspaces in one repository are one project, two workspaces with tabs in one folder are one checkout row, and a workspace whose tabs sit in two repositories is split between them.
The facts come from the device's helper (`Call::Project`, answered by `hide_project::facts` there), asked on a worker once per directory; the runtime keeps Herdr's session as it came (`device_raw_sessions`) and publishes the grouped one.
A new connection asks about every directory again and re-reads every repository's worktrees, whether its helper or its Herdr session arrives first, and what the last connection answered stays until the new answer replaces it (`reread_device_facts`), through a Retry as well; removing the device forgets it (`forget_device_directories`), and so does a helper whose machine is no longer the one the operator allowed (`IdentityChanged`).
The answers are kept while the device is, so a pane back in a folder it left is grouped at once; past `MAX_KNOWN_PATHS` directories, or repositories, only those the session still shows are kept, and the prune is logged.
A pane's `hide` command is bound to its checkout when it starts and refused as `pane_changed` if that changed before it ran, so forgetting the facts would regroup the device's panes by folder for the moment the helper takes to answer and refuse every command that straddled it.
Regrouping happens whenever a device's facts, worktrees, registrations or kit change, and the raw rows carry no lineage, so a regrouped session is published only after the lineage pass across every machine (`refresh_agent_lineage`) has put back what a subtree close waits on (`close_descendant_pane_ids`).
Ids stay scoped to the device: a project is `remote:<device>:project:<sha256(device, root)>`, so the same path or repository on two devices is two projects, and a checkout is keyed by its folder (`remote:<device>:checkout:<hash of its root>`, `device_catalog::checkout_id`), so it names no Herdr workspace and keeps its id as workspaces open and close there.
What a command sends to the host is read from the session instead of parsed from an id (`remote_control`): `create_tab` and a purpose go to the checkout's owner (see Checkout owner workspace), opened first when none is; `focus_workspace` brings the checkout's most recently used tab forward: the core remembers the last tab the device's Herdr had in focus under each checkout (`device_recent_tabs`, as `visible_tab_ids` does here), and with nothing remembered or the tab gone, the owner's active tab, else the first workspace's; it opens the owner only for a checkout with no tab; a tab or pane command goes to the workspace holding that tab.
A directory the helper has not answered for is a row named by its folder (never by a Herdr workspace label), holding every workspace in that folder, and `status.remote[].catalog` says why (`resolving`, `unavailable` with the reason, or the folders it refused); it is never grouped into a repository by a guess and never read on this machine, and a registration on another device is never inspected here.
Whether such a folder is a Git checkout is unknown, so a request that would open its owner is refused (`remote.control.checkout_unconfirmed`) rather than opened as the wrong kind; an owner already open there still takes its tabs.
View areas are stored by device and path and file tabs are re-found by path, and a fold saved under an old, workspace-keyed id is renamed to the folder-keyed id when the device's session names that workspace (`device_catalog::legacy_checkout_ids`), so the change loses nothing a workspace-keyed id held.
A device's worktrees are read by its helper too (`Call::Worktrees`, the same `hide_host::worktrees::read` this machine runs in process, keeping what it measured in the device's own state folder across its restarts), on a worker after the device's facts answer and again after each task there, and are carried onto its rows by path as the helper reported them (`device_catalog::apply_worktrees`), so a same path on this machine never lends a device its branch, dirt or gate.
Creating or deleting a device's worktree takes that device's Herdr and helper together (`WorktreeTarget::device`): the branch is checked (`Call::BranchCheck`) and the new directory resolved (`Call::Directory`) by the helper before Herdr's `worktree.create` runs there, a deletion is gated on the device's facts, closes that device's panes by Herdr's own ids and removes on the helper, and the receipts (`task_operation`, `worktree_removal`) carry `device_id`; the branch-description mirror of a purpose is written on this machine only.
A device's registrations live with this machine's in `ui_state.workspace_registrations`, keyed by the device's project id, and are carried onto its grouped session (`device_catalog::apply_registrations`): a registered project Herdr has no workspace in keeps its row with its main checkout, keyed by its folder, and no tabs, and only a registered row carries a pin and the panes a removal would close.
Registering on a device asks its helper (`Call::Registrable`, `hide_host::register::check`), which judges the folder against that device's own home, `~` included, and names the project it belongs to; hided passes a device's `create_workspace` through instead of applying this machine's home, and nothing is created on the device.
Removing a device's project closes its panes through that device's Herdr by their own ids and then removes only the registration, as on this machine.
Opening a registered device project Herdr has no workspace in, from its row or a new tab, opens its owner at the registered folder through that device's remote control, keyed by the folder while in flight.

A device's checkouts are found in the same catalog lookup as this machine's (`catalog_checkout`: the navigator, then each device's Herdr session), and the checkout in front is the selected device's own focus (`front_checkout`), so a file opened on a device lands in the checkout that asked and showing it moves no focus on this machine.
Each device authenticates as its own ssh config says: an absent `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` stops only a device whose config names no `IdentityFile` or `IdentityAgent`, and that device's row says so.
A local path is judged inside its checkout after resolving the folder that holds it, so a shell that spells `/var` for `/private/var` still opens it, while the document keeps the path as the shell sent it.

### Home

Each device has one Home, `~/hide` in its account's home directory (PRD home-device-rail D-01..D-08, `runtime/home.rs`, `hide-host/src/home.rs`).
Home is a registration, not a node type: a pinned `WorkspaceRegistration` flagged `home`, carried onto its row as `WorkspaceSnapshot::is_home`, so tabs, agent starts, lineage and Browser displays use the plain-folder pipeline unchanged; the shell draws no sidebar row for it and never lists or counts it as a project.
Nothing is written on a device until its Home is first used: a Home start or a new tab there (`agent_start_in_checkout` with `home`) asks that device's helper for `home_sync` (`Call::HomeSync`, helper protocol 11; this machine's runs in process) on a worker with the runtime lock released, and only when it answers does the core record the registration and open the tab in Home's Folder owner.
`home_sync` makes `~/hide` holding a marker file (`.hide-home.json`) that names every link Hide made, one link per registered project of that device (named by its folder, with the parent folder's name when two share one, then `-2`, `-3`), an `AGENTS.md` Hide rewrites on each sync and a `CLAUDE.md` link to it.
A `~/hide` without the marker, an empty folder included, is the operator's: nothing is written and the start is refused as `home.conflict` with what to do; a new Home is staged beside it and renamed in whole, so it is never seen without its marker.
Only an entry that is still the link Hide recorded is ever removed, and nothing is followed through a link; a link whose project left the registrations or whose folder is gone is dropped at the next sync and logged (`home.link_dropped`), never shown.
Link names are told apart as a case-insensitive disk tells them apart, and never take the name of Home's own files; a project whose path has a control character, which would break `AGENTS.md` and which Herdr could not pass as a root, and a link the disk still refuses (a name too long) skip that project with a log line (`home.link_skipped`) rather than failing the Home, and the folder and its files are owner-only (0700 and 0600), since they name every project of the account.
A Home links at most 256 projects; more is a reported failure, and so is a helper whose `HOME` is unset or not absolute.
Once a device has a Home, every registration change there (every one is persisted, which is where `request_home_link_syncs` runs) sends one background sync with the new set; one runs per device at a time, a change during it is caught up when it answers, and the answer reaches only the diagnostic log.
A set counts as sent only once it is: launching Hide writes nothing, the first registration change after it syncs, a change that found a device's helper not ready asks that helper to connect once and is sent when it is ready, with no retry on each later write, and a set that failed is not sent again until the registrations change or a Home start runs.
The helper holds an exclusive `flock` on the account's home folder for each sync, so a start's sync and a background one, or two Macs' helpers on one device, run one after the other and each reads the marker the other wrote; a sync waits at most 20 s for another and then answers `busy`.
They run in the order they take the lock, not the order they were sent, so each answer records the set it applied and a newer set that ran first is sent again.
A Home agent is started with each linked project's real folder as `--add-dir <folder>` (both CLIs take it, one flag per folder), so its sandbox lets it edit a project through its link; the edit shows as that project's checkout change, and the agent row stays under Home.
Removing a device, or losing its connection, leaves its `~/hide` and its agents on it; registering it again continues them.
Home does not bump the consent contract on its own (PRD home-device-rail D-05): the helper's scope includes `~/hide`, its links and instruction files for devices that already allowed it, and the add-device wording names Home.
The later device-parity contract is 3 (D-12), and a contract-2 device carries its consent forward on connection without another prompt (D-13).

### The desktop host

`desktop/` is the Electron app this repository ships: it shows the web shell hided serves in its own window, holds no UI state and embeds no daemon.
It meets the daemon only through the `hide` CLI: `hide connect` (reuse the daemon the state file names, or start one) and the attach-only `hide status --json`, so there is one discovery path and never a second daemon.
The CLI is found in this order, every tried path logged once (`desktop/src/main/cli.ts`): `HIDE_CLI_PATH` (an unusable value ends the search), this worktree's newest `target/{debug,release}/hide` when run unpackaged, the packaged app's own `Contents/Resources/hide` when run packaged, PATH, the CLI that last attached, the login shell's PATH (packaged only), then the install folders (`wellKnownDirs`): `~/.local/bin`, `/opt/homebrew/bin` and `/usr/local/bin` on macOS and Linux.
On Windows the CLI and the bundled Herdr are `hide.exe` and `herdr.exe`, PATH is split on `;` and an entry without a drive (`\tools`) is skipped, as `hide_platform::host::find_program` skips it, and no login shell is asked: Windows has none, and an app started from the Start menu already has the account's PATH.
The Windows install folders are each installer's own, for the programs Hide runs: `~\.local\bin` (`USERPROFILE`; Claude Code's installer puts `claude.exe` there, the kit puts `hide.cmd` there for the operator's shell, while the host uses its packaged `resources/hide.exe` directly), `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\OpenAI\Codex\bin` and `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\Herdr\bin` (the Codex and Herdr installers), `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\Git\cmd` and `%ProgramFiles%\Git\cmd` (Git for Windows for the account or the machine), `%APPDATA%\npm` (npm's global folder, where `npm install -g` puts `claude` and `codex`), `%ProgramFiles%\GitHub CLI` and `%ProgramFiles%\nodejs`; a folder whose variable is unset is left out rather than guessed, and `env.locations_missing` names the keys once at start.
An app opened from Finder or the Dock gets launchd's PATH (`/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin`), not the operator's, so a packaged app normally stops at its own bundled CLI; the steps after PATH exist for a bundle that is missing it: the login shell (`$SHELL`, or the account's login shell in the user database when SHELL is unset, run `-ilc` through `env` with shell-env's Oh My Zsh guards; with neither the step is skipped and `cli.login_path` says `no_shell`, bounded at ten seconds, VS Code's default for the same question) costs seconds on a real rc file and once timed out at five, so it is asked only when PATH and the remembered CLI both miss, and asked again on the next search when it failed.
A CLI found on PATH, through the login shell or in an install directory is remembered in `cli-path.json` under the profile once it attaches, and read back as a candidate that must still be executable; an override, a worktree build or the app's own bundled CLI is never remembered, and an unreadable file is a diagnostic, not a stop.
A packaged app also passes `HERDR_BIN_PATH=<Contents/Resources>/herdr` to every `hide` child it starts, so a pane terminal attach uses the exact Herdr binary the app shipped (see The bundled Herdr runtime).
An inherited `HERDR_BIN_PATH` is passed through unchanged only as an explicit override, which is one that arrives without `HERDR_PANE_ID` (`desktop/src/main/herdr.ts`).
Herdr exports both into every pane, and the path it exports is its own server's, which dies when the bundle that started that server is replaced; a packaged app opened from a pane (`open /Applications/hide.app`) would inherit it, and `hide connect` would refuse it as `start_failed` on every attempt (issue 214).
So a value that arrives with `HERDR_PANE_ID` is Herdr's, not the operator's: the packaged app replaces it with its bundled binary and records the replaced path as `replaced_pane_herdr` on `discovery.start`.
An override typed on a pane's command line cannot be told apart from Herdr's own export and is replaced too; an unpackaged host has no bundled binary and passes whatever it inherited.
Every `hide` child also receives PATH with the install folders appended after the inherited entries, with duplicates removed; a daemon newly started by the app inherits that PATH and can find an installed `git`, `gh`, agent CLI or Herdr even when Finder launched the app with launchd's bare PATH.
When PATH is absent, the child starts with the system's own folders (`systemDirs`) so setting it for user tools does not lose system commands: `/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin`, and on Windows the default system Path under `%SystemRoot%` (`System32`, the Windows folder, `Wbem`, Windows PowerShell, OpenSSH), none when SystemRoot is unset.
Attaching to an already-running daemon does not change that daemon's environment.
The host is a small state machine (`desktop/src/main/host.ts`): `connecting` shows the host's own status page and becomes `attached` or `failed` with one of `cli_missing`, `start_failed`, `no_response`, `other_build`, `state_refused` (with the file the daemon refused) `core_newer` (titled as an app to update) or `update_failed` (titled as a core not updated), each naming the core's machine and both builds, and a Retry; `attached` polls `/health` every two seconds without forking, leaves the shell for the `core_newer` or `update_failed` page when a node's health names that reason with its `builds`, and becomes `lost` after two misses, leaving the shell's own disconnected state on screen; `lost` asks `hide status --json` every three seconds and loads the next daemon's URL when it names a different one.
